An Information-Theoretic Approach to Generalization Theory
Borja Rodríguez-Gálvez, Ragnar Thobaben, and Mikael Skoglund KTH Royal Institute of Technology borjabrg12@gmail.com, {ragnart, skoglund}@kth.se

Abstract
In this manuscript, we investigate the in-distribution generalization of machine learning algorithms, focusing on establishing rigorous upper bounds on the generalization error . We depart from traditional complexity-based approaches by introducing and analyzing information-theoretic bounds that quantify the dependence between a learning algorithm and the training data . We consider two categories of generalization guarantees: • Guarantees in expectation . These bounds measure performance in the average case. Here, the dependence between the algorithm and the data is often captured by the mutual information or other information measures based on f f -divergences . While these measures offer an intuitive interpretation, they might overlook the geometry of the algorithm’s hypothesis class . To address this limitation, we introduce bounds using the Wasserstein distance , which incorporates geometric considerations at the cost of being mathematically more involved. Furthermore, we propose a structured, systematic method to derive bounds capturing the dependence between the algorithm and an individual datum, and between the algorithm and subsets of the training data, conditioned on knowing the rest of the data. These types of bounds provide deeper insights, as we demonstrate by applying them to derive generalization error bounds for the stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics algorithm. • PAC-Bayesian guarantees . These bounds measure the performance level with high probability. Here, the dependence between the algorithm and the data is often measured by the relative entropy . We establish connections between the Seeger–Langford and Catoni’s bounds, revealing that the former is optimized by the Gibbs posterior . Additionally, we introduce novel, tighter bounds for various types of loss functions, including those with a bounded range, cumulant generating function, moment, or variance. To achieve this, we introduce a new technique to optimize parameters in probabilistic statements. We also study the limitations of these approaches . We present a counter-example where most of the existing (relative entropy-based) information-theoretic bounds fail and where traditional approaches do not. Finally, we explore the relationship between privacy and generalization . We show that algorithms with a bounded maximal leakage generalize. Moreover, for discrete data, we derive new bounds for differentially private algorithms that vanish as the number of samples increases, thus guaranteeing their generalization even with a constant privacy parameter. This contrasts with previous bounds in the literature, which require the privacy parameter to decrease with the number of samples to ensure generalization. Keywords: Generalization, Information-Theoretic Bounds

Chapter 1
Introduction

Currently,
machine learning
silently shapes the world around us: from the curated content we see on social media to high-stakes industries such as healthcare, finance, or autonomous driving
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
]
. Machine learning algorithms are used to analyze data for early disease detection, fraud prevention, or steering the wheels, breaking, and speeding up autonomous vehicles.
Therefore, it is crucial to have a robust theoretical understanding of
when
and
why
these algorithms will perform appropriately in critical situations.

1
Background

A machine learning algorithm (or just a
learning algorithm
) is a mechanism that observes a sequence of instances of a problem (often referred to as a
training set
) and returns a hypothesis for the said problem. For example, given a training set of medical records containing two biological markers and a label stating if a person has a certain disease or not, a learning algorithm returns a model that predicts the presence of that disease from a medical record. In this case, the hypothesis is the mapping between medical records and the presence of the disease characterized by the predictive model, as depicted in
Figure

1.1
.

The hypothesis returned by the algorithm may perform really well in the training set. However, this does not necessarily mean that it will perform well on new, unseen data. Consider, for example, the complex hypothesis from
Figure

1.1
; while it can perfectly explain the relationship between the medical markers and the presence of disease for all instances in the training data, it fails when it comes to describing unseen data from the same distribution.
The field of
generalization theory
focuses on finding mathematical guarantees on the performance of the hypotheses returned by learning algorithms applied to new, unseen data.

Figure 1.1
:
Fictitious relationship between two biological markers and the presence (orange crosses) or absence (blue dots) of a disease. The marks without a box represent the training set available to the algorithm, and the ones with a box represent new, unseen data. On the left, there is a complex hypothesis that overfits the training set. On the right, there is a simple hypothesis that performs slightly worse in the training set but generalizes to new instances.

Often, we describe the performance of an algorithm through a
loss function
. In the example above, the loss function returns 0 if the algorithm correctly determines if a person has a disease from their medical record, and 1 otherwise. Therefore, smaller values of the loss function mean a better performance. The aforementioned
generalization guarantees
are often in the form of an upper bound on the expected value of the loss function on samples from the problem distribution, also known as the
population risk
. Alternatively, the guarantees come as an upper bound on the
generalization error
, which is defined as the difference between the population risk and the average loss function on the instances from the training set, or
empirical risk
.

When studying the generalization of learning algorithms, we may consider either that the new data come from the same distribution (
in-distribution generalization
) or from a close, albeit different distribution (
out-of-distribution generalization
). For example, the medical records from patients from the same demographic collected in the same hospital following the same protocol may be considered from the same distribution. However, the medical records from patients from a different demographic are often considered from a different distribution. As an example, the blood oxygen levels of people living in high-altitude cities are lower than those of people living at sea level. This change in distribution is often known as a
distribution shift
. In this monograph, and thus throughout the forthcoming text, we will focus only on in-distribution generalization.

There are two main types of generalization guarantees:

•

Guarantees in expectation.
These guarantees are the less specific of the two. They ensure that the average performance of the hypotheses returned by the algorithm is never smaller than a certain amount.

•

High-probability guarantees.
These guarantees ensure that, with high probability, the performance of the hypotheses returned by the algorithm is never smaller than a certain amount. In the context of information-theoretic generalization, this kind of guarantees are referred to as
PAC-Bayesian guarantees
.

Classical approaches to studying the generalization of learning algorithms focus on the complexity of the class of hypotheses considered by the algorithm. This refers to the richness of the possible hypotheses the algorithm can select. The idea behind these approaches is that hypotheses from more complex classes can fit the training set really well, but they may potentially
overfit
, that is, they may adapt to the sampling noise for the specific data they observe, which may differ from future instances. On the other hand, hypotheses from simpler classes cannot adapt to the sampling noise and need to focus on the high signal patterns in the data, representative of the data distribution. The downside of the more simple classes is that their lack of richness may mean that none of their hypotheses can describe well the patterns in the training set. However, they still have a small
generalization error
; their bad performance in the training set is predictive of a bad performance on new, unseen data. See
Figure

1.1
for an example of this dichotomy.

An important feature of classical approaches like those based on
uniform convergence
or on the
Rademacher complexity
is that they provide generalization guarantees for the
whole hypothesis class
considered by the algorithm. This can be too restrictive, as within a complex hypothesis class, there may still be hypotheses that generalize well. A step away from this restriction is given by the
algorithmic stability framework
, which gives generalization guarantees for
stable algorithms
, that is, algorithms that return similar hypotheses when presented with a similar training set. In this way, algorithmic stability can guarantee the generalization of algorithms that consider complex hypotheses, as long as they are consistent in returning these hypotheses for similar training sets. Often, the concept of “similar training sets” only takes into account every possible value of the observed data, however unlikely, rendering the framework too restrictive for certain situations. Finally, the framework of
information-theoretic generalization
gives guarantees that are
specific
to both the algorithm and the data distribution. The idea is to use an
information measure
to capture the dependence between the hypothesis returned by the algorithm and the training set. Intuitively, the more the hypothesis depends on the specific instances it has seen, the more it overfits and the less it generalizes. This individualized approach, while more nuanced, allows for stronger theoretical results in situations where the previous approaches fail.

The information-theoretic framework to generalization elucidates a
connection between privacy and generalization
. An algorithm is private if the hypothesis it returns does not leak or rely on much information about the instances used for training. Therefore, the more private an algorithm is, the smaller the dependence between the hypothesis returned by the algorithm and training set, and the better it generalizes.

2
Overview of the Monograph

In this monograph, we study the information-theoretic framework for generalization. We introduce the topic in this chapter, which is followed by two other chapters to prepare the reader for the later content.

In
Chapter

2
, we detail the notation and definitions necessary to follow the monograph. Particularly, we describe the elements of probability and information theory that we use throughout the monograph. Moreover, we discuss the concepts of convex conjugacy and Wasserstein distances and present a straightforward extension of the Bobkov–Götze theorem connecting the Wasserstein distance to the relative entropy that will be of use later on.

In
Chapter

3
, we introduce the notation and definitions related to generalization theory and go into greater detail about the classical frameworks to study it. We use this chapter to further motivate and contextualize the information-theoretic framework to generalization with respect to the other alternatives.

Once the reader has the proper context of the elements of information theory, probability, and generalization theory, we commence with our investigation. Our study may be separated into three main blocks: the study of guarantees in expectation, the study of PAC-Bayesian guarantees, and the connections between privacy and generalization. Each of these topics has a dedicated chapter to them, namely
Chapters

4
,
5
, and
6
. Nonetheless, the analysis and findings of each block are interconnected. Below, in
Sections

2.1
,
2.2
, and
2.3
, we outline the scope and contents of these chapters.

Finally, in
Chapter

7
, we reflect on the state of the art and the results from the monograph and we discuss potential future avenues of research.

2.1
Guarantees in Expectation

Xu and Raginsky [5]
, based on
[
6
]
, sparked the interest in information-theoretic generalization guarantees in expectation. Their result captured the dependence between the hypothesis returned by the algorithm and the training set using mutual information. After that,
Bu et al. [7]
showed that considering the information that the returned hypothesis contains about each individual datum provides a better characterization of the expected generalization performance. Similarly,
Negrea et al. [8]
realized that there are situations where considering the information contained in a subset of the training data, given that the rest of the data is known, is beneficial to characterize the generalization of certain algorithms. Later,
Steinke and Zakynthinou [9]
discussed how instead of studying the information the hypothesis contains about the training data, studying if the training data can be
identified
from the hypothesis was more appropriate.

Throughout
Chapter

4
, especially in
Sections

13
,
14
,
15
, and
16
, we organize all these different observations into a structured, systematic methodology to derive information-theoretic guarantees in expectation. Furthermore, this methodology is agnostic to the measure considered to characterize the dependence between the algorithm’s returned hypothesis and the training set.

Despite the fact that the methodology can be made agnostic to the metric used to capture the dependence between the hypothesis and the training data, traditionally, the relative entropy and the mutual information are often chosen for this endeavor. This is due to their simplicity, interpretability, and mathematical tractability.

In
Sections

17
,
16
, and
19
, we delve deeper into the choice of this metric. First, in
Section

16
, we show that the relative entropy-based guarantees are tight in non-trivial scenarios. Then, in
Section

17
, we demonstrate how the relative entropy-based guarantees can be employed to gain a better understanding of the generalization of noisy, iterative algorithms such as the stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics. Second, in
Section

19
, we show that there are situations where the relative entropy-based guarantees cannot predict the generalization of an algorithm, while other frameworks like uniform stability can.

Usually, to capture the hypothesis’ dependence on the training data, we measure the discrepancy between the algorithm’s output hypothesis distribution and a reference distribution that does not depend on the data. As mentioned above, this discrepancy is often determined by the relative entropy, albeit there exist other works that consider other
f
-divergences or the Rényi divergence
[
10
,
11
,
12
]
. Unfortunately, all these divergences lead to vacuous guarantees if the reference distribution places no density on a hypothesis that may be returned by the algorithm, even if it very rarely returns it. The reason for this failure is that these divergences are agnostic to the geometry, and their value solely depends on the ratio of densities of the algorithm’s output and the reference distributions.

In
Section

16
, we study how to capture the algorithm’s returned hypothesis dependence on the training set using the Wasserstein distance. This metric takes the geometry of the hypothesis’ space into consideration and does not depend on the ratio of the densities of the algorithm’s output and the reference distributions.

Throughout
Chapter

4
, we obtain other findings and insights. For example, the development of new generalization guarantees when the performance metric considered has heavy tails.

2.2
PAC-Bayesian Guarantees

The study of information-theoretic high-probability generalization guarantees can be traced back to
Shawe-Taylor et al. [13]
or, even earlier, to the minimum description length principle from
Rissanen [14]
and its relationship with generalization from, for example, Barron, Cover, Kearns, and others
[
15
,
16
,
17
]
. Nonetheless, the field started to gain traction with the seminal works from
McAllester [18]
,
McAllester [19]
,
McAllester [20]
, where he introduced the nomenclature of PAC-Bayesian generalization.

Similarly to what we mentioned above in
Section

2.1
, the main result from
McAllester [18]
,
McAllester [19]
,
McAllester [20]
related the generalization of a learning algorithm to the relative entropy of the returned hypothesis distribution (also known as
posterior
) with respect to a reference measure (also known as
prior
) independent of the training data. His results were restricted to settings where the performance was evaluated by a loss function with a bounded range. This guarantee presents a
slow rate
with respect to the number of samples, that is, the generalization error is inversely proportional to the square root of the number of samples
(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})
. Moreover, it is of
high-probability
, that is, it depends logarithmically with the probability of failure
(\log\nicefrac{{1}}{{\beta}})
.

For losses with a bounded range, Seeger and Langford
[
21
,
22
]
and
Catoni [23]
,
Catoni [24]
improved upon the works from
McAllester [18]
,
McAllester [19]
,
McAllester [20]
and developed some of the tightest guarantees for this setting. Unfortunately, these results have the shortcoming of being rather involved and/or not having a closed-form solution for the posterior characterizing the optimal algorithm (that is, the algorithm that optimizes the generalization guarantee). After that, there have been some other developments bringing more interpretable guarantees
[
25
,
26
,
27
]
, albeit at the cost of providing a worse characterization of the generalization error.

In
Section

3
, we recover a connection between the results from Seeger and Langford
[
21
,
22
]
and
Catoni [23]
,
Catoni [24]
. Using this connection, we then obtain an equivalent guarantee that highlights that the bound has a
fast rate
of
\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}}
. Moreover, this guarantee reveals that the Gibbs posterior characterizes the algorithm that optimizes the bound from Seeger and Langford
[
21
,
22
]
.

When the loss has an unbounded range, there have been several attempts at extending
McAllester [18]
,
McAllester [19]
,
McAllester [20]
’s theory
[
28
,
12
,
29
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
33
,
34
,
35
,
36
]
. However, most of these guarantees are either not of high probability (that is, they depend
linearly
or
polynomially
with the probability of failure
\beta
), contain terms that often make the guarantees non-decreasing with the number of samples
n
, decrease at a slower rate than the results from
McAllester [18]
,
McAllester [19]
,
McAllester [20]
when restricted to the case of losses with a bounded range, or depend on parameters that need to be chosen before the draw of the training data.

In
Section

4
, we devote our attention to these shortcomings. First, in
Section

4.1
, we obtain a PAC-Bayesian Chernoff analogue for losses with light tails, that is, with a bounded cumulant generating function. Then, in
Section

4.2
, we build on the previous results to obtain PAC-Bayesian guarantees for losses with a bounded raw moment and a bounded variance. We emphasize that all the guarantees developed in
Sections

4.1
and
4.2
are of high-probability, improve upon (or are equivalent) to the bound from
McAllester [18]
,
McAllester [19]
,
McAllester [20]
when restricted to losses with a bounded range, and are parameter independent (or, in case to be parameterized, they hold simultaneously for all values of the parameter, permitting a free optimization).

Finally, it is worth mentioning that the PAC-Bayesian results from
McAllester [18]
,
McAllester [19]
,
McAllester [20]
, even though they hold with high probability with respect to the training set, they provide guarantees for the average hypothesis returned by the algorithm. That is, the guarantees state that, with high probability, we will observe a training set for which the algorithm will generalize
on average
. If the algorithm is deterministic, this does not mean much, as the algorithm always returns the same hypothesis when it observes the same training set. However, for randomized algorithms, this subtlety makes this kind of guarantees less specific.

An alternative to this kind of guarantees is provided by
Catoni [23]
,
Catoni [24]
, who studied single-draw PAC-Bayesian guarantees.
1
1

1

This nomenclature is due to
Hellström and Durisi [12]
.
These guarantees state that, with high probability, we will observe a training set for which the algorithm returns a hypothesis that generalizes.

In
Section

5
, we obtain single-draw PAC-Bayesian analogues to every previous PAC-Bayesian guarantee seen previously in
Chapter

5
, where the relative entropy is substituted by the Radon–Nikodym derivative between the posterior and the prior.

Throughout the chapter, we also obtain other findings and realizations. For instance, to develop the results from
Section

4
, we developed a technique to optimize parameters in probabilistic statements that can be of independent interest.

2.3
Connection Between Privacy and Generalization

There are three main frameworks to define and describe the privacy of mechanisms. The first one is
semantic security

[
37
]
, which guarantees an absolute disclosure prevention of the information from the data. Unfortunately, applied to machine learning algorithms, this implies that the algorithm cannot even learn the patterns regarding the underlying population distribution
[
38
]
. A second framework is
quantitative information flow

[
39
]
. This framework encompasses many different sub-frameworks defining notions of information leakage and relate the amount of privacy to the amount of leakage of the mechanism. In this framework, one first defines a
threat model
, that is, an adversary with certain capabilities that has a specific objective, and the leakage is defined as the adversary’s ability to achieve their objective. Two particularly compelling leakage definitions, due to their strong operational meaning, are
maximal leakage

[
40
]
and
maximal
g
-leakage

[
41
]
. Luckily, it was shown that the two are equivalent
[
40
,
42
]
. The third framework is
differential privacy

[
43
,
44
]
, which ensures that two neighbouring training sets, that is, two training sets differing in a single instance, cannot be distinguished based on their returned hypothesis.

With respect to the maximal leakage, previous studies have shown that, for potentially unbounded losses with light tails, algorithms with a finite maximal leakage generalize
[
10
,
12
]
. However, it is generally known that most of the dependence measures employed to characterize the information-theoretic guarantees are smaller than the maximal leakage. Therefore, algorithms with a bounded maximal leakage generalize more broadly.

In
Section

2
, we use the results from
Chapters

4
and
5
to recover known results and develop generalization guarantees for algorithms with a bounded maximal leakage. Although the presented results in this section are considered “folklore”, they are now formalized and collected explicitly.

The level of privacy of differentially private algorithms depends on two privacy parameters,
\varepsilon
and
\delta
. There have been several dedicated studies deriving generalization guarantees for differentially private algorithms
[
45
,
46
,
47
,
48
]
. However, in most of these results, the generalization guarantees do not improve as the number of samples
n
increases. In these guarantees, the generalization error is proportional to the privacy parameter
\varepsilon
, and thus they require this parameter to decrease with the number of samples to imply a strong generalization performance.

Similarly to what happened with the maximal leakage, most of the dependence measures used to derive information-theoretic guarantees can be bounded by the privacy parameter
\varepsilon
. Unfortunately, this analysis also leads to generalization guarantees where the generalization error is proportional to the privacy parameter
\varepsilon
but does not decrease with the number of samples
n
.

In
Section

3
, we first recover results similar to those in the state of the art, which guarantee the generalization of differentially private algorithms as long as their privacy parameters decrease with the number of samples. Then, we restrict ourselves to permutation invariant algorithms that operate on discrete data. We show that every algorithm of that kind generalizes in this setting. Moreover, we show that if the algorithm is also differentially private, then the generalization guarantees are stronger and vanish even for constant privacy parameters.

Chapter 2
Preliminaries

In this chapter, we detail the notation and definitions necessary to follow the manuscript. In
Section

3
, we discuss the notation employed to treat the elements of probability theory and justify the usage and assumption of standard Borel spaces throughout the manuscript. This first section will also serve to avoid measurability problems later on. After that, in
Section

4
, we review some important quantities in information theory, present some of their properties, and give some intuition about them. Finally, in
Section

5
, we discuss the concepts of convex conjugacy and Wasserstein distances and offer a trivial extension of the Bobkov–Götze theorem to functions with a bounded cumulant generating function.

The intention of this chapter is briefly introduce the above topics and the notation we will use to refer to them. Thus, an advanced reader may skip this chapter and only come back when they do not recognize some of the notation. The other readers are welcome to go over this chapter since, even if the treatment of the subjects is simplified, it contains insights that aid the understanding of the following chapters. Moreover, some of the insights may help more novice readers to introduce themselves into the fields before inquire into them using the bibliography provided.

3
Probability Theory

Probability theory deals with the study of random phenomena. Since this article is centered around the generalization properties of (potentially stochastic) algorithms, this section lays down the notation used throughout the manuscript and some fundamental definitions and results that aid the understanding of what follows. The covering of the topic, even at an introductory and fundamental level, is far from complete, and we refer the reader to the excellent books
[
49
,
50
,
51
]
for further reading.

3.1
Probability Spaces and Random Objects

A
sample space

\mathcal{A}
is a collection of elements that nature can select randomly. A set of elements from the sample space is an
event
, and any collection
{A}
of events that form a
\sigma
-field (or
\sigma
-algebra
[
49
, Section 1.3]
) is an
event space
. Together, the tuple
(\mathcal{A},{A})
, or simply
\mathcal{A}
if the event space
{A}
is clear from the context, is a
measurable space
. Let
\varnothing
be the empty set. A function
\mathbb{P}:{A}\to[0,1]
such that
\mathbb{P}\big[\varnothing\big]=0
,
\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{A}\big]=1
, and
\mathbb{P}\big[\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{E}_{i}\big]=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{E}_{i}\big]
for all disjoint events
\mathcal{E}_{i}
is a
probability measure
, and
\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{E}\big]
is interpreted as the probability of the event
\mathcal{E}\in{A}
.

Definition 2.1
.

A
probability space

(\mathcal{A},{A},\mathbb{P})
consists of a measurable space
(\mathcal{A},{A})
and a probability measure
\mathbb{P}:{A}\to[0,1]
that assigns probabilities to events.

Throughout the manuscript, there are times where a condition
C
is said to hold
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely (or
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.).
2
2

2

This is also known as almost everywhere (a.e.), almost always (a.a.), for almost all
a\in\mathcal{A}
, almost certainly (a.c.), and with probability one (w.p.1).
This means that the set of elements for which the condition does not hold is contained in a set that has probability zero, that is, there is a set
\mathcal{S}
such that
\{a\in\mathcal{A}:\textnormal{{C} does not hold}\}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
and
\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{S}\big]=0
. This concept is useful, for example, to define the concepts of
essential supremum
and
essential infimum
. Consider a function
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}
, the essential supremum of
f
with respect to
\mathbb{P}
is defined as the smallest value
b\in\mathbb{R}
that bounds
f
from above
\mathbb{P}
-almost surely. That is,
\esssup{P}f\coloneqq\inf\{b\in\mathbb{R}:f\leq b\ \mathbb{P}\textnormal{-a.s.}\}
. The essential infimum is defined analogously.

Definition 2.2
.

A
random object

X
is an
{A}
-measurable function
X:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{X}
from a source measurable space
(\mathcal{A},{A})
to a target measurable space
(\mathcal{X},{X})
. That is, for all events
\mathcal{E}
in
{X}
, their anti-images
X^{-1}(\mathcal{E})
are in
{A}
.

The pair of an abstract probability space
(\mathcal{A},{A},\mathbb{P})
and a random object
X
on a measurable space
(\mathcal{X},{X})
give a model for random phenomena: nature chooses an outcome at random from
\mathcal{A}
and we observe the outcome
X(a)
. In particular, the probability that we observe an event
\mathcal{E}\in{X}
is

\mathbb{P}\big[\{a\in\mathcal{A}:X(a)\in\mathcal{E}\}\big]=\mathbb{P}\circ X^{-1}\big[\mathcal{E}\big].

The probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{X}:=\mathbb{P}\circ X^{-1}:{X}\to[0,1]
is the
probability distribution
(or just distribution) of
X
, and it describes the probability space
(\mathcal{X},{X},\mathbb{P}_{X})
. The set of all probability distributions on
\mathcal{X}
is denoted as
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
. Often, we make probabilistic statements involving random objects such as: “condition
\texttt{C}(X)
holds”. When we want to measure the probability of such statements we abuse notation and write
\mathbb{P}\big[\texttt{C}(X)\big]=\mathbb{P}\big[\{a\in\mathcal{A}:\texttt{C}\circ X(a)\textnormal{ holds}\}\big]
.

Finally, the
expectation
(or expected value or mean) of a random object
X
is

\mathbb{E}[X]:=\int_{\mathcal{A}}X(a)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}[a]=\int_{\mathcal{X}}x\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{X}[x]

whenever each integral exists.

Throughout the manuscript, random objects
X
are written in capital letters, their realizations or outcomes
x
in lowercase letters, their target sample space
\mathcal{X}
in calligraphic letters, their target event space
{X}
in script-style letters, and their probability distribution is written as
\mathbb{P}_{X}
. Expectations of random objects are written as
\mathbb{E}[X]
unless when their probability distribution
\mathbb{Q}=\mathbb{P}_{X}
is not clear, in which case they are written as
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{Q}}[x]
.

3.2
Polish and Standard Borel Spaces

Modeling random phenomena as pairs of abstract probability spaces
(\mathcal{A},{A},\mathbb{P})
and random objects
X
allows us to study the probability space
(\mathcal{X},{X},\mathbb{P}_{X})
induced by the said objects. In this way, one may demand certain structural properties to the target space
(\mathcal{X},{X})
generated by the outcomes of the random object and maintain the source space
(\mathcal{A},{A})
from nature abstract. Ideally, the demanded properties are restrictive enough to allow us to develop fundamental results in probability theory, while being general enough to capture the behavior of many random phenomena of interest.

A natural
3
3

3

Arguably the most general measurable spaces that allow us to proof fundamental results in probability theory are standard spaces
[
49
]
. For brevity and simplicity, the discussion and results developed are for standard Borel spaces, though some of the results can be directly extended to standard spaces.
approach to equipping a target sample space
\mathcal{X}
with structure is to consider a metric
\rho:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
. The tuple
(\mathcal{X},\rho)
, or simply
\mathcal{X}
if the metric
\rho
is clear from the context, is called a
metric space
. A desirable property of a metric space
\mathcal{X}
is that every element
x\in\mathcal{X}
can be well approximated. This is formalized with the following two properties:

1.

Completeness
: The metric space
(\mathcal{X},\rho)
is
complete
if all Cauchy sequences converge to points in
\mathcal{X}
. A sequence
\{x_{n}\in\mathcal{X}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is Cauchy if for all
\varepsilon>0
there is a
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\rho(x_{n},x_{m})<\varepsilon
whenever
n,m\geq k
.

2.

Separability
: The sample space
\mathcal{X}
is
separable
if there is a countable, dense subset
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
. That is, a set
\mathcal{E}
whose closure is the whole space (
\overline{\mathcal{E}}=\mathcal{X}
).

Definition 2.3
.

A
Polish space

(\mathcal{X},\rho)
is a complete and separable metric space.

Given a metric space
(\mathcal{X},\rho)
, one may consider the collection of open balls
\mathcal{B}_{r}(x)\coloneqq\{y\in\mathcal{X}:\rho(x,y)<r\}
(or its induced topology
[
49
, Section 1.2]
). Then, the
Borel
\sigma
-field
is the
\sigma
-field generated by such a collection; that is,

{B}(\mathcal{X})\coloneqq\sigma\big(\{\mathcal{B}_{r}(x):x\in\mathcal{X},r>0\}\big).

Therefore, this manuscript focuses on target spaces
(\mathcal{X},{X})
where the sample space
\mathcal{X}
is Polish and the event space is its Borel
\sigma
-field
{X}={B}(\mathcal{X})
. These measurable spaces are called
standard Borel spaces
.

Definition 2.4
.

A
standard Borel space

(\mathcal{X},{X})
is a measurable space that is Borel isomorphic to
(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{S}))
, where
\mathcal{S}
is some subset of
[0,1]
. That is, there is a measurable one-to-one and onto function
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{S}
such that
f^{-1}
is also measurable.

Theorem 2.1

(Polish and standard Borel spaces
[
49
, Theorem 4.3]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},\rho)
be a Polish space. If
\mathcal{S}
is a subset of
\mathcal{X}
, then
(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{S}))
is a standard Borel space.

Definition

2.4
tells us that any uncountable standard Borel space inherits the properties of the measurable space
\big((0,1),\mathcal{B}((0,1))\big)
and
Theorem

2.1
states that all the target spaces
(\mathcal{X},{X})
constructed as described before are indeed standard Borel. At first glance, these attractive properties may seem very limiting, but in fact many common and useful measurable spaces are standard Borel. For example, all the following sample spaces construct standard Borel spaces with the following selected, usual metrics:

•

Any finite set
\mathcal{X}
such that
|\mathcal{X}|<\infty
with the discrete metric
\rho(x,y)=\mathbb{I}[x\neq y]
.

•

The rational
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{Q}
, the irrational
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
, and the real numbers
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}
with the metric
\rho(x,y)=|x-y|
.

•

The product of the rational
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{Q}^{n}
, the irrational
\mathcal{X}=(\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q})^{n}
, and the real numbers
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{n}
with the metric
\rho(x,y)=\lVert x-y\rVert_{2}
.

•

Any separable Hilbert space, such as the space of square-integrable functions
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mu)
with metric
\rho(f,g)^{2}=\int(f-g)^{2}d\mu
.

For any distribution
\mathbb{P}
on a Polish space
\mathcal{X}
, the
support
of the distribution is defined as the subset
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{X})
of
\mathcal{X}
such that every neighbourhood of an element of
\mathrm{supp}(\mathcal{X})
has a positive probability, that is,
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbb{P})=\{x\in\mathcal{X}:\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{B}_{r}(x)\big]>0\textnormal{ for all }r>0\}
.

3.3
Conditional Probability and Conditional Expectation

Consider a probability space
(\mathcal{A},{A},\mathbb{P})
, a random object
X
in
(\mathcal{X},{X})
, and an event
\mathcal{F}\in{A}
such that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{F}]>0
. Given that the event
\mathcal{F}
occurred, the probability that the event
\mathcal{E}\in{A}
occurs is the
conditional probability of
\mathcal{E}
given
\mathcal{F}

\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{F}}\big[\mathcal{E}\big]=\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{E}\mid\mathcal{F}\big]:=\frac{\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{F}\big]}{\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{F}\big]},

and the expectation of
X
is the
conditional expectation of
X
given
\mathcal{F}

\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{F}}\big[X\big]=\mathbb{E}[X\mid\mathcal{F}]:=\int_{\mathcal{A}}X\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{F}}=\frac{1}{\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{F}\big]}\int_{\mathcal{F}}X\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}.

Therefore, combining the definition of a probability measure from
Section

3.1
and the definition of the conditional probability and conditional expectation, we obtain the so called
law of total probability
and
total expectation
. Namely, that for every collection of disjoint events
\mathcal{F}_{i}
such that
\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{F}_{i}\big]>0
and that
\sum_{i}\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{F}_{i}\big]=1
, for every event
\mathcal{E}
and every random object
X

\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{E}\big]=\sum_{i}\mathbb{P}^{\mathcal{F}_{i}}\big[\mathcal{E}\big]\cdot\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{F}_{i}\big]\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\mathbb{E}\big[X\big]=\sum_{i}\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{F}_{i}}\big[X\big]\cdot\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{F}_{i}\big],

where the summation over
i
is kept vague as this holds true for both a finite and infinitely countable number of events
\mathcal{F}_{i}
.

In this way, the
conditional probability of an event
\mathcal{E}\in{A}
given a sub
\sigma
-field
{F}\subseteq{A}
is the probability that the event
\mathcal{E}
occurred knowing if each event
\mathcal{F}\in{F}
occurred or not. It is written as
\mathbb{P}^{F}\big[\mathcal{E}\big]
or
\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{E}\mid{F}\big]
, and
\mathbb{P}^{F}
is the
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. unique
{F}
-measurable function such that for all events
\mathcal{F}\in{F}
,

\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{F}\big]=\int_{\mathcal{F}}\mathbb{P}^{F}\big[\mathcal{E}\big]\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}.

Similarly, the
conditional expectation of
X
given a sub
\sigma
-field
{F}\subseteq{A}
is written as
\mathbb{E}^{F}\big[X\big]
or
\mathbb{E}\big[X\mid{F}\big]
and
\mathbb{E}^{F}
is the
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. unique
{F}
-measurable function such that for all events
\mathcal{F}\in{F}
,

\int_{\mathcal{F}}Xd\mathbb{P}=\int_{\mathcal{F}}\mathbb{E}^{F}\big[X\big]\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}.

Finally, the
conditional distribution of an event
\mathcal{E}\in{A}
given a random object
Y
is defined as
\mathbb{P}^{Y}\big[\mathcal{E}\big]:=\mathbb{P}^{\sigma(Y)}\big[\mathcal{E}\big]
, where
\sigma(Y)\subseteq{A}
is the smallest
\sigma
-field for which
Y
is measurable. Moreover, the
conditional expectation of the random object
X
given
Y
is
\mathbb{E}^{Y}\big[X\big]=\mathbb{E}^{\sigma(Y)}\big[X\big]
.

3.4
Several Random Objects

Consider a couple of random objects
4
4

4

The following text applies to a countable set of random objects analogously, but the exposition is reduced to only two for simplicity and clarity.

X:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{X}
and
Y:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{Y}
, where
(\mathcal{X},{X})
and
(\mathcal{Y},{Y})
are standard Borel spaces. Then, they may be treated as a single random object
(X,Y):\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
with target space
(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y},{X}\otimes{Y})
, where
{X}\otimes{Y}
is the
\sigma
-field generated by the measurable rectangles of
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
. That is,

{X}\otimes{Y}\coloneqq\sigma\big(\{\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{E}\in{X},\mathcal{F}\in{Y}\}\big).

Moreover, the space
(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y},{X}\otimes{Y})
is also standard Borel and the event space is the Borel
\sigma
-field of the sample space, i.e.
{X}\otimes{Y}=\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})
[
50
, Lemma 1.2]
.

The distribution
\mathbb{P}_{X,Y}
of the random object
(X,Y)
is called the
joint distribution
of the random objects. The
marginal distribution
on
\mathcal{X}
of a probability distribution
\mathbb{Q}
on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
is defined as
\mathbb{Q}\big[\cdot,\mathcal{Y}\big]:{X}\to[0,1]
. In particular, the marginal distribution on
\mathcal{X}
of the joint distribution agrees with the distribution of
X
, that is,
\mathbb{P}_{X}=\mathbb{P}_{X,Y}\big[\cdot,\mathcal{Y}\big]
. However, there are more distributions in
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
that have
\mathbb{P}_{X}
and
\mathbb{P}_{Y}
as marginals. For example, the
product distribution

\mathbb{P}_{X}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{Y}
, where
\mathbb{P}_{X}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{Y}[\mathcal{E}\times\mathcal{F}]=\mathbb{P}_{X}[\mathcal{E}]\mathbb{P}_{Y}[\mathcal{F}]
for all
\mathcal{E}\in{X},\mathcal{F}\in{Y}
. The set of all such distributions is called the
couplings
of
\mathbb{P}_{X}
and
\mathbb{P}_{Y}
and is denoted as
\Pi(\mathbb{P}_{X},\mathbb{P}_{Y})
. The random objects
X
and
Y
are said to be
independent
if
\mathbb{P}_{X,Y}=\mathbb{P}_{X}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{Y}
.

Given two standard Borel spaces
(\mathcal{X},{X})
and
(\mathcal{Y},{Y})
, a
probability (or Markov) kernel
from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{Y}
is a mapping
\kappa:\mathcal{X}\times{Y}\to[0,1]
such that (i)
\kappa(x,\cdot)
is a probability measure on
(\mathcal{Y},{Y})
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
; and (ii)
\kappa(\cdot,\mathcal{F})
is a measurable function from
\mathcal{X}
to the set of all probability measures on
\mathcal{Y}
,
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})
, for all
\mathcal{F}\in{Y}
.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
X
and
Y
be random objects with target standard Borel spaces
(\mathcal{X},{X})
and
(\mathcal{Y},{Y})
. The (regular) conditional distribution of
Y
given
X
is the
\mathbb{P}_{X}
-a.s. unique probability kernel
\kappa
from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{Y}
satisfying that
\mathbb{P}^{X}\big[Y\in\mathcal{F}]=\kappa(X,\mathcal{F})

\mathbb{P}
-a.s. for all
\mathcal{F}\in{Y}
. In what follows, it is denoted as
\mathbb{P}^{X}_{Y}\coloneqq\kappa(X,\cdot)

This way, the conditional distribution
\mathbb{P}^{X}_{Y}
of
Y
given
X
is a random object on
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})
where for each realization
x
of
X
, the realization
\mathbb{P}^{X=x}_{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})
is a probability distribution on
\mathcal{Y}
. The marginal distribution of
Y
is obtained from the conditional distribution as
\mathbb{P}_{Y}=\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}\circ\mathbb{P}_{X}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{P}_{X}}\big[\mathbb{P}^{X=x}_{Y}\big]
and the joint distribution of
(X,Y)
satisfies that

\mathbb{P}_{X,Y}\big[\mathcal{E}\big]=\int\bigg(\int\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{E}}(x,y)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}^{X=x}_{Y}(y)\bigg)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{X}(x)

for all
\mathcal{E}\in{X}\otimes{Y}
, where
\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{E}}
is the indicator function of the set
\mathcal{E}
which returns 1 if
(x,y)\in\mathcal{E}
and 0 otherwise. Therefore, the joint distribution is sometimes written as
\mathbb{P}_{X,Y}=\mathbb{P}_{X}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{X}_{Y}
.

3.5
Densities

At times, working with probability measures and distributions is cumbersome: for instance, when comparing two probability measures
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
, or when calculating expectations or probabilities of events in a target space under certain distributions
\mathbb{P}_{X}
. In these cases, it is often helpful to consider a suitable
\sigma
-finite
[
49
, Section 1.6]
base measure
\mu
and study the
densities
of said probability measures with respect to
\mu
.

Given two measures
\mathbb{P}
and
\mu
on a measurable space
(\mathcal{A},{A})
, the measure
\mathbb{P}
is
absolutely continuous
with respect to
\mu
(or
\mathbb{P}\ll\mu
) if
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}]=0
whenever
\mu(\mathcal{E})=0
for all events
\mathcal{E}\in{A}
.

Theorem 2.2

(Radon–Nikodym theorem
[
51
, Theorem 6.10]
)
.

Consider a probability measure
5
5

5

Technically, it is enough for
\mathbb{P}
to be
\sigma
-finite, but we restrict the theorem to probability measures for our purposes.

\mathbb{P}
and a
\sigma
-finite measure
\mu
on a measurable space
(\mathcal{A},{A})
such that
\mathbb{P}\ll\mu
. Then, there is a
\mu
-a.s. unique
{A}
-measurable function
\mathsf{p}:{A}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for all
\mathcal{E}\in{A}

\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{E}\big]=\int_{\mathcal{E}}\mathsf{p}\mathrm{d}\mu.

Such a function is written
\mathsf{p}\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}
and is called the Radon–Nikodym derivative of
\mathbb{P}
with respect to
\mu
.

Definition 2.6
.

Given a probability measure
\mathbb{P}
and a
\sigma
-finite measure
\mu
on a measurable space
(\mathcal{A},{A})
such that
\mathbb{P}\ll\mu
, the
density
of
\mathbb{P}
with respect to
\mu
(or just the density of
\mathbb{P}
if
\mu
is clear from context) is the Radon–Nikodym derivative
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\mu}
.

An immediate consequence of
Theorem

2.2
is that the expected value of a random object
X
with density
\mathsf{p}_{X}
with respect to
\mu
is

\mathbb{E}\big[X\big]=\int_{\mathcal{E}}x\mathsf{p}_{X}\mathrm{d}\mu.

This result is often also presented in a slightly different way and is referred to as the
change of measure
result. More precisely, let
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
be two distributions on
\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathbb{P}\ll\mathbb{Q}
, then

\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{P}}\big[x\big]=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{Q}}\bigg[x\cdot\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(x)\bigg].

(1)

Throughout the manuscript, densities of probability measures
\mathbb{P}
and distributions
\mathbb{P}_{X}
are written with their lowercase sans serif letters
\mathsf{p}
,
\mathsf{p}_{X}
.
A canonical example when densities are helpful are
probability density functions
(pdfs)
\mathsf{f}_{X}
of a random object
X:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where the base measure is the Lebesgue measure
\lambda

[
49
, Sections 3.4 and 7.2]
and
\mathsf{f}_{X}=\mathsf{p}_{X}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{X}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda}
. In this situation, the densities of many distributions are known and Lebesgue integration
[
51
, Section 4.3]
(which is an extension of the standard Riemann integral that coincides with it in the measurable rectangles on
\mathbb{R}^{n}

[
51
, Section 4.3 and Section 6.4]
) can be employed to calculate events’ probabilities. We will refer to this kind of random objects as
(absolutely) continuous random variables
.
Another example are
probability mass functions
(pmfs) of a random object
X:\mathcal{A}\to[k]
, where the base measure is the counting measure
\mathfrak{c}
. In this situation, the density of
X
is
\mathsf{p}_{X}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{X}}{\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{c}}
and
\mathsf{p}_{X}(i)=\mathbb{P}_{X}\big[\{i\}\big]
. We will refer to this kind of random objects as
discrete random variables
. More generally, we will refer to random objects that can either be continuous or discrete random variables simply as
random variables
.

3.6
Moments and Generating Functions

Consider a random variable
X
with a target space
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. The
p
-th
absolute moment
of
X
is
\mathbb{E}\big[|X|^{p}\big]
and it is said to exist when
\mathbb{E}\big[|X|^{p}\big]<\infty
. By either Hölder’s or Jensen’s inequality it follows that the absolute moments of a random variable are non-decreasing, that is,
\mathbb{E}\big[|X|^{p}\big]\leq\mathbb{E}\big[|X|^{q}\big]
for all
p\leq q
. Hence, if the
p
-th absolute moment does not exist, no larger moment does.
The
p
-th
moment
(or
raw moment
) of
X
is
\mathbb{E}\big[X^{p}\big]
and it is said to exist when the
p
-th absolute moment exists. When the random variable
X
is non-negative, then its
p
-th moment and its
p
-th absolute moment coincide. This will be the case throughout most of this manuscript and therefore we will use both terms interchangeably.
For
p>1
, we are often interested in the
p
-th
central moment
, which is defined as
\mathbb{E}\big[|X-\mathbb{E}[X]|^{p}\big]
and gives us properties of the random variable independent of translation. For example, a notable central moment is the
variance
, which is defined as

\mathrm{Var}[X]\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\big[(X-\mathbb{E}[X])^{2}\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[X^{2}\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[X\big]^{2}.

Another important quantity related to a random variable
X
is its
moment
(or
probability
)
generating function
(MGF), which is defined as the Laplace transform of
-X
.

Definition 2.7
.

The
moment-generating function
of a random variable
X
is
M_{X}(\lambda)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\big[e^{\lambda X}\big]
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. We say that it exits if it is bounded in
(-b,b)
for some
b>0
.

The MGF of a random variable
X
completely characterizes it, to the point that
M_{X}(\lambda)=M_{Y}(\lambda)
if and only if
X=Y
almost surely. Moreover, every moment of a random variable
X
can be obtained from its MGF as

\mathbb{E}\big[X^{p}\big]\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{d}^{p}M_{X}}{\mathrm{d}\lambda^{p}}(0).

(2)

Therefore, the existence of the MGF implies the existence of every moment. However, the reverse is not true: for example, the Pareto distribution of the first kind with parameters
a=3
and
k=1
does not have an MGF but its variance is
\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}

[
52
, Chapter 20]
, and the lognormal distribution does not have an MGF but all its moments are finite
[
52
, Chapter 14]

[
53
]
.

In this manuscript, we will not deal with the MGFs of random variables, but with their logarithm, which is referred to as the
cumulant-generating function
(CGF). More precisely, we will abuse notation and refer to the CGF of a random variable
X
to mean the CGF of the centered random variable
X-\mathbb{E}\big[X\big]
to simplify the calculations and adhere to the standard usage in the machine learning theory community. In practice, this is not problematic since
\log M_{X-a}(\lambda)=\log M_{X}(\lambda)-\lambda a
for every
\lambda,a\in\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 2.8
.

The
cumulant-generating function
of a random variable
X
is
\Lambda_{X}(\lambda)\coloneqq\log\mathbb{E}\big[e^{\lambda(X-\mathbb{E}[X])}\big]
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. We say that it exits if it is bounded in
(-b,b)
for some
b>0
.

If it exists, the CGF is a convex, continuously differentiable function such that
\Lambda_{X}(0)=\Lambda_{X}^{\prime}(0)=0

[
54
,
55
]
. For the purpose of this manuscript, there are other two properties of the CGF that we will consider:

1.

Scaling a random variable scales the argument of the CGF, that is,
\Lambda_{\alpha X}(\lambda)=\Lambda_{X}(\alpha\lambda)
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\alpha\lambda\in(-b,b)
.

2.

The CGF of the sum of independent random variables is the sum of their CGFs, that is,
\Lambda_{X+Y}(\lambda)=\Lambda_{X}(\lambda)+\Lambda_{Y}(\lambda)
for all independent
X
and
Y
.

4
Information Theory

Information theory deals with the intangible concept of information. Informally, the occurrence of a rare event gives us more information than the occurrence of common ones. Although information theory was originated to deal with problems from communications’ theory, it has grown into a field of its own and has applications to probability theory, statistics, computer science, and even physics.

This section describes certain quantities paramount to information theory, presents some of their properties, and gives some intuition about them. These quantities and intuition will be useful later in the manuscript to better understand some conditions under which learning algorithms generalize. As in the previous section, the coverage of the topic is really scarse and the interested reader may refer to the wonderful books
[
56
,
57
,
58
]
and papers
[
43
,
44
,
59
,
60
,
61
,
62
,
40
,
63
,
64
]
for further reading.

4.1
Entropy

Perhaps the most important quantity in information theory is the
entropy
. It is a measure of the uncertainty of a random object
X
. The larger the entropy of the random object
X
, the more uncertain we are about its outcome.

Definition 2.9
.

The
entropy
of a random object
X
with density
\mathsf{p}_{X}
is

\mathrm{H}(X)\coloneqq-\mathbb{E}\big[\log\mathsf{p}_{X}(X)\big].

For multiple random variables
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
this concept is readily extended and
\mathrm{H}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})=-\mathbb{E}\big[\log\mathsf{p}_{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})\big]
. Usually, the entropy is considered for either discrete or absolutely continuous random variables
[
56
,
57
]
, and thus that
\mathsf{p}_{X}
is a pmf or a pdf is understood from the context.

In information theory, a Markov kernel
\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}
is often called a
channel
and it is understood as a mechanism that processes a random object
X
into another random object
Y
(see
Figure

2.2
above). In this manuscript, a common channel will be an algorithm
\mathbb{A}
that transforms a dataset
S
into a hypothesis
W=\mathbb{A}(S)
. It is natural to wonder what is the uncertainty of the processed object
Y

given
the input
X
. This concept is referred to as the
disintegrated entropy of
Y
given
X
and it is a random object depending on
X

[
8
]
. The expectation of this random object is referred to as the
conditional entropy of
Y
given
X
.

Definition 2.10
.

The
disintegrated entropy of
Y
given
X
is

\mathrm{H}^{X}(Y)\coloneqq-\mathbb{E}^{X}\big[\log\mathsf{p}_{Y}^{X}(Y)\big]

and the
conditional entropy of
Y
given
X
is

\mathrm{H}(Y|X)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{H}^{X}(Y)\big]=-\mathbb{E}\big[\log\mathsf{p}_{Y}^{X}(X)\big].

X
\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}
Y

Figure 2.2
:
Illustration of a channel processing
X
to
Y
.

Two important properties of the entropy are summarized below
[
56
,
57
]
. The first one states that the knowledge of the input of a Markov kernel can only decrease its uncertainty, and the second one describes how to decompose the entropy of a collection of random objects.

Proposition 2.1

(​​
[
57
, Theorems 1.4 and 2.6]
)
.

Consider the random objects
X
,
Y
, and
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}
. Then,

•

Conditioning reduces entropy:
\mathrm{H}(Y|X)\leq\mathrm{H}(Y)
.

•

Chain rule:
\mathrm{H}(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{H}(X_{i}|X_{1},\cdots,X_{i-1})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{H}(X_{i})
.

Shannon’s Entropy.

Originally,
Shannon [65]
introduced the concept of entropy for discrete random variables. For this reason, for discrete random variables, we will refer to the entropy as Shannon’s entropy. Shannon introduced it to describe the amount of information contained in a random variable, which we now measure in
bits
if the logarithm is in base 2 or
nats
if it is in base
e
. Shannon showed that the minimum description length (on average) of realizations of a discrete random variable
X
is within one bit of the entropy
\mathrm{H}(X)
. Therefore, the entropy is a fundamental limit of
compression
. With this interpretation, there are a couple important properties of Shannon’s entropy.

Proposition 2.2

(​​
[
57
, Theorem 1.4]
)
.

Consider a discrete random variable
X
, a bijective function
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
, and a one-to-one function
g:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
. Then,

•

Shannon’s entropy is non-negative:
\mathrm{H}(X)\geq 0
.

•

The uniform distribution maximizes the Shannon’s entropy:
\mathrm{H}(X)\leq\log|\mathcal{X}|
.

•

Shannon’s entropy is invariant to relabeling:
\mathrm{H}(X)=\mathrm{H}(f(X))
.

•

Shannon’s entropy is monotone:
\mathrm{H}(X,Y)\geq\mathrm{H}(X)
with equality if and only if
Y=g(X)
.

4.2
Relative Entropy

The
relative entropy

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})
of a distribution
\mathbb{P}
with respect to another distribution
\mathbb{Q}
(or
Kullback–Leibler divergence
) is a measure of the dissimilarity between the distributions
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
.
It is important to note that the relative entropy is asymmetric, i.e.
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})\neq\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{Q}\|\mathbb{P})
, and therefore it is not a distance. This is natural in certain situations like hypothesis testing. For instance, consider that we want to study the bias of a die and we consider the distribution of a fair die
\mathbb{P}
and of a biased die
\mathbb{Q}
that always lands on even numbers. If we observe the sequence “
\mathrm{2,4,4,2,6}
”, then we may believe that the distribution is
\mathbb{Q}
, but we cannot be absolutely sure. On the other hand, if we observe the sequence “
\mathrm{2,4,4,2,3}
”, then we are absolutely certain that the distribution is
\mathbb{P}
. The relative entropy describes this imbalance as
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{Q}\|\mathbb{P})=\log 2
whereas
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})\to\infty

[
57
, Section 2.1]
.

From an information-theoretic perspective, it describes the inefficiency of assuming that a random variable
X
has distribution
\mathbb{Q}
, when in reality it has distribution
\mathbb{P}_{X}=\mathbb{P}
. That is, if we constructed a code for a discrete random variable
X
assuming it has a distribution
\mathbb{Q}
, then we would need
at least

\mathrm{H}(X)+\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})
bits (on average) to describe the said random variable
[
56
, Section 2.3]
. The example with the die above describes a pathological worst-case situation. Since the odd outcomes have probability zero under
\mathbb{Q}
, the code constructed assuming this distribution may not even have codewords to describe them, and therefore the outcome cannot be described with this code even if we were to have access to infinite extra bits.

Definition 2.11
.

The
relative entropy
of a distribution
\mathbb{P}
with respect to another distribution
\mathbb{Q}
is

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{P}}\bigg[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(x)\bigg]=\int\log\Big(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}\Big)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}

(3)

if
\mathbb{P}\ll\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})\to\infty
otherwise.

Another formulation of the relative entropy comes from defining it as an instance of an
f
-divergence (see
Section

4.4
below). More precisely,
Equation

3
can be equivalently written using a change of measure (
Theorem

2.2
) as

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{Q}}\bigg[\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(x)\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(x)\bigg]=\int\Big(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}\Big)\log\Big(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}\Big)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}.

When both
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
have a density with respect to a common dominating measure
\mu
(e.g.
\mu=\mathbb{P}+\mathbb{Q}
), then it is convenient to write the relative entropy in terms of these densities using the
likelihood ratio
\nicefrac{{\mathsf{p}}}{{\mathsf{q}}}
.

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{P}}\bigg[\log\frac{\mathsf{p}(x)}{\mathsf{q}(x)}\bigg].

As with the entropy, we sometimes want to study the relative entropy of a channel
\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}
with respect to another channel
\mathbb{Q}_{Y}^{X}
, or of a channel with respect to a distribution. Moreover, if we consider two channels
\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{Y}^{X}
and a common input distribution
\mathbb{P}_{X}
, then the relative entropy of
\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}
with respect to
\mathbb{Q}_{Y}^{X}
is a continuous random variable depending on
X
and its expectation is the
conditional relative entropy given
X
.

Definition 2.12
.

The conditional relative entropy of the channel
\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}
with respect to the channel
\mathbb{Q}_{Y}^{X}
given
X
is

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}\|\mathbb{Q}_{Y}^{X}|\mathbb{P}_{X})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}\|\mathbb{Q}_{Y}^{X})\big].

The relative entropy enjoys several properties that are paramount in many important results in information theory. Some of these properties are summarized below
[
56
,
57
]
.

Proposition 2.3
.

Consider two distributions
\mathbb{P}_{X}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{X}
on
\mathcal{X}
, two channels
\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{Y}^{X}
from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{Y}
, and a one-to-one function
f
from
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathcal{Y}
. Moreover, let
\mathbb{P}_{X,Y}=\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{X}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{X,Y}=\mathbb{Q}_{Y}^{X}\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{X}
and also consider the joint distributions
\mathbb{P}_{X^{n}}=\mathbb{P}_{X_{1},\ldots X_{n}}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{X^{n}}=\mathbb{Q}_{X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}}
. Then,

•

The relative entropy is non-negative:
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{X}\|\mathbb{Q}_{X})\geq 0
.

•

The relative entropy
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{X}\|\mathbb{Q}_{X})
is convex and lower semicontinuous with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{X}
for every fixed
\mathbb{Q}_{X}
.

•

The relative entropy is monotone:
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{X,Y}\|\mathbb{Q}_{X,Y})\geq\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{Y}\|\mathbb{Q}_{Y})
.

•

Chain rule.
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{X^{n}}\|\mathbb{Q}_{X^{n}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{X_{i}|X^{i-1}}\|\mathbb{Q}_{X_{i}|X^{i-1}}|\mathbb{P}_{X^{i-1}})
.

•

The relative entropy tensorizes: If
Q_{X^{n}}=Q_{X_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes Q_{X_{n}}
, then

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{X^{n}}\|\mathbb{Q}_{X^{n}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{X^{n}}\|\mathbb{P}_{X_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbb{P}_{X_{n}})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{X_{i}}\|\mathbb{Q}_{X_{i}})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{X_{i}}\|\mathbb{Q}_{X_{i}})

with equality if and only if
\mathbb{P}_{X^{n}}=\mathbb{P}_{X_{1}}\otimes\cdots\mathbb{P}_{X_{n}}
.

•

Conditioning increases the relative entropy (
Figure

2.3
left). Assume that
\mathbb{P}_{Y}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{Y}
are both obtained after passing a random object
X
with distribution
\mathbb{P}_{X}
through the channels
\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{Y}^{X}
, i.e.
\mathbb{P}_{Y}=\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}\circ\mathbb{P}_{X}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{Y}=\mathbb{Q}_{Y}^{X}\circ\mathbb{P}_{X}
. Then
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{Y}\|\mathbb{Q}_{Y})\leq\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}\|\mathbb{Q}_{Y}^{X}|\mathbb{P}_{X})
.

•

Data processing inequality (
Figure

2.3
right). Now consider that
\mathbb{P}_{Y}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{Y}
are obtained after passing a random object
X
and a random object
\bar{X}
with distributions
\mathbb{P}_{X}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{X}=\mathbb{P}_{\bar{X}}
through the channel
\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}
, i.e.
\mathbb{P}_{Y}=\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}\circ\mathbb{P}_{X}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{Y}=\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}\circ\mathbb{Q}_{X}
. Then,
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{Y}\|\mathbb{Q}_{Y})\leq\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{X}\|\mathbb{Q}_{Y})
with equality if and only if
\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}
is a deterministic and losseless channel described by
f
.

X
\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}
\mathbb{Q}_{Y}^{X}
Y\sim\mathbb{P}_{Y}
\bar{Y}\sim\mathbb{Q}_{Y}

X\sim\mathbb{P}_{X}
\bar{X}\sim\mathbb{Q}_{X}
\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}
Y\sim\mathbb{P}_{Y}
\bar{Y}\sim\mathbb{Q}_{Y}

Figure 2.3
:
Illustrations of a single random object
X
processed with different channels (left) and two different random objects processed with the same channel (right).

Finally, an important result concerning the relative entropy that will be repeatedly used and referred to during this manuscript is that it enjoys a variational representation due to Donsker and Varadhan
[
66
]
and Gibbs
[
67
]
.

Lemma 2.1

(Donsker and Varadhan lemma
[
66
, Lemma
2.1]
and Gibbs variational principle
[
67
, Theorem III on Chapter XI]
, respectively)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a measurable space and
\mathbb{Q}
be a probability measure on
\mathcal{X}
.

•

Let
\mathbb{P}
be also a probability measure on
\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathbb{P}\ll\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathcal{G}{Q}
be the set of all measurable functions on
\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{Q}}\Big[e^{g(x)}\Big]<\infty
. Then,

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})=\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}{Q}}\Big\{\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{P}}\big[g(x)\big]-\log\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{Q}}\Big[e^{g(x)}\Big]\Big\}.

(4)

•

Let
g
be a measurable function on
\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{Q}}\Big[e^{g(x)}\Big]<\infty
and
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathcal{X})
be the set of all probability measures
\mathbb{P}
on
\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathbb{P}\ll\mathbb{Q}
. Then,

\log\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{Q}}\Big[e^{g(x)}\Big]=\sup_{\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}{Q}(\mathcal{X})}\Big\{\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{P}}\big[g(x)\big]-\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})\Big\}.

(5)

Consider two fixed random variables
X
and
Y
on a measurable space
\mathcal{X}
distributed according to
\mathbb{P}_{X}=\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{P}_{Y}=\mathbb{Q}
. The Donsker and Varadhan lemma
[
66
]
tells us that the relative entropy of
\mathbb{P}
with respect to
\mathbb{Q}
is no smaller than the difference of the expected value of
g(X)
and the CGF of
g(Y)
for all functions
g
such that the CGF exists. The Gibbs variational principle
[
67
]
is its dual dual formulation. It says that, for a fixed function
g
and a fixed random variable
X^{\prime}
with distribution
\mathbb{P}_{Y}=\mathbb{Q}
, the CGF of
g(Y)
is no smaller than the difference between the expected value of
g(X)
and the relative entropy of
\mathbb{P}
with respect to
\mathbb{Q}
for all random variables
X
distributed according to
\mathbb{P}_{X}=\mathbb{Q}
. As we will see in
Chapters

4
and
5
, these results are very useful to bound the expectation of functions of random variables with complicated distributions when we know that the CGF of those functions with respect to simpler distributions is bounded. We also found these results helpful to characterize the regret of the Thompson sampling in different Markov decision problems
[
68
,
69
,
70
]
.

4.3
Mutual Information

The
mutual information

\mathrm{I}(X;Y)
between two random objects
X
and
Y
represents the information that they contain about each other. More precisely, it is defined as the reduction of uncertainty of one random object when the other is known. Mathematically, it is also equal to the relative entropy of the joint distribution
\mathbb{P}_{X,Y}
with respect to the product of the priors
\mathbb{P}_{X}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{Y}
. That is, it can be interpreted as the inefficiency of describing the two random objects independently instead of jointly.

Definition 2.13
.

The
mutual information
between two random variables
X
and
Y
is

\mathrm{I}(X;Y)\coloneqq\mathrm{H}(X)-\mathrm{H}(X|Y)=\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{X,Y}\|\mathbb{P}_{X}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{Y}).

Consider a situation like the one in
Figure

2.2
where a random object
X
is passed through a channel
\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}
to generate
Y
. The mutual information can also be written as the conditional relative entropy of the channel
\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}
with respect to the output distribution
\mathbb{P}_{Y}
given the input distribution
\mathbb{P}_{X}
, i.e.

\mathrm{I}(X;Y)=\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}\|\mathbb{P}_{Y}|\mathbb{P}_{X}).

This suggests that the mutual information is the average inefficiency of describing the output of a channel
Y
without using the input
X
when it is known. This interpretation coincides with the original definition of uncertainty reduction. However, it is especially useful in communications as the
capacity
of a channel
\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X})
, or maximum rate rate at which we can send information over the channel and recover it at the output with a vanishingly low probability of error, is exactly the supremum over all input distributions of the mutual information, that is,
\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X})=\sup_{\mathbb{P}_{X}}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}\|\mathbb{P}_{Y}|\mathbb{P}_{X})
. This interpretation will also be useful to understand some results concerning the generalization error and mutual information later in the manuscript.

It is often useful to consider the mutual information between two random objects when a third one is known. This is quantified with the
disintegrated mutual information

[
8
]
and its expectation, the
conditional mutual information
.

Definition 2.14
.

The
disintegrated mutual information between
X
and
Y
given
Z
is

\mathrm{I}^{Z}(X;Y)\coloneqq\mathrm{H}^{Z}(X)-\mathrm{H}^{Z}(X|Y)=\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}^{Z}_{X,Y}\|\mathbb{P}^{Z}_{X}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{Z}_{Y})

and the
conditional mutual information between
X
and
Y
given
Z
is

\mathrm{I}(X;Y|Z)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{I}^{Z}(X;Y)\big]=\mathrm{H}(X|Z)-\mathrm{H}(X|Y,Z)=\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}^{Z}_{X,Y}\|\mathbb{P}^{Z}_{X}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{Z}_{Y}|\mathbb{P}_{Z}).

Some properties of the mutual information are described below
[
56
,
57
]
.

Proposition 2.4

(​​
[
57
, Theorems 3.2, 3.7, and 4.1]
)
.

Consider the random objects
X
,
Y
,
Z
, and
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}
. Then,

•

The mutual information is symmetric:
\mathrm{I}(X;Y)=\mathrm{I}(Y;X)
.

•

The mutual information is non-negative:
\mathrm{I}(X;Y)\geq 0
with equality if and only if
X
and
Y
are independent.

•

More data, more information:
\mathrm{I}(X;Y,Z)\geq\mathrm{I}(X;Y)
.

•

Data processing inequality: If
X
,
Y
, and
Z
form a Markov chain
X-Y-Z
, then
\mathrm{I}(X;Y)\geq\mathrm{I}(X;Z)
with equality if and only if they also form the Markov chain
Y-Z-X
. Moreover
\mathrm{I}(X;Y|Z)\leq\mathrm{I}(X;Y)
with equality if and only if
X
is independent of
Z
.

•

Permutation invariance:
\mathrm{I}(X;Y)\geq\mathrm{I}(f(X);g(Y))
, with equality if and only if
f:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{A}
and
g:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{B}
are one-to-one functions with a measurable inverse.

•

Chain rule:
\mathrm{I}(X^{n};Y)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(X_{i};Y|X^{i-1})
.

•

Golden formula:
\mathrm{I}(X;Y)=\inf_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}\|\mathbb{Q}|\mathbb{P}_{X})
.

4.4
f
-divergences

The relative entropy
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}
is a special case of an
f
-divergence
, which is a family of dissimilarity measures between probability distributions. They are defined as an expectation of a convex function of the likelihood ratio of the probability densities.

Definition 2.15
.

Consider two probability distributions
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
on
\mathcal{X}
with densities
\mathsf{p}
and
\mathsf{q}
. Further consider a convex function
f:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
f(1)=0
and let
f(0)\coloneqq\lim_{x\to 0^{+}}f(x)
. Then, the
f
-
divergence
of
\mathbb{P}
with respect to
\mathbb{Q}
is

\mathrm{D}_{f}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{Q}}\bigg[f\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(x)\bigg)\bigg]=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{Q}}\bigg[f\bigg(\frac{\mathsf{p}(x)}{\mathsf{q}(x)}\bigg)\bigg]

if
\mathbb{P}\ll\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathrm{D}_{f}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})\to\infty
otherwise.

Like the relative entropy, there are many common dissimilarity measures that are instances of
f
-divergences. Consider two probability distributions
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
on
\mathcal{X}
with densities
\mathsf{p}
and
\mathsf{q}
. Below, we define some of the ones that will be considered in this manuscript and give some interpretation about them.

•

The
total variation
is defined as an
f
-divergence with
f(x)=\frac{1}{2}|x-1|
, that is,

\mathrm{TV}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{Q}}\left[\Big|\frac{\mathsf{p}(x)}{\mathsf{q}(x)}-1\Big|\right]=\sup_{\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{X}}\Big\{\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{A}\big]-\mathbb{Q}\big[\mathcal{A}\big]\Big\}\in[0,1].

The total variation has a direct interpretation via
binary hypothesis testing

[
57
,
71
]
. Consider a random object
X
with an unknown distribution. We know that
X
can either be distributed according to
\mathbb{P}
or
\mathbb{Q}
, and therefore its distribution is
\mathbb{P}_{X}=\mathbb{P}\cdot\mathbb{P}_{U}[0]+\mathbb{Q}\cdot\mathbb{P}_{U}[1]
, where
U
is a binary random variable indicating if the distribution of
X
is
\mathbb{P}
(when
U=0
) or
\mathbb{Q}
(when
U=1
). If we have no prior knowledge of which distribution is more likely we let
\mathbb{P}_{U}[0]=\mathbb{P}_{U}[1]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
. Then, in binary hypothesis testing one tries to design a decision function
\phi:\mathcal{X}\to\{0,1\}
that accurately predicts the indicating variable
U
(or hypothesis). The error probability achieved with the best decision function is completely characterized by the total variation

\inf_{\phi}\mathbb{P}\Big[\phi(X)\neq U\Big]=\frac{1}{2}\Big(1-\mathrm{TV}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})\Big),

that is, the more different are the distributions
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
, the smaller error probability can be achieved by the best decision function.

Another interesting property of the total variation is that it is a
distance
between probability distributions. More precisely, it is a
Wasserstein distance
with respect to the discrete metric (as shown in
Proposition

2.5
below).

•

The
squared Hellinger distance
(or
Hellinger squared divergence
) is defined as an
f
-divergence with
f(x)=(1-\sqrt{x})^{2}
, that is,

\mathrm{Hel}^{2}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{Q}}\left[\Bigg(\sqrt{\frac{\mathsf{p}(x)}{\mathsf{q}(x)}}-1\Bigg)^{2}\right]\in[0,2].

The Hellinger distance, like the total variation, is a distance between probability measures. One of its main interests is that it (weakly) characterizes the total variation (see
[
57
, Section 7.3]
) and that it tensorizes, that is,
\mathrm{Hel}^{2}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})=2-2\prod_{i=1}^{n}\big(1-\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}\cdot\mathrm{Hel}^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{i},\mathbb{Q}_{i})\big)
for all
\mathbb{P}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}_{i}
and
\mathbb{Q}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{Q}_{i}
. This makes this divergence very useful for the problem of composite hypothesis testing and therefore a well studied quantity.

•

The
\chi^{2}
-divergence
is defined as an
f
-divergence with
f(x)=(x-1)^{2}
, that is,

\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{Q}}\bigg[\Big(\frac{\mathsf{p}(x)}{\mathsf{q}(x)}-1\Big)^{2}\bigg].

Most
f
-divergences locally behave like the
\chi^{2}
-divergence
[
57
, Section 7.10]
. This is one of the reasons for which this quantity is well understood in the information theory community.

It is often useful to know the relationships between different
f
-divergences. For instance, sometimes we can prove a result with the total variation, but we are really interested in the properties of the relative entropy. In this case, Pinsker’s inequality
[
72
]
or the Bretagnolle–Huber’s inequality
[
73
]
are useful.

Lemma 2.2

(
Pinsker [72]
with constants from
[
74
,
75
,
76
]
​​)
.

Let
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
be two probability distributions on
\mathcal{X}
. Then,

\mathrm{TV}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})}.

Lemma 2.3

(
Bretagnolle and Huber [73, Lemma 2.1]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
be two probability distributions on
\mathcal{X}
. Then,

\mathrm{TV}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})\leq\sqrt{1-e^{-\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})}}.

These relationships exist between any two
f
-divergences as shown by Harremoës
and Vajda with their joint range technique
[
77
]
. The joint range theorem gives the sharpest possible inequalities between any two
f
-divergences, although sometimes these are cumbersome. Some other good relationships involving the total variation, the relative entropy, the Hellinger distance, and the
\chi^{2}
divergence are
[
57
, Sections 7.3 to 7.6]

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{Hel}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})\sqrt{1-\frac{1}{4}\cdot\mathrm{Hel}^{2}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})},

(6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})},\textnormal{ and}

(7)

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\leq\log\left(1+\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})\right)\leq\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})

(8)

All
f
-divergences share some important properties with the relative entropy, like the non-negativity, the data processing inequality, or the fact that conditioning increases their value
[
57
, Theorems 7.4 and 7.5]
. However, in this manuscript, we will only employ that they have a variational representation.

Lemma 2.4

(Variational representation of
f
-divergences
[
57
, Theorem 7.24]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
be two probability distributions on
\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathbb{P}\ll\mathbb{Q}
,
f_{*}
be the convex conjugate of
f
(see
Definition

2.17
), and
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{Q},f}
be the set of all measurable functions on
\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{Q}}\big[f_{*}\circ g(x)\big]<\infty
. Then,

\mathrm{D}_{f}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})=\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{Q},f}}\Big\{\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{P}}\big[g(x)\big]-\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{Q}}\big[f_{*}\circ g(x)\big]\Big\}.

In particular, we will employ the corollaries stating a variational characterization of the
\chi^{2}
-divergence and the relative entropy.

Corollary 2.1

(Variational representation of the
\chi^{2}
divergence)
.

Let
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
be two probability distributions on
\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathbb{P}\ll\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathcal{G}{Q}
be the set of all measurable functions on
\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{Q}}\big[e^{g(x)}\big]<\infty
. Then,

\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})=\sup_{g:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}}\bigg\{\frac{\big(\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{P}}[g(x)]-\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{Q}}[g(x)]\big)^{2}}{\mathrm{Var}_{x\sim\mathbb{Q}}[g(x)]}\bigg\}.

Corollary 2.2

(Variational representation of the relative entropy)
.

Let
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
be two probability distributions on
\mathcal{X}
such that
\mathbb{P}\ll\mathbb{Q}
. Then,

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})=1+\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}{Q}}\Big\{\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{P}}\big[g(x)\big]-\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{Q}}\Big[e^{g(x)}\Big]\Big\}

The variational representation of the relative entropy borrowed from
f
-divergences (
Corollary

2.2
) is looser than the one by Donsker and Varadhan (
Lemma

2.1
).
6
6

6

A slight modification of the definition of
f
-divergences can recover the Donsker and Varadhan lemma, as shown in
[
57
, Section 7.13]
.
However, it is still useful, as
Corollary

2.2
is
still an equivalence
of the relative entropy which sometimes is easier to manipulate as shown in
Section

13.2
.

4.5
Rényi Divergence

To conclude with this section, we describe another family of dissimilarity measures between probability distributions: the
Rényi divergences of order
\alpha
. In particular, the relative entropy
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}
is the Rényi divergence of order 1.

Definition 2.16
.

Consider two probability distributions
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
on
\mathcal{X}
with densities
\mathsf{p}
and
\mathsf{q}
. If
\mathbb{P}\ll\mathbb{Q}
, for every
\alpha\in(0,1)\cup(1,\infty)
, the
Rényi divergence
of order
\alpha
of
\mathbb{P}
with respect to
\mathbb{Q}
is

\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})\coloneqq\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{Q}}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(x)\bigg)^{\alpha}\bigg]=\frac{1}{\alpha-1}\log\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{Q}}\bigg[\bigg(\frac{\mathsf{p}(x)}{\mathsf{q}(x)}\bigg)^{\alpha}\bigg].

For the orders
\alpha=0
,
\alpha=1
, and
\alpha\to\infty
, the definition is obtained by continuity. To be precise, if
\alpha=0
, then

\mathrm{D}_{0}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})\coloneqq-\log\mathbb{Q}\big[\mathrm{supp}(\mathbb{P})\big]=-\log\mathbb{Q}\big[\{x\in\mathcal{X}:\mathsf{p}(x)>0\}\big];

if
\alpha=1
, then by continuity it is equivalent to the relative entropy, that is,
\mathrm{D}_{1}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})
; and if
\alpha\to\infty
, then
