, and these are the ones that enjoy the privacy guarantees. Luckily, most privacy frameworks like differential privacy or maximal leakage have
post-processing
guarantees
[
44
,
40
,
63
]
. This guarantee states that no amount of post-processing can degrade the privacy guarantees. Therefore, the hypothesis
h_{W}
maintains the same privacy guarantees and therefore also the same generalization guarantees derived from them.

•

Finally, for algorithmic stability based on information measures or information-theoretic generalization (
Sections

10.3
and
11
), the maintainance of the generalization guarantees comes from the data processing inequality (see
Proposition

2.3
and the rest of
Section

4
). Consider, for example, the relative entropy: for two weights
W
and
W^{\prime}
, this inequality guarantees that
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{h_{W}}\|\mathbb{P}_{h_{W^{\prime}}})\leq\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}\|\mathbb{P}_{W^{\prime}})
. Therefore, the guarantees obtained for the weights also translate to the hypotheses.

For this reason, a good volume of the information-theoretic generalization literature presents their results abusing notation and describing the random hypothesis returned by the algorithm with the letter
W
and using it indistinguishably from the model’s weights. Henceforth, in this manuscript, we will also present our results in this way. However, it is important to mention that, until the weights are employed to find specific guarantees for some particular algorithm like the SGLD in
Section

17
, the results hold for any hypothesis and not only for parameterized models.

Remark 3.2
.

In information theory, the letter
H
is historically reserved to the Shannon entropy
\mathrm{H}
and expressions like
\mathrm{H}(H)
can be confusing. Pragmatically, this is also a reason why in information-theoretic generalization the letter
W
is employed instead of the classical
H
in learning theory.

Chapter 4
Generalization Guarantees in Expectation

In this chapter, we discuss generalization guarantees in expectation within the framework of information-theoretic generalization introduced in
Section

11
. This generalization guarantee is the least specific within the three levels of specificity described in
Section

11.1
. To be precise, the guarantees of this kind describe what is the smallest expected difference between the population risk and the empirical risk. In other words, if we consider the empirical risk as an estimator of the population risk, these guarantees look for an expression that characterizes (or bounds from above) the bias of the said estimator.

The first question we should ask ourselves before continuing the study is: “
What do we desire from guarantees in expectation?
”. If the purpose is to find an estimator with a small bias, we could evaluate the loss on a single sample and obtain an unbiased estimator. The objective with these kinds of bounds is to gain
understanding
. The reason why the estimator taken is the empirical risk is no coincidence, as most learning algorithms return a hypothesis trying to minimize this objective. Therefore, we seek to know what drives apart the performance of an algorithm on its objective (the population risk) with respect to the proxy it uses to return a hypothesis (the empirical risk). Bounds in expectation, due to their simplicity, allow us to find the average performance discrepancy more easily and get a better idea of a problem. For example, through the lens of the uniform stability of
Section

10.1
, we know that, under certain conditions on the loss and the parameter’s space, the parameters of gradient descent can be chosen so that the expected generalization rate is in
\mathcal{O}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}})
(see
Section

19.1
).

A second question should be:
“Why do we study information-theoretic bounds?”
. The reason has been mostly outlined in
Chapter

3
, particularly in
Section

11
, and it is because this framework can give us guarantees that are specific to the learning algorithm and the data distribution. This greatly differs from previous frameworks where the guarantees are given
uniformly
to a whole class of hypothesis classes and data distributions like the ones in
Sections

7
and
8
. As we will see shortly in
Section

13
, a common information-theoretic bound states that the generalization error of an algorithm evaluated on a loss with a bounded range is bounded from above by

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W;S)\big]\leq(b-a)\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{2n}},

where
n
is the number of samples and
\mathrm{I}(W;S)
is the mutual information that the algorithm’s output hypothesis
W
has about the training data
S
(see
Figure

4.5
). If, in our problem, the data distribution
\mathbb{P}_{Z}
belongs to a well-behaved class, we may work out how much information the algorithm will capture about the data and find a
specific
bound in that situation, even though the bound does not hold
uniformly
over all algorithms and all data distributions. Moreover, a bound of this kind gives a better understanding of what can hinder the generalization performance of an algorithm: the more information about the data that is encoded into the output of an algorithm, the worse it generalizes.

S
\mathbb{A}\equiv\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
W

W
\mathbb{P}_{S}^{W}
S

Figure 4.5
:
Illustration of a learning algorithm
\mathbb{A}
viewed as a channel processing a dataset
S
to obtain a hypothesis
W
(left), and of the backward channel describing the processing of a hypothesis
W
to obtain the dataset with which it was trained (right).

With this motivation in mind, the chapter starts in
Section

13
describing bounds using mutual information as their main contributor. This section is based on the results from
Xu and Raginsky [5]
and
Bu et al. [7]
in
Section

14.1
, and our results from
[
142
,
164
]
in
Sections

13.2
and
4.2
, including some new reflections that are not published. Then, the chapter continues in
Section

14
, describing bounds using conditional mutual information as the main element and it is based on the results from
Steinke and Zakynthinou [9]
and
Hellström and Durisi [144]
. This conditional mutual information shifts the focus from how much information the algorithm’s output has about the training data to how much we can identify the training data based on the algorithm’s output. This change in perspective will prove relevant.

After that, in
Section

15
, we describe some variants of the bounds in the previous two sections that have proven useful for gaining insights into the elements that hinder generalization: single-letter and random-subset bounds. The first kind notes that we can focus on the information that the algorithm’s output has
individually
about each data instance, and the second kind allows us to leverage some of the problem’s information to design problem-specific bounds later on. This section is based mostly on the works from
Bu et al. [7]
,
Negrea et al. [8]
,
Haghifam et al. [156]
, and our results in
[
157
,
165
]
.

In
Section

16
, we abstract the formula to obtain the bounds so far and present bounds using the Wasserstein distance. These bounds serve two purposes. First, they allow us to consider the geometry of the hypothesis space
and
the dependence between the algorithm’s output and the training set. Second, they allow us to re-discover most of the bounds in the previous
Sections

13
,
14
, and
15
with different assumptions and to prove that these new bounds are tight for non-trivial situations. This section is mainly based on our results in
[
165
]
and part of our results in
[
136
]
.

Then, in
Section

17
, we describe an application of the mutual information and conditional mutual information-based bounds in the context of noisy, iterative learning algorithms. We show how the presented bounds can be used to find factors that may drive the generalization performance of the stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics and stochastic gradient descent. This chapter is based on the results from
Pensia et al. [166]
,
Bu et al. [7]
,
Negrea et al. [8]
,
Haghifam et al. [156]
, our results from
[
157
]
, and
Neu et al. [145]
.

In
Section

18
, we describe further advances in information-theoretic generalization error bounds. This section is mainly based on the results from many other works
[
9
,
167
,
144
,
168
,
169
,
170
,
171
,
172
]
, with the exception of
Theorem

4.30
, which our result from
[
136
]
. Finally, in
Section

19
, we challenge the bounds described throughout the chapter and show that, despite being tight, there are problems where frameworks like uniform stability from
Section

10.1
can guarantee that an algorithm generalizes, but that the information-theoretic bounds previously discussed fail. This last section is based on our results from
[
136
]
.

The chapter intends to be didactic and convey the key messages and results from the current information-theoretic bounds on the expected generalization error. This is why many other works are included together with our own. This is also the reason why many of both the other works and ours are (i) written in a different way than what can be found in the papers, (ii) contain different, more pedagogical proofs, and/or (iii) are more general and extend the published results. We hope the reader can benefit from this chapter, which complements the recent survey from
Hellström et al. [133]
.

13
Bounds Using Mutual Information

In this section, we will consider the problem of bounding the generalization error from above. To do so, we will consider the analogous problem of bounding the bias of the empirical risk in estimating the population risk when the hypothesis is obtained using the same training set used to calculate the empirical risk. This framework inspired
Xu and Raginsky [5]
to adapt the work from
Russo and Zou [6]
studying the bias in generic adaptive data analysis that later, to the context of generalization theory.
12
12

12

Interestingly, essentially the same building result appeared previously in
[
82
, Exercise 4.13]
in an abstract manner, in the context of information inequalities.
Their derived bounds depend on the amount of information that the hypothesis returned by the algorithm
W
has about the training set
S
that it received, that is,
\mathrm{I}(W^{\prime}S)
. Throughout the section, we will discuss the implications of such an information measure and different generalization bounds that can be derived depending on the generality of the assumptions on the loss function
\ell
.

In
Section

13.1
, after understanding the ideas from
Xu and Raginsky [5]
for losses with a bounded range in
Section

13.1
, we will see
Bu et al. [7]
’ generalization the results to losses with a bounded CGF. These bounds are characterized by their slow rate in
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\nicefrac{{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}}{{n}}})
. Then, we will present our
[
164
]
tighter bounds when the loss has a bounded range along with
Catoni [24]
’s results in this setting in
Section

13.2
. These bounds are characterized by their fast rate in
\mathcal{O}(\nicefrac{{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}}{{n}})
. Finally, in
Section

4.2
, we will conclude discussing our results from
[
164
]
showing how we can interpolate between fast rate and slow-rate bounds depending on which moments of the loss function are bounded. In particular, when only the second moment is bounded, we can achieve a slow-rate bound, and in the limit, when all moments are bounded, we recover a fast-rate bound.

13.1
A Slow-Rate Bound: Losses With a Bounded CGF

Consider a random hypothesis
W^{\prime}
distributed according to the data-independent distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W^{\prime}}=\mathbb{Q}
. The expected generalization error of this hypothesis
W^{\prime}
is
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{gen}(W^{\prime},S)]=0
since the instances are identically distributed and hence the empirical risk is an unbiased estimator of the population risk, namely

\mathbb{E}[\widehat{{R}}(W^{\prime},S)]=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{w^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{Q}}\big[\mathbb{E}[\ell(w^{\prime},Z_{i})]\big]=\mathbb{E}[{R}(W^{\prime})].

However, by construction, a learning algorithm returns a hypothesis sampled from the conditional distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
, and therefore, it depends on the training data
S
. If the empirical risk is considered an estimator of the population risk, then this dependence makes it potentially biased.

A way around this bias problem is to do a change of measure and study the generalization error of the data-independent hypothesis
W^{\prime}
. A change of measure is a result that relates the expectation of two random objects with distinct distributions and a correction term that determines how far apart these two distributions are. The canonical example is the change of measure (
1
) resulting from the Radon–Nikodym
Theorem

2.2
. Nonetheless, now we will employ the Donsker and Varadhan
Lemma

2.1
. In (
4
), let the measurable space be
\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{Z}^{\otimes n}
, the distributions be
\mathbb{P}_{W,S}
and
\mathbb{Q}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{S}
, and the function be
\lambda\mathrm{gen}(w,s)
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
; then

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W,S}\|\mathbb{Q}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{S})\geq\lambda\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]-\log\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{\lambda\mathrm{gen}(W^{\prime},S)}\Big].

(21)

Re-arranging the equation, we obtain an upper bound of the expected generalization error that holds for every
\lambda>0
, namely

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\bigg(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W,S}\|\mathbb{Q}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{S})+\log\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{\lambda\mathrm{gen}(W^{\prime},S)}\Big]\bigg).

(22)

Dissecting (
22
), we note that the Donsker and Varadhan
Lemma

2.1
served effectively to decouple the hypothesis
W
and the data
S
. The effects of this decoupling are manifested in the right-hand side of the equation:

1.

The dependence between the two random objects is now captured by the relative entropy
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W,S}\|\mathbb{Q}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{S})
, where
\mathbb{P}_{W,S}
is the joint distribution (or coupling) between
W
and
S
, and where
\mathbb{Q}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{S}
is the distribution between the data
S
and the independent (or decoupled) hypothesis
W^{\prime}
. The larger the dependence, the less effective the decoupling lemma, and the worse the upper bound.

2.

The concentration of the decoupled function is now captured by
\log\mathbb{E}\big[\exp\big(\lambda\mathrm{gen}(W^{\prime},S)\big)\big]
. Since under the decoupled distribution
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W^{\prime},S)\big]=0
, this term is equivalent to the CGF
\Lambda_{\mathrm{gen}(W^{\prime},S)}(\lambda)
. The CGF of a random variable is intimately connected to its tails through the Cramér–Chernoff method
[
82
, Section 2.2]
. The faster the CGF grows as
\lambda
increases, the less the random variable
\mathrm{gen}(W^{\prime},S)
concentrates around zero. This makes this conversion less helpful and the upper bound larger.

13.1.1
Warm-Up: A Slow-Rate Bound for Losses With a Bounded Range

A requirement for the Donsker and Varadhan
Lemma

2.1
and (
22
) to hold is that the MGF of
\mathrm{gen}(W^{\prime},S)
is bounded. We will deal with this more generally below. For now, it suffices to know that if the loss has a range contained in
[a,b]
and the instances are independent and identically distributed, then
\log\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{\lambda\mathrm{gen}(W^{\prime},S)}\Big]\leq\nicefrac{{\lambda^{2}(b-a)^{2}}}{{8n}}
. Therefore,
Equation

22
can be further bounded by

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W,S}\|\mathbb{Q}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{S})}{\lambda}+\frac{\lambda(b-a)^{2}}{8n}

for all
\lambda>0
. The right-hand side of this equation is convex with respect to
\lambda
; therefore, it has a minimum that tightens the bound. The optimization of the parameter
\lambda
results in the following theorem due to
Xu and Raginsky [5]
.

Theorem 4.1

(
Xu and Raginsky [5, Theorem 1, adapted to bounded losses]
)
.

Consider a loss function with a range contained in
[a,b]
. Then, for every data-independent distribution
\mathbb{Q}
on
\mathcal{W}

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq(b-a)\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W,S}\|\mathbb{Q}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{S})}{2n}}

In particular, choosing
\mathbb{Q}
to be the marginal distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\circ\mathbb{P}_{S}
is optimal and

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq(b-a)\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{2n}}.

The optimality of the choice of the prototypical distribution on samples from the data distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\circ\mathbb{P}_{S}
comes from the golden formula from
Proposition

2.4
and the theorem holds for distributions
\mathbb{Q}
such that
\mathbb{P}_{W,S}\not\ll\mathbb{Q}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{S}
by the convention that in that case
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W,S}\|\mathbb{Q}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{S})\to\infty
. For this reason, after this subsection, all results in this chapter will be presented in terms of mutual information only. This result was particularly celebrated since it provides us with a simple relationship between the generalization error of a learning algorithm and the mutual information between the algorithm’s input (the training data
S
) and its output hypothesis
W
. Moreover, this relationship resembles the classical relationships provided by the uniform convergence property of a hypothesis class
\mathcal{W}
where the upper bound on the generalization error was of the order
\sqrt{\nicefrac{{\mathfrak{C}}}{{n}}}
, where
\mathfrak{C}
represents the complexity of the class
\mathcal{W}
measured by, for example, the VC or the Natarajan dimension (
Section

7
). In this case, however, the complexity term describes the algorithm and its relationship with the data and is represented by the mutual information, suggesting that the more the algorithm depends on the data, the less it generalizes.
If the hypothesis returned by the algorithm is independent of the data and
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}=\mathbb{Q}
a.s.; then, the mutual information is 0 and the generalization error is also 0, as we expected from the start. On the other hand, if the returned hypothesis is a deterministic function of the training set
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}=\delta_{f(S)}
and both the hypothesis
W
and the instances
Z_{i}
are continuous, then the mutual information tends to infinity and the bound is vacuous.

If we consider the algorithm as a channel processing a training set
S
to obtain a hypothesis
W
(
Figure

4.5
left), the mutual information describes the average inefficiency in trying to encode the hypothesis
W
without employing the training set
S
, when this set is known. That is, it describes how much knowing the training set
S
reduces the uncertainty about the hypothesis
W
on average with respect to the data distribution.

Due to the symmetry of the mutual information (
Proposition

2.4
), the reverse is also true. Consider the backward channel
\mathbb{P}_{S}^{W}
that is naturally defined from the joint distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W,S}
. Then, the mutual information describes the average inefficiency in trying to encode the dataset
S
without employing the hypothesis
W
when this hypothesis is known. That is, how much does knowing the hypothesis
W
reduces the uncertainty of the dataset
S
on average with respect to the prototypical hypothesis distribution. In particular, for discrete data and hypotheses, it amounts to the average extra bits needed to encode the training set if the returned hypothesis is not used. Therefore, the hypothesis contains information on the training set. Considering this rather artificial channel already elucidates some connections between privacy and generalization: if an algorithm is private, the information that an algorithm contains on the training set should be small, and therefore
\mathrm{I}(W;S)
should be bounded from above. This intuition will be formalized later in
Chapter

6
.

13.1.2
Losses With a Bounded CGF

The technique described above is not restricted to bounded losses. As mentioned before, a requirement for the Donsker and Varadhan
Lemma

2.1
and (
22
) to hold is that the MGF of
\mathrm{gen}(W^{\prime},S)
is bounded, that is, that
\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{\lambda\mathrm{gen}(W^{\prime},S)}\Big]<\infty
. Since we are actually interested in controlling the CGF of
\mathrm{gen}(W^{\prime},S)
as per (
22
), it seems logical to consider losses with a bounded CGF. This includes unbounded losses with sufficiently behaved tails, that is, losses that have a probability of taking large values that vanishes exponentially fast.

Definition 4.1

(Bounded CGF)
.

A loss function
\ell:\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{Z}
is of
bounded CGF
with respect to the data distribution
\mathbb{P}_{Z}
(or just of bounded CGF, if the data distribution is clear from the context) if, for every hypothesis
w\in\mathcal{W}
, there is a convex and continuously differentiable function
\psi(\lambda)
defined on
[0,b)
for some
b\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
\psi(0)=\psi^{\prime}(0)=0
and
\Lambda_{-\ell(w,Z)}\leq\psi(\lambda)
for all
\lambda\in[0,b)
.

For a fixed hypothesis
w\in\mathcal{W}
, using the algebra of CGFs from
Section

3.6
yields that

\Lambda_{\mathrm{gen}(w,S)}(\lambda)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda_{-\ell(w,Z_{i})}\Big(\frac{\lambda}{n}\Big)\leq n\psi\Big(\frac{\lambda}{n}\Big).

for all
\lambda\in[0,nb)
. Therefore, the CGF of
\mathrm{gen}(W^{\prime},S)
can be bounded by

\log\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{\lambda\mathrm{gen}(W^{\prime},S)}\Big]=\log\mathbb{E}_{w\sim\mathbb{Q}}\bigg[\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{\lambda\mathrm{gen}(w,S)}\Big]\bigg]\leq n\psi\Big(\frac{\lambda}{n}\Big)

(23)

for all
\lambda\in[0,nb)
. Combining this upper bound and the change of variable
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}n
with (
22
) results in

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{\prime}}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W,S}\|\mathbb{Q}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{S})}{n}-\psi(\lambda^{\prime})\bigg),

which holds for all
\lambda^{\prime}\in[0,b)
. Finally, optimizing this equation using
Lemma

2.5
from
Section

5.1
gives us the following guarantee in expectation.

Theorem 4.2

(
Bu et al. [7, Theorem 2 and Proposition 2]
)
.

Consider a loss function with a bounded CGF (
Definition

4.1
). Then, for every data-independent distribution
\mathbb{Q}
on
\mathcal{W}

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\psi_{*}^{-1}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W,S}\|\mathbb{Q}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{S})}{n}\bigg).

In particular, choosing
\mathbb{Q}
to be the marginal distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\circ\mathbb{P}_{S}
,

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\psi_{*}^{-1}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}\bigg).

The inverse of the convex conjugate of the function dominating the CGF
\psi_{*}^{-1}
characterizes the tail behavior of the loss function through the Cramér–Chernoff method
[
82
, Section 2.2]
as we will see in
Section

1
. For now, it is enough to know that
\psi_{*}^{-1}
is a non-decreasing function such that
\psi_{*}^{-1}(0)=0
and
\lim_{y\to\infty}\psi_{*}^{-1}(y)\to\infty
, and that, for a fixed hypothesis
w
, for all
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

{R}(w)\leq\widehat{{R}}(w,S)+\psi_{*}^{-1}\bigg(\frac{\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n}\bigg).

That is, the rate at which the empirical risk of a fixed hypothesis concentrates around the population risk is at least
\psi_{*}^{-1}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}})
. With this in mind,
Theorem

4.2
becomes clearer. The bias of the empirical risk estimator of the population risk decreases at the rate of concentration of the said estimator for a fixed hypothesis, inflated by the dependence between the hypothesis and the training set, that is
\psi_{*}^{-1}\big(\nicefrac{{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}}{{n}}\big)
. As previously, if the hypothesis is independent of the training data, then
\mathrm{I}(W;S)=0
and the theorem indicates that the empirical risk is an unbiased estimator of the population risk. On the other hand, for continuous hypotheses obtained deterministically from continuous training data
\mathrm{I}(W;S)\to\infty
and the bound is vacuous.

There are several tail behaviors that are sufficiently common to have a proper name. Below, we discuss three of them:
sub-gamma
,
sub-exponential
13
13

13

Here, we are considering the sub-exponential characterization of random variables from
Wainwright [71, Theorem 2.13]
and not the one given by
[
82
, Excercise 2.22]
.
, and
sub-Gaussian
. Loosely speaking, they represent losses whose tail is lighter than a gamma, exponential, and Gaussian random variable respectively. As a result, these three behaviors have in common that the generalization error bound has a dominating term that vanishes at a slow rate
\sqrt{\nicefrac{{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}}{{n}}}
.

Sub-Gamma Losses.

A loss is
(\sigma^{2},c)
-sub-gamma
if it has a CGF bounded by
\psi(\lambda)=\nicefrac{{\lambda^{2}\sigma^{2}}}{{2(1-c\lambda)}}
for all
\lambda\in(0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{c}})
in the sense of
Definition

4.1
. Then, since
\psi_{*}^{-1}(y)=\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}y}+cy

[
82
, Section 2.4]
, a corollary of
Theorem

4.2
is that if the loss is
(\sigma^{2},c)
-sub-gamma, then

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}}+c\cdot\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n},

combining a slow and a fast rate of bias decrease.

Sub-Exponential Losses.

A loss is
(\sigma^{2},c)
-sub-exponential
if it has a CGF bounded by
\psi(\lambda)=\nicefrac{{\lambda^{2}\sigma^{2}}}{{2}}
for all
\lambda\in(0,\frac{1}{c})
in the sense of
Definition

4.1
. Therefore,

\psi_{*}^{-1}(y)=\begin{cases}\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}y}&\textnormal{if }\lambda=\sqrt{\frac{2y}{\sigma^{2}}}\leq\frac{1}{c}\\
cy+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2c^{2}}&\textnormal{otherwise}\end{cases}.

(24)

As we show in
[
142
, Appendix B.4]
, the condition for the first case may be re-written as
y\leq\nicefrac{{\sigma^{2}}}{{2c^{2}}}
and similarly the condition for the second case as
y>\nicefrac{{\sigma^{2}}}{{2c^{2}}}
, which simplifies the equation to

\psi_{*}^{-1}(y)=\begin{cases}\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}y}&\textnormal{if }y\leq\nicefrac{{\sigma^{2}}}{{2c^{2}}}\\
(c+1)y&\textnormal{otherwise}\end{cases}.

(25)

In this way, a corollary of
Theorem

4.2
is that if the loss is
(\sigma^{2},c)
-sub-exponential, then

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\begin{cases}\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}}&\textnormal{if }\mathrm{I}(W;S)\leq\frac{n\sigma^{2}}{2c^{2}}\\
(c+1)\cdot\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}&\textnormal{otherwise}\end{cases},

which also combines a slow and a fast rate of bias decrease. For smaller values of the dependence
\mathrm{I}(W;S)
, the slow rate dominates; while for larger values the fast rate dominates.

Sub-Gaussian Losses.

A loss is
\sigma^{2}
-sub-Gaussian
if it has a CGF bounded by
\psi(\lambda)=\nicefrac{{\lambda^{2}\sigma^{2}}}{{2}}
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
in the sense of
Definition

4.1
. Then, since
\psi_{*}^{-1}(y)=\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}y}
, a corollary of
Theorem

4.2
is that if the loss is
\sigma^{2}
-sub-Gaussian, then

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}}.

(26)

This was, in fact, the theorem presented by
Xu and Raginsky [5]
.
Theorem

4.1
is a corollary of this result using the property that if a loss has a range contained in
[a,b]
a.s., then the loss is
\nicefrac{{(b-a)^{2}}}{{4}}
-sub-Gaussian.

13.2
A Fast-Rate and a Mixed-Rate Bound: Losses With a Bounded Range

In the subsection above, we noted that for losses with a range contained in
[a,b]
, the bias of the empirical risk estimator of the population risk vanishes at a slow rate
\sqrt{\nicefrac{{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}}{{n}}}
. However, we can benefit from using a different estimator. If we consider a linear function of the empirical risk as our estimator, we can find a bound that vanishes at a fast rate
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}}{{n}}
. This advantage comes from considering a tighter characterization of the CGF of bounded losses than the one provided by their sub-Gaussian nature. Without loss of generality, in this subsection, we will consider losses with a range bounded in
[0,1]
. If, instead, the loss has a range bounded in
[a,b]
, the bound can be shifted by
-a
and scaled by
\nicefrac{{1}}{{(b-a)}}
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Consider a loss function with a range contained in
[0,1]
. Then, for every
c\in(0,1]
and every
\gamma>1
,

\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq c\gamma\log\left(\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}\right)\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]+c\gamma\cdot\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}+\gamma\kappa(c),

where
\kappa(c)\coloneqq 1-c(1-\log c)
.
In particular, for interpolating algorithms (that is, when
\mathbb{E}^{S}[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)]=0
), the optimal parameters are
\gamma\to 1
and
c=\exp\big({-\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}}\big)
and then

\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq 1-e^{-\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}}\leq\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}.

This bound shows that the population risk can be bounded from above by a linear combination of the empirical risk and the normalized complexity measured by
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}}{{n}}
, where the coefficients for each term and the bias can change depending of the values of each term and ensure that the bound is always in
[0,1]
.

The parameter
\gamma
controls the influence of the empirical risk compared to the normalized complexity: if the empirical risk is large relative to the normalized complexity, then
\gamma
is larger and the normalized complexity coefficient increases, if instead the empirical risk is small or even close to interpolation, then
\gamma
is close to
1
and the empirical risk coefficient increases. In particular, for a fixed value of
c
, the optimal value of
\gamma
is

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=1+\left[-1-\mathtt{W}\left(-\exp\left(-1-\frac{c\cdot\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}+\kappa(c)}{c\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]}\right)\right)\right]^{-1}

\displaystyle\approx 1+\left[\sqrt{2\cdot\frac{c\cdot\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}+\kappa(c)}{c\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]}}+\frac{5}{6}\cdot\frac{c\cdot\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}+\kappa(c)}{c\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]}\right],

where
\mathtt{W}
is the Lambert W function and the -1 branch is approximated following
[
173
]
.

The parameter
c\in(0,1]
controls how much weight is given to the empirical risk and the normalized complexity terms compared to the bias. For larger values of the empirical risk and the normalized complexity term, the value of
c
is small, decreasing their contribution to the bound and increasing the contribution of the bias
\kappa(c)\in[0,1)
. If the empirical risk and the normalized complexity term are smaller, then the value of
c
approaches
1
, where the contribution of these two terms is only controlled by
\gamma
and the bias is
0
. In fact, a weaker version of
Theorem

4.3
can be obtained considering this small empirical risk and small normalized complexity regime by letting
c=1
.

Corollary 4.1
.

Consider a loss function with a range contained in
[0,1]
. Then, for every
\gamma>1
,

\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq\gamma\log\Big(\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}\Big)\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]+\gamma\cdot\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}.

This weaker version of the result was discovered by
Catoni [24, Theorem 1.2.1]
and presented in another form using a parameter
\lambda>0
such that
\lambda=n\log\nicefrac{{\gamma}}{{(\gamma-1)}}
around a decade prior to the
Xu and Raginsky [5]
’s
Theorem

4.1
, but it did not receive as much attention. In part, this was because the implications of such a result and the nice properties of the mutual information
\mathrm{I}(W;S)
to describe the complexity of an algorithm were not emphasized. As in
Theorem

4.3
, for interpolating algorithms, the optimal parameter is
\gamma\to 1
and the bound has a fast rate
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}}{{n}}
. This is reminiscent of the classical generalization guarantees from uniform convergence in the realizable setting for classification problems (see
Section

7.2
).

Catoni [24, Theorem 1.2.1]
also presented an equivalent bound to
Theorem

4.3
, although in a different form that does not make evident its fast-rate nature. Throughout this subsection, we will show that the two results are equivalent and how to obtain one from the other. We will focus the exposition following our proof of the result as it follows naturally from an application of a variant of
Theorem

4.2
.
To prove
Theorem

4.3
, we need to first introduce the following result
[
149
]
.

Lemma 4.1

(
Maurer [149, Lemma 3]
)
.

Let
X
be a random variable taking values in
[0,1]
and
Y
be the unique Bernoulli random variable such that
\mathbb{P}_{Y}[1]=1-\mathbb{P}_{Y}[0]=\mathbb{E}[Y]=\mathbb{E}[X]
. Then, if
f:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
is convex, then
\mathbb{E}\big[f(X)\big]\leq\mathbb{E}\big[f(Y)\big]
.

Consider a loss
\ell(W^{\prime},Z)
with a range in
[0,1]
and let
L
be the unique Bernoulli random variable with
\mathbb{P}_{L}[1]=\mathbb{E}[\ell(W^{\prime},Z)]=\mathbb{E}[L]
, where
W^{\prime}
is distributed according to
\mathbb{P}_{W}
. Now, let
f(l;\lambda)=e^{-\lambda(l-\mathbb{E}[L])}
be a convex function on
l
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, it follows from
Lemma

4.1
that the CGF of
-\ell(W^{\prime},Z)
is bounded from above by the mirror image of the CGF of
L
, that is, for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}

\Lambda_{-\ell(W^{\prime},Z)}(\lambda)=\log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-\lambda(\ell(W^{\prime},Z)-\mathbb{E}[\ell(W^{\prime},Z)])}\right]\leq\log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-\lambda(L-\mathbb{E}[L])}\right]=\Lambda_{L}(-\lambda).

In this way, since the CGF of a Bernoulli random variable is known
[
82
, page 23]
, it follows that
\Lambda_{-\ell(W^{\prime},Z)}(\lambda)\leq\psi(\lambda)\coloneqq\lambda\mathbb{E}[{R}(W)]+\log\big(1-\mathbb{E}[{R}(W)](1-e^{-\lambda})\big)
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, where we noted that
\mathbb{E}[L]=\mathbb{E}[{R}(W)]
. This means that the CGF of the loss is bounded in a weaker sense than in
Definition

4.1
, as the random loss considered takes into account the random hypothesis
W
and the upper bound depends on its marginal distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}
. However, it is this weakening of the condition which allows us to obtain a tighter guarantee following a similar procedure.
In the previous section, we mentioned that requiring that the CGF of an unbounded loss
-\ell(W^{\prime},Z)
is, for example, sub-Gaussian is complicated since we need to know the distribution of the marginal distribution of the hypothesis
\mathbb{P}_{W}
. Hence, we resorted to the stronger requirement that the CGF of the unbounded loss
-\ell(w,Z)
is sub-Gaussian for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
. On the other hand, now we can make use of this weaker assumption since it follows from the boundedness of the loss.

Similarly to before, using the algebra of the CGFs to bound the CGF of
\mathrm{gen}(W^{\prime},S)
as in (
23
) and combining it with (
21
) with the change of variable
\lambda=\lambda^{\prime}n
results in

\psi_{*}\Big(\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\Big)\leq\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n},

where the left hand side is obtained by optimizing
\lambda\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]-\psi(\lambda)
and by the definition of the convex conjugate
Definition

2.17
. Operating similarly to
[
82
, page 23]
we see that for every
t\in\big[0,\mathbb{E}[{R}(W)]\big]
,

\psi_{*}(t)=\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{KL}}\Big(\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]-t\big\|\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]\Big),

where
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\hat{r}\|r)\coloneqq\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathrm{Ber}(\hat{r})\|\mathrm{Ber}(r)\big)
, leading us the next intermediate result.

Lemma 4.2
.

Consider a loss function with a range contained in
[0,1]
. If
\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]\leq\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]
, then

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{KL}}\Big(\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]\big\|\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]\Big)\leq\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}.

This lemma is the “in expectation” analogue to the Seeger–Langford
[
21
,
22
]
PAC-Bayes bound. This suggests that the difference between the empirical risk estimation of the population risk decreases at a fast rate
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}}{{n}}
when measured with the metric
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{KL}}
. However, from this result, it is hard to obtain a bound on the population risk that we can interpret, as the generalized inverse of
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{KL}}
does not have an analytical form. This issue can be resolved by employing the variational representation of the relative entropy borrowed from
f
-divergences (
Corollary

2.2
) to
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\hat{r},r)
. Let
g_{0}=g(0)
and
g_{1}=g(1)
, then for all
g_{0}
and
g_{1}
in
(-\infty,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\hat{r}\|r\big)

\displaystyle\geq 1+\hat{r}g_{1}+(1-\hat{r})g_{0}-re^{g_{1}}-(1-r)e^{g_{0}}

\displaystyle\geq 1+g_{0}+(g_{1}-g_{0})\hat{r}-e^{g_{0}}+r(e^{g_{0}}-e^{g_{1}})

(27)

For (
27
) to be relevant to us, we require that
g_{0}\geq g_{1}
, as otherwise we would obtain a lower bound instead of an upper bound. To simplify the equations, let
\gamma=\nicefrac{{e^{g_{0}}}}{{(e^{g_{0}}-e^{g_{1}})}}\geq 1
, which implies that
g_{0}-g_{1}=\log\big(\gamma/(\gamma-1)\big)
and therefore

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\hat{r}\|r\big)\geq 1+g_{0}-e^{g_{0}}-\log\Big(\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}\Big)\hat{r}+\gamma^{-1}e^{g_{0}}r.

(28)

To finalize the proof, note that the optimal value of the parameter
g_{0}
is
\log\big(\nicefrac{{\gamma}}{{(\gamma-r)}}\big)
and therefore since
\gamma>1
and
r\in[0,1]
, then
g_{0}\geq 0
. Finally, letting
c\coloneqq e^{-g_{0}}\in(0,1]
, using the inequality from (
28
) in
Lemma

4.2
, and re-arranging the terms recovers
Theorem

4.3
. Note that
Lemma

4.2
only proves
Theorem

4.3
when the empirical risk is smaller than the population risk, however, in the other scenario the bound holds trivially.

Before moving to the next subsection, let us recover
Catoni [24, Theorem 1.2.1]
’s stronger bound from
Lemma

4.2
. This bound is also obtained employing a variational representation of the relative entropy, although this time it follows from the Donsker and Varadhan
Lemma

2.1
. Let
g_{0}=g(0)
and
g_{1}=g(1)
, then for all
g_{0}
and
g_{1}
in
(-\infty,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\hat{r}\|r\big)

\displaystyle\geq g_{1}\hat{r}+g_{0}(1-\hat{r})-\log\left(e^{g_{1}}r+e^{g_{0}}(1-r)\right)

\displaystyle=g_{0}+(g_{1}-g_{0})\hat{r}-\log\left(e^{g_{0}}+\left(e^{g_{1}}-e^{g_{0}}\right)r\right)

\displaystyle=-(g_{0}-g_{1})\hat{r}-\log\left(1-r\big(1-e^{-(g_{0}-g_{1})}\big)\right).

(29)

As before, the bound resulting from (
29
) is only relevant to us when
g_{0}\geq g_{1}
, as otherwise it results in a lower bound instead of an upper bound. The equations can be simplified by letting
\lambda=n(g_{0}-g_{1})\geq 0
. After this change of variable, using the inequality from (
29
) in
Lemma

4.2
, and re-arranging the terms yields the desired result.

Theorem 4.4

(
[
24
, Theorem 1.2.1]
)
.

Consider a loss function with a range contained in
[0,1]
. Then, for every
\lambda>0

\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq\frac{1}{1-e^{-\frac{\lambda}{n}}}\left[1-e^{-\frac{\lambda}{n}\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]-\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}}\right].

Theorems

4.3
and
4.4
are equivalent in the sense that they are both obtained via a variational representation of the relative entropy from
Lemma

4.2
. That is, for every value of the empirical risk and the mutual information, there exists a value of
\lambda>0
,
\gamma>1
, and
c\in(0,1]
such that the population risk upper bounds in
Theorems

4.3
and
4.4
and
Lemma

4.2
are the same.

Starting from
Theorem

4.4
, one could have also easily obtained
Theorem

4.3
as follows. Let
\lambda=n\gamma\log\big(\nicefrac{{\gamma}}{{\gamma-1}}\big)
, then
\gamma>1
and

\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq\gamma\left[1-e^{-\gamma\log\left(\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}\right)\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]-\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}}\right].

Finally, one can note that the function
1-e^{-x}
is a non-decreasing, concave, continuous function for
x>0
and therefore can be upper bounded by its envelope, that is,
1-e^{-x}=\inf_{a>0}\{e^{-a}x+1-e^{-a}(1+a)\}
. Using the envelope in the equation above and letting
c\coloneqq e^{-a}\in(0,1]
completes the equivalence.

13.2.1
A Mixed-Rate Bound

To get a better understanding of
Theorem

4.3
, consider the small empirical risk and small normalized complexity regime, that is, let
c=1
and consider
Corollary

4.1
. Now, one can relax the bound and optimize the parameter
\gamma
to derive a parameter-free bound.

When the empirical risk is smaller than the normalized complexity and
\gamma
is small, a good upper bound for
\log\big(\nicefrac{{\gamma}}{{(\gamma-1)}}\big)
is
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}(x+\nicefrac{{1}}{{x}})
. Using this approximation in
Corollary

4.1
yields that

\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq\frac{2\gamma-1}{\gamma-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]+\gamma\cdot\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}.

Optimizing this equation with respect to
\gamma
yields the following result.

Theorem 4.5
.

Consider a loss function with a range contained in
[0,1]
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]+\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}+\sqrt{2\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]\cdot\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}}.

The bound above is symmetric and gives the same weight to the empirical risk and the normalized complexity measured by
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}}{{n}}
. Moreover, it has a mixed rate: on the one hand, it has the linear dependence on the normalized complexity of a fast-rate bound, but on the other hand, it also contains a squared root dependence typical of a slow-rate bound. However, the squared root dependence on the normalized complexity is weighted by the empirical risk. Hence, if the empirical risk is smaller than the normalized complexity, then the fast rate dominates.

13.3
Interpolating Between the Slow and the Fast Rate: Losses With a Bounded Moment

In the previous subsections we saw that when the CGF of the loss is bounded by some function
\psi
, then the population risk can be bounded from above by a function of the empirical risk and the ratio between the mutual information between the algorithm’s output and the training set and the number of data instances. The rate of the bound, or the speed at which it vanishes with respect to the number of samples, is determined by the convex conjugate of the dominating function
\psi_{*}
. For example, if the loss is bounded, we can obtain a fast rate (
Theorem

4.3
), and if it is sub-Gaussian, we can obtain a slow rate (
26
).

However, as discussed in
Section

3.6
, there are situations where the loss may not even have a CGF. If the loss has a bounded
p
-th moment (that is,
\mathbb{E}\big[\ell(w,Z)^{p}\big]\leq m_{p}
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
) for some
p>1
, but it does not have a CGF, then we say that the loss has a
heavy tail
. A reasonable question is then: “For losses with a heavy tail, can we find generalization bounds? If so, at which rate?”. It turns out that we can, and the bound’s rate interpolates between a slow rate when
p=2
and a fast rate when
p\to\infty
.

Theorem 4.6
.

Consider a loss function
\ell(w,Z)
with a
p
-th moment bounded by
m_{p}
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
. Then, for every
c\in(0,1]
and every
\gamma>1
,

\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq c\gamma\log\left(\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}\right)\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}_{\leq t^{\star}}\big]+m_{p}^{\frac{1}{p}}\left[\frac{p}{p-1}\right]\left[c\gamma\cdot\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}+\gamma\kappa(c)\right]^{\frac{p-1}{p}},

where
\kappa(c)\coloneqq 1-c(1-\log c)
and

t^{\star}\coloneqq m_{p}^{\frac{1}{p}}\cdot\left[c\gamma\cdot\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}+\gamma\kappa(c)\right]^{-\frac{1}{p}}

To simplify the interpretation, let
c=1
, then the rate is
m_{p}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{p}}}\cdot\left(\nicefrac{{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}}{{n}}\right)^{\nicefrac{{(p-1)}}{{p}}}
.
The term
m_{p}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{p}}}
controls the weight given to the complexity term and is equal to the
\mathcal{L}_{p}
norm of the loss. Note that by the non-decreasing nature of the
\mathcal{L}_{p}
norms a trivial bound would be
\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq m_{p}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{p}}}
. Hence, the presented bound improves upon this as for increasing number of samples, the contribution of
m_{p}^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{p}}}
vanishes.
Then, the term
\left(\nicefrac{{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}}{{n}}\right)^{\nicefrac{{(p-1)}}{{p}}}
shows how the rate is interpolating between a slow rate when
p=2
and a fast rate when
p\to\infty
. When
p=2
, the tail of the loss could be as heavy as a Pareto distribution without a 3rd moment (or with a parameter
a\in[2,3)

[
52
, Chapter 20]
), while when
p\to\infty
, the loss is essentially bounded, that is,
\esssup\ell(w,Z)\leq m_{\infty}
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
. For even heavier tails, that is, when
p\in(1,2)
, the rate is slower than the standard slow rate since we are considering a scenario where the loss does not even have a finite variance. The term
\widehat{{R}}_{\leq t^{\star}}
represents a truncated version of the empirical risk and comes from a refinement of
Alquier [30]
’s truncation method, which is discussed next.

13.3.1
Alquier’s Truncation Method

We derived
Theorem

4.6
in
[
164
]
borrowing the
truncation method
developed by
Alquier [30]
in his Ph.D. thesis in the context of PAC-Bayesian bounds. This method consisted of decomposing the loss as
\ell\leq\ell^{-}_{\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}+\ell^{+}_{\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}
, where

\ell^{-}_{\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}\coloneqq\min\left\{\ell,\frac{n}{\lambda}\right\}\textnormal{ and }\ell^{+}_{\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}\coloneqq\left[\ell-\frac{n}{\lambda}\right]_{+}

represent a truncated version of the loss (hence the name) and its unbounded remainder, where
[x]_{+}\coloneqq\max\{x,0\}
. With this decomposition, one may bound the population risk
{R}^{-}_{\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}
associated with the truncated loss using the techniques described above, and bound the population risk
{R}^{+}_{\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}
associated with the unbounded remainder using standard tail inequalities. Then, an appropriate selection of the truncation point
\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}
, that trades off the tightness of the generalization bounds for bounded losses and the likelihood (or lack thereof) of the tail event, results in a bound with the desired rate.

To be precise, the population risk of the unbounded remainder
{R}^{+}_{\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}
can be bounded by the relationship between moments and tails
[
50
, Lemma 4.4]
. That is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[{R}^{+}_{\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}(W)\big]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\big[{R}^{+}_{\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}(W)\geq t\big]\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[{R}(W)\geq t+\frac{n}{\lambda}\right]\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{\frac{n}{\lambda}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[{R}(W)\geq t\right]\mathrm{d}t.

In
[
164
]
, we employ a refinement of this truncation method, which was already suggested by
Alquier [132]
itself. Consider instead the decomposition of the loss
\ell=\ell_{<\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}+\ell_{\geq\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}
, where

\ell_{<\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}\coloneqq\ell\cdot\mathbb{I}_{\{\ell<\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}\}}\textnormal{ and }\ell_{\geq\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}\coloneqq\ell\cdot\mathbb{I}_{\{\ell\geq\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}\}}

again represent a truncated version of the loss and its unbounded reminder. The technique follows analogously, with the main advantage that
\ell_{\leq\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}
has the potential to be much smaller than
\ell^{-}_{\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}
since all losses larger than
\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}
contribute
\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}
to the former and
0
to the latter, while the population risk of the unbounded remainder
{R}_{\geq\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}
can be bounded by the exact same quantity as
{R}_{\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}^{+}
, that is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[{R}_{\geq\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}(W)\big]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\big[{R}_{\geq\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}(W)\geq t\big]\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{\frac{n}{\lambda}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left[{R}(W)\geq t\right]\mathrm{d}t.

(30)

Hence, we continue with this version of the method for the rest of the subsection.

13.3.2
Optimal Truncation Point

If the loss
\ell(w,Z)
has a
p
-th moment bounded by
m_{p}
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
, combining Markov’s inequality with (
30
) yields

\mathbb{E}\big[{R}_{\geq\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}(W)\big]\leq\int_{\frac{n}{\lambda}}^{\infty}\frac{m_{p}}{t^{p}}\cdot\mathrm{d}t=\frac{m_{p}}{p-1}\left(\frac{\lambda}{n}\right)^{p-1}.

(31)

Then, the above bound on the unbounded remainder together with
Theorem

4.3
yield the following intermediate result.

Lemma 4.3
.

Consider a loss function
\ell(w,Z)
with a
p
-th moment bounded by
m_{p}
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
. Then, for every
c\in(0,1]
and every
\gamma>1

\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq c\gamma\log\left(\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}\right)\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}_{\leq\frac{n}{\lambda}}(W,S)\big]+c\gamma\cdot\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{\lambda}+\gamma\kappa(c)\cdot\frac{n}{\lambda}+\frac{m_{p}}{p-1}\left(\frac{\lambda}{n}\right)^{p-1},

where
\kappa(c)\coloneqq 1-c(1-\log c)
.

Finally, choosing the optimal parameter
\lambda
trades off (i) the penalty of the loss’ tail after the truncation point
\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}
for (ii) the penalty of that range range
\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}
while exploiting the existing sharp bounds for losses with a bounded range. This optimization results in
Theorem

4.6
.

13.3.3
A Bound for Losses With a Bounded Variance

A particularly important case of the results above is the one for losses with a bounded second moment, as it presents the weaker assumption that still recovers a slow rate of
\mathcal{O}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})
. However, the raw second moment can be much larger than the variance as for every random variable
X
it holds that
\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]=\mathrm{Var}(X)+\mathbb{E}[X]^{2}
. It is of interest, then, to derive bounds that only assume that the loss has a bounded variance. In the following theorem, we present a bound of this type that essentially substitutes the second moment dependence by the variance. However, the bound has an extra multiplicative factor that prevents it to have exactly a slow rate. When the mutual information is small, the rate is essentially
\mathcal{O}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})
, but this degenerates as it increases towards
n
, where the bound is essentially vacuous.

Theorem 4.7
.

Consider a loss function
\ell(w,Z)
with a variance bounded by
\sigma^{2}
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
. Then, for every
c\in(0,1]
and every
\gamma>1

\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq\Bigg[1-2\sqrt{\kappa_{2}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}+\kappa_{3}}\Bigg]_{+}^{-1}\Bigg[\kappa_{1}\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]+2\sigma\sqrt{\kappa_{2}\cdot\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}+\kappa_{3}}\Bigg],

where
\kappa_{1}\coloneqq c\gamma\log\big(\nicefrac{{\gamma}}{{(\gamma-1)}}\big)
,
\kappa_{2}\coloneqq c\gamma
, and
\kappa_{3}\coloneqq\gamma\big(1-c(1-\log c)\big)
.

Note that there is nothing preventing us to show the equivalent of
Theorem

4.6
only assuming that the
p
-th moment of the loss
\mathbb{E}[\ell(W^{\prime},Z)]
is bounded with respect to the product distribution of the data and the algorithm’s hypothesis
\mathbb{P}_{W}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{Z}
. Although this condition is weaker, it is harder to guarantee as it requires some knowldege of the data distribution
\mathbb{P}_{Z}

and
the algorithm’s Markov kernel
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
, a knowledge that could have been used instead to directly find a bound on the population risk
\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]
. However, considering a result of this type is useful to prove
Theorem

4.7
.

The proof of this theorem follows by first considering the equivalent of
Theorem

4.6
for
p=2
, where instead of
m_{2}
one directly has
\mathbb{E}\big[\ell(W^{\prime},Z)^{2}]
. Then, we may employ the equality
\mathbb{E}\big[\ell(W^{\prime},Z)^{2}]\leq\mathrm{Var}(\ell(W^{\prime},Z))+\mathbb{E}[{R}(W)]^{2}
together with the inequality
\mathrm{Var}(\ell(W^{\prime},Z^{\prime}))\leq\sup_{w\in\mathcal{W}}\mathrm{Var}(\ell(w,Z))=\sigma^{2}
obtain an expression that only depends on the parameter
\sigma^{2}
bounding the algorithm-independent variance. Finally, using the inequality
\sqrt{x+y}\leq\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{y}
and re-arranging the equation while accepting the convention that
1/0\to\infty
completes the proof.

14
The randomized-subsample Setting

As mentioned previously, the bounds presented in
Section

13
become vacuous as the mutual information diverges, that is
\mathrm{I}(W;S)\to\infty
. This can happen in situations where the hypothesis is a deterministic function of the training set
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}=\delta_{f(S)}
and both the hypothesis and the problem instances
Z_{i}
are continuous. An example of this situation would be when the hypothesis returned by the algorithm is the empirical average of the data instances and those are Gaussian. Other pathological examples where the generalization error is small but the mutual information is large, leading to vacuous bounds, are presented in
[
134
]
.

For this reason,
Steinke and Zakynthinou [9]
, inspired by
Vapnik and Chervonenkis [174]
, introduced the
randomized-subsample setting
14
14

14

In
[
12
]
, this is called the random subset setting. However, this may cause confusion with the random-subset bounds in this monograph and in the literature.
. In this setting, it is considered that the training set
S
is obtained through the following mechanism. First, a super sample

\tilde{S}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{Z}_{1,0}&\tilde{Z}_{2,0}&\cdots&\tilde{Z}_{n,0}\\
\tilde{Z}_{1,1}&\tilde{Z}_{2,1}&\cdots&\tilde{Z}_{n,1}\end{pmatrix}^{\intercal}

of
2n
i.i.d. instances is obtained by sampling from the data distribution
\mathbb{P}_{Z}
. Then, an independent sequence of indices
U\coloneqq(U_{1},\ldots,U_{n})
is generated, where each index is distributed as a Bernoulli random variable, that is,
\mathbb{P}_{U_{i}}[0]=\mathbb{P}_{U_{i}}[1]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
. Finally, the training set is sub-sampled from the superset so that
Z_{i}=\tilde{Z}_{i,U_{i}}
. Unused samples, or
ghost samples

S_{\textnormal{ghost}}=\tilde{S}\setminus S
, are virtual and only exist for the purpose of the analysis. To disambiguate, in the following, we will often refer to the
standard setting
to consider the setting where
S
is collected by sampling
n
samples directly from
\mathbb{P}_{Z}
.

Imagine that we were to have access to a realization of the super sample
\tilde{s}
and the indices
u
. Then, for a hypothesis
w
, an estimate of the generalization error is the
empirical generalization error

\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(w,\tilde{s},u)\coloneqq\widehat{{R}}(w,s_{\textnormal{ghost}})-\widehat{{R}}(w,s),

where the empirical risk on the ghost samples
\widehat{{R}}(w,s_{\textnormal{ghost}})
acts as a proxy to the population risk. In fact, they have the same expected value since the hypothesis returned by the algorithm is independent of the ghost samples, that is
\mathbb{E}[\widehat{{R}}(W,S_{\textnormal{ghost}})]=\mathbb{E}[{R}(W)]
. Therefore, studying the bias of the empirical risk on the training data with respect to the empirical risk on the ghost samples is equivalent to studying its bias with respect to the population risk, that is
\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(W,\tilde{S},U)]=\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)]
.

Connection to the Rademacher Complexity.

The empirical generalization error is connected to the Rademacher complexity discussed in
Section

8
. For example, recall that the Rademacher complexity may be understood as a measure of the discrepancy between fictitious training and test sets
[
86
, Section 26.1]
. Similarly, the empirical generalization error is the discrepancy between a fictitious test and the real training set. In fact, ghost samples were used prior to
Steinke and Zakynthinou [9]
’s work to prove generalization bounds based on the Rademacher complexity
[
174
,
175
]
, and these works inspired them in their formalism. As an example of this connection, let
R_{i}=-1^{U_{i}}
and
R_{i}^{\prime}=R_{i}
, and note that
R_{i}
and
R_{i}^{\prime}
are Rademacher random variables. Then, for a fixed hypothesis
w
and a fixed supersample
\tilde{s}
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(w,\tilde{s},U)\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\big(\ell(w,z_{i,1})-\ell(w,z_{i,0})\big)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\ell(w,z_{i,1})\right]+\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}R^{\prime}_{i}\ell(w,z_{i,0})\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathrm{Rad}(\ell\circ\mathcal{W},\tilde{s}),

where the inequality comes from taking the supremum over all hypotheses
w\in\mathcal{W}
.

Similarly to what we did in
Section

13.1
, we can bound from above this bias through a change of measure employing the Donsker and Varadhan
Lemma

2.1
. However, the structure introduced by the super sample and the indices, allows us to consider the dependence with respect to the indices instead of the one with respect to the training data directly. To see this, consider a fixed super sample
\tilde{s}\in\mathcal{Z}^{n\times 2}
. Now, we can consider a random hypothesis
W^{\prime}
that depends on the super sample in some capacity
\mathbb{P}_{W^{\prime}}^{\tilde{S}=\tilde{s}}=\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{s})
. In this case, in (
4
), the measurable space is
\mathcal{W}\times\{0,1\}^{n}
, the distributions are
\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{S}=\tilde{s}}_{W,U}
and
\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{s})\otimes\mathbb{P}_{U}
, and the function is
\lambda\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(w,\tilde{s},u)
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. In this way, for a fixed super sample
\tilde{s}
, the equivalent to (
22
) is

\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{S}=\tilde{s}}\big[\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(W,\tilde{s},U)\big]\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\bigg(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{S}=\tilde{s}}_{W,U}\|\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{s})\otimes\mathbb{P}_{U})+\log\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{S}=\tilde{s}}\Big[e^{\lambda\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(W^{\prime},\tilde{s},U)}\Big]\bigg).

(32)

Finally, considering the super sample as a random variable and taking the expectation to both sides completes the analogy with (
22
).

14.1
A Slow Rate for Losses With a Bounded Range

Consider that the loss has a range bounded in
[a,b]
. Now, note that for a fixed hypothesis
w\in\mathcal{W}
and a fixed super sample
\tilde{s}
, we may write the empirical generalization error as

\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(w,\tilde{s},U)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big\{\ell(w,\tilde{z}_{i,1-U_{i}})-\ell(w,\tilde{z}_{i,U_{i}})\Big\}.

When written in this way, we may focus on the random variable
\ell(w,\tilde{z}_{i,1-U_{i}})-\ell(w,{z}_{i,U_{i}})
and note that it has mean zero and that it is contained in
[a-b,b-a]
. Therefore, this random variable is
(b-a)^{2}
-sub-Gaussian and by the algebra of CGFs from
Section

3.6
it follows that

\Lambda_{\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(w,\tilde{s},U)}\leq\frac{\lambda^{2}(b-a)^{2}}{2n},

which in turn means that
\log\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{\lambda\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(W^{\prime},\tilde{s},U)}\Big]\leq\nicefrac{{\lambda^{2}(b-a)^{2}}}{{2n}}
for all
\tilde{s}\in\mathcal{Z}^{n\times 2}
. Therefore,
Equation

32
can be further bounded by

\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{S}=\tilde{s}}\big[\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(W,\tilde{s},U)\big]\leq\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{S}=\tilde{s}}_{W,U}\|\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{s})\otimes\mathbb{P}_{U})}{\lambda}+\frac{\lambda(b-a)^{2}}{2n}

for all
\lambda>0
and all
\tilde{s}\in\mathcal{Z}^{n\times 2}
. As before, the right-hand side of this equation is convex with respect to
\lambda
and hence it has a minimum that tightens the bound. The optimization of the parameter
\lambda
results in the following extension of the theorem due to
Steinke and Zakynthinou [9]
.

Theorem 4.8

(
Steinke and Zakynthinou [9, Theorem 2, extended]
)
.

Consider a loss function with a range contained in
[a,b]
. Then, for every index-independent Markov kernel
\mathbb{Q}
from
\mathcal{Z}^{n\times 2}
to distributions on
\mathcal{W}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]

\displaystyle\leq(b-a)\mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{\frac{2\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{S}}_{W,U}\|\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S})\otimes\mathbb{P}_{U})}{n}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(b-a)\sqrt{\frac{2\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W,\tilde{S},U}\|\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S})\otimes\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{S}}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{U})}{n}}.

In particular, choosing
\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S})
to be the marginal Markov kernel
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}}=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U}\circ\mathbb{P}_{U}
is optimal and

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq(b-a)\mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{\frac{2\mathrm{I}^{\tilde{S}}(W;U)}{n}}\right]\leq(b-a)\sqrt{\frac{2\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})}{n}}.

As previously, the optimality of the choice of marginal distribution comes from the golden formula from
Proposition

2.4
and the theorem also holds for all Markov kernels
\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S})
such that
\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{S}}_{W,U}\not\ll\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S})\otimes\mathbb{P}_{U}
a.s. by the convention that in that case
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}^{\tilde{S}}_{W,U}\|\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S})\otimes\mathbb{P}_{U})\to\infty
for every realization
\tilde{s}
in which the absolute continuity condition does not hold. Also, the conditional mutual information arises from the
chain rule

\mathrm{I}(W,\tilde{S};U)=\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})+\mathrm{I}(\tilde{S};U)
and the fact that the indices are independent of the super sample. For this reason, we will also focus only on the result concerning the conditional mutual information. The extension with respect to
[
9
, Theorem 2]
comes from the first inequality, where the expected value is pulled outside of the square root, tightening the bound due to Jensen’s inequality.

This result is very similar in form to
Theorem

4.1
. It maintains the classical relationship provided by the uniform convergence property of a hypothesis class
\mathcal{W}
, where the upper bound on the generalization error is of the order
\sqrt{\nicefrac{{\mathfrak{C}}}{{n}}}
and
\mathfrak{C}
represents the complexity of the class (
Section

7
). However, now the conditional mutual information
\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})
captures the information that the hypothesis has about the
identity
of the data used for training, instead of
the whole training set
. As before, if the hypothesis returned by the algorithm is independent of the data and
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}=\mathbb{Q}
a.s., then the conditional mutual information is 0 as expected. Since
U
is a discrete random variable, this conditional mutual information is bounded from above by
\mathrm{H}(U|\tilde{S})\leq\log n
. This change in perspective is important, as this better captures our intuition about what makes models generalize poorly. Informally, the more the hypothesis can be used to detect which instances were employed for training, when presented with equally distributed samples, the more it depends on these particular training samples and the worse it generalizes.

Since the indices
U
are a discrete random variable, the conditional mutual information can be directly interpreted as the average extra bits needed to encode the indices corresponding to the training data when the hypothesis and the super sample are known, that is
\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})=\mathrm{H}(U)-\mathrm{H}(U|W,\tilde{S})
, where we employed that
\mathrm{H}(U|\tilde{S})=\mathrm{H}(U)
since the indices are independent of the super sample. Again, this elucidates connections between privacy and generalization: if an algorithm is private, given samples from the same distribution, the information that the algorithm contains about which of the samples where used for training should be small, and therefore
\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})
should be bounded from above. This improves upon the intuition gained from
Theorem

4.1
and, as mentioned previously, will be formalized later in
Chapter

5
.

14.2
Beyond Bounded Losses and the Slow Rate

Steinke and Zakynthinou [9, Theorem 2]
proved a fast rate bound for losses with a bounded range featuring the conditional mutual information
\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})
. This bound was later improved by
Hellström and Durisi [176, Corollary 1]
to obtain better constants. The bound is given below for completeness, although the proof is outside of the scope of this monograph.

Theorem 4.9
.

Consider a loss function with a range contained in
[a,b]
. Then, let us consider the set of positive parameters
\mathcal{G}=\{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}:\gamma_{1}(1-\gamma_{2})+(e^{\gamma_{1}}-1-\gamma_{1})(1+\gamma_{2}^{2})\leq 0\}
. Then,

\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq(b-a)\inf_{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})\in\mathcal{G}}\left\{\gamma_{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]+\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})}{\gamma_{1}n}\right\}.

These bounds maintain the same interpretation as the bounds from
Section

14.1
, but providing a fast rate. In particular, in the realizable case, the bound can be slightly modified to achieve that
\gamma_{1}=\log 2
and therefore

\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})}{n\log 2},

which is again, similarly to the bounds from
Section

13.2
, reminiscent of the classical generalization guarantees from uniform convergence in the realizable setting for classification problems (see
Section

7.2
).

To be precise, the bounds from
Steinke [177]
do not necessarily require that the loss is bounded. Instead, they require that for every two instances
z
and
z^{\prime}
, the loss is Lipschitz with respect to the sample space and
|\ell(w,z)-\ell(w,z^{\prime})|\leq\rho(z,z^{\prime})
. Then, instead of the range
(b-a)
, in
Theorem

4.8
, one could have
\mathbb{E}[\rho(Z,Z^{\prime})]
for two independent instances distributed according to
\mathbb{P}_{Z}
. Another attempt at circumventing the bounded range assumption was taken by
Zhou et al. [178]
, where they engineered a conditional version of the CGF to obtain similar bounds to those in
Theorem

4.2
.

15
Single-Letter and Random-Subset Bounds

In
Section

13
, we derived bounds on the expected generalization error for an algorithm returning a hypothesis
W
after observing a training set
S
consisting of
n
samples. Essentially, these bounds exploited the Donsker and Varadhan
Lemma

2.1
to
change the measure
from the joint distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W,S}
that we wanted to study to a product distribution where the generalization error is zero. In other words, they
decoupled
the hypothesis and the training data. After that, the extra terms appearing in the change of measure can be bounded using
concentration inequalities
such as those following the Cramér–Chernoff method
[
82
, Section 2.2]
.

15.1
Single-Letter Bounds

Note that, for a fixed hypothesis
w\in\mathcal{W}
and a fixed training set
s\in\mathcal{Z}^{n}
, we may write the generalization error as

\mathrm{gen}(w,s)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big\{\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(w)\big]-\ell(w,z_{i})\Big\}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{gen}(w,z_{i}),

(33)

where
\mathrm{gen}(w,z_{i})\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[{R}(w)]-\ell(w,z_{i})
is the
single-letter generalization error
on sample
z_{i}
. Then, due to the linearity of the expectation, the expected generalization error is equal to the average of the expected single-letter generalization errors. That is, for each random instance
Z_{i}
, we may consider the expected single-letter generalization error
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{gen}(W,Z_{i})]
, where the expectation is taken with respect to the joint distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W,Z_{i}}=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{Z_{i}}
, where the Markov kernel
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}}
represents the expected kernel obtained with an instance
Z_{i}
, that is
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}}=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\circ\mathbb{P}_{S^{-i}}
, where we recall that given a dataset
s=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
, the dataset obtained by removing the
i
-th instance from
s
is defined as
s^{-1}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{i-1},z_{i+1},\ldots,z_{n})
. In this way, all the results obtain in
Section

13
for the expected generalization error with respect to the mutual information
I(W;S)
can now be replicated for the single-letter expected generalization error with respect to the single-letter mutual information
I(W;Z_{i})
.

For example, considering
Theorem

4.2
for each single-letter generalization error results in the following bound from
Bu et al. [7]
, where we directly write the results in terms of the single-letter mutual information with the understanding that equivalent results follow with a choice of an arbitrary data-independent distribution
\mathbb{Q}
on
\mathcal{W}
.

Theorem 4.10

(
Bu et al. [7, Theorem 2]
)
.

Consider a loss function with a bounded CGF (
Definition

4.1
). Then,

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{*}^{-1}\big(\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i})\big).

Similarly to
Bu et al. [7]
, we also extended our fast-rate results for bounded losses and the results for losses with a bounded moment to the single-letter setting in
[
164
]
.

Theorem 4.11
.

Consider a loss function with a range contained in
[0,1]
. Then, for every
i\in[n]
and every
c_{i}\in(0,1]
and every
\gamma_{i}>1
,

\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq\bar{\kappa}_{1}\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]+\frac{\bar{\kappa}_{2}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i})+\bar{\kappa}_{3}

where
\bar{\kappa}_{1}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\gamma_{i}\log\big(\nicefrac{{\gamma_{i}}}{{(\gamma_{i}-1)}}\big)
,
\bar{\kappa}_{2}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\gamma_{i}
, and
\bar{\kappa}_{3}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\gamma_{i}(1-c_{i}(1-\log c_{i}))
.
In particular, for interpolating algorithms (that is, when
\mathbb{E}^{S}[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)]=0
), the optimal parameters are
\gamma_{i}\to 1
and
c_{i}=\exp\big({-\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i})}\big)
and then

\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big(1-e^{-\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i})}\Big)\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i}).

Theorem 4.12
.

Consider a loss function
\ell(w,Z)
with a
p
-th moment bounded by
m_{p}
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
. Then, for every
i\in[n]
and every
c_{i}\in(0,1]
and every
\gamma_{i}>1
,

{R}\leq\bar{\kappa}_{1}\cdot\widehat{{R}}_{\leq t^{\star}}+m_{p}^{\frac{1}{p}}\Big(\frac{p}{p-1}\Big)\Big(\bar{\kappa}_{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i})+\bar{\kappa}_{3}\Big)^{\frac{p-1}{p}},

where

t^{\star}\coloneqq m_{p}^{\frac{1}{p}}\Big(\bar{\kappa}_{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i})+\bar{\kappa}_{3}\Big)^{-\frac{1}{p}}

and where
\bar{\kappa}_{1}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\gamma_{i}\log\big(\nicefrac{{\gamma_{i}}}{{(\gamma_{i}-1)}}\big)
,
\bar{\kappa}_{2}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\gamma_{i}
, and
\bar{\kappa}_{3}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\gamma_{i}(1-c_{i}(1-\log c_{i}))
.

Theorem 4.13
.

Consider a loss function
\ell(w,Z)
with a variance bounded by
\sigma^{2}
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
. Then, for every
i\in[n]
and every
c_{i}\in(0,1]
and every
\gamma_{i}>1

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq

\displaystyle\left[1-2\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i})+\bar{\kappa}_{3}}\right]_{+}^{-1}\cdot

\displaystyle\left[\bar{\kappa}_{1}\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]+2\sigma\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}_{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i})+\bar{\kappa}_{3}}\right],

where
\bar{\kappa}_{1}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\gamma_{i}\log\big(\nicefrac{{\gamma_{i}}}{{(\gamma_{i}-1)}}\big)
,
\bar{\kappa}_{2}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\gamma_{i}
, and
\bar{\kappa}_{3}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\gamma_{i}(1-c_{i}(1-\log c_{i}))
.

The motivation behind this kind of result is that the single-letter mutual information
I(W;Z_{i})
often does not go to infinity even when the mutual information
\mathrm{I}(W;S)
does. Consider the following example.

Example 4.1

(Gaussian location model problem)
.

In this problem, we observe
n
instances of a
d
-dimensional Gaussian, that is
Z_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}I_{d})
. Then, the algorithm returns the average of these instances
W=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}
with the objective of minimizing the distance
\|w-z\rVert_{2}
between the returned hypothesis and the instances.
In this setting, we established that
I(W;S)\to\infty
. On the other hand, the single-letter mutual information has a bounded closed form expression that decreases with the number of samples, namely
I(W;Z_{i})=\frac{d}{2}\log\frac{n}{n-1}\in\Theta(\nicefrac{{d}}{{n}})
.

In the particular setting of
Example

4.1
, if the considered loss is the squared distance
\ell(w,z)=\lVert w-z\rVert_{2}^{2}
, then the CGF is bounded and
\psi_{*}^{-1}
can be calculated leading to the expected generalization bound
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\sigma^{2}d\sqrt{\nicefrac{{8}}{{n-1}}}

[
7
, Section IV.A]
. Although the bound is sub-optimal, since the expected generalization error is in
\mathcal{O}(\nicefrac{{\sigma^{2}d}}{{n}})
, it has the same rate as the bounds obtained from uniform stability
[
179
]
and algorithmic stability
[
110
]
and showcases an improvement with respect to bounds using the mutual information
I(W;S)
.

Moreover, these results also expand the intuition obtained from
Section

13
. Although these results do not have the classical shapes from
Section

7
with a complexity term divided by the number of samples, they showcase that the generalization error degrades as the returned hypothesis keeps more information about
each
of the training samples. In contrast, the results from
Section

13
told us that the generalization error degrades as the returned hypothesis has more information about the
whole
training set. This seemingly innocuous difference is important, as the mutual information between the hypothesis and the training set also contemplates artificial, spurious dependencies between the instances generated by the hypothesis. Mathematically, this can be understood through the
chain rule
of the mutual information from
Proposition

2.4
, namely

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}(W;S)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i}|Z^{i-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\mathrm{I}(W,Z^{i-1};Z_{i})-I(Z_{i};Z^{i-1})\big\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i})+\mathrm{I}(Z_{i};Z^{i-1}|W)\big\}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i}),

(34)

where the artificial dependence is captured by the conditional mutual information
\mathrm{I}(Z_{i};Z^{i-1}|W)
and where
\mathrm{I}(Z_{i};Z^{i-1})=0
as the samples are independent of each other. For example, consider the Gaussian location model from above and only two instances
Z_{0}
and
Z_{1}
. These two instances are independent and therefore
\mathrm{I}(Z_{0};Z_{1})=0
. However, once the average of the two is known, they become dependent and
\mathrm{I}\big(Z_{0};Z_{1}\big|\nicefrac{{(Z_{0}+Z_{1})}}{{2}}\big)>0
.

This extension is not unique to the bounds from
Section

13
, and it can also be applied considering the randomized-subsample setting from
Section

14
. Similarly to before, for a fixed hypothesis
w\in\mathcal{W}
, a fixed supersample
\tilde{s}\in\mathcal{Z}^{n}
, and a fixed sequence of indices
u\in\{0,1\}^{n}
we may write the empirical generalization error as

\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(w,\tilde{s},u)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big\{\ell(w,\tilde{z}_{i,1-u_{i}}-\ell(w,\tilde{z}_{i,u_{i}})\Big\}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(w,\tilde{z}_{i,0},\tilde{z}_{i,1},u_{i}),

(35)

where
\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(w,\tilde{z}_{i,0},\tilde{z}_{i,1},,u_{i})\coloneqq\ell(w,\tilde{z}_{i,1-u_{i}})-\ell(w,\tilde{z}_{i,u_{i}})
is the
single-letter empirical generalization error
on samples
\tilde{z}_{i,0}
and
\tilde{z}_{i,1}
. Again, due to the linearity of the average of the expectation the expected single-letter empirical generalization errors is equal to the expected generalization error. Then, for each random index
U_{i}
, we may consider the expected single-letter empirical generalization error
\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(W,\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1},U_{i})]
where the expectation is taken with respect to the joint distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W,\tilde{Z}_{i},U_{i}}
. Hence, all the results obtained in
Section

14
for the expected generalization error with respect to the conditional mutual information
\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})
can be replicated for the single-letter empirical generalization error with respect to the single-letter conditional mutual information
\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})
. This is what we did in
[
157
]
to obtain the following result, which was re-discovered later by
Zhou et al. [178]
.

Theorem 4.14
.

Consider a loss function with a range contained in
[a,b]
. Then,

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\frac{b-a}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{2\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})}.

Theorem

4.14
also expands the intuition obtained from
Section

14
. This single-letter bound tells us that the generalization error degrades the more we can identify
a single
training sample when presented with another sample from the same distribution on average. This is in contrast to the bounds in
Section

14
, where the bounds state that the generalization error degrades the more we can identify the training samples when presented with an additional training set sampled from the same distribution. Similarly to what we discussed above, the conditional mutual information of the hypothesis and the indices given the super sample considers artificial, spurious dependencies between the instances and the indices generated by the hypothesis. Mathematically, this can also be understood using the
chain rule
of the mutual information from
Proposition

2.4
, namely

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{S},U^{i-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\mathrm{I}(W,\tilde{S}^{-i},U^{i-1};U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})-\mathrm{I}(\tilde{S}^{-i},U^{i-1};U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})\big\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})+\mathrm{I}(\tilde{S}^{-i},U^{i-1};U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1},W)\big\}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1}),

(36)

where
\tilde{S}=S\setminus\{\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1}\}
, where the artificial dependence is captured by the conditional mutual information
\mathrm{I}(\tilde{S}^{-i},U^{i-1};U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1},W)
, and where
\mathrm{I}(\tilde{S}^{-i},U^{i-1};U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})=0
as the instances and the indices are all independent of each other. As before, consider the Gaussian location model as an example with only two instances. In the randomized-subsample setting, we consider a super sample consisting of four instances
\tilde{Z}_{0,0},\tilde{Z}_{0,1},\tilde{Z}_{1,0}
, and
\tilde{Z}_{1,1}
and two indices
U_{0}
and
U_{1}
that select the training instances as
Z_{0}=\tilde{Z}_{0,U_{0}}
and
Z_{1}=\tilde{Z}_{1,U_{1}}
. The samples and the indices are independent and hence
\mathrm{I}(\tilde{Z}_{0,0},\tilde{Z}_{0,1},U_{0};U_{1}|\tilde{Z}_{1,0},\tilde{Z}_{1,1})=0
. However, once the average between the two training instances is known, they become dependent and

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\Big(\tilde{Z}_{0,0},\tilde{Z}_{0,1},

\displaystyle U_{0};U_{1}\Big|\tilde{Z}_{1,0},\tilde{Z}_{1,1},\frac{\tilde{Z}_{0,U_{0}}-\tilde{Z}_{1,U_{1}}}{2}\Big)\geq

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}\Big(\tilde{Z}_{0,U_{0}};U_{1}\Big|\tilde{Z}_{1,0},\tilde{Z}_{1,1},\frac{\tilde{Z}_{0,U_{0}}-\tilde{Z}_{1,U_{1}}}{2}\Big)\geq 0,

where the first inequality comes from the
permutation invariance
and the more data more information properties of the mutual information from
Proposition

2.4
.

15.2
Random-Subset Bounds
