Often, it is useful to consider conditional versions of the mutual information where some samples are known in order to derive comprehensible results for known algorithms. An example of such a situation is given later in
Section

17
. To obtain this kind of bounds one may want to note that, for a fixed hypothesis
w\in\mathcal{W}
and a fixed training set
s\in\mathcal{Z}^{n}
, the generalization error can be written as

\mathrm{gen}(w,s)={R}(w)-\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(w,s_{J})\big],

where
J
is a uniformly distributed random subset of
[n]
such that
|J|=m
and
s_{J}=\{z_{i}\}_{i\in J}
. To note this, we need to realize that since
J
is uniformly distributed, there are
\binom{n}{m}
possible subsets of size
m
in
[n]
, and that each sample
z_{i}\in s
belongs to
\binom{n-1}{m-1}
of those subsets. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(w,s_{J})\big]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\binom{n}{m}}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i\in j}\ell(w,z_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{\binom{n-1}{m-1}}{\binom{n}{m}}\cdot\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(w,z_{i})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(w,z_{i})=\widehat{{R}}(w,s).

Remark 4.1
.

Similarly, it is often useful to consider that a source of randomness in the algorithm is known. This can be modeled assuming that the hypothesis depends on a random variable
R
that is known to us and that is independent of all other random variables in the system. Incorporating this known variable into the bounds on the expected generalization error is simple as we can always write
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}^{R}[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)]]
and, since
R
only depends on the hypothesis
W
, then we may proceed to bound
\mathbb{E}^{R}[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)]
exactly as before in
Sections

13
and
14
substituting the mutual information
\mathrm{I}
by the disintegrated mutual information
\mathrm{I}^{R}
and taking the expectation with respect to
R
afterward. However, as in this monograph we will only use the extra random variable
R
for random-subset bounds, we chose not to include it previously to simplify the exposition.

With this in mind, consider a fixed realization
r
of the random variable
R
, a fixed subset
j\subseteq[n]
, and a fixed set of samples
s_{j^{c}}
. Then, the expected generalization error
\mathbb{E}^{R=r,J=j,S_{j^{c}}=s_{j^{c}}}[\mathrm{gen}(W,S_{j})]
can be bounded from above using the techniques described in
Sections

15.1
and
13
. To see that, if the loss has a CGF bounded by
\psi
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{R=r,J=j,S_{j^{c}}=s_{j^{c}}}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S_{j})\big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i\in j}\psi_{*}^{-1}\big(\mathrm{I}^{R=r,J=j,S_{j^{c}}=s_{j^{c}}}(W;Z_{i})\big)\textnormal{ and }

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{R=r,J=j,S_{j^{c}}=s_{j^{c}}}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S_{j})\big]

\displaystyle\leq\psi_{*}^{-1}\Big(\frac{\mathrm{I}^{R=r,J=j,S_{j^{c}}=s_{j^{c}}}(W;S_{j})}{m}\Big).

Finally, taking the expectation to both sides leads to the following result, which is a generalization of our result
[
157
, Proposition 2]
extending
[
8
, Theorem 2.4]
.

Theorem 4.15

(Generalization of the our extension of
[
8
, Theorem 2.4]
)
.

Consider a loss function with a bounded CGF (
Definition

4.1
). Also consider a random subset
J\subseteq[n]
such that
|J|=m
, which is uniformly distributed and independent of
W
and
S
, and a random variable
R
that is independent of
S
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{m}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\in J}\psi_{*}^{-1}\big(\mathrm{I}^{R,J,S_{J^{c}}}(W;Z_{i})\big)\right]\textnormal{ and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\bigg[\psi_{*}^{-1}\Big(\frac{\mathrm{I}^{R,J,S_{j^{c}}}(W;S_{j})}{m}\Big)\bigg].

Remark 4.2
.

As we did in
Section

15.1
in
Theorems

4.11
and
4.12
, the random-subset result from
Theorem

4.15
can be replicated to obtain analogue results for losses with a bounded range and with bounded moments. As these results are neither the tighter (as we will see later in
Section

15.3
) nor are used later in the monograph, they are not explicitly written.

As previously, we can consider a similar situation in the randomized-subsample setting. In this case, consider again a fixed hypothesis
w\in\mathcal{W}
, a fixed super sample
\tilde{s}\in\mathcal{Z}^{n\times 2}
, and a fixed sequence of indices
u\in\{0,1\}^{n}
. Then, the empirical generalization error can be written as

\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(w,\tilde{s},u)=\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(w,s_{\textnormal{ghost},J})\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(w,s_{J})\big]

where again
J
is a uniformly distributed random subset of
[n]
such that
|J|=m
. Using the same argument as before we observe that
\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(w,s_{\textnormal{ghost},J})\big]=\widehat{{R}}(w,s_{\textnormal{ghost}})
and
\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(w,s_{J})\big]=\widehat{{R}}(w,s)
. Therefore, consider a fixed realization
r
of a random variable
R
that is only dependent to the hypothesis
W
and independent of all other variables of the system, a fixed subset
j\subseteq[n]
, a fixed super set
\tilde{s}
, and a fixed sequence of indices
u_{j^{c}}
. Then, the expected empirical generalization error
\mathbb{E}^{R=r,J=j,\tilde{S}=\tilde{s},U_{j^{c}}=u_{j^{c}}}[\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(W,\tilde{s}_{j},U_{j})]
can be bounded from above using the techniques described in
Sections

14
and
15.1
. To see that, if the loss has a range bounded in
[a,b]
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{R=r,J=j,\tilde{S}=\tilde{s},U_{j^{c}}=u_{j^{c}}}\big[

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(W,\tilde{s}_{j},U_{j})\big]\leq

\displaystyle\frac{b-a}{m}\sum_{i\in j}\sqrt{2\mathrm{I}^{R=r,J=j,\tilde{S}=\tilde{s},U_{j^{c}}=u_{j^{c}}}(W;U_{i})}

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{R=r,J=j,\tilde{S}=\tilde{s},U_{j^{c}}=u_{j^{c}}}\big[

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(W,\tilde{s}_{j},U_{j})\big]\leq

\displaystyle(b-a)\sqrt{\frac{2\mathrm{I}^{R=r,J=j,\tilde{S}=\tilde{s},U_{j^{c}}=u_{j^{c}}}(W;U_{j})}{m}}

Finally, taking the expectation to both sides leads to our
[
157
, Proposition 4]
, although a similar result could be directly obtained combining a slight modification of
[
156
, Theorem 3.1]
(where
R
is included) and
[
156
, Lemma 3.6]
.

Theorem 4.16
.

Consider a loss function with a range contained in
[a,b]
. Also consider a random subset
J\subseteq[n]
such that
|J|=m
, which is uniformly distributed and independent of
W
,
\tilde{S}
, and
U
, and a random variable
R
that is indepdendent of
\tilde{S}
, and
U
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{b-a}{m}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\in J}\sqrt{2\mathrm{I}^{R,J,\tilde{S},U_{j^{c}}}(W;U_{i})}\right]\textnormal{ and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]

\displaystyle\leq(b-a)\mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{\frac{2\mathrm{I}^{R,J,\tilde{S},U_{j^{c}}}(W;U_{j})}{m}}\right].

As mentioned at the start of this subsection, the appeal of the random-subset bounds is that they present
disintegrated, conditional
versions of the mutual information, where some elements such as large subsets of the data (or the indices in the randomized-subsample setting) and other sources of the randomness of the algorithm are known.
In
[
157
]
, we realize that the approach presented so far is limited, in the sense that there are no more random objects that can be conditioned in the relative entropy. That is,
Theorems

4.15
and
4.16
contain the maximum conditioning possible for the approach of finding random variables whose expectation is equal to the expected generalization error, and then bounding these random variables using the results from
Sections

13
and
14
.
However, for bounded losses, it is possible to derive tighter bounds where even more of the random elements are conditioned. One way to do so is with a dedicated analysis of the Donsker and Varadhan
Lemma

2.1
as shown by
Negrea et al. [8, Theorem 2.5]
and
Haghifam et al. [156, Theorem 3.7]
. A simpler way to achieve similar bounds, without sacrificing the understanding of the generalization error random variable and the distinct random variables with equal expectation, is to first bound the generalization error with the Kantorovich–Rubenstein duality from
Lemma

2.6
, realize that the Wasserstein distance is dominated by the total variation, and finally bound the total variation with Pinsker’s and Bretagnolle–Huber’s inequalities from
Lemmata

2.2
and
2.3
. An extra benefit from this approach is that the obtained bound ensures that heavily influential samples (from which the hypothesis has a high amount of information) do not contribute too negatively to the bound, which is ensured to be non-vacuous. The derivation of similar bounds and the discussion of the method will appear shortly in
Section

16
. For now, we only introduce the bounds.

Theorem 4.17

(
Negrea et al. [8, Theorem 2.5]
)
.

Consider a loss function with a range contained in
[a,b]
. Also consider a random index
J\in[n]
, which is uniformly distributed and independent of
W
and
S
, and a random variable
R
that is independent of
S
. Then, for every Markov kernel
\mathbb{Q}
from
\mathcal{Z}^{n-1}\otimes\mathcal{R}\otimes J
to distributions on
\mathcal{W}

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\frac{b-a}{\sqrt{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S,R}\|\mathbb{Q}(S^{-J},R,J)\big)}\right].

Theorem 4.18

(Extension of
[
156
, Theorem 3.7]
)
.

Consider a loss function with a range contained in
[a,b]
. Also consider a random index
J\in[n]
, which is uniformly distributed and independent of
W
,
\tilde{S}
, and
U
, and a random variable
R
that is independent of
\tilde{S}
, and
U
. Then, for every Markov kernel
\mathbb{Q}
from
\mathcal{Z}^{n\times 2}\otimes\{0,1\}^{n-1}\otimes\mathcal{R}\otimes\mathcal{J}
to distributions on
\mathcal{W}

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\sqrt{2}(b-a)\mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U,R}\|\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}},R)\big)}\right].

Notably, the two equations in
Theorems

4.17
and
4.18
are only given for
m=1
. This case, when
m=1
, will be of interest in
Section

17
as it is the setting in which the two distributions in the relative entropy terms of
Theorems

4.17
and
4.18
are the closest to each other. In fact,
Negrea et al. [8]
saw that the case
m=1
is optimal.

15.3
Comparison of the Bounds

In
Section

15.1
, we already noted that the single-letter mutual and conditional mutual information were tighter than the full mutual and conditional mutual information counterparts as they did not account for artificial dependencies between the instances or between the instances and the indices given the mutual information, c.f. (
34
) and (
36
). This also implies that single-letter bounds (e.g.
Theorem

4.10
) are tighter than the full mutual information counterparts (e.g.
Theorem

4.2
). This is clear in the fast-rate bounds for bounded losses, but it is also true for more general losses as one can first employ Jensen’s inequality to pull the average of single-letter mutual information terms inside of the concave functions
\psi_{*}^{-1}
,
\sqrt{\cdot}
,
(\cdot)^{\frac{p-1}{p}}
for
p>1
. Only for the more complicated bounds for losses with a bounded variance the two bounds are not comparable.

Therefore, it is of interest to compare the different mutual information terms to get a mental map of the relationships between the different bounds. That was the purpose of our
[
157
, Appendix J]
and
[
165
, Appendix D]
. Since the random-subset bounds are incomparable when all the expectations are outside of the concave functions, we will consider weaker versions where the expectation is inside the concave function after applying Jensen’s inequality. Furthermore, as the most relevant case is the one where
m=1
, this will be the one studied. Finally, we will drop the dependence on
R
for the comparison, although the results also hold when it is present as per
Remark

4.1
. Hence, for the random-subset bounds the quantities to study are
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{J}|S^{-J})]
and
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{I}(W;U_{J}|\tilde{S},U^{-J})]
. A summary of these relationships is given in
Figure

4.6
.

Theorem

4.10
[
7
]
\frac{(b-a)}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i})}{2}}
\leq
Theorem

4.15
[
8
]
(after Jensen’s)
(b-a)\sqrt{\frac{\mathbb{E}[I(W;S_{J}|S^{-J})]}{2}}
\leq
Theorem

4.1
[
5
]
(b-a)\sqrt{\frac{I(W;S)}{2n}}
Theorem

4.10
\frac{(b-a)}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{2I(W;U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})}
\leq
\big(\textnormal{if }3\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})\leq\mathrm{I}(W;\tilde{S})\big)
\leq
Theorem

4.16
[
156
]
(after Jensen’s)
(b-a)\sqrt{2\mathbb{E}[I(W;U_{J}|\tilde{S},U^{-J})]}
\leq
\big(\textnormal{if }3\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})\leq\mathrm{I}(W;\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})\big)
\leq
Theorem

4.8
[
9
]
(b-a)\sqrt{\frac{2\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})}{n}}
\leq
\big(\textnormal{if }3\mathrm{I}(W;U_{j}|\tilde{S},U^{-i})\leq I(W;\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1}|S^{-i})\big)

Figure 4.6
:
Summary of the comparison between the full, the single-letter, and the random-subset bounds (weakened using Jensen’s inequality) of both the standard and the randomized-subsample setting for losses with a bounded range.

15.3.1
Comparison in the Standard Setting

In the standard setting, the ordering of the bounds follows the proposition below.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
J
be a uniformly distributed random index of
[n]
that is independent of
W
,
\tilde{S}
, and
U
. Then,

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i})\leq\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{J}|S^{-J})\big].

The first inequality of this proposition was proven in (
34
) and previously in
[
7
, Proposition 2]
. For the second inequality, note that
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{J}|S^{-J})\big]=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i}|S^{-i})
. Then, the proof proceeds similarly to (
34
). More precisely,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i}|S^{-i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\mathrm{I}(W,S^{-i};Z_{i})-\mathrm{I}(Z_{i};S^{-i})\big\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\mathrm{I}(Z^{i-1};Z_{i})+\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i}|Z^{i-1})+\mathrm{I}(Z_{i+1}^{n};Z_{i}|W,Z^{i-1})\big\}

\displaystyle\geq\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i}|Z^{i-1}),

where the first and the second inequalities follow from the
chain rule
of the mutual information and the independence of the instances.
Therefore, the random-subset bounds are capturing more dependencies than the full mutual information bounds and therefore more than the single-letter bounds.

15.3.2
Comparison in the randomized-subsample Setting

In the randomized-subsample setting, the bounds can be similarly ordered providing us with similar insights.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
J
be a uniformly distributed random index of
[n]
that is independent of
W
and
S
. Then,

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{S})\leq\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})}{n}\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{I}(W;U_{J}|\tilde{S},U^{-J})\big].

The relationship between the first and the third element was proven previously in (
34
). The second element did not appear previously in the monograph as it will not be employed and it is looser than the single-letter bound shown here. Nonetheless, this was the state-of-the art bound prior to the development of our single-letter bound in
[
157
]
. For completeness, we provide here a proof of each of these inequalities in a similar fashion to before, by repeatedly using the
chain rule
of the mutual information and the fact that the instances and indices are mutually independent of each other. Namely,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{S},U^{-i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\mathrm{I}(W,U^{-i};U_{i}|\tilde{S})-\mathrm{I}(U^{-i};U_{i}|\tilde{S})\big\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\mathrm{I}(U^{i-1};U_{i}|\tilde{S})+\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{S},U^{i-1})+\mathrm{I}(U_{i+1}^{n};U_{i}|\tilde{S},U^{i-1},W)\big\}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{S},U^{i-1})\big\}=I(W;U|\tilde{S})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\mathrm{I}(W,U^{i-1};U_{i}|\tilde{S})-\mathrm{I}(U^{i-1};U_{i}|\tilde{S})\big\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{S})+\mathrm{I}(U^{i-1};U_{i}|\tilde{S})\big\}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{S})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\mathrm{I}(W,\tilde{S};U_{i})-\mathrm{I}(\tilde{S};U_{i})\big\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\mathrm{I}(W,\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1};U_{i})+\mathrm{I}(\tilde{S}^{-i};U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})\big\}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big\{\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})+\mathrm{I}(\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1};U_{i})\big\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})

15.3.3
Comparison between the settings

It is easy to see that
\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})\leq\mathrm{I}(W;S)

[
156
]
. To see that, note that the Markov chain
(\tilde{S},U)-S-W
holds. Then, by the data processing inequality from
Proposition

2.4
it follows that
\mathrm{I}(W;S)\geq\mathrm{I}(W;\tilde{S},U)
. Finally, by the
chain rule
of the mutual information and its non-negativity it follows that

\mathrm{I}(W;\tilde{S},U)=\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})+\mathrm{I}(W;\tilde{S})\geq\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S}).

An analogous reasoning reveals similar insights for the single-letter and random-subset terms. Nonetheless, the additional factor of two in the bounds of the randomized-subsample setting makes the comparison between the different settings harder.

Hellström and Durisi [12]
note that since
S
is a deterministic function of
\tilde{S}
and
U
, then
\mathrm{I}(W;S)=\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})+\mathrm{I}(W;\tilde{S})
holds with equality. Then, for bounded losses, the bound from the randomized-subsample setting from
Theorem

4.8
is tighter than the one from the standard setting from
Theorem

4.1
if
3\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})\leq\mathrm{I}(W;\tilde{S})
.

Similarly, we can show that

\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i})=\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})+\mathrm{I}(W;\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1}).

To see that, note that
\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i})=0
as the index
U_{i}
is independent of the hypothesis; that
\mathrm{I}(W;\tilde{Z}_{i,1-U_{i}}|U_{i})=0
as, given the index
U_{i}
, the hypothesis is independent of the sample not used for training
\tilde{Z}_{i,1-U_{i}}
; and that
\mathrm{I}(W;\tilde{Z}_{i,U_{i}}|U_{i},\tilde{Z}_{i,1-U_{i}})=\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i})
as, given the index
U_{i}
, the hypothesis is independent of the sample not used for training
\tilde{Z}_{i,1-U_{i}}
and the only dependence captured is the one of the hypothesis and the sample used for training
Z_{i}=\tilde{Z}_{i,U_{i}}
. With that in mind, using the
chain rule
of the mutual information we have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i})+\mathrm{I}(W;\tilde{Z}_{i,1-U_{i}}|U_{i})+\mathrm{I}(W;\tilde{Z}_{i,U_{i}}|U_{i},\tilde{Z}_{i,1-U_{i}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i},\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1}).

A further application of the
chain rule
completes the proof. Therefore,
Theorem

4.14
is tighter than the particularization of
Theorem

4.10
to bounded losses if
3\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})\leq\mathrm{I}(W;\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1}))
.

For the random-subset bounds, we can show that

\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i}|S^{-i})=\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{S},U^{-i})+\mathrm{I}(W;\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1}|S^{-i}).

Using the same argument as above, we can establish that
\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i}|S^{-i})=\mathrm{I}(W;\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1},U_{i}|S^{-i})
since the conditioning on
S^{-i}
does not change the fact that, given the index
U_{i}
, the hypothesis depends on the sample used for training
Z_{i}=\tilde{Z}_{i,U_{i}}
and is independent of the one that is not used for training
\tilde{Z}_{i,1-U_{i}}
. Then, using the
chain rule
of the mutual information we may establish that

\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i}|S^{-i})=\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1},S^{-i})+\mathrm{I}(W;\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1}|S^{-i}).

All that is left to prove is that
\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1},S^{-i})=\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{S},U^{-i})
. This can be seen by noting that, given the samples
\tilde{Z}_{i,0}
,
\tilde{Z}_{i,1}
, and the rest of the training set
\tilde{S}^{-i}
, then the hypothesis
W
and the index
U_{i}
are independent of the rest of the ghost samples
S_{\textnormal{ghost}}^{-i}
and indices
U^{-i}
. Hence,
\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1},S^{-i})=\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1},S^{-i},S_{\textnormal{ghost}}^{-i},U^{-i})
, which completes the equality. Therefore,
Theorem

4.16
is tighter than the particularization of
Theorem

4.15
to bounded losses if
3\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{S},U^{-i})\leq\mathrm{I}(W;\tilde{S}_{i}|S^{-i})
for all indices
i\in[n]
.

16
Bounds Using the Wasserstein Distance

From
Sections

13
,
14
, and
15
, we described how to obtain different bounds on the generalization error that depend on the relative entropy between different distributions. At their core, all these bounds come from a clever application of the Donsker and Varadhan
Lemma

2.1
to decouple the hypothesis
W
and the training data
S
as shown in (
22
).

More generally, this lemma can be employed to measure the difference between expectations. In (
4
), consider the measurable space
\mathcal{X}
and the random variables
X
and
Y
distributed according to
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
respectively. Then, fix the function
g(x)=\lambda\big(f(x)-\mathbb{E}[f(Y)]\big)
for some auxiliary function
f
and some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. In this way, the equivalent of (
22
) is summarized in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a measurable space and
X
and
Y
be two random variables distributed according to
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
respectively. Consider a measurable function
f
on
\mathcal{X}
such that the CGF
\Lambda_{g(Y)}
exists, where
g(x)=\lambda\big(f(x)-\mathbb{E}[f(Y)]\big)
. Then, for all
\lambda>0

\mathbb{E}\big[f(X)\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[f(Y)\big]\leq\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})+\Lambda_{g(Y)}(\lambda)}{\lambda}.

(37)

For example,
Equation

32
, from which most of the theorems presented so far stemmed from can be derived from (
37
) by letting
X=(W,S)
,
Y=(W^{\prime},S)
, and
f(w,s)=\mathrm{gen}(w,s)
, and by noting that
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{gen}(W^{\prime},S)]=0
.

If instead of considering
Lemma

4.4
, we consider the Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality (
Lemma

2.6
), we may obtain a similar result with respect to the Wasserstein distance.
Namely, consider some fixed dataset
s
. Then, let
X=W
,
Y=W^{\prime}
, and
f(w;s)=\widehat{{R}}(w,s)
in (
11
), where
W
and
W^{\prime}
are distributed according to the algorithm’s distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}
and the prototypical marginal distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}
respectively. Then, if the loss
\ell(\cdot,z)
is
L
-Lipschitz under some metric
\rho
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, so is
\widehat{{R}}(w,s)
for all
s\in\mathcal{Z}^{n}
. Indeed, note that

\left|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(w,z_{i})-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(w^{\prime},z_{i})\right|\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|\ell(w,z_{i})-\ell(w,z_{i})\right|\leq L\rho(w,w^{\prime}),

where the first inequality comes from the triangle inequality and the second one from the Lipschitzness of
\ell
. Therefore,

\mathbb{E}^{S=s}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W^{\prime},s)\big]-\mathbb{E}^{S=s}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,s)\big]\leq L\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s},\mathbb{P}_{W}).

Finally, since
\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W^{\prime},S)\big]={R}(W^{\prime})
as discussed in
Section

13
, taking the expectation with respect to the training data recovers
[
180
, Theorem 2]
. This effectively bounds the expected generalization error from above by the expected Wasserstein distance between the algorithm’s output distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
and the hypothesis marginal distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}
.

We can extend this reasoning to include any data-independent distribution
\mathbb{Q}
. To do so, fix a dataset
s
and let
X=W
and
Y=W^{\prime}
be defined as before in (
11
). However, now let
f(w,s)=\mathrm{gen}(w,s)
. If the loss
\ell(\cdot,z)
is
L
-Lipschitz under some metric
\rho
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, then so is
{R}(\cdot)
. Namely,

\left|{R}(w)-{R}(w^{\prime})\right|\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\ell(w,Z)-\ell(w^{\prime},Z)\right|\right]\leq L\rho(w,w^{\prime}),

where the first inequality comes from Jensen’s inequality and the second one from the Lipschitzness of
\ell
. Therefore, by the triangle inequality
\mathrm{gen}(\cdot,s)
is
2L
-Lipschitz for all
s\in\mathcal{Z}^{n}
and

\mathbb{E}^{S=s}[\mathrm{gen}(W,s)]-\mathbb{E}^{S=s}[\mathrm{gen}(W^{\prime},s)]\leq 2L\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s},\mathbb{Q}).

Finally, taking the expectation with respect to the training data and noting again that
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{gen}(W^{\prime},S)]=0
extends upon
[
180
, Theorem 2]
by allowing any data-independent distribution
\mathbb{Q}
. This flexibility will prove useful, for example, in
Section

17
. These two results are summarized below.

Theorem 4.19

(Extension of
[
180
, Theorem 2]
)
.

Consider an
L
-Lipschitz loss function
\ell(\cdot,z)
with respect to some metric
\rho
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
. Then, for every data-independent distribution
\mathbb{Q}
on
\mathcal{W}

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq L\mathbb{E}\big[\min\left\{\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{P}_{W}),2\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q})\right\}\big].

The main appeal of bounds with the form of
Theorem

4.19
is that they consider the geometry of the space by construction. This is reminiscent of classical approaches considering the complexity of the hypothesis class such as the Rademacher complexity from
Section

8
, with the added benefit that they also capture the dependence of the algorithm and the training data. Note that
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{P}_{W})\big]
still measures the difference between the distribution of the hypothesis returned by the algorithm after observing a training set, and the prototypical distribution on samples from the data distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\circ\mathbb{P}_{S}
as discussed in
Sections

10.3
,
11
, and
13
. The consideration of the space geometry is lost in the bounds featuring the relative entropy unless the distributions are absolutely continuous to each other. For example, consider
Figure

4.7
, where different algorithm distributions
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
are shown for different datasets along with the data-independent distribution
\mathbb{Q}
. Since
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}
is not absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathbb{Q}
for some training sets
s
, the relative entropy
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})
will go to infinity, even though the two distributions are clearly not far from each other. On the other hand, the Wasserstein distance with some standard metric like the
\ell_{2}
norm
\rho(x,y)=\rVert x-y\rVert
captures this similarity.

Figure 4.7
:

Example of four hypothesis distributions
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s_{1}}
,
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s_{2}}
,
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s_{3}}
, and
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s_{4}}
after observing four different training sets, and a data-independent distribution
\mathbb{Q}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. The darker areas indicate regions with higher probability density and the white background indicates the region of probability zero. Even if the distributions characterizing the algorithm are not absolutely continuous with respect to the data-independent distribution
\mathbb{Q}
, they are still close in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

We recall from
Section

15.1
that
\mathrm{gen}(w,s)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{gen}(w,z_{i})
and that therefore
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)]=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{gen}(W,Z_{i})]
by the linearity of the expectation. Therefore, one may consider
Theorem

4.19
for each single-letter generalization error to obtain the following theorem, which generalizes the independently developed
[
181
, Corollary 3]
to algorithms that may consider the ordering of the samples, and extends our
[
165
, Theorem 1]
considering arbitrary data-independent distributions
\mathbb{Q}
instead of requiring it to be the prototypical marginal distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}
.

Theorem 4.20
.

Consider an
L
-Lipschitz loss function
\ell(\cdot,z)
with respect to some metric
\rho
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
. Then, for every data-independent distribution
\mathbb{Q}
on
\mathcal{W}

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\frac{L}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\min\left\{\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}},\mathbb{P}_{W}),2\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}},\mathbb{Q})\right\}\right].

Similarly, we recall from
Section

15.2
that
\mathrm{gen}(w,s)={R}(w)-\mathbb{E}[\widehat{{R}}(w,s_{J})]
, where
J
is a uniformly distributed random subset of
[n]
such that
|J|=m
and
s_{J}=\{z_{i}\}_{i\in J}
. Therefore, one may fix a realization
r
of the random variable
R
, a fixed subset
j\subseteq[n]
, and a fixed set of samples
s_{j^{c}}
and employ
Theorem

4.19
and
Theorem

4.20
to the expected generalization error
\mathbb{E}^{R=r,J=j,S_{j^{c}}=s_{j^{c}}}[\mathrm{gen}(W,S_{j})]
. Finally, taking the expectation with respect to
R
,
J
, and
S_{J^{c}}
extends our
[
165
, Theorem 2]
.

Theorem 4.21
.

Consider an
L
-Lipschitz loss function
\ell(\cdot,z)
with respect to some metric
\rho
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
. Also consider a random subset
J\subseteq[n]
such that
|J|=m
, which is uniformly distributed and independent of
W
and
S
, and a random variable
R
that is independent of
S
. Then, for every Markov kernel
\mathbb{Q}
from
\mathcal{Z}^{n-m}\otimes\mathcal{R}
to distributions on
\mathcal{W}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]

\displaystyle\leq L\mathbb{E}\left[\min\left\{\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S,R},\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{J^{c}},R}),2\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S,R},\mathbb{Q}(S_{J^{c}},R))\right\}\right]\textnormal{ and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{L}{m}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\in J}\min\left\{\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{J^{c}},Z_{i},R},\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{J^{c}},R}),2\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{J^{c}},Z_{i},R},\mathbb{Q}(S_{J^{c}},R))\right\}\right].

In particular, when
m=1
, both equations in
Theorem

4.21
coincide and

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq L\mathbb{E}\left[\min\left\{\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S,R},\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S^{-J},R}),2\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S,R},\mathbb{Q}(S^{-J},R))\right\}\right].

After
Theorem

4.19
, we noted how the Wasserstein-based bounds resolved the relative entropy-based bounds’ problem of non-absolutely continuous algorithm distributions
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}
with respect to the data independent distribution
\mathbb{Q}
. Shortly, in
Section

16.2
, we will show that even when the geometry of the space is ignored and the discrete metric is considered, the Wasserstein distance-based bounds improve upon the relative entropy-based bounds when the loss is bounded. Now, we will present a simple scenario where some Wasserstein-based bounds achieve the desired generalization error rate, while previous bounds based on the relative entropy fail to do so.

Recall the Gaussian location model from
Example

4.1
and let the loss function be the Euclidean distance
\ell(w,z)=\lVert w-z\rVert_{2}
. In this example, the expected generalization error can be calculated exactly (see
Appendix

4.A
):

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]=\sqrt{\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{n}}\Big(\sqrt{n+1}-\sqrt{n-1}\Big)\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{d+1}{2}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{d}{2})}\in\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}d}}{n}\bigg).

As discussed in
[
7
]
, the bound from
[
5
]
is not applicable in this setting since
\mathrm{I}(W;S)\to\infty
and since
\ell(w,Z)
is not sub-Gaussian given that
\mathrm{Var}[\ell(w,Z)]\to\infty
as
\lVert w\rVert_{2}\to\infty
.
When
d=1
, the loss
\ell(W^{\prime},Z)
is
1
-sub-Gaussian if
W^{\prime}
is distributed according to the marginal distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}
and single-letter mutual information bound from
Bu et al. [7]
in
Theorem

4.10
produces a bound in
\mathcal{O}\big(\sqrt{\nicefrac{{\sigma^{2}}}{{n}}}\big)
, which decreases slower than the true generalization error, see
Figure

4.8
. This happens since the bound grows as the square root of
\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i})
, which is in
\mathcal{O}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}})
.

In this scenario, the loss is 1-Lipschitz under
\rho(w,w^{\prime})=\lVert w-w^{\prime}\rVert_{2}
, and thus the bounds based on the Wasserstein distance are applicable.
Applying the bound from
Wang et al. [180]
in
Theorem

4.19
yields a bound in
\mathcal{O}\big(\sqrt{\nicefrac{{\sigma^{2}d}}{{n}}}\big)
, which decreases at the same sub-optimal rate as the single-letter mutual information bound.
However, both the single-letter and random-subset Wasserstein distance bounds from
Theorems

4.20
and
4.21
produce bounds in
\mathcal{O}\big(\frac{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}d}}{n}\big)
, which decrease at the same rate as the true generalization error (see
Figure

4.8
).

Figure 4.8
:
Expected generalization error and generalization error bounds for the Gaussian location model from
Example

4.1
with
\mathcal{N}(\mu,1)
(left) and
\mathcal{N}(\mu,I_{250})
(right). See
Appendix

4.A
for the derivations and details.

The analysis that led to
Theorems

4.19
,
4.20
, and
4.21
can be adapted to the randomized-subsample setting with a few modifications. Recall that the empirical generalization error is defined as
\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(w,\tilde{s},u)=\widehat{{R}}(w,s_{\textnormal{ghost}})-\widehat{{R}}(w,s)
. Then, even if it is true that

\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)]=\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(W,\tilde{S},U)]=\mathbb{E}[\widehat{{R}}(W,S_{\textnormal{ghost}})]-\mathbb{E}[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)],

the functions
\widehat{{R}}(\cdot,s_{\textnormal{ghost}})
and
\widehat{{R}}(\cdot,s)
are distinct and therefore the Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality from
Lemma

2.6
cannot be invoked. Nonetheless, we may also note that

\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(W,\tilde{S},U)=\widehat{{R}}(W,S_{\textnormal{ghost}})-\widehat{{R}}(W,S)-\mathbb{E}[\widehat{{R}}(W^{\prime},S_{\textnormal{ghost}})-\widehat{{R}}(W^{\prime},S)]

for every random variable
W^{\prime}
that is independent of the indices
U
since the training data
S
and the ghost samples
S_{\textnormal{ghost}}
are identically distributed.

With this in mind, one may consider a fixed super sample
\tilde{s}
and some fixed indices
u
. Then, as before, one may let
X=W
and
Y=W^{\prime}
in (
11
), where
W
and
W^{\prime}
are distributed according to the algorithm’s distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}=\tilde{s},U=u}
a.s. and an indices-independent Markov kernel
\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{s})
. Then, one may apply the Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality from
Lemma

2.6
twice, first with
f(w;\tilde{s},u)=\widehat{{R}}(w,s_{\textnormal{ghost}})
and then with
f(w;\tilde{s},u)=\widehat{{R}}(w,s)
. In this way, one has that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{S}=s,U=u}

\displaystyle[\widehat{{R}}(W,s_{\textnormal{ghost}})]-\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{S}=s,U=u}[\widehat{{R}}(W^{\prime},s_{\textnormal{ghost}})]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{S}=s,U=u}[\widehat{{R}}(W^{\prime},s)]-\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{S}=s,U=u}[\widehat{{R}}(W,s)]\leq 2L\mathbb{W}_{\rho}\big(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}=s,U=u},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{s})\big),

where we used that the Wasserstein distance is a distance and therefore symmetric in its arguments. Finally, taking the expectation with respect to the super sample and the indices extends our
[
165
, Equation (3)]
by allowing any arbitrary Markov kernel
\mathbb{Q}
.

Theorem 4.22
.

Consider an
L
-Lipschitz loss function
\ell(\cdot,z)
with respect to some metric
\rho
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
. Then, for every data-independent Markov kernel
\mathbb{Q}
from
\mathcal{Z}^{n\times 2}
to distributions on
\mathcal{W}

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq 2L\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S}))\big].

We may recall again from
Section

15.1
and (
35
) that
\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(w,\tilde{s},u)=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(w,\tilde{z}_{i,0},\tilde{z}_{i,1},u_{i})
, where
\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(w,\tilde{z}_{i,0},\tilde{z}_{i,1},u_{i})=\ell(w,\tilde{z}_{i,{1-u_{i}}})-\ell(w,\tilde{z}_{i,u_{i}})
is the single-letter empirical generalization error. Hence, we may apply
Theorem

4.22
to each term to obtain a generalization of our
[
165
, Theorem 3]
.

Theorem 4.23
.

Consider an
L
-Lipschitz loss function
\ell(\cdot,z)
with respect to some metric
\rho
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
. Then, for every data-independent Markov kernel
\mathbb{Q}
from
\mathcal{Z}^{2}
to distributions on
\mathcal{W}

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\frac{2L}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{S}_{i,1},U_{i}},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1}))\right].

Similarly, we may recall from
Section

15.2
how
\widehat{\mathrm{gen}}(w,\tilde{s},u)=\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(w,s_{\textnormal{ghost},J})\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(w,s_{J})\big]
for every hypothesis
w\in\mathcal{W}
, super sample
\tilde{s}\in\mathcal{Z}^{n\times 2}
, and indices
u\in\{0,1\}^{n}
if
J
is a uniformly distributed random subset of
[n]
such that
|J|=m
. Therefore, we may apply
Theorems

4.22
and
4.23
to the expected generalization error
\mathbb{E}^{R=r,J=j,\tilde{S}=s,U_{j^{c}}=u_{j^{c}}}[\mathrm{gen}(W,\tilde{s}_{j},U_{j})]
and take the expectation with respect to the remaining random objects to obtain an extension of our
[
165
, Theorem 4]
.

Theorem 4.24
.

Consider an
L
-Lipschitz loss function
\ell(\cdot,z)
with respect to some metric
\rho
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
. Also consider a random subset
J\subseteq[n]
such that
|J|=m
, which is uniformly distributed and independent of
W
and
S
, and a random variable
R
that is independent of
S
. Then, for every Markov kernel
\mathbb{Q}
from
\mathcal{Z}^{n\times 2}\otimes\{0,1\}^{n-m}\otimes\mathcal{R}
to distributions on
\mathcal{W}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]

\displaystyle\leq 2L\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U,R},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}},R))\right]\textnormal{ and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2L}{m}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\in J}\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}},U_{i},R},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}},R))\right].

In particular, when
m=1
both equations in
Theorem

4.24
coincide and

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq 2L\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U,R},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S},U^{-J},R))\right]

Now that we derived Wasserstein distance-based bounds analogous to those based in the relative entropy from
Sections

13
,
14
, and
15
, it is important to understand how they relate to each other. Next, in
Section

16.1
, we compare the different Wasserstein distance bounds to each other; in
Section

16.2
, we show the implications that these bounds have on the bounds based on the relative entropy (and therefore the mutual information) and their relationship with them; and in
Section

16.3
, we describe how these bounds spur new bounds based on other
f
-divergences.

16.1
Comparison of the Bounds

As with the mutual information-based bounds, we note that the single-letter Wasserstein distance-based bounds (for example
Theorem

4.20
) are tighter than the bounds considering the full training set (for example
Theorem

4.19
) and the random-subset bounds (for example
Theorem

4.21
). However, now we see that the random-subset bounds are tighter than twice the bounds considering the full training set. This discrepancy with the comparison of the mutual information bounds from
Section

15.3
may come from the fact that now all bounds can be compared more fairly, in the sense that now the random-subset bounds do not need to be weakened with Jensen’s inequality for the comparison.
Comparing these bounds was the purpose of our
[
165
, Appendix D]
. A schematic of the relationships between the different bounds is given in
Figure

4.9
, albeit with the simplification that the Markov kernels
\mathbb{Q}
are assumed to follow from smoothing the algorithm’s distribution, for example
\mathbb{Q}=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\circ\mathbb{P}_{S}
or
\mathbb{Q}(S_{J^{c}})=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\circ\mathbb{P}_{S_{J}}
.

Theorem

4.20
\frac{L}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}},\mathbb{P}_{W})\big]
\leq
Theorem

4.19
[
180
]
L\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{P}_{W})\big]
\leq
(\times 2)
Theorem

4.21
L\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}},\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{J^{c}}})\big]
\leq
Theorem

4.23
\frac{2L}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1},U_{i}},\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1}})\big]
\leq
(\times 4)
\leq
Theorem

4.22
2L\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U},\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}})\big]
\leq
(\times 4)
\leq
(\times 2)
Theorem

4.24
2L\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U},\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}}})\big]
\leq
(\times 4)
\leq

Summary of the comparison between the full, the single-letter, and the random-subset bounds based on the Wasserstein distance of both the standard and the randomized-subsample setting for

16.1.1
Comparison in the Standard Setting

In the standard setting, the relationship between the different bounds can be summarized with the following proposition.

Proposition 4.3
.

Consider a random subset of indices
J\subseteq[n]
such that
|J|=m
. Then, for every data independent distribution
\mathbb{Q}
on
\mathcal{W}
, and every Markov kernel
\mathbb{Q}(\cdot)
from
\mathcal{Z}^{n-m}
to distributions on
\mathcal{W}
such that
\mathbb{Q}=\mathbb{Q}(S_{J^{c}})\circ\mathbb{P}_{S_{J^{c}}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}},\mathbb{Q})\big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q})\big],

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}},\mathbb{Q})\big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q}(S_{J^{c}})\big],\textnormal{ and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q}(S_{J^{c}})\big]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q})\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W},\mathbb{Q})\big].

The first inequality in
Proposition

4.3
is easy to prove by noting that
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}}
is a smooth version of
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
for all
i\in[n]
, namely
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}}=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\circ\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S^{-i}}
. Let
W^{\prime}
be the random variable independent of the training data
S
distributed according to
\mathbb{Q}
. Then
\mathbb{E}^{S}[f(W^{\prime})]=\mathbb{E}[f(W^{\prime})]
. Therefore, employing the Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality from
Lemma

2.6
we see that for every
i\in[n]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},Q)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{f\in 1\textnormal{-}\mathrm{Lip}(\rho)}\left\{\mathbb{E}^{S}[f(W)]-\mathbb{E}[f(W^{\prime})]\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{f\in 1\textnormal{-}\mathrm{Lip}(\rho)}\left\{\mathbb{E}^{Z_{i}}[f(W)]-\mathbb{E}[f(W^{\prime})]\right\}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}},Q)\right],

where the inequality follows from the fact that
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{f}f(X)]\geq\sup_{f}\mathbb{E}[f(X)]
.

The second inequality in
Proposition

4.3
follows similarly. First, note that the statement can be re-written as

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}},\mathbb{Q})\big]\leq\frac{1}{\binom{n}{m}}\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q}(S_{j^{c}}))\big]

writing the expectation with respect to
J
explicitly. Then, we can prove this statement by proving the stronger statement that for all
i\in[n]
and all
j\subseteq[n]
such that
i\in j

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}},\mathbb{Q})\big]\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q}(S_{j^{c}}))\big].

This statement is stronger since, without loss of generality, we could consider the instances
Z_{i}
ordered so that the sequence
\{\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}},\mathbb{Q})\}_{i=1}^{n}
is non-increasing. Then, this statement would make sure that
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{1}},\mathbb{Q})\big]
is smaller than
\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q}(S_{j^{c}}))\big]
for the
\binom{n-1}{m-1}
subsets
j
in which sample
Z_{1}
appears; that
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{2}},\mathbb{Q})\big]
is smaller than
\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q}(S_{j^{c}}))\big]
for the subsets
j
in which sample
Z_{2}
appears and
Z_{1}
does not; and so on therefore proving the original statement.

Now, we may proceed as before using that
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}}
is a smoothed version of
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
and that
\mathbb{Q}
is a smoothed version of
\mathbb{Q}(S_{j^{c}})
, namely
\mathbb{Q}=\mathbb{Q}(S_{j^{c}})\circ\mathbb{P}_{S_{j^{c}}}
. Employing the Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality from
Lemma

2.6

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q}(S_{j^{c}}))\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{f\in 1\textnormal{-}\mathrm{Lip}(\rho)}\left\{\mathbb{E}^{S}[f(W)]-\mathbb{E}^{S_{j^{c}}}[f(W^{\prime})]\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{f\in 1\textnormal{-}\mathrm{Lip}(\rho)}\left\{\mathbb{E}^{Z_{i}}[f(W)]-\mathbb{E}[f(W^{\prime})]\right\}\right]=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}},\mathbb{Q})\big],

where we let
W^{\prime}
be the random variable distributed according to
\mathbb{Q}
and with joint distribution with
S_{j^{c}}
equal to
\mathbb{Q}(S_{j^{c}})\otimes\mathbb{P}_{S^{j}}
, and where we also used that
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{f}f(X)]\geq\sup_{f}\mathbb{E}[f(X)]
.

Finally, the third inequality from
Proposition

4.3
follows from employing the triangle inequality of the Wasserstein distances
[
83
, Chapter 6]
. Note that we cannot employ the smoothing argument as above since while
\mathbb{Q}
is a smoothed version of
\mathbb{Q}(S_{J^{c}})
, including the expectation with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{S_{J^{c}}}
inside the supremum of
\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q}(S_{J^{c}})
would also smooth the first term
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
, leading to the inequality
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q}(S_{J^{c}}))\big]\geq\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{J}},\mathbb{Q})\big]
, while at the same time, and for the same arguments,
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q})\big]\geq\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{J}},\mathbb{Q})\big]
. Employing the triangle inequality we have that

\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q}(S_{J^{c}})\leq\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q})+\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P}_{W})+\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W},\mathbb{Q}(S_{J^{c}})).

From the same arguments outlined above,
\mathbb{P}_{W}
is a smoothed version of
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
and
\mathbb{Q}
is a smoothed version of
\mathbb{Q}(S_{J^{c}})
it follows that

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q}(S_{J^{c}})\big]\leq 2\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q})\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W},\mathbb{Q})\big].

An interesting thing to notice is that if we choose a Markov kernel
\mathbb{Q}(\cdot)
such that
\mathbb{Q}=\mathbb{P}_{W}
, then the term
\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P}_{W})
disappears. For example, choosing
\mathbb{Q}(S_{J^{c}})=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{J^{c}}}
. Moreover, in case that the distance between
\mathbb{P}_{W}
and
\mathbb{Q}
cannot be quantified, since
\mathbb{P}_{W}
is a smoothed version of
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
, we can re-use the previous arguments to note that

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q}(S_{J^{c}})\big]\leq 3\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q})\big].

16.1.2
Comparison in the Randomized-Subsample Setting

In the randomized-subsample setting, the relationship between the different bounds can be summarized with a proposition analogous to
Proposition

4.3
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Consider a random subset of indices
J\subseteq[n]
such that
|J|=m
. Further consider a Markov kernel
\mathbb{Q}
from
\mathcal{Z}^{n\times 2}\otimes\{0,1\}^{n-m}
to distributions on
\mathcal{W}
and let us abuse notation to also denote by
\mathbb{Q}
the Markov kernels from
\mathcal{Z}^{n\times 2}
and from
\mathcal{Z}^{2}
to distributions on
\mathcal{W}
defined as
\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{s})=\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{s},U_{J^{c}})\circ\mathbb{P}_{U_{J^{c}}}
and
\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{z}_{i,0},\tilde{z}_{i,1})=\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S})\circ\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{S}\setminus\{\tilde{z}_{i,0},\tilde{z}_{i,1}\}}
. Then, for every such Markov kernel

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1},U_{i}},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1}))\big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S}))\big],

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1},U_{i}},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1}))\big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}})\big],\textnormal{ and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}})\big]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S}))\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S}))\big].

Similarly to the standard setting, the first two inequalities of
Proposition

4.4
follow by noting that
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1},U_{i}}
is a smoothed version of
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U}
, that is,
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1},U_{i}}=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U}\circ\mathbb{P}_{\tilde{S}\setminus\{\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1}\},U^{-i}}
; and that the Markov kernel
\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})
is a smooth version of
\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S})
and therefore also of
\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}})
. The proof follows by employing the Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality from
Lemma

2.6
and the fact that
\sup_{f}\mathbb{E}[X]\leq\mathbb{E}[\sup_{f}f(X)]
and it is almost verbose to those shown previously.

The third inequality from
Proposition

4.4
follows from employing the triangle inequality of the Wasserstein distances
[
83
, Chapter 6]
similarly to what we did to prove
Proposition

4.3
. In this case, we have that

\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}}))\leq\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S}))+\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S}),\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}})+\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}})).

Using the smoothing arguments from this section, noting that
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}}
and
\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}})
are both smoothed versions of
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U}
and
\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}})
with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{U}
yields that

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}}))\big]\leq 2\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S}))\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S}))\big].

Finally, we may note that choosing the Markov kernel
\mathbb{Q}
from
\mathcal{Z}^{n}
to distributions on
\mathcal{W}
such that
\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S})=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}}
makes the term
\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S}))
disappear. Also, another smoothing argument with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{U}
reveals that

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}}))\big]\leq 3\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S}))\big].

16.1.3
Comparison Between the Settings

Comparing the Wasserstein distance-based bounds from the standard and the randomized-subsample setting is simple if we note that
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U}=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
a.s., that is, the distribution of the hypothesis when the supersample and the indices of the instances used for training are given is almost surely equal to the distribution of the hypothesis when the training samples are given. With this observation, the comparisons follow a combination of the triangle inequality and the smoothing technique shown in the previous two subsections. Their relationship can be summarized with the following proposition.

Proposition 4.5
.

Consider a random subset of indices
J\subseteq[n]
such that
J=m
. Further consider a Markov kernel
\mathbb{Q}
from
\mathcal{Z}^{n\times 2}\times\{0,1\}^{n-m}
to distributions on
\mathcal{W}
. Furthermore, let us also denote by
\mathbb{Q}
any smoothing, or marginalization, of this Markov kernel. Then, for every such Markov kernel,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U}\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S}))]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q})\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S}))],

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1},U_{i}},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1}))\big]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}},\mathbb{Q})\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1}},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1}))\big],\textnormal{ and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}})\big]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q}(S_{J^{c}})\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}}},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}})\big].

Before showing these inequalities, note that they are all analogous to each other and that the second term on the right-hand side always disappears if the Markov kernel
\mathbb{Q}
is chosen to be the Markov kernel associated with smoothing the algorithm’s Markov kernel
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U}
.

Since the proof of the three statements is analogous and almost verbose using the techniques outlined previously, we only write explicitly the proof of the first inequality. Employing the triangle inequality of the Wasserstein distances
[
83
, Chapter 6]
we have that

\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S}))\leq\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U},\mathbb{Q})+\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}})+\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S})).

(38)

Then, noting that
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}}
is a smoothed version of
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U}
with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{U}
and that
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U}=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
a.s. results in the desired inequality

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U},\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S}))\big]\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q})\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}})\big].

Rather than just stating the comparison of the different bounds, we may inspect them a little further. To get a better understanding, let
\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S})=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}}
. Instead of applying the triangle inequality twice in (
38
), we may just apply it once to see that, after taking expectations to both sides,

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U},\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}})\big]\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{P}_{W})\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}},\mathbb{P}_{W})\big].

The term on the left-hand side tells us what the difference is between the hypothesis distribution when the training set is known and when the supersample is known but not which indices are used for training. This describes how much the algorithm will change the distribution of its output when changing the samples used for training from a given set of identically distributed instances. The first term on the right-hand side is already known to us, it tells us the difference between the hypothesis distribution after observing the training data and the prototypical hypothesis distribution from samples of the data distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\circ\mathbb{P}_{S}
. We might expect that, similarly to what happened for the mutual information-based bounds (
Section

15.3
), the inequality
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U},\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}})\big]\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{P}_{W})\big]
holds, since the knowledge of the super sample
\tilde{S}
restricts the smoothing of
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U}
to all
2^{n}
possible training set arrangements described by
U
, whereas the smoothing of
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
considers potentially infinite (if
\mathcal{Z}
is not finite) training sets. It is possible that such an inequality holds, although we have not been able to prove it. Nonetheless, if we focus on the extra term
\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}},\mathbb{P}_{W})
that appears in the inequality, we see that it captures precisely how different the smoothed distributions
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}}
and
\mathbb{P}_{W}
are. A final smoothing argument tells us that

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U},\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}})\big]\leq 2\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{P}_{W})\big]

holds, capturing the essence of the expected relationship between the two terms.

16.2
Implications for Bounds Using Mutual Information

At the beginning of this section, we noted how the relative entropy or mutual information-based bounds were the result of the decoupling
Lemma

4.4
that stems from the Donsker and Varadhan
Lemma

2.1
, and how employing instead the decoupling that stems from the Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality of
Lemma

2.6
led to similar results based on the Wasserstein distance. A benefit of the latter bounds is that they take into account the geometry of the space in an explicit way, which is not often the case for the relative entropy-based bounds. In this subsection, we will first show that if we disregard the geometry of the space, we can transform the bounds based on the Wasserstein distance into bounds on the total variation. Later, we will see that this allows us to extend the set of relative entropy and mutual information-based bounds to Lipschitz losses, which, as we will show, are tight. Moreover, this implies that, for losses with a bounded range, the Wasserstein distance-based bounds are always tighter than those based on the relative entropy. Finally, we will conclude by showing how the total variation bounds also help us derive new bounds based on other
f
-divergences.

16.2.1
Disregarding the Geometry: Bounds Using Total Variation

Recall from
Proposition

2.5
that the Wasserstein distance is dominated by the total variation. Applying this consideration to the first result in this section results in the following corollary.

Corollary 4.2
.

Consider an
L
-Lipschitz loss function
\ell(\cdot,z)
with respect to some metric
\rho
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
. Further consider a bounded space
\mathcal{W}
with
\mathrm{diam}_{\rho}(\mathcal{W})=B
. Then, for every data-independent distribution
\mathbb{Q}
on
\mathcal{W}

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq LB\mathbb{E}\left[\min\left\{\mathrm{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{P}_{W}),2\mathrm{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q})\right\}\right].

By the interpretation of the total variation as the limit accuracy in binary hypothesis testing from
Section

4.4
, we may strengthen our understanding that the less the final hypothesis depends on the data, the more it generalizes. More precisely, given a dataset
S
and upon observing a hypothesis
W
, we may wonder if this hypothesis comes from the posterior distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
characterizing an algorithm
\mathbb{A}
or a data-independent prior distribution
\mathbb{Q}
. Let
T
denote a binary random variable describing if
W
is sampled from the posterior distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
(when
T=1
) or from the prior distribution
\mathbb{Q}
(when
T=0
). Further consider the set
\mathcal{F}
of decision rules
f:\mathcal{W}\to\{0,1\}
that try to estimate
T
and tell if the hypothesis
W
comes from the prior or the posterior. Then, we have that

\mathbb{E}[\textnormal{gen}(W,S)]\leq 2LB\left(1-2\mathbb{E}\Big[\inf_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\mathbb{P}^{S}\big[f(W)\neq T\big]\Big]\right).

(39)

In other words, the harder it is to distinguish hypotheses sampled from the algorithm after observing the training data
S
and from the data-independent prior
\mathbb{Q}
, the better the algorithm generalizes.

Note as well that by considering the total variation, we have essentially disregarded the geometrical information provided to us by the metric
\rho
.
Indeed, if one considers the discrete metric
\rho_{\mathrm{H}}(x,y)=\mathbb{I}_{\{x\neq y\}}(x,y)
, then
\mathbb{W}_{\rho_{\mathrm{H}}}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})=\mathrm{TV}(\mathbb{P},\mathbb{Q})
(
Proposition

2.5
). Then, for example, if the distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
is not absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathbb{Q}
like in
Figure

4.7
,
Corollary

4.1
only guarantees that the generalization error is bounded by
2LB
even if the supports of the distributions were close and
\mathbb{W}_{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{2}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q})=\varepsilon
for some positive
\varepsilon\to 0
.

The diameter term
B
can be arbitrarily large as, for example, when the hypothesis are the weights of a differentiable network and the metric
\rho
is the
\ell_{2}
norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{2}
.
Nonetheless, this term can still be small and relevant for practical settings.
For instance, consider again that the hypothesis are the weights of a differentiable network. However, consider now that the metric
\rho
is the infinity norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty}
and that each weight is enforced to be smaller than some small constant
c
. Then, the diameter of the space
B=\mathrm{diam}_{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\infty}}(\mathcal{W})
is (at most) equal to
c
.

A particularly interesting example is the one we obtained for bounded losses. In this setting, the loss is always
(b-a)
-Lipschitz under the discrete metric and therefore
Corollary

4.2
reduces to

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq(b-a)\mathbb{E}\left[\min\left\{\mathrm{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{P}_{W}),2\mathrm{TV}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q})\right\}\right].

(40)

Therefore, the generalization bound scales with the total variation and, if the prior
\mathbb{Q}
is chosen to be
\mathbb{P}_{W}
, it is always confined within the range
[0,b-a]
, which ensures it is never vacuous.

It is tempting to think that considering an
L
-Lipschitz loss and a hypothesis space with a diameter bounded by
B
is equivalent to considering a loss with a bounded range. Indeed, if a function
f:\mathcal{W}\to\mathbb{R}
is both
L
-Lipschitz and
\mathrm{diam}_{\rho}(\mathcal{W})=B
, we have that
|f(w)-f(w^{\prime})|\leq LB
for all
w,w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}
and therefore there is some constant
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that
f(w)\in[c,c+LR]
. However, this is not necessarily true for loss functions
\ell:\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
since they take two arguments: the hypothesis
w
and the instances
z
. As a simple example, for some dimension
d
, consider the
1
-Lipschitz loss function
\ell(w,z)=\langle w,z\rangle
, the unit ball as the hypothesis space
\mathcal{W}=\{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\lVert w\rVert_{2}=1\}
, and the reals as the instance space
\mathcal{Z}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, the diameter of the hypothesis space is bounded
(\mathrm{diam}_{\rho}(\mathcal{W})=2)
but the loss can be infinite, that is
\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\langle w,z\rangle\to\infty
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}\setminus\{0\}
.

This reasoning extends analogously for all the presented Wasserstein distance-based bounds from
Theorems

4.20
,
4.21
,
4.22
,
4.23
, and
4.24
. In what follows, we will refer to these corollaries without explicitly writing them to avoid verbosity.

16.2.2
From Total Variation to Relative Entropy and Mutual Information

Consider the analogue of
Corollary

4.2
for
Theorem

4.20
. Moreover, for simplicity, let
\mathbb{Q}=\mathbb{P}_{W}
. Applying Pinsker’s and the Bretagnolle-Huber inequalities from
Lemmata

2.2
and
2.3
leads to the following result.

Corollary 4.3
.

Consider an
L
-Lipschitz loss function
\ell(\cdot,z)
with respect to some metric
\rho
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
. Further consider a bounded space
\mathcal{W}
with
\mathrm{diam}_{\rho}(\mathcal{W})=B
. Then,

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\frac{LB}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi\left(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}}\|\mathbb{P}_{W})\right)\right]\leq\frac{LB}{n\sqrt{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i})},

where
\Psi(x)^{2}=\min\{\nicefrac{{x}}{{2}},1-\exp(-x)\}
.

If the loss is bounded, this corollary improves upon
Theorem

4.10
in two different ways. First, it pulls the expectation with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{Z_{i}}
outside of the concave square root, thus strengthening the result via Jensen’s inequality. Second, the addition of the Bretagnolle–Hubert inequality ensures that heavily influential samples with high
\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i})
do not contribute too negatively to the bound, which is ensured to be non-vacuous. The main difference between the two results are their assumptions:
Theorem

4.10
needs losses with a bounded CGF and
Corollary

4.3
needs them to be Lipschitz with respect to some metric on a space with a bounded diameter. When the loss is bounded, the two coincide with the aforementioned improvements of
Corollary

4.3
with respect to
Theorem

4.10
. Moreover, since
\Psi
is a concave function, a further application of Jensen’s inequality and
Proposition

4.1
yields the following result.

Corollary 4.4
.

Consider an
L
-Lipschitz loss function
\ell(\cdot,z)
with respect to some metric
\rho
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
. Further consider a bounded space
\mathcal{W}
with
\mathrm{diam}_{\rho}(\mathcal{W})=B
. Then,

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq LB\Psi\left(\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{n}\right)\leq LB\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}{2n}},

where
\Psi(x)^{2}=\min\{\nicefrac{{x}}{{2}},1-\exp(-x)\}
.

These results prove that that
Theorem

4.20
improves upon
Theorems

4.1
and
4.10
when the loss has a bounded range.

The same logic can be applied to the analogue of
Corollary

4.2
for
Theorem

4.23
, which results in the following Corollary. However, in these cases, the Bretagnolle–Huber inequality from
Lemma

2.3
is not considered. The reason is that the relative entropy terms in these equations are bounded by values in the region where the Pinsker’s inequality from
Lemma

2.2
is tighter than the Bretagnolle–Huber inequality. The details of this consideration are not relevant to the main text and are delegated to
Appendix

4.C
.

Corollary 4.5
.

Consider an
L
-Lipschitz loss function
\ell(\cdot,z)
with respect to some metric
\rho
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
. Further consider a bounded space
\mathcal{W}
with
\mathrm{diam}_{\rho}(\mathcal{W})=B
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{LB}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{2\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1},U_{i}}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1}})}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{LB}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{2\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})}.

In this case, this corollary improves upon
Theorem

4.14
in the ways described above and in its generality, as now it holds for all Lipschitz losses and not only those with a bounded range. Again, since
\sqrt{\cdot}
is concave, a further application of Jensen’s inequality and
Proposition

4.1
yields the following result, which indicates that
Theorem

4.23
improves upon
Theorems

4.8
and
4.14
.

Corollary 4.6
.

Consider an
L
-Lipschitz loss function
\ell(\cdot,z)
with respect to some metric
\rho
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
. Further consider a bounded space
\mathcal{W}
with
\mathrm{diam}_{\rho}(\mathcal{W})=B
. Then,

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq LB\sqrt{\frac{2\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})}{n}}\leq LB\sqrt{\frac{2\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})}{n}}.

To conclude this part of the subsection, we note that we can apply the same reasoning to the analogues of
Corollary

4.2
for the random subset
Theorems

4.21
and
4.24
. However, let us do so considering an arbitrary
\mathbb{Q}
. Then, after the application of Pinsker’s and the Bretagnolle–Huber inequalities from
Lemmata

2.2
and
2.3
we essentially recover
Theorems

4.17
and
4.18
as promised in
Section

15.2
.For
Corollary

4.7
, we gain a factor of two compared to
Theorem

4.17
, while
Corollary

4.8
maintains the constants. However, we benefit by extending the bound to general Lipschitz losses. We present the results for
m=1
to make the relationship with the bounds in the previous section evident, although the results can be obtained for a general
m\in[n]
.

Corollary 4.7
.

Consider an
L
-Lipschitz loss function
\ell(\cdot,z)
with respect to some metric
\rho
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
. Further consider a bounded space
\mathcal{W}
with
\mathrm{diam}_{\rho}(\mathcal{W})=B
. Also consider a random index
J\in[n]
, which is uniformly distributed and independent of
W
and
S
, and a random variable
R
that is independent of
S
. Then, for every Markov kernel
\mathbb{Q}
from
\mathcal{Z}^{n-1}\otimes\mathcal{R}\otimes J
to distributions on
\mathcal{W}

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq 2LB\mathbb{E}\left[\Psi\left(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S,R}\|\mathbb{Q}(S^{-J},R,J)\big)\right)\right],

where
\Psi(x)^{2}=\min\{\nicefrac{{x}}{{2}},1-\exp(-x)\}
.

Corollary 4.8
.

Consider an
L
-Lipschitz loss function
\ell(\cdot,z)
with respect to some metric
\rho
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
. Further consider a bounded space
\mathcal{W}
with
\mathrm{diam}_{\rho}(\mathcal{W})=B
. Also consider a random index
J\in[n]
, which is uniformly distributed and independent of
W
,
\tilde{S}
, and
U
, and a random variable
R
that is independent of
\tilde{S}
, and
U
. Then, for every Markov kernel
\mathbb{Q}
from
\mathcal{Z}^{n\times 2}\otimes\{0,1\}^{n-1}\otimes\mathcal{R}\otimes\mathcal{J}
to distributions on
\mathcal{W}

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq 2LB\mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U,R}\|\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S},U^{-J},R)\big)}\right].

16.2.3
Generality of the Mutual Information Bounds

For losses with a bounded CGF such as sub-Gaussian, we know that in the trivial case where the output of a learning algorithm is independent of the training set,
Xu and Raginsky [5]
’s result from
Theorem

4.1
is tight. However, this result is not so interesting as, in that case, the algorithm did not learn anything from the data. Our following theorem
[
136
]
states that for Lipschitz losses, the bounds are tight even when the learning algorithm depends on the training set.

Theorem 4.25
.

There exists an
L
-Lipschitz loss function
\ell(\cdot,z)
with respect to some metric
\rho
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, a bounded space
\mathcal{W}
with
\mathrm{diam}_{\rho}(\mathcal{W})=B
, a data distribution
\mathbb{P}_{S}
, and a learning algorithm
\mathbb{A}
such that: (i) the expected generalization error satisfies that
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\geq\frac{LB}{2\sqrt{2n}}
and (ii) the upper bounds from
Corollary

4.4
is
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq LB\sqrt{\frac{\log 2}{2n}}
.

The theorem immediately implies that the bounds from
Theorems

4.20
and
4.23
and
Corollaries

4.3
,
4.6
, and
4.5
are also tight since the Wasserstein distance bounds led to the mutual information bounds, and due to the relationships between the mutual information bounds from
Section

15.3
.

Theorem

4.25
also shows that there exists a learning algorithm for which the bounds are tight. This implies that the bounds cannot be improved
simultaneously
(or
uniformly
) for every learning algorithm. Note, however, that there may exist a tighter bound for some
specific
learning algorithms. The proof of this theorem is inspired by
[
182
]
. Since it does not give any additional insight into the discussion, we delegate it to
Appendix

4.B
.

Finally, we note that the results from this
Section

16.2
can be extended to losses that are Lipschitz and have a bounded CGF, without the need for the hypothesis space
\mathcal{W}
to be bounded. Therefore, the results from
Corollaries

4.4
,
4.3
,
4.6
,
4.5
,
4.7
, and
4.8
are valid in a more general setting. The result follows from our extension of the Bobkov–Götze theorem
[
84
, Theorem 4.8]
from
Lemma

2.7
. To realize this, consider a random variable
X
distributed according to
\mathbb{P}
. Also consider an
L
-Lipschitz function
f
that has a CGF
\Lambda_{f(X)}(\lambda)
bounded by
\varphi(\lambda)
, then
\nicefrac{{f(X)}}{{L}}
is
1
-Lipschitz and has a CGF bounded by
\varphi(\nicefrac{{\lambda}}{{L}})
. Hence, if
f(X)-\mathbb{E}[X]\leq L\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{P})
for some distribution
\mathbb{Q}
, it follows that
f(X)-\mathbb{E}[X]\leq L\varphi_{*}^{-1}\big(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{Q}\|\mathbb{P})\big)
.

As an example, assume that the loss
\ell(\cdot,z)
is
L
-Lipschitz and that
\ell(W^{\prime},z)
has a CGF bounded by
\varphi
for every
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, where
W^{\prime}
is distributed according to
\mathbb{Q}
. Then, we may obtain the following result as a corollary of
Theorem

4.20
due to our extension of the Bobkov–Götze’s theorem in
Lemma

2.7
:

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\frac{2L}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{*}^{-1}\big(I(W;Z_{i})\big)\leq 2L\varphi_{*}^{-1}\left(\frac{I(W;S)}{n}\right).

This result encompasses the case where the loss is bounded in
[a,b]
since if a random variable is bounded in
[a,b]
it is
\nicefrac{{(b-a)}}{{2}}
-sub-Gaussian.

Note, however, that in this case the
“bounded CGF” condition is different from the one in
Definition

4.1
and
Sections

14
and
15
. There, the loss
\ell(w,Z)
is supposed to have a CGF bounded by
\psi
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
, while now we are considering that the loss
\ell(W^{\prime},z)
has a CGF bounded by
\varphi
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
. Therefore, instead of considering that the loss is concentrated around its expectation with respect to the data, we can engineer a prior distribution
\mathbb{Q}
such that the loss is concentrated around its expectation with respect to hypotheses sampled from the said prior.

16.3
Bounds Using Other
f
-divergences

In
[
165
]
, we note how using the joint range technique from Harremoës
and Vajda
[
77
]
discussed in
Section

4.4
allows us to find expected generalization bounds based on different
f
-divergences.

In particular, as an example, starting from
Corollary

4.2
we may obtain the corollaries

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]

\displaystyle\leq LB\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{Hel}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{P}_{W})\sqrt{1-\frac{\mathrm{Hel}^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{P}_{W})}{4}}\right]\textnormal{ and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{LB}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{P}_{W})}\right],

although this extends more broadly to any
f
-divergence and it holds for the single-letter, random subset, and randomized subset versions of
Corollary

4.2
as well, including those with a generic distribution or Markov kernel
\mathbb{Q}
. We only present these two as the Hellinger distance and the
\chi^{2}
divergence are special due to their connections to hypothesis testing and the fact that most
f
-divergences locally behave like the
\chi^{2}
divergence as described in
Section

4.4
.

Moreover, recall from the start of this
Section

16
, that the results based on the relative entropy and the Wasserstein distance are obtained through decoupling lemmas, either
Lemma

4.4
powered by the Donsker and Varadhan
Lemma

2.1
or the Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality from
Lemma

2.6
. This idea can be extended to other
f
-divergences using the variational representation of
f
-divergences from
Lemma

2.4
. As an example, considering the variational representation of the
\chi^{2}
divergence from
Corollary

2.1
, the following result stems directly by noting that if
\ell(w,Z)
has a variance bounded by
\sigma^{2}
, then
\widehat{{R}}(w)
has a variance bounded by
\nicefrac{{\sigma^{2}}}{{n}}
due to the Independence of the samples.

Proposition 4.6
.

Consider a loss function
\ell(w,Z)
with a variance bounded by
\sigma^{2}
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
. Then,

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}\chi^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{P}_{W})}{n}}\right].

This proposition offers an alternative to
Theorem

4.7
in terms of the
\chi^{2}
divergence. Note that since
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\leq\log(1+\chi^{2})
, although more complicated,
Theorem

4.7
is often tighter than
Proposition

4.6
. A similar discussion will appear later in
Section

4.2
in the context of PAC-Bayes bounds, where these ideas are refined by
Ohnishi and Honorio [11]
and
Esposito et al. [10]
.

Finally, we may also mention that since the total variation is symmetric, applying Pinsker’s inequality with the distributions in the opposite order to
Corollaries

4.4
,
4.3
,
4.7
,
4.6
,
4.5
, and
4.8
and further applying Jensen’s inequality yields bounds based on the lautum information
L

[
183
]
.
For instance, a corollary of
Theorem

4.20
is

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\frac{LB}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Psi\big(\textnormal{{L}}(W;Z_{i})\big),

where the lautum information
\textnormal{{L}}(W;Z_{i})
also measures the level of dependence between the hypothesis
W
and the samples
Z_{i}
, has ties to independence testing, and has similar properties to the mutual information
[
183
]
.

16.4
The Choice of the Metric and the Backward Channel

In
[
184
]
, the authors study the characterization of the expected generalization error in terms of the discrepancy between the data distribution
\mathbb{P}_{S}
and the backward channel distribution
\mathbb{P}_{S}^{W}
from
Figure

4.5
motivated by its connection to rate-distortion theory, see, for example,
[
57
, Chapters 24–27]
or
[
56
, Chapter 10]
.

More concretely, they proved that the generalization error is bounded from above by the discrepancy of these distributions, where the discrepancy is measured by the Wasserstein distance of order
p
with the Minkowski distance of order
p
as a metric, that is,
\rho(x,y)=\lVert x-y\rVert_{p}
. Namely,

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\frac{L}{n^{1/p}}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{p,\lVert\cdot\rVert}^{p}(\mathbb{P}_{S},\mathbb{P}_{S}^{W})]^{1/p}.

Similarly, every result in this
Section

16
can be replicated considering the backward channel instead of the forward channel, for example, using
\mathbb{P}_{S}^{W}
instead of
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
in
Theorem

4.19
or
\mathbb{P}_{Z_{i}}^{W}
instead of
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}}
in
Theorem

4.20
.
However, in this case, the loss
