\ell
would be required to be Lipschitz with respect to the instances in the sample space
\mathcal{Z}
for hypotheses
w\in\mathcal{W}
and not for all the hypothesis in the space
\mathcal{W}
, thus exploiting the geometry of the samples’ space and not the hypotheses’ one.

As an example, noting that

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S^{\prime})-\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big],

where
S^{\prime}
is an independent copy of the training set
S
such that
\mathbb{P}_{W,S^{\prime}}=\mathbb{P}_{W}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{S}
produces the bound

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq L\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{S},\mathbb{P}_{S}^{W})\big].

Compared to
[
184
]
, these results (i) are valid for any metric
\rho
as long as the loss
\ell
is Lipschitz under
\rho
,
and (ii) have single-letter and random-subset versions, and (iii) have variants in both the standard and randomized-subsample settings.

The choice of the metric can be decisive for a tight analysis of the presented bounds, and there are times when a loss function can be Lipschitz under several metrics. For example, a bounded loss function represented as a norm is Lipschitz with respect to that norm and the discrete metric. Nonetheless, in many situations, the metric of choice becomes apparent based on the loss function. For example, if we consider the forward channel bounds and samples of the type
z=(x,y)
and the following two common supervised tasks:

•

Regression. If a norm is used as the loss function
\ell(w,z)=\lVert w-y\rVert
, then such a norm is also a good choice for a metric since by the reverse triangle inequality the loss is 1-Lipschitz under that metric:
\big\lvert\lVert w-y\rVert-\lVert w^{\prime}-y\rVert\big\rvert\leq\lVert w-w^{\prime}\rVert
for all
w,w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}
.

•

Classification. If the 0-1 loss is used as the loss function
\ell(w,z)=\mathbb{I}_{\{f_{w}(w)\neq y\}}(w,z)
for some parameterized model
f_{w}
, then the discrete metric is a good choice since the loss is also 1-Lipschitz under this metric:
\lvert\mathbb{I}_{\{f_{w}(x)\neq y\}}(w,z)-\mathbb{I}_{\{f_{w^{\prime}}(x)\neq y\}}(w^{\prime},y)\big\rvert\leq\mathbb{I}_{\{w\neq w^{\prime}\}}(w,w^{\prime})
.

Similarly, for the backward channel bounds, it is known that the logistic loss, the softmax loss,
15
15

15

This result can be derived from
[
185
, Proposition 3]
and the
L_{1}-L_{2}
inequality.
the Hinge loss, and many distance-based losses like norms, the Huber,
\epsilon
-insensitive, and pinball losses, are Lipschitz under the
L_{1}
norm metric
\rho(z,z^{\prime})=\lvert z-z^{\prime}\rvert

[
186
, Chapter 2]
[
185
]
.

17
Application: Noisy, Iterative Learning Algorithms

This section discusses an application of the previously presented generalization error bounds for noisy, iterative algorithms. This application and line of work was initiated by
Pensia et al. [166]
, who were the first to note that a combination of the
chain rule
of the mutual information (and the relative entropy) and the
more data, more information
(or the
monotonicity
of the relative entropy) made these kind of bounds very suitable to study iterative algorithms (see
Propositions

2.4
and
2.3
to recall these properties). In their paper,
Pensia et al.
studied the stochastic gradient Langevin dynamics (SGLD)
[
154
,
155
]
and the stochastic gradient Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (SGHMC)
[
187
]
algorithms. Later works, including ours, mostly focused on improving the bounds for SGLD
[
188
,
7
,
8
,
156
,
157
,
189
,
190
,
191
]
and developing new bounds for stochastic gradient descent (SGD)
[
145
,
192
,
170
]
.

17.1
Stochastic Gradient Langevin Dynamics

The SGLD algorithm is an iterative, optimization procedure to learn the parameters of a parameterized model, often a differentiable network, from a training set
s\in\mathcal{Z}^{n}

[
154
,
155
]
. As discussed previously in
Section

12
, since the parameters returned by the algorithm
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
completely characterize the hypothesis, we may employ the two terms without distinction.

The method originates from the Bayesian inference community and combines ideas from stochastic optimization and Langevin dynamics. This combination helps SGLD to explore the parameter space more effectively.
The algorithm is simple:

•

First, it starts with a random initialization
W_{0}
of the model’s parameters.

•

Then, for each iteration
t\in[T]
, it samples a random batch
s_{V_{t}}
from the dataset
s
; updates the previous parameter
W_{t-1}
with a scaled
(-\eta_{t})
version of the gradient of the empirical risk of that parameter in the random batch, that is,
\nabla_{w}\widehat{{R}}(W_{t-1},s_{V_{t}})
; and adds a scaled
(\sigma_{t})
isotropic Gaussian random noise
\Xi_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
. More precisely, the update rule is

W_{t}=W_{t-1}-\eta_{t}\nabla_{w}\widehat{{R}}(W_{t-1},s_{V_{t}})+\sigma_{t}\Xi_{t},

(41)

where
W_{t}
are the parameters (hypothesis) at iteration
t
.

•

Finally, it returns the final hypothesis
W_{T}
.

When the batch is composed of all samples, that is, there is no stochasticity in the sample selection, the algorithm is called simply
Langevin dynamics (LD).

17.1.1
Unconditional Bounds

Pensia et al. [166]
leveraged the generalization bounds from
Theorem

4.1

[
5
]
and analyzed the information captured by the final hypothesis returned by SGLD about the training set. As discussed in the section opening, the development of their bounds follows from a sequential application of the
more data, more information
and the
chain rules
of the mutual information (
Proposition

2.4
), that is

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}(W_{T};S)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{I}(W^{T},W_{0};S)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{I}(W_{0};S)+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{I}(W_{t};S|W^{t-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{I}(W_{t};S|W_{t-1}),

where in the last equation we used that the initial parameter
W_{0}
is independent of the data
S
and the Markovian nature of the problem (
41
). Then, we may note that
\mathrm{I}(W_{t};S|W_{t-1})=\mathrm{H}(W_{t}|W_{t-1})-\mathrm{H}(W_{t}|S,W_{t-1})
. Now, given
S
and
W_{t-1}
, the random variable
W_{t}
follows a Gaussian distribution with covariance
\sigma_{t}^{2}I_{d}
and mean given by (
41
), and therefore
\mathrm{H}(W_{t}|S,W_{t-1})=\frac{d}{2}\log(2\pi e\sigma_{t}^{2})
. On the other hand, the distribution of
W_{t}
given
W_{t-1}
is unknown. Since the differential entropy is invariant to translations
[
56
, Theorem 8.6.3]
, we may instead study the random variable
W_{t}-W_{t-1}
given
W_{t-1}
. Now, we may bound this variable’s expected squared norm as

\mathbb{E}^{W_{t-1}}\Big[\lVert W_{t}-W_{t-1}\rVert_{2}^{2}\Big]\leq\eta_{t}^{2}\mathbb{E}^{W_{t-1}}\Big[\lVert\nabla_{w}\widehat{{R}}(W_{t-1},S_{V_{t}})\rVert^{2}\Big]+\sigma_{t}^{2}\mathbb{E}^{W_{t-1}}\Big[\lVert\Xi_{t}\rVert_{2}^{2}\Big].

When studying gradient-based, iterative algorithms, it is common to consider Lipschitz-continuous losses (
Definition

2.19
).
The main reason for this assumption is that if a loss is
L
-Lipschitz-continuous, then it has a bounded gradient, that is
\sup_{w\in\mathcal{W},z\in\mathcal{Z}}\lVert\ell(w,z)\rVert_{2}\leq L
. Under this assumption, the expected squared norm is bounded by
\eta_{t}^{2}L^{2}+d\sigma^{2}
. Notably, the entropy of a variable with an expected squared norm bounded by
a
is bounded by the entropy of a Gaussian random variable with an isotropic covariance with scale
\nicefrac{{a}}{{d}}
. Combining this upper bound with the prior calculation of the entropy
\mathrm{H}(W_{t}|W_{t-1},S)
gives us an upper bound for the mutual information

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}(W_{t};S|W^{t-1})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{d}{2}\log\left(\frac{2\pi e(\eta_{t}^{2}L^{2}+d\sigma_{t}^{2})}{d}\right)-\frac{d}{2}\log\left(2\pi e\sigma_{t}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{2}\log\left(1+\frac{\eta_{t}^{2}L^{2}}{d\sigma_{t}^{2}}\right).

The sum of all these terms bounds the mutual information and, as per
Section

13
, it also bounds the expected generalization error.

Proposition 4.7

(
[
166
, Theorem 1]
)
.

Consider an
L
-Lipschitz continuous loss. Then, the mutual information
\mathrm{I}(W_{T};S)
for the SGLD algorithm is bounded by

\mathrm{I}(W_{T};S)\leq\frac{d}{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\log\left(1+\frac{\eta_{t}^{2}L^{2}}{d\sigma_{t}^{2}}\right).

Let the noise variance be
\sigma_{t}^{2}=\eta_{t}
, the batch size be fixed
|v_{t}|=|v|
for all
t\in[T]
, and the algorithm run for
k
epochs so that
T\approx\nicefrac{{kn}}{{|v|}}
. If we let the step size decrease as
\eta_{t}=\nicefrac{{c}}{{t}}
for some constant
c>0
, then, a corollary of this proposition is that the mutual information is bounded by

\mathrm{I}(W_{T};S)\leq\frac{L^{2}c}{2}\left(1+\log\frac{nk}{|v|}\right).

This follows by first applying the bound
\log(1+x)\leq x
for all
x>0
and then the common bound on the Harmonic numbers
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\nicefrac{{1}}{{t}}\leq 1+\log T
.
Pensia et al. [166]
considered the bound from
Theorem

4.1

[
5
]
to establish that if the loss was sub-Gaussian, then the SGLD algorithm had a generalization error with a vanishing rate in
\mathcal{O}(L\sqrt{\nicefrac{{\log(\frac{nk}{|v|})}}{{n}}})
. At the time, this result compared negatively with respect to
[
193
]
in terms of the relationship with the data, as they showed that the generalization error was in
\mathcal{O}(L\nicefrac{{\sqrt{\log nk}}}{{n}})
for a single sample batch size
|v|=1
.
16
16

16

The logarithmic dependence in
[
193
]
is actually better, although an exact portrait of this dependence goes beyond the scope of this exposition.
In hindsight, considering a fast-rate bound like our
Theorem

4.3
,
Pensia et al. [166]
’s result implies that the population risk deviates from an estimate based on the empirical error at a rate in
\mathcal{O}(L^{2}\nicefrac{{\log(\frac{nk}{|v|})}}{{n}})
, essentially matching the rate from
[
193
]
.

Bu et al. [7]
refined
Pensia et al. [166]
’s analysis by considering the single-letter mutual information. The analysis follows similarly to the one above employing the
more data, more information
and the
chain rules
of the mutual information properties from
Proposition

2.4
. Although for a particular iterate
t
, the resulting mutual information did not improve substantially, as they showed that

\mathrm{I}(W_{t};Z_{i}|W_{t-1})\leq\frac{d}{2}\log\left(1+\frac{\eta_{t}^{2}L^{2}}{d\sigma_{t}^{2}}\right);

Bu et al. [7]
introduced an innovation that improved the logarithmic dependence
\log(nk)
in the final rate. Their trick was to consider a random variable
R
that is only dependent on the parameters (or hypothesis)
W_{t}
and condition on this random variable as described in
Remark

4.1
. In this case, the randomness was chosen to be the trajectory of batches used for each of the steps in (
41
), that is,
R=V^{T}
. In this way, the mutual information
\mathrm{I}(W_{t};Z_{i}|W_{t-1},V^{T}=v^{T})
would only be included in the final summation for the iterations
t\in[T]
in which
i\in v_{T}
, which are denoted as
\mathcal{T}_{i}(v^{T})
. This observation led to the following result.

Proposition 4.8

(
[
7
, Proposition 3, modified]
)
.

Consider an
L
-Lipschitz continuous loss. Then, the single-letter mutual information
\mathrm{I}(W_{T};Z_{i})
for the SGLD algorithm for a particular set of batches
v^{T}
is bounded by

\mathrm{I}(W_{T};Z_{i}|V^{T}=v^{T})\leq\frac{d}{2}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{i}(v^{T})}\log\left(1+\frac{\eta_{t}^{2}L^{2}}{d\sigma_{t}^{2}}\right).

Under the same assumptions from before, that is, the noise variance be
\sigma_{t}^{2}=\eta_{t}
, the algorithm runs for
k
epochs so that
T\approx\nicefrac{{nk}}{{|v|}}
, and the step size decrease as
\eta_{t}=\nicefrac{{c}}{{t}}
for some constant
c>0
, it follows from
Proposition

4.8
that

\mathrm{I}(W_{T};Z_{i}|V^{T}=v^{T})\leq\frac{L^{2}c}{2}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}(v^{T})}\frac{1}{t}.

Under the assumption that each sample is only used once per epoch, combining this bound on the individual mutual information with a slow-rate single-letter bound like
Theorem

4.10
ensures that the generalization error rate is in
\mathcal{O}(L\sqrt{\nicefrac{{\log k}}{{n}}})
, which improves the previous analysis by a factor of
\mathcal{O}(\log(\frac{n}{|v|}))

[
7
]
, with the maximal benefit for a batch size of
|v|=1
. The key is to take the expectation with respect to the random indices outside of the square root for bounded or sub-Gaussian losses. Unfortunately, this technique does not offer a benefit with respect to the prior analysis when using fast-rate bounds since

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(W_{T};Z_{i}|V^{T})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{L^{2}c}{2n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\frac{1}{i}+\sum_{t=1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{nt}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{L^{2}c}{2n}\big(\log(n)+1+\log(k-1)+2\big)\in\mathcal{O}\left(L^{2}\cdot\frac{\log nk}{n}\right).

17.1.2
Random-Subset Bounds

Negrea et al. [8]
considered a similar analysis to the one from
Pensia et al. [166]
and
Bu et al. [7]
. The main difference is that they considered a version of the random-subset bound from
Theorem

4.17
with
m=1
. The main object to consider in this bound is the relative entropy
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S,R}\|\mathbb{Q}(S^{-J},R))
. The main appeal to considering this object instead of the mutual information or the single-letter mutual information is that it is data-dependent. To simplify the notation, let
\mathbb{Q}_{W_{T}}^{S^{-J},R}=\mathbb{Q}(S^{-J},R)
be the distribution of a random variable
W_{T}^{\prime}
.
Negrea et al. [8]
’s analysis also begins considering the
monotonicity
and the
chain rules
of the relative entropy from
Proposition

2.3
. More precisely

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W_{T}}^{S,V^{T}}

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{Q}_{W_{T}}^{S^{-J},V^{T}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W^{T}}^{S,V^{T}}\|\mathbb{Q}_{W^{T}}^{S^{-J},V^{T}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W_{0}}^{S,V^{T}}\|\mathbb{Q}_{W_{0}}^{S^{-J}V^{T}})+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W_{t}}^{W^{t-1},S,V^{T}}\|\mathbb{Q}_{W_{t}}^{W^{t-1},S^{-J},V^{T}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W_{t}}^{W_{t-1},S,V^{T}}\|\mathbb{Q}_{W_{t}}^{W_{t-1},S^{-J},V^{T}}),

where we also considered the randomness
R
to be the trajectory of batches used for each of the steps in (
41
). The last inequality follows by choosing
\mathbb{Q}_{W_{0}}^{S^{-J},V^{T}}=\mathbb{P}_{W_{0}}
since
W_{0}
is independent of the data, making the relative entropy zero; and also choosing
\mathbb{Q}_{W_{t}}^{W^{t-1},S^{-J},V^{T}}
to only depend on the past iterate.

In this way, one may focus on bounding the relative entropy
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W_{t}}^{W_{t-1},S,V^{T}}\|\mathbb{Q}_{W_{t}}^{W_{t-1},S^{-J},V^{T}})
by designing an appropriate data-depending distribution
\mathbb{Q}_{W_{t}}^{W_{t-1},S^{-J},V^{T}}
. For this endeavor, consider
W_{t}
and
W_{t}^{\prime}
be the random variables distributed according to
\mathbb{P}_{W_{t}}^{W_{t-1},S,V^{T}}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{W_{t}}^{W_{t-1},S_{-J},V^{T}}
for some fixed
J=j
,
S^{-J}=s^{-j}
, and
V^{T}=v^{T}
. The random variable
W_{t}
is Gaussian with mean

w_{t-1}-\eta_{t}\nabla_{w}\widehat{{R}}(w_{t-1},s_{v_{t}})

(42)

and covariance
\sigma_{t}I_{d}
. Similarly, as in the analysis from
Bu et al. [7]
, when the index
j
is not chosen in the batch
v_{t}
, we may choose
W_{t}^{\prime}
to be equal to
W_{t}
, thus having a zero relative entropy. When,
j
belongs to
v_{t}
, a sensible choice for the design random variable
W_{t}^{\prime}
is another Gaussian with mean

W^{\prime}=w_{t-1}-\eta_{t}\Big(\frac{|v|-1}{|v|}\nabla_{w}\widehat{{R}}(w_{t-1},s_{v_{t}\setminus\{j\}})+\frac{1}{|v|}\nabla_{w}\widehat{{R}}(w_{t-1},s^{-j})\Big)

(43)

and also with covariance
\sigma_{t}I_{d}
, where
|v|=|v_{t}|
is the size of the batch, which is assumed to be equal for all iterations. In this way, as the relative entropy between Gaussian distributions is known, we have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W_{t}}^{W_{t-1},S,V^{T}}

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{Q}_{W_{t}}^{W_{t-1},S^{-J},V^{T}})=

\displaystyle\frac{\eta_{t}^{2}}{2|v|^{2}\sigma_{t}^{2}}\Big\lVert\nabla_{w}\ell(W_{t-1},Z_{J})-\nabla_{w}\widehat{{R}}(W_{t-1},S^{-j})\Big\rVert_{2}^{2}.

Negrea et al. [8]
defined the random variable
\Gamma_{t}\coloneqq\nabla_{w}\ell(W_{t-1},Z_{J})-\nabla_{w}\widehat{{R}}(W_{t-1},S^{-j})
as the
gradient incoherence
, which measures how different the gradient at a sample is from the average gradient on all the other samples. This quantity can be much smaller than the gradient norms or a Lipschitz constant. This is often the case, as confirmed empirically in
[
8
]
. Combining the particularization of
Theorem

4.17
for
m=1
with the above analysis results in the following result.

Theorem 4.26

(
Negrea et al. [8, Theorem 3.1]
)
.

Consider a loss with a range bounded in
[a,b]
. Then, the expected generalization error of SGLD is bounded from above by

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W_{T};S)\big]\leq\frac{b-a}{\sqrt{2}|v|}\mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{J}(V^{T})}\frac{\eta_{t}^{2}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\mathbb{E}^{S^{-J},J,V^{T}}\big[\lVert\Gamma_{t}\rVert_{2}^{2}\big]}\right]

Theorem

4.26
has essentially the same form as the generalization bound obtained by combining
Proposition

4.8
and
Theorem

4.10
for a loss with a bounded range. The main difference is the appearance of the gradient incoherence instead of the norm of the gradients or the Lipschitz constant. Therefore, assuming that the loss is
L
-Lipschitz results again in a bound in
\mathcal{O}(\frac{L}{|v|}\sqrt{\nicefrac{{\log k}}{{n}}})
with the assumption that the noise variance is
\sigma_{t}^{2}=\eta_{t}
, that the algorithm runs for
k
epochs and
T\approx\nicefrac{{kn}}{{b}}
, and that the step size decreases as
\eta_{t}=\nicefrac{{c}}{{t}}
for some constant
c>0
. Moreover, it has the extra benefit of having the
\nicefrac{{1}}{{|v|}}
factor. However, note that for larger batch sizes
|v|
, one typically needs a larger number of epochs
k
.
17
17

17

Other, slightly improved rates can be obtained with different choices of the learning rate
\eta_{t}

[
8
, Appendix E]
. This is, however, outside of the scope of this exposition.

Haghifam et al. [156]
and we
[
157
]
continued this reasoning considering the randomized-subsample setting and bounds similar to
Theorem

4.18
with
m=1
.
Haghifam et al. [156]
provided a result valid for full batch SGLD, also called just Langevin dynamics (LD). We extended such a result for the general SGLD and improved it to be resilient to losses with large gradient norms or large Lipschitz constants.

The main object in
Theorem

4.18
is the relative entropy
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U,R}\|\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S},U^{-J},R))
. The main advantage of this quantity with respect to the quantity considered by
Negrea et al. [8]
is that now, for our analysis, we have access to all the super samples
\tilde{S}
and the only information missing in the design Markov kernel
\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S},U^{-J},R))
is the index
J
. As we will see shortly, this extra knowledge will help us to design better random variables associated with the data-dependent distributions.

As previously, let
\mathbb{Q}_{W_{T}}^{\tilde{S},U^{-J},R}=\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S},U^{-J},R))
be the distribution of a random variable
W_{T}^{\prime}
to simplify the notation. By the
monotonicity
and the
chain rules
of the relative entropy from
Proposition

2.3

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{W_{T}}^{\tilde{S},U,V^{T}}\|\mathbb{Q}_{W_{T}}^{\tilde{S},U^{-J},V^{T}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{WT}^{\tilde{S},U,V^{T}}\|\mathbb{Q}_{W^{T}}^{\tilde{S},U^{-J},V^{T}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W_{0}}^{\tilde{S},U,V^{T}}\|\mathbb{Q}_{W_{0}}^{\tilde{S},U^{-J},V^{T}})+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W_{t}}^{W_{t-1},\tilde{S},U,V^{T}}\|\mathbb{Q}_{W_{t}}^{W^{t-1},\tilde{S},U^{-J},V^{T}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{J}(V^{T})}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W_{t}}^{W_{t-1},\tilde{S},U,V^{T}}\|\mathbb{Q}_{W_{t}}^{W^{t-1},\tilde{S},U^{-J},V^{T}}),

where similarly to before we considered the randomness
R
to be the trajectory of batches used for each of the steps in (
41
), and we chose
\mathbb{Q}_{W_{0}}^{\tilde{S},U^{-J},V^{T}}=\mathbb{P}_{W_{0}}
due to the independence of
W
with the data and the indices. Moreover, we directly noted that we can choose
\mathbb{Q}_{W_{t}}^{W^{t-1},\tilde{S},U^{-J},V^{T}}=\mathbb{P}_{W_{t}}^{W_{t-1},\tilde{S},U,V^{T}}
for the iterations where
J
is not included in the batch since, at those iterations,
W_{t}
is independent of
\tilde{Z}_{J,0}
and
\tilde{Z}_{J,1}
given
W_{t-1}
. Finally, the main change to previous analyses is that we kept the information of all previous hypotheses available in the design Markov kernels.

Now we may focus on the terms
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W_{t}}^{W_{t-1},\tilde{S},U,V^{T}}\|\mathbb{Q}_{W_{t}}^{W^{t-1},\tilde{S},U^{-J},V^{T}})
separately. Consider, as before,
W_{t}
and
W_{t}^{\prime}
be the random variables distributed according to
\mathbb{P}_{W_{t}}^{W_{t-1},\tilde{S},U,V^{T}}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{W_{t}}^{W^{t-1},\tilde{S},U^{-J},V^{T}}
for some fixed
J=j
,
\tilde{S}=s
,
U^{-J}=u^{-j}
, and
V^{T}=v^{T}
. We already established that
W_{t}
is a Gaussian random variable with mean (
42
) and covariance
\sigma_{t}^{2}I_{d}
. However, it is useful to re-write its mean using
u_{j}
and
\tilde{z}_{j,0}
and
\tilde{z}_{j,1}
, namely the mean is

\displaystyle w_{t-1}-\frac{\eta_{t}}{|v|}\Big[(|v|-1)

\displaystyle\nabla_{w}\widehat{{R}}(w_{t-1},s_{v_{t}\setminus\{j\}})+

\displaystyle(1-u_{j})\nabla_{w}\ell(w_{t-1},\tilde{z}_{j,0})+u_{j}\nabla_{w}\ell(w_{t-1},\tilde{z}_{j,1})\Big],

where
|v|=|v_{t}|
is the size of the batch, which is assumed to be constant for all iterations.

A first approach to designing the random variable
W_{t}^{\prime}
, proposed by
Haghifam et al. [156]
in the context of LD, is to design
W_{t}^{\prime}
also as a Gaussian random variable with the same covariance as
W_{t}
and a mean as close as possible to the mean of
W_{t}
. Since
U_{j}
is unknown, we can substitute it by an estimate
\pi_{j,t}
of the probability that
U_{j}=1
. This estimator uses the information available at that iteration, namely all the data
\tilde{s}
, all the other indices
u^{-j}
, all the previous weights
w^{t-1}
, and all the indices of samples employed up to (and including) that iteration
v^{t}
. In the end of this subsection, we will show an example of such an estimator. For the time being, it suffices to understand that it will start with no information and
\pi_{j,0}=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
, and it will become closer to
U_{j}
as the iterations advance if the information of the index
U_{j}
leaks into the weights
w^{t}
. In this way, the mean of
W_{t}^{\prime}
is

\displaystyle w_{t-1}-\frac{\eta_{t}}{|v|}\Big[(|v|-1)

\displaystyle\nabla_{w}\widehat{{R}}(w_{t-1},s_{v_{t}\setminus\{j\}})+

\displaystyle(1-\pi_{j,t})\nabla_{w}\ell(w_{t-1},\tilde{z}_{j,0})+\pi_{j,t}\nabla_{w}\ell(w_{t-1},\tilde{z}_{j,1})\Big].

Finally, since the relative entropy between Gaussian distributions is known we have that

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W_{t}}^{W_{t-1},\tilde{S},U,V^{T}}\|\mathbb{Q}_{W_{t}}^{W^{t-1},\tilde{S},U^{-J},V^{T}})=\frac{\eta^{2}}{2|v|^{2}\sigma^{2}}\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{S},U,V^{T},J}\Big[(U_{J}-\pi_{J,t})^{2}\lVert\Gamma_{J,t}\rVert_{2}^{2}\Big],

where
\Gamma_{J,t}\coloneqq\nabla_{w}\ell(w_{t-1},\tilde{z}_{j,0})-\nabla_{w}(w_{t-1},\tilde{j,1})
is the
two-sample incoherence
at iteration
t
. Now, combining the particularization of
Theorem

4.18
for
m=1
with the above analysis results in the following result.

Theorem 4.27
.

Consider a loss with a range bounded in
[a,b]
. Then, the expected generalization error of SGLD is bounded from above by

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W_{T},S)]\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}(b-a)}{|v|}\mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{J}(V^{T})}\frac{\eta_{t}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{t}}\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{S},U,V^{T},J}\Big[(U_{J}-\pi_{J,t})^{2}\lVert\Gamma_{J,t}\rVert_{2}^{2}\Big]}\right].

This result is similar to
Theorem

4.26
. The incoherence terms
\Gamma_{t}
and
\Gamma_{J,t}
are similar and both represent how the gradient of a sample differs from other gradients. The main innovation is the addition of
(U_{j}-\pi_{J,t})^{2}
, which takes into account how well one can predict which sample is used in the training set at iteration
t
. In this way, samples
j
that are very informative at early iterations, do not contribute negatively to the generalization error in later iterations as
(U_{j}-\pi_{j,t})^{2}
will be close to zero. On the other hand, if a sample is close to indistinguishable, then
\pi\approx 0.5
and
(U_{j}-\pi_{j,t})^{2}\approx\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
, essentially recovering
Theorem

4.26
.

Even with these advances, in the early iterations, the estimator
\pi_{j,t}
is still not a good estimate of the generalization error. In this case, if the gradient incoherence is large, then the bound may be affected by this. As an alternative, we proposed to design
W_{t}^{\prime}
as a random variable distributed as a mixture of two Gaussian distributions with means

\displaystyle\mu_{j,t,0}=

\displaystyle w_{t-1}-\frac{\eta_{t}}{|v|}\Big[(|v|-1)\nabla_{w}\widehat{{R}}(w_{t-1},s_{v_{t}\setminus\{j\}})+\nabla_{w}\ell(w_{t-1},\tilde{z}_{j,0})\Big]\textnormal{ and}

\displaystyle\mu_{j,t,1}=

\displaystyle w_{t-1}-\frac{\eta_{t}}{|v|}\Big[(|v|-1)\nabla_{w}\widehat{{R}}(w_{t-1},s_{v_{t}\setminus\{j\}})+\nabla_{w}\ell(w_{t-1},\tilde{z}_{j,1})\Big]

and with weights, or responsibilities
(1-\pi_{j,t})
and
\pi_{j,t}
respectively, that is
W_{t}^{\prime}\sim(1-\pi_{j,t})\mathcal{N}(\mu_{j,t,0},\sigma_{t}^{2}I_{d})+\pi_{j,t}\mathcal{N}(\mu_{j,t,1},\sigma_{t}^{2}I_{d})
. Then, we may employ
Lemma

6.1
to see that
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W_{t}}^{W_{t-1},\tilde{S},U,V^{T}}\|\mathbb{Q}_{W_{t}}^{W^{t-1},\tilde{S},U^{-J},V^{T}})
is bounded from above by

\displaystyle-\log\Bigg[(1-\pi_{J,t})

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{\eta^{2}}{2|v|^{2}\sigma^{2}}\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{S},U,V^{T},J}\Big[U_{J}^{2}\lVert\Gamma_{J,t}\rVert_{2}^{2}\Big]}+

\displaystyle\quad\pi_{J,t}e^{-\frac{\eta^{2}}{2|v|^{2}\sigma^{2}}\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{S},U,V^{T},J}\Big[(1-U_{J})^{2}\lVert\Gamma_{J,t}\rVert_{2}^{2}\Big]}\Bigg].

(44)

We can make this equation more compact if we note that when
U_{J}=0
, then it reduces to

-\log\left[(1-\pi_{J,t})+\pi_{J,t}e^{-\frac{\eta^{2}}{2|v|^{2}\sigma^{2}}\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{S},U,V^{T},J}\Big[(1-U_{J})^{2}\lVert\Gamma_{J,t}\rVert_{2}^{2}\Big]}\right]

and that when
U_{J}=1
, then it reduces to

-\log\left[(1-\pi_{J,t})e^{-\frac{\eta^{2}}{2|v|^{2}\sigma^{2}}\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{S},U,V^{T},J}\Big[U_{J}^{2}\lVert\Gamma_{J,t}\rVert_{2}^{2}\Big]}+\pi_{J,t}\right].

Hence, since
|U_{J}-\pi_{J,t}|
is equal to
\pi_{J,t}
when
U_{J}=0
, and to
(1-\pi_{J,t})
when
U_{J}=1
, we may write the right-hand side of (
44
) as

-\log\left[|U_{J}-\pi_{J,t}|\exp\left(-\frac{\eta_{t}^{2}\lVert\Gamma_{J,t}\lVert^{2}}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}|v|^{2}}\right)+|1-U_{J}-\pi_{J,t}|\right].

Again, combining the particularization of
Theorem

4.18
for
m=1
with the above analysis results in the following result.

Theorem 4.28
.

Consider a loss with a range bounded in
[a,b]
. Then, the expected generalization error of SGLD is bounded from above by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[

\displaystyle\mathrm{gen}(W_{T},S)]\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{2}(b-a)\mathbb{E}\left[-\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{J}(V^{T})}\log\left[|U_{J}-\pi_{J,t}|\exp\left(-\frac{\eta_{t}^{2}\lVert\Gamma_{J,t}\lVert^{2}}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}|v|^{2}}\right)+|1-U_{J}-\pi_{J,t}|\right]\right].

For example, if
\pi_{J,t}=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
, which is a reasonable value during the first iterations, the bound from
Theorem

4.28
is tighter than the one from
Theorem

4.27
for
\|\Gamma_{J,t}\|\gtrsim 2.21\nicefrac{{\sigma_{t}|v|}}{{\eta_{t}}}
. This may likely be the case when
\nicefrac{{\eta_{t}}}{{\sigma_{t}}}\in\Theta(n^{\alpha/2})
for
\alpha\in(0,1)

[
8
, Appendix E]
.
In fact, for large values of
{\frac{\eta_{t}^{2}\|\Gamma_{J,t}\|^{2}}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}|v|^{2}}}
, for instance, when the Lipschitz condition is not met, the terms inside the square root in
Theorem

4.28
tend to
{-}\log|1-U_{J}-\pi_{J,t}|
from below, which is upper bounded by
\log 2
for a random estimator
\pi_{J,t}=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
.

Moreover, we note that the only terms that change between
Theorem

4.27
and
Theorem

4.28
are the summands inside the square root.
These terms are obtained by considering different data-dependent distributions
Q_{\smash{W_{t}|W^{t-1},U_{J^{c}},\tilde{S},V^{t}}}
, which we can choose arbitrarily at each iteration. Hence, we may choose the tightest form for each summand, as it is stated next.

Corollary 4.9
.

Consider a loss with a range bounded in
[a,b]
. Furthermore, consider the random variables

\displaystyle F_{J,t}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta^{2}}{2|v|^{2}\sigma^{2}}\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{S},U,V^{T},J}\Big[(U_{J}-\pi_{J,t})^{2}\lVert\Gamma_{J,t}\rVert_{2}^{2}\Big]\textnormal{ and }

\displaystyle G_{J,t}

\displaystyle=-\log\left[|U_{J}-\pi_{J,t}|\exp\left(-\frac{\eta_{t}^{2}\lVert\Gamma_{J,t}\lVert^{2}}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}|v|^{2}}\right)+|1-U_{J}-\pi_{J,t}|\right].

Then, the expected generalization error of SGLD is bounded from above by

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W_{T},S)\big]\leq\sqrt{2}(b-a)\mathbb{E}\left[\sqrt{\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}_{J}(V^{T})}\min\{F_{J,t},G_{J,t}\}}\right].

To conclude, we may write an explicit estimator
\pi_{J,t}
. An example of how to build such an estimate, based on binary hypothesis testing, is presented in
[
156
]
for LD. Adapted to SGLD, we let the estimate
\pi_{J,t}
be a function
\phi:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow[0,1]
of the log-likelihood ratio between the probability that
U_{J}=1
and
U_{J}=0
, based on
(W^{t-1},J,\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}},V^{t})
. That is,

\displaystyle\pi_{J,t}\coloneqq\phi\left(\log\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\smash{U_{J}|W^{t-1},\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}},V^{t}}}(1)}{\mathbb{P}_{\smash{U_{J}|W^{t-1},\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}},V^{t}}}(0)}\right).

We can calculate this log-likelihood exactly since density functions
\mathsf{p}_{W^{t-1}}^{\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}},V^{t},U=u}
exist and are Gaussian. That is,

\displaystyle\mathsf{p}_{W^{t-1}}^{\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}},V^{t},U=u}(w^{t-1})=\prod_{t^{\prime}=1}^{t-1}\left[\frac{1}{2\pi\sigma_{t}^{2}}\right]^{\frac{d}{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2\sigma_{t}^{2}}Y_{J,t^{\prime},u}\right),

where

Y_{J,t^{\prime},u}\coloneqq\left\lVert w_{t^{\prime}}-w_{t^{\prime}-1}+\frac{\eta_{t}(|v|-1)}{|v|}\nabla_{w}\widehat{{R}}(w_{t^{\prime}-1},s_{v_{t}\setminus\{j\}})+\frac{\eta_{t}}{|v|}\nabla_{w}\ell(w_{t^{\prime}-1},\tilde{z}_{J,U})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.

Therefore, the estimate is

\pi_{J,t}=\phi\left(\sum_{t^{\prime}=1}^{t-1}(Y_{J,t^{\prime},0}-Y_{J,t^{\prime},1})\right).

(45)

Note how
Y_{J,t^{\prime},u}
is close to zero when
U=u
since the only difference between the weights
w_{t^{\prime}}
and the update comes from the inherent randomness
\Xi_{t}
from SGLD. On the other hand
Y_{J,t^{\prime},u}
is larger when
U=1-u
since now the difference comes both from the SGLD randomness
\Xi_{t}
as well as choosing the wrong gradient in the estimate. Hence, the value inside the function
\phi
in (
45
) is expected to be positive when
U=1
and negative when
U=0
with a larger absolute value after each iteration. Then, a good choice for the function
\phi
could be a sigmoid function, which will bring positive values closer to 1 and negative values closer to 0. In fact, the sigmoid function is often used to bring log-likelihood ratios to probabilities.

After these developments, new studies on the generalization error of SGLD using different assumptions and new information-theoretic bounds appeared
[
189
,
190
,
191
]
. Those reached similar results and focused on eliminating the time dependence in their results.

17.2
Beyond SGLD

Pensia et al. [166]
considered the study of the stochastic gradient Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (SGHMC)
[
187
]
. This algorithm is similar to SGLD with the addition of a “velocity” vector to include momentum in the parameter updates. Namely, the parameters
W_{0}\in\mathcal{W}
are still initialized randomly and the velocity parameters are initialized to all zeros
v_{0}\in\mathcal{W}
. After that, at each iteration
t\in[T]
, a random batch
s_{V_{t}}
from the dataset
s
is collected and the parameters are updated according to

\displaystyle V_{t}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{t}V_{t-1}+\eta_{t}\nabla_{w}\widehat{{R}}(W_{t-1},s_{V_{t}})

\displaystyle W_{t}

\displaystyle=W_{t-1}-\alpha_{t}V_{t-1}-\eta_{t}\nabla_{w}\widehat{{R}}(W_{t-1},s_{V_{t}})+\sigma_{t}\Xi_{t}.

Instead of directly studying this algorithm, they considered a slight variant where, at each iteration, the velocity parameters are also perturbed with some noise
\sigma_{t}\Xi^{\prime}_{t}
that is independent of all other random variables. In this way, considering the tuple
(W_{t},V_{t})
as a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, they could replicate their results for SGLD within a factor of
\sqrt{2}
.

After that,
Neu et al. [145]
adapted
Pensia et al. [166]
’s analysis to stochastic gradient descent (SGD). The challenge for studying SGD is that, at each iteration
t\in[T]
, given a fixed dataset
s
, the indices of the batch
v_{t}
and a fixed value of the parameters at the previous iteration
w_{t-1}
, the parameters at that iteration are deterministic and hence their distribution is

\mathbb{P}_{W_{t}}^{S=s,V_{t}=v_{t},W_{t-1}=w_{t-1}}(w)=\mathbb{I}_{\{w=w_{t-1}-\eta_{t}\nabla_{w}\widehat{{R}}(w_{t-1},s_{v_{t}})\}}(w).

Therefore, the relative entropy
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W_{t}}^{S=s,V_{t}=v_{t}W_{t-1}=w_{t-1}}\|\mathbb{P}_{W_{t}}^{V_{t}=v_{t},W_{t-1}=w_{t-1}})
cannot be evaluated.
Neu et al. [145]
circumvented this problem by considering an artificial perturbed trajectory of the weights and analyzing the said trajectory.

To be precise, let
W_{T}
be the final iterate of SGD and consider some noise
\Xi_{T}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{1:T}^{2}I_{d})
. Then, one may construct a noisy surrogate
\tilde{W}_{T}=W_{T}+\Xi_{T}
and write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[

\displaystyle\mathrm{gen}(W_{T},S)\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(\tilde{W}_{T},S)\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[{R}(W_{T})-{R}(\tilde{W}_{T})\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(\tilde{W}_{T},S)+\widehat{{R}}(W_{T},S)\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(\tilde{W}_{T},S)\big]+\mathbb{E}\big[\Delta_{\sigma_{1:T}}(W_{T},S^{\prime})-\Delta_{\sigma_{1:T}}(W_{T},S)\big],

(46)

where
\Delta_{\sigma}(w,s)=\mathbb{E}[\widehat{{R}}(w,s)-\widehat{{R}}(w+\Xi,s)]
with
\Xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma I_{d})
is referred to as the
local value sensitivity
of the loss around
w\in\mathcal{W}
to perturbations at a level
\sigma
. This term evaluates how stable is the loss of the final iterate to perturbations. Intuitively, this term becomes small if SGD outputs a parameter vector in a flat area of the loss surface. This connects with the widely held belief that algorithms that find “wide
optima” of the loss landscape generalize well
[
194
,
195
]
, a hypothesis with contradicting theories
[
196
,
197
,
198
,
199
]
.

The decomposition from (
46
) permits the analysis of the generalization error of the noisy version of the last iterate (or surrogate)
\tilde{W}_{T}
. Due to the fact that if
X
and
Y
are independent Gaussian random variables with variances
\sigma_{x}^{2}
and
\sigma_{y}^{2}
, then
X+Y
is also Gaussian with variance
\sigma_{x}^{2}+\sigma_{y}^{2}
, we may construct an artificial iterative procedure to generate the said surrogate. Namely, the process starts by generating
\tilde{W}_{0}=W_{0}
and then, at each iteration
t\in[T]
, the surrogate parameters are updated with the rule

\tilde{W}_{t}=\tilde{W}_{t-1}-\eta_{t}\nabla_{w}\widehat{{R}}(W_{t-1},S_{V_{t}})+\Upsilon_{t},

where
\Upsilon_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{t}^{2})
. In this case, we can verify that
\tilde{W}_{T}=W_{T}+\Xi
as desired, where
\sigma_{1:T}^{2}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sigma_{t}^{2}
. In this way,
Neu et al. [145]
adapted the techniques from
Pensia et al. [166]
to study the generalization error of the surrogate leading to the following result. Their result depends on the
gradient sensitivity
of the iterations at each step, which they define as

\Gamma_{\sigma}(w)=\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lVert\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{w}\ell(w,Z)]-\mathbb{E}^{\Xi}[\nabla_{w}\ell(w+\Xi,Z)]\right\rVert^{2}\right]

and which measures the sensitivity of the gradients of the expected loss function to perturbations around
w
; and the
gradient variance
of the loss of the iterations at each step, which they define as

V_{t}(w)=\mathbb{E}^{W_{t}=w}\left[\left\lVert\nabla_{w}\widehat{{R}}(w,S_{V_{t}})-\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{w}\ell(w,Z)]\right\rVert^{2}\right].

Theorem 4.29

(
Neu et al. [145, Theorem 1, adapted to bounded losses]
)
.

Consider a loss with a bounded range in
[a,b]
. For every sequence of positive numbers
(\sigma_{t}^{2})_{t=1}^{T}
, let
\sigma_{1:t}^{2}=\sum_{t^{\prime}=1}^{t}\sigma_{t}^{2}
. Then, the generalization of SGD is bounded by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W_{T},S)\big]\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{(b-a)^{2}}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\frac{\eta_{t}^{2}}{\sigma_{t}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\big[\Gamma_{\sigma_{1:t}}(W_{t})+V_{t}(W_{t})\big]}

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\big[\Delta_{\sigma_{1:T}}(W_{T},S^{\prime})-\Delta_{\sigma_{1:T}}(W_{T},S)\big].

This result was celebrated because it provided the first information-theoretic analysis of SGD, and gave us insights into some of the elements that contributed to its generalization performance. For example, consider a fixed learning rate
\eta_{t}=\eta
, a fixed batch size
|V_{t}|=|v|
, and assume that the batches are chosen so that each sample
i
is chosen in a batch exactly once. Consider a fixed noise parameter
\sigma=\sigma_{t}
. Assume that the gradient variance
\mathbb{E}\big[\lVert\nabla_{w}\ell(w,Z)-\mathbb{E}\big[\nabla_{w}\ell(w,Z)\big]\rVert^{2}\big]
is bounded by
\nu
and that the loss is globally
\mu
-smooth in the sense that
\lVert\nabla_{w}\ell(w,z)-\nabla_{w}(w+u,z)\rVert\leq\mu\lVert u\rVert
for all
w,u\in\mathcal{W}
and all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
. Then,
Theorem

4.29
implies that

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W_{T},S)\big]\in\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{(b-a)^{2}\eta^{2}T}{n}\left(\mu^{2}dT+\frac{\nu}{|v|\sigma^{2}}\right)+\mu\sigma^{2}dT\right).

(47)

The rate becomes better as the number of iterations
T
or the learning rate
\eta
are decreased, which may decrease the training performance. Also, the rate gets better as
|v|
increases until the term
\mu^{2}dT
dominates the expression. The noise parameter
\sigma
has a complicated trade-off in the expression: smaller values improve the terms coming from the loss sensitivity, while larger values improve those coming from the variance to noise ratio
\nicefrac{{\nu}}{{\sigma^{2}}}
. Starting from (
47
),
Neu et al. [145]
discussed the achievable rates for two different, common settings:

•

Small batch SGD
. In this setting,
T\in\mathcal{O}(n)
and
|v|\in\mathcal{O}(1)
. The first observation they make is that choosing
\eta\in\mathcal{O}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})
could not guarantee a vanishing generalization error, even though it is known it does
[
118
,
120
]
. However, choosing
\eta\in\mathcal{O}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}})
and the noise
\sigma\in\mathcal{O}(n^{-\nicefrac{{4}}{{3}}})
still guarantees that
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)]\in\mathcal{O}(n^{-\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}}})
.

•

Large batch SGD
. In this setting,
T\in\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{n})
and
b\in\Omega(\sqrt{n})
. Choosing the learning rate
\eta\in\mathcal{O}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})
and the noise parameter
\sigma\in\Theta(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})
guarantees that
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)]\in\mathcal{O}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})
.

Although the rates depend on the dimension
d
and are not tight, especially for small-batch SGD, they show the promise that an information-theoretic analysis can shed light on the problem with more refined analyses. After that, further analyses of SGD appeared
[
192
,
170
]
, improving the analysis but still not achieving tight rates.

18
Further Advances in Bounds Using Information Measures

Both the mutual information
\mathrm{I}(W;S)
in the standard setting and the conditional mutual information
\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})
in the randomized-subsample setting depend on the joint distribution of the algorithm’s output and other variables. In contrast, the generalization error depends on the algorithm’s output only through the losses it incurs. Therefore, it is possible to increase both these mutual information and conditional mutual information by
embedding
information about the training set in the output of a learning algorithm without affecting the algorithm’s statistical properties
[
135
,
134
]
. As a remedy,
Steinke and Zakynthinou [9]
propose an alternative framework, the
evaluated
conditional mutual information, that considers the information about the data captured by the
incurred loss
rather than the output itself.

Consider the indices
U\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and the supersample
\tilde{S}\in\mathcal{Z}^{n\times 2}
from the randomized-subsample setting of
Section

14
. Then, define the loss vector
\mathbf{L}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times 2}
as the array with entries
\mathbf{L}_{i,u}=\ell(W,\tilde{Z}_{i,u})
for all
i\in[n]
and all
u\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, where
W
is the output of the algorithm. The
evaluated conditional mutual information
is defined as the conditional mutual information of the loss vector
\mathbf{L}
and the indices
U
given the supersample
\tilde{S}
, that is
\mathrm{I}(\mathbf{L};U|\tilde{S})
. Intuitively, this describes how much information does the output of the algorithm have about the identities of the samples used for training. Moreover, since for a given supersample
\tilde{S}
, the Markov chain
U-W-\mathbf{L}|\tilde{S}
holds, by the data processing inequality from
Proposition

2.4
we have that
\mathrm{I}(\mathbf{L};U|\tilde{S})\leq\mathrm{I}(W;U|\tilde{S})

[
9
]
.

Steinke and Zakynthinou [9]
proved both slow-rate and fast-rate upper bounds on the expected generalization error for losses with a bounded range based on this information measure. Later,
Haghifam et al. [167]
showed that these bounds can provide a sharp characterization of generalization in the realizable setting with the 0–1 loss.
Hellström and Durisi [12]
extended these results proving bounds for the evaluated conditional mutual information similar to
Lemma

4.2
, and also proved that bounds featuring could guarantee the generalization of classes with a bounded Natarajan dimension
\mathrm{Ndim}(\mathcal{W})
, thus recovering the classical results from
Section

7.2
. That is, if a hypothesis class has a finite Natarajan dimension, then

\mathrm{I}(\mathbf{L};U|\tilde{S})\leq\mathrm{Ndim}(\mathcal{W})\log\left(\binom{|\mathcal{Y}|}{2}\frac{2en}{\mathrm{Ndim}(\mathcal{W})}\right)

and therefore the generalization error bounds vanish as the number of samples increases.
After that, in
[
136
]
, we extended the slow-rate bounds from
[
9
]
to Lipschitz losses.

Theorem 4.30
.

Consider an
L
-Lipschitz loss function
\ell(\cdot,z)
with respect to some metric
\rho
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
. Further consider a bounded space
\mathcal{W}
with
\mathrm{diam}_{\rho}(\mathcal{W})=B
. Then,

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq LB\sqrt{\frac{8\mathrm{I}(\mathbf{L};U|\tilde{S})}{n}}.

The theorem follows, like most of the previous results, from the decoupling based on the Donsker and Varadhan
Lemma

2.1
from
Lemma

4.4
. In this case, let
X=(\mathbf{L},\tilde{S},U)
and
Y=(\mathbf{L}^{\prime},\tilde{S},U)
, where
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}
is a copy of
\mathbf{L}
independent of
U
such that
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{L}^{\prime},\tilde{S},U}=\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{L},\tilde{S}}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{U}
. Furthermore, let the function
f
from the lemma be

f(\mathbf{l},\tilde{s},u)=\frac{\lambda}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\mathbf{l}_{i,1-u}-\mathbf{l}_{i,u}\big).

Therefore, with these definitions we can see how
\mathbb{E}[f(\mathbf{L},\tilde{S},U)]=\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)]
and
\mathbb{E}[f(\mathbf{L}^{\prime},\tilde{S},U)]=0
. Hence, according to
Lemma

4.4
we have that

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\mathrm{I}(\mathbf{L},\tilde{S};U)-\log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\lambda}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\mathbf{L}^{\prime}_{i,1-U_{i}}-\mathbf{L}^{\prime}_{i,U_{i}}\big)}\right]\right),

where we noted that
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{L},\tilde{S},U}\lVert\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{L},\tilde{S}}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{U})=\mathrm{I}(\mathbf{L},\tilde{S};U)
and where we wrote the CGF explicitly.

Then, we can see how by the independence of the supersamples and the indices and the
chain rules
of the mutual information
\mathrm{I}(\mathbf{L},\tilde{S};U)=\mathrm{I}(\tilde{S};U)+\mathrm{I}(\mathbf{L};U|\tilde{S})=\mathrm{I}(\mathbf{L};U|\tilde{S})
.
Finally, the theorem follows by noting how, for all
i\in[n]
, the random variables
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}_{i,1-U_{i}}-\mathbf{L}^{\prime}_{i,U_{i}}
are independent of each other and have a range bounded in
[-2LB,2LB]
. Since they have a bounded range, we may note that they are also
2(LB)^{2}
-sub-Gaussian and then we may proceed like we did in
Theorems

4.1
and
4.8
.

To prove that these random variables have a bounded range, note that we may re-write the random variables as

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}^{\prime}_{i,1-U_{i}}-\mathbf{L}^{\prime}_{i,U_{i}}

\displaystyle=\ell(W^{\prime},\tilde{Z}_{i,1-U_{i}})-\ell(W^{\prime},\tilde{Z}_{i,U_{i}})

\displaystyle=\big(\ell(W^{\prime},\tilde{Z}_{i,1-U_{i}})-\ell(w,\tilde{Z}_{i,1-U_{i}})\big)+\big(\ell(W^{\prime},\tilde{Z}_{i,U_{i}})-\ell(W^{\prime},\tilde{Z}_{i,U_{i}})\big),

where
W^{\prime}
is a random variable such that, for a fixed supersample
\tilde{S}=s
and some fixed indices
U=u
is distributed according to the distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\prime\tilde{S}=\tilde{s},U=u}=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S}=\tilde{s}}
, and where
w
is any fixed hypothesis. Then, since the loss is
L
-Lipschitz and the hypothesis space has diameter
B
, by the triangle inequality

|\mathbf{L}^{\prime}_{i,1-U_{i}}-\mathbf{L}^{\prime}_{i,U_{i}}|\leq 2L\rho(W^{\prime},w)\leq 2LB,

and therefore
\mathbf{L}^{\prime}_{i,1-U_{i}}-\mathbf{L}^{\prime}_{i,U_{i}}\in[-2LB,2LB]
.

Further developments came later, when
Wang and Mao [169]
combined the ideas from the evaluated conditional mutual information from
[
9
]
above, with the single-letter bounds from
[
7
,
157
]
of
Section

15.1
, and the Wasserstein distance bounds from
[
180
,
165
]
of
Section

16
to find bounds based on the
loss difference
. To be precise, they considered the loss vector
\mathbf{L}
from above and defined the loss difference
\Delta\mathbf{L}_{i}
as the difference of the losses on the
i
-th coordinate of the loss vector, that is,
\Delta\mathbf{L}_{i}\coloneqq\mathbf{L}_{i,0}-\mathbf{L}_{i,1}
. In this way, they proved generalization bounds based on the average of the mutual information between each loss difference
\Delta\mathbf{L}_{i}
and the index
U_{i}
given the supersamples. That is, they showed that

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(\Delta\mathbf{L}_{i};U_{i}|\tilde{S}),

among other similar bounds. Again, since given the supersample
\tilde{S}
, the Markov chain
U_{i}-(\mathbf{L}_{i,0},\mathbf{L}_{i,1})-\Delta\mathbf{L}_{i}|\tilde{S}
holds, this information measure is smaller than the previous ones, rendering the bounds tighter. Using these bounds,
Wang and Mao [169]
could also relate a notion of “sharpness” to generalization, finding similar results to those specific to SGD found by
Neu et al. [145]
and
Wang and Mao [192]
that we saw in
Section

17.2
.

Another advance from
Wang and Mao [170]
came by combining stability notions similar to those in
Section

10.1
with the mutual information bounds that we have seen throughout this chapter. Simplifying their results, they obtained bonds of the type
\gamma\cdot\mathrm{IM}
, where
\gamma
represented a stability parameter similar to those in
Section

10.1
, and
\mathrm{IM}
represents an information measure. In this way, they unified both frameworks, when the uniform stability framework yields a valid generalization for a problem, the parameter
\gamma
ensures it as
\mathrm{IM}\leq c
for some
c
; while when the information-theoretic generalization framework yields a valid generalization for a problem, then
\mathrm{IM}
ensures a vanishing rate. The only problem with this framework is that it is complicated, which considers
n+1
hypotheses generated with different combinations of the supersample and uses them to define both the stability parameter and the information measure.

Remark 4.3
.

A similar approach was taken by
Harutyunyan et al. [168]
, where instead of the evaluated conditional mutual information, they considered the
functional conditional mutual information
. Consider a supervised learning setting where
\mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
and a parameterized model
f_{w}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
. Then, as discussed in
Section

6
, the loss function may be written as
\ell(w,z)=\rho(f_{w}(x),z)
for some measure of dissimilarity between the predicted
f_{w}(x)
and the true
y
target associated with the feature
x
. In this setup, for an output hypothesis
W
,
Harutyunyan et al. [168]
considered the
functional vector
to be the random variable
\mathbf{F}\in\mathcal{Y}^{n\times 2}
with entries
\mathbf{F}_{i,u}=f_{W}(\tilde{X}_{i,u})
for all
i\in[n]
and all
u\in\{0,1\}
, where
\tilde{X}_{i,u}
is the feature of the instance
\tilde{Z}_{i,u}
. Note that given a supersample
\tilde{S}
, the Markov chain
U-\mathbf{F}-\mathbf{L}|\tilde{S}
, and therefore the functional conditional mutual information is looser than the evaluated mutual information and every generalization error bound using the latter can also be employed with the former.

Finally, a different line of work considered the chaining technique to bound the suprema of random processes
[
84
, Chapter 5]
to bound the generalization error
[
171
,
172
]
. In this framework, the authors consider that
\{\mathrm{gen}(w,S)\}_{w\in\mathcal{W}}
is a separable, sub-Gaussian process in the metric space
(\mathcal{W},\rho)
. Similarly to the separability of a space from
Section

3.2
, the separability assumption in a process requires that for every
w\in\mathcal{W}
, there is a countable subset
\mathcal{W}_{0}\subseteq W
such that
\mathrm{gen}(w,S)=\lim_{w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}_{0}\to w}\mathrm{gen}(w^{\prime}S)
almost surely. The sub-Gaussian assumption is a statistical relaxation of the Lipschitzness assumption and requires that the generalization error does not vary much for close hypotheses, that is, that for all
\lambda>0
and all
w,w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}
the following holds:

\log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\lambda\big(\mathrm{gen}(w,S)-\mathrm{gen}(w^{\prime},S)\big)}\right]\leq\frac{\lambda^{2}\rho(w,w^{\prime})^{2}}{2}.

Before showing their results, let us recall what an
\varepsilon
-net is: A set
\mathcal{N}
is called an
\varepsilon
-net
for the metric space
(\mathcal{W},\rho)
if, for every
w\in\mathcal{W}
, there is a map
\pi(w)\in\mathcal{N}
such that
\rho(w,\pi(w))\leq\varepsilon
.

With these assumptions,
Asadi et al. [171]
showed that the expected generalization error is bounded from above by

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq 3\sqrt{2}\sum_{k=k_{0}+1}^{\infty}2^{-k}\sqrt{\mathrm{I}(W_{k};S)},

(48)

where
W_{k}
are finer and finer quantizations of the hypothesis such that
W_{k}=\pi_{k}(W)
. To be precise, the quantizations were built by considering a series of
2^{-k}
-nets of the hypothesis space with an associated mapping
\pi_{k}:\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{N}_{k}
restricted to the form
\pi_{k}=\pi_{k}^{\prime}\circ\pi_{k+1}
, where
\pi_{k}^{\prime}:\mathcal{N}_{k+1}\to\mathcal{N}_{k}
. The initial net
\mathcal{N}_{k_{0}}
is constructed such that
2^{-k_{0}}\geq\mathrm{diam}_{\rho}(\mathcal{W})
and therefore the mutual information for that coarse quantization is zero, that is
\mathrm{I}(W_{k_{0}};S)=0
.

With these developments, the intention was to simultaneously exploit the geometrical dependencies of the hypothesis and the statistical dependencies between the hypothesis and the training data, similarly to our results from
Section

16
. Although the bound from (
48
) eludes the problems of the absolute continuity from the original mutual information bound from
Xu and Raginsky [5]
in
Theorem

4.1
, its complicated nature makes it difficult to apply compared to the other, posterior alternatives presented above.

19
Limitations of the Bounds Using Mutual Information

As discussed in
Section

11
and seen throughout this monograph, information-theoretic bounds are, by their nature, distribution- and algorithm-dependent. As seen in
Section

17
, these key properties enable the bounds from this framework to achieve numerically non-vacuous generalization guarantees for SGLD with modern deep-learning tasks and architectures. For SGD, they can also obtain non-vacuous guarantees after employing a surrogate. Moreover, as shown in
Theorem

4.25
from
Section

16.2
, these bounds are shown to be tight for Lipschitz losses and bounded hypothesis spaces, in the sense that they cannot be improved
simultaneously
for all algorithms. It is natural to wonder, then, if these techniques can ever achieve the minimax rates. That is, if they can guarantee the generalization of whole hypothesis classes.

Bassily et al. [134]
showed that the mutual information bound from
Xu and Raginsky [5]
of
Theorem

4.1
provably fail to characterize the learnability of hypothesis classes with a finite VC dimension, even though they are known to generalize as seen in
Section

7.2
. However, as discussed above,
Haghifam et al. [167]
and
Hellström and Durisi [144]
, showed, respectively, that the evaluated conditional mutual information from
Section

18
(i) achieves the minimax rate for binary classification in the realizable setting, and that (ii) it recovers the generalization guarantees for hypothesis classes with a bounded Natarajan dimension from
Section

7.2
.

In light of these exciting advancements, in
[
136
]
, we studied if the bounds based on mutual information and conditional mutual information-based bounds from
Sections

16.2
and
18
, despite being tight for non trivial problems, cannot achieve the minimax rates for convex, Lipschitz losses with a bounded parameter space. To do so, we considered the gradient descent (GD) algorithm, since it is known to generalize in this setting at a rate in
\mathcal{O}(\nicefrac{{LB}}{{\sqrt{n}}})
. Then, we showed that there is a convex, Lipschitz loss and a data distribution for which all of these bounds have a rate in
\Omega(1)
, proving that they cannot achieve the desired minimax rate. Furthermore, we also prove that considering a Gaussian surrogate as in (
46
) from
Section

17.2
still fails, casting doubt on this approach to enhance this framework.

After this result,
Livni [137]
and
Attias et al. [200]
continued this line of research proving a stronger statement. Namely, they show that there is a convex, Lipschitz function such that, for each of the mutual information and conditional mutual information bounds from
Section

16.2
, for every algorithm that generalizes, there exists a data distribution under which the bounds are loose. In other words, for every algorithm that generalizes there exists a data distribution for which the existing bounds will fail. These results are based on the “fingerprinting lemma”
[
201
,
177
,
202
]
from the differential privacy community and differ from our proof techniques.

Our proofs for the failure of GD with Gaussian surrogates are quite technical and do not provide further insights, except from the result itself, which is now a corollary of the results in
[
137
,
200
]
. Therefore, in light of these new developments, we restrict the rest of the section to our proofs of the failure of these bounds to characterize the generalization of GD. Although some of these results are also included in the theorems from
[
137
,
200
]
, the construction of the counterexample is still useful. First, it showcases how the information of the data can be leaked into the algorithm. This pathological construction can be useful for future privacy research. Moreover, our negative results for GD also extend to the evaluated (and therefore also to the functional) conditional mutual information bound from
Theorem

4.30
, which is, at the moment, still not covered by the fingerprinting results from
[
137
,
200
]
.

Remark 4.4
.

An open question to both our results and those from
[
135
,
200
]
is if the new bounds from
Wang and Mao [169]
, which include a stability parameter
\gamma
, have counter-examples that make them vacuous in non-trivial settings. These bounds escape the presented counter-example since GD can be shown to have a vanishing stability parameter with techniques similar to those shown above in
Section

19.1
, and therefore the bound in
[
180
]
generalize in this situation.

19.1
Gradient Descent in the Convex-Lipschitz-Bounded Setting

The field of stochastic convex optimization (SCO)
[
203
]
studies the problem of minimizing the population risk, that is

\argmin_{w\in\mathcal{W}}{R}(w),

where either the loss
\ell(\cdot,z)
or the population risk itself
{R}
are assumed to be convex. In this field, researchers try to design algorithms that return hypotheses
w\in\mathcal{W}
that achieve a small excess risk on large classes of problems.

A common class of problems are those within the so-called convex-Lipschitz-bounded (CLB) setting. This setting considers loss functions
\ell(\cdot,z)
that are convex and
L
-Lipschitz for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
and that have a convex, bounded domain, that is, where the parameter space has a bounded diameter under some metric
\mathrm{diam}_{\rho}(\mathcal{W})=B
. For simplicity, we will consider the space to be a subset of the
d
-dimensional reals
\mathcal{W}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the metric to be the Euclidean distance
\rho=\lVert\cdot\rVert_{2}
. A problem in this class is often described as the triple
(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{Z},\ell)
and the set of all these triples is
\mathcal{C}_{L,B}
.

In this setting, the (projected) GD is guaranteed to generalize and to have a small excess risk. This algorithm has been studied for a long time
[
204
,
205
]
. The algorithm is equivalent to the standard GD, with an additional projection operator at every step to ensure that the iterates are within the bounded parameter space
\mathcal{W}
. Namely, let
\Pi_{\mathcal{W}}(w)=\min_{w^{\prime}\in\mathcal{W}}\lVert w-w^{\prime}\rVert_{2}
be the projection operator from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathcal{W}
. Then, GD starts initializing the parameters
W_{0}
and, at each time step
t\in[T]
, it updates the parameters following the rule

W_{t}=\Pi_{\mathcal{W}}\left(W_{t-1}-\eta_{t}\nabla_{w}\widehat{{R}}(W_{t-1},s)\right)

(49)

for some learning rate
\eta_{t}
and some training set
s\in\mathcal{Z}^{n}
.

For simplicity, we restrict the discussion to GD with a constant learning rate
\eta_{t}=\eta
for all iterations
t\in[T]
. In
[
206
]
, the optimization error of the final iterate of GD in the CLB setting is shown to satisfy

\sup_{(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{Z},\ell)\in\mathcal{C}_{L,B}}\sup_{\mathbb{P}_{Z}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})}\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W_{T},S)-\widehat{{R}}(W^{\star}_{S}\big)\big]\leq\frac{B^{2}}{2\eta T}+\frac{\eta L^{2}(2+\log T)}{2}.

(50)

(See
Lemma

4.6
for a re-statement of this result in the context of this monograph). A similar result also appears in
[
207
, Theorem 5.3]
. Recently,
Bassily et al. [121, Theorem 3.2 and Section 3.4]
proved a generalization bound for GD,

\sup_{(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{Z},\ell)\in\mathcal{C}_{L,B}}\sup_{\mathbb{P}_{Z}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W_{T},S)\big]\leq 4L^{2}\sqrt{T}\eta+\frac{4L^{2}T\eta}{n}.

(51)

Recalling the excess risk decomposition from (
13
), we observe that combining (
50
) and (
51
) yields the following bound

\displaystyle\sup_{(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{Z},\ell)\in\mathcal{C}_{L,B}}\sup_{\mathbb{P}_{Z}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})}\mathbb{E}\big[

\displaystyle\mathrm{excess}(W_{T},\mathcal{W})\big]\leq

\displaystyle\min 4L^{2}\eta\left(\sqrt{T}+\frac{T}{n}\right)+\frac{B^{2}}{2\eta T}+\frac{(2+\log T)\eta L^{2}}{2}.

(52)

For all
\alpha\geq 2
,
Equation

52
guarantees that GD achieves an excess risk in
\mathcal{O}\left(\nicefrac{{LB}}{{\sqrt{n}}}\right)
for a number of iterations
T\in\Theta\left(n^{\alpha}\right)
and a step-size
\eta\in\Theta\left(\nicefrac{{B\sqrt{n}}}{{Ln^{\alpha}}}\right)
.
This, in fact, is the best achievable excess risk rate for the class
\mathcal{C}_{L,B}
in the distribution-free setting
[
205
]
, that is, the best rate that holds
uniformly
for all distributions
\mathbb{P}_{Z}
. In
[
208
,
209
]
, it is shown that GD cannot attain this excess risk rate when the number of iterations satisfies
T\in o\left(n^{2}\right)
.

To establish our negative results, what matters is that choosing the number of iterations to be
T=n^{2}
and the learning rate to be
\eta=\nicefrac{{B}}{{(8Ln\sqrt{n})}}
ensures that the expected generalization error is bounded as
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W_{T},S)\big]\leq\nicefrac{{LB}}{{\sqrt{n}}}
for all CLB problems and all data distributions.

19.2
Failure of the Bounds Using Mutual Information

In
Section

15.3
, we noted how the conditional mutual information measures from the randomized-subsample setting are smaller than the mutual information measures from the standard setting, and how the single-letter conditional mutual information bounds are tighter than the random-subset bounds or the bounds considering the full training set. Moreover, when describing the evaluated conditional mutual information in
Section

18
, we saw that it is tighter than the conditional mutual information bound from
Corollary

4.6
. Therefore, proving the failure of the single-letter conditional mutual information bound from
Corollary

4.5
and the evaluated conditional mutual information from
Theorem

4.30
also proves the failure of the mutual information versions of
Corollaries

4.4
,
4.7
,
4.3
,
4.6
, and
4.8
.

Theorem 4.31
.

Let
T=n^{2}
and
\eta=\nicefrac{{1}}{{n\sqrt{n}}}
. There is a CLB problem
(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{Z},\ell)\in\mathcal{C}_{1,2}
and a data distribution
\mathbb{P}_{Z}
for which the information-theoretic bounds from
Corollary

4.5
and
Theorem

4.30
do not generalize. More precisely, let
W_{T}
be the final iterate of gradient descent, then
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{gen}(W_{T},S)]\in\mathcal{O}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})
while

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{I}(W_{T};U|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})\in\Omega(1)\ \textnormal{ and }\ \mathrm{I}(\textbf{L};U|\tilde{S})\in\Omega(1).

Although the problem is stated for a problem in
\mathcal{C}_{1,2}
, just scaling the loss
\ell
and the parameter space
\mathcal{W}
directly converts it into a problem in
\mathcal{C}_{L,B}
. Therefore,
Theorem

4.31
maintains its full generality. Moreover, the generalization rate of
\mathcal{O}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})
is guaranteed by the choice of the number of iterations and the learning rate as discussed above in
Section

19.1
.

The proof of
Theorem

4.31
is constructive. First, we construct the instance space as the set of all coordinate vectors in
\{0,1\}^{d}
for some dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}
. That is,
\mathcal{Z}=\{\mathsf{e}({k}):k\in[d]\}
, where the coordinate vectors are defined as

\mathsf{e}({k})\coloneqq(\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{k-1\textnormal{ times}},1,\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{d-k\textnormal{ times}}).

Then, we consider the data distribution on this space to be uniform, that is
\mathbb{P}_{Z}[\mathsf{e}({k})]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{d}}
for all
k\in[d]
. As a loss function, we consider the convex,
1
-Lipschitz function
\ell(w,z)=-\langle w,z\rangle
, and as a parameter’s space, the Euclidean unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, that is,
\mathcal{W}=\{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\lVert w\rVert_{2}\}
. Therefore, the problem
(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{Z},\ell)
belongs to the CLB class
\mathcal{C}_{1,2}
. Throughout this example,
w(k)
denotes the
k
-th coordinate of
w\in\mathcal{W}
.

Under this construction, the dynamics of GD are simple to analyze. Note that, for some fixed hypothesis
w
, the empirical risk is
\widehat{{R}}(w)=-\langle w,\bar{Z}\rangle
, where
\bar{Z}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}
is the mean of the instances of the training set. Since the initial point of GD is known, without loss of generality (as we will see shortly), assume that it is
0
. Moreover, the gradient of the empirical risk is
\nabla_{w}\widehat{{R}}(w)=-\bar{Z}
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
. Considering the update rule of GD from (
49
), we see from induction that

W_{t}=\begin{cases}\eta t\bar{Z}&\eta t\lVert\bar{Z}\rVert_{2}\leq 1\\
\nicefrac{{\bar{Z}}}{{\lVert\bar{Z}\rVert_{2}}}&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}.

(53)

Now, consider the
\sigma(\tilde{S})
-measurable random variable
E
that is equal to one if and only if all the data instances in the supersample are distinct. That is

E=\mathbb{I}_{\left\{\tilde{z}_{i,u}\neq\tilde{z}_{j,v}\textnormal{ for all }i,j\in[n]\textnormal{ and all }u,v\in\{0,1\}\right\}}(\tilde{S}).

(54)

As in the
birthday paradox problem

[
210
, Section 5]
, we may bound the probability that
E=1
as follows

\mathbb{P}[E=1]=\prod_{k=0}^{2n-1}\Big(1-\frac{k}{d}\Big)\geq\Big(1-\frac{2n-1}{d}\Big)^{2n-1}.

(55)

This way, we may engineer a dimension
d
for which
\mathbb{P}[E=1]\geq c
for all
n\geq 1
, where
c
is a constant probability, independent of
n
. Solving for (
55
) results in

d\geq\frac{2n-1}{1-c^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{(2n-1)}}}}.

For instance, for
c=0.1
, a dimension
d=2n^{2}
suffices, and therefore
\mathbb{P}[E=0]\leq 0.9
.
Now, we are ready to study what happens to both the single-letter conditional mutual information
\mathrm{I}(W_{T};U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})
and the evaluated conditional mutual information
\mathrm{I}(\textbf{L},U|\tilde{S})
for this particular example.

The idea behind the proofs is that, given the event
E=1
, we can completely determine the indices of the samples used for training either by looking (i) at the non-zero coordinates of the parameters
W_{T}
returned by the GD, since if
W_{T}(k)\neq 0
and
\tilde{Z}_{i,u}=\mathsf{e}({k})
, then
U_{i}=u
; or by looking (ii) at the non-zero entries of the loss vector
\mathbf{L}
, since if
\mathbf{L}_{i,u}\neq 0
, then
U_{i}=u
. Therefore, given the event
E=1
, the single-letter conditional mutual information and the evaluated conditional mutual information are maximized. Finally, the fact that the event
E=1
occurs with constant probability ensures that neither these information measures nor their respective generalization bounds decrease with respect to the number of samples.

Finally, let us show how the choice of the known initial parameter
w_{0}
is not an issue for the analysis and can be assumed to be
0
without loss of generality. After the GD update rule from (
49
), from induction we see that

W_{t}=\begin{cases}w_{0}+\eta t\bar{Z}&\lVert\eta t\bar{Z}\rVert_{2}\leq 1\\
\alpha w_{0}+\beta\bar{Z}&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases},

where
\alpha=\nicefrac{{1}}{{\lVert w_{0}+\eta t\tilde{Z}\rVert_{2}}}
and
\beta=\nicefrac{{\eta t}}{{\lVert w_{0}+\eta t\tilde{Z}\rVert_{2}}}
. Then, if
\lVert W_{t}\rVert_{2}\leq 1
, one may just subtract
w_{0}
and extract the same conclusions. On the other hand, if
\lVert W_{t}\rVert_{2}>1
, one may find the unique vector
\bar{Z}
such that
\Pi_{\mathcal{W}}(w_{0}+\eta t\bar{Z})=W_{t}
, calculate
\alpha
, subtract
w_{0}
, and again extract the same conclusions.

19.2.1
Failure of the Single-Letter Conditional Mutual Information

Note that the single-letter conditional mutual information may be written as follows

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}(W_{T};U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}(U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})-\mathrm{H}(U_{i}|W_{T},\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}(U_{i})-\mathrm{H}(U_{i}|W_{T},\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})

\displaystyle=\log 2-\mathrm{H}(U_{i}|W_{T},\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1}),

(56)

where the second and third equations follow from the independence of the indices
U_{i}
of the data instances
\tilde{Z}_{i,0}
and
\tilde{Z}_{i,1}
and from the fact that
\mathrm{H}(U_{i})=\log 2
, respectively.
Then, we may use Fano’s inequality to bound
\mathrm{H}(U_{i}|W_{T},\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})
and obtain the desired result. More precisely, Fano’s inequality states that

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}(U_{i}|W_{T},\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{b}}\big(\mathbb{P}[U_{i}\neq\hat{U}_{i}]\big),

for every estimator
\hat{U}_{i}(W_{T},\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})
and where
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{b}}
is the binary entropy
[
57
, Theorem 3.12]
. The binary entropy is defined as
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{b}}(p)\coloneqq-\log p-(1-p)\log(1-p)
for all
p\in[0,1]
and it is maximized at
p=0.5
for which
\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{b}}(0.5)=\log 2
. Notice that
\hat{U}_{i}
is a function of
W_{T},\tilde{Z}_{i,0},
and
\tilde{Z}_{i,1}
. Therefore, showing that
\mathbb{P}[U_{i}\neq\hat{U}_{i}]<0.5
is a constant, independent of
n
ensures that

\mathrm{I}(W_{T};U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})\geq\log 2-\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{b}}\left(\mathbb{P}[U_{i}\neq\hat{U}_{i}]\right)\in\Omega(1)

(57)

and completes the proof.

From (
53
), we can see that the non-zero coordinates of the last iterate
W_{T}
are precisely the coordinates of the training samples. That is, if
\tilde{Z}_{i,U_{i}}=\mathsf{e}({k})
, then
W_{T}(k)\neq 0
.
Therefore, under the event
E=1
defined in (
54
), one can precisely determine if sample
\tilde{Z}_{i,0}
or sample
\tilde{Z}_{i,1}
was used for training after observing the parameters
W_{T}
returned by GD since the samples are all distinct.
In other words, one can completely determine the index
U_{i}
from the triple
(W_{T},\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})
.

More precisely, consider a realization in which
\tilde{Z}_{i,0}=\mathsf{e}({k})
and
\tilde{Z}_{i,1}=\mathsf{e}({l})
. Then, the estimator
\hat{U}_{i}(W_{T},\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})
is defined as
\hat{U}_{i}(W_{T},\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})=0
if
W_{T}(k)\neq 0
and
W_{T}(l)=0
; and as
\hat{U}_{i}(W_{T},\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})=1
if
W_{T}(k)=0
and
W_{T}(l)\neq 0
. In the case that both
W_{T}(k)\neq 0
and
W_{T}(l)\neq 0
, let
\hat{U}_{i}(W_{T},\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})
be a Bernoulli random variable with parameter
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
independent of the supersample
\tilde{S}
and the indices
U
. This estimator has a probability of error equal to
0
given the event
E=1
. Therefore, by the law of total probability, the probability of error is

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[U_{i}\neq\hat{U}_{i}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[E=0]\cdot\mathbb{P}^{E=0}[U_{i}\neq\hat{U}_{i}]+\mathbb{P}[E=1]\cdot\mathbb{P}^{E=1}[U_{i}\neq\hat{U}_{i}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[E=0]\cdot\mathbb{P}^{E=0}\mathbb{P}[U_{i}\neq\hat{U}_{i}]

\displaystyle\leq 0.9\cdot\mathbb{P}^{E=0}[U_{i}\neq\hat{U}_{i}],

where the last line follows from the construction. Next, consider the following random variables for all
i\in[n]

G_{i}=\mathbb{I}_{\left\{\tilde{z}_{i,0}\neq\tilde{z}_{i,1}\textnormal{ and }\tilde{z}_{i,1-u_{i}}\neq\tilde{z}_{i,u_{j}}\textnormal{ for all }j\neq i\in[n]\right\}}(\tilde{S},U)

which describe the situation where the given samples
\tilde{Z}_{i,0}
and
\tilde{Z}_{i,1}
are distinct and the sample that is not chosen is also distinct from all other samples in the training set
S
, even when some of these samples are equal between themselves or to the chosen sample
\tilde{Z}_{i,U_{i}}
(for example, when
E=0
). Therefore, given the event
E=0
and
G_{i}=1
, the estimator
\hat{U}_{i}
still has a probability of error equal to zero. Hence, similarly to before we may bound the probability of error of the estimator as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[U_{i}\neq\hat{U}_{i}]

\displaystyle\leq 0.9\cdot\Big(\mathbb{P}^{E=0}[G_{i}=0]\cdot\mathbb{P}^{E=0,G_{i}=0}[U_{i}\neq\hat{U}_{i}]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\mathbb{P}^{E=0}[G_{i}=1]\cdot\mathbb{P}^{E=0,G_{i}=1}[U_{i}\neq\hat{U}_{i}]\Big)

\displaystyle\leq 0.9\cdot\mathbb{P}^{E=0,G_{i}=0}[U_{i}\neq\hat{U}_{i}].

Next, we claim that under the event where
E=0
and
G_{i}=0
, the estimator
\hat{U}_{i}
is a Bernoulli random variable with parameter
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
. Consider a realization in which
U_{i}=u
,
\tilde{Z}_{i,0}=\mathsf{e}({k})
, and
\tilde{Z}_{i,1}=\mathsf{e}({l})
. Then, we claim that under the event
E=0
and
G_{i}=0
,
W_{T}(k)\neq 0
and
W_{T}(l)\neq 0
. The reason is that, under this event, the following cases may happen: either (i)
\tilde{Z}_{i,0}=\tilde{Z}_{i,1}
, or (ii)
\tilde{Z}_{i,0}\neq\tilde{Z}_{i,1}
but there exists another sample in the training set which is equal to
\tilde{Z}_{i,1-u}
. It is easy to see that in these two cases
W_{T}(k)\neq 0
and
W_{T}(l)\neq 0
.

Thus, we conclude that, given
E=0
and
G_{i}=0
, we have that
\mathbb{P}^{E=0,G_{i}=0}[U_{i}\neq\hat{U}_{i}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
. This is true since
\hat{U}_{i}
is a Bernoulli random variable with parameter
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
independent of the indices
U
and the supersample
\tilde{S}
. Therefore, we have that
\mathbb{P}[U_{i}\neq\hat{U}_{i}]\leq 0.45
, which completes the proof as per (
57
). In particular, we have that
\mathrm{I}(W;U_{i}|\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1})\geq 0.005
.

19.2.2
Failure of the Evaluated Conditional Mutual Information

Similarly to before, note that the evaluated conditional mutual information may be written as

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}(\mathbf{L};U|\tilde{S})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}(U|\tilde{S})-\mathrm{H}(U|\mathbf{L},\tilde{S})

(58)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}(U)-\mathrm{H}(U|\mathbf{L},\tilde{S})

(59)

\displaystyle=n\log 2-\mathrm{H}(U|\mathbf{L},\tilde{S}),

(60)

where the second and third equations follow from the independence of the indices
U
and the supersample
S
and the fact that
\mathrm{H}(U)=n\log 2
, respectively.

Then, as previously, the proof relies upon the fact that
U
can be completely determined by the loss vector
\mathbf{L}
given the event
E=1
. More precisely, note that
\mathbf{L}_{i,u}=\ell(W_{T},\tilde{Z}_{i,u})=-\langle W_{T},\tilde{Z}_{i,u}\rangle
. Also, remember from the above that the non-zero coordinates of
W_{T}
are precisely the non-zero coordinates of the samples that are used for training. Therefore, given the event
E=1
,
\mathbf{L}_{i,u}=0
if and only if
\tilde{Z}_{i,u}
was not used for training and therefore the training instance is
Z_{i}=\tilde{Z}_{i,1-u}
. Hence, one can completely determine
U
from
\mathbf{L}
or, equivalently,
\mathrm{H}^{E=1}(U|\mathbf{L},\tilde{S})=0
. We may use this fact to bound
\mathrm{H}(U|\mathbf{L},\tilde{S})
and obtain the desired result. Namely, if we recall that the entropy is defined as an expectation (
Definition

2.9
), using the law of total expectation

\displaystyle\mathrm{H}(U|\mathbf{L},\tilde{S})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{H}(U|\mathbf{L},\tilde{S},E)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[E=0]\cdot\mathrm{H}^{E=0}(U|\mathbf{L},\tilde{S})+\mathbb{P}[E=1]\cdot\mathrm{H}^{E=1}(U|\mathbf{L},\tilde{S})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}[E=0]\cdot\mathrm{H}^{E=0}(U|\mathbf{L},\tilde{S})

\displaystyle\leq n\cdot 0.9\log 2

(61)

where the first line follows since
E
is a function of the supersample
\tilde{S}
. The last inequality follows from the bound
\mathrm{H}^{E=0}(U|\mathbf{L},\tilde{S})\leq n\log n
, which comes from the fact that
|\mathcal{U}|=|\{0,1\}^{n}|=2^{n}
and that
\mathrm{H}(U)\leq\log|\mathcal{U}|
(
Proposition

2.2
), and the fact that
\mathbb{P}[E=0]\leq 0.9
.

Finally, combining (
60
) and (
61
) results in

\mathrm{I}(\mathbf{L};U|\tilde{S})\geq n\log 2-n\cdot 0.9\log 2\in\Omega(n),

and completes the proof. In particular, we have that
\mathrm{I}(\mathbf{L};U|\tilde{S})\geq 0.069n
.

Appendices

Appendix 4.A
Derivations for the Gaussian Location Model Example

The problem considered in
Example

4.1
in
Section

16
is the estimation of the mean
\mu
of a
d
-dimensional Gaussian distribution with known covariance matrix
\sigma^{2}I_{d}
. Furthermore, there are
n
samples
S=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})
available, the loss is measured with the Euclidean distance
\ell(w,z)=\lVert w-z\rVert_{2}
, and the estimation is their empirical mean
W=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}
.

To calculate the expected generalization error and derive different bounds, it is convenient to know how the random variables are distributed.
For example, in this setting
\mathbb{P}_{Z}=\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2}I_{d})
,
\mathbb{P}_{W}=\mathcal{N}\Big(\mu,\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}I_{d}\Big)
,
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}}=\mathcal{N}\Big(\frac{(n-1)\mu+Z_{i}}{n},\frac{\sigma^{2}(n-1)}{n^{2}}I_{d}\Big)
,
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S^{-j}}=\mathcal{N}\Big(\frac{\mu}{n}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\neq j}Z_{i},\sigma^{2}I_{d}\Big)
, and
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}=\delta\Big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{Z_{i}}\Big)
.
Another important feature of this problem is that the loss function is
1
-Lipschitz under
\rho(w,w^{\prime})=\lVert w-w^{\prime}\rVert_{2}
.

4.A.1
Expected Generalization Error

In order to derive an exact expression of the generalization error, it is suitable to write it in the following explicit form:

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]=\mathbb{E}[\ell(W,Z)]-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[\ell(W,Z_{i})],

where
Z\sim\mathbb{P}_{Z}
is independent of
W
. Then, the two terms can be evaluated independently.
The first term is equivalent to

\mathbb{E}[\ell(W,Z)]=\mathbb{E}[\lVert W-Z\rVert_{2}]=\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}\Big(1+\frac{1}{n}\Big)}\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{d+1}{2}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{d}{2}\big)},

where the first equality follows from the definition of the loss function. The second equality follows from noting that
(W-Z)\sim\mathcal{N}\big(0,\sigma^{2}\big(1+\frac{1}{n}\big)I_{d}\big)
and therefore
\lVert W-Z\rVert_{2}=\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\big(1+\frac{1}{n}\big)}X
, where
X
is distributed according to the
\chi
distribution with
d
degrees of freedom.

Similarly, the summands of the second term are equivalent to

\mathbb{E}[\ell(W,Z_{i})]=\mathbb{E}[\lVert W-Z_{i}\rVert_{2}]=\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)}\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{d+1}{2}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{d}{2}\big)},

where, as before, the first equality follows from the definition of the loss function.
The second equality follows from noting that
(W-Z_{i})\sim\mathcal{N}\big(0,\sigma^{2}\big(1-\frac{1}{n}\big)I_{d}\big)
and therefore
\lVert W-Z_{i}\rVert_{2}=\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\big(1-\frac{1}{n}\big)}X
, where
X
is distributed according to the
\chi
distribution with
d
degrees of freedom.
In this case,
W
and
Z_{i}
are not independent random variables. In fact,
(W,Z_{i})
is normally distributed with covariance matrix

\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}I_{d}&\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}I_{d}\\
\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}I_{d}&\sigma^{2}I_{d}\end{pmatrix},

from which the distribution of
W-Z_{i}
is deduced.

Finally, subtracting both terms results in

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]=\sqrt{\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{n}}\Big(\sqrt{n+1}-\sqrt{n-1}\Big)\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{d+1}{2}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{d}{2}\big)}\leq\frac{\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}d}}{n}.

where the inequality follows from the following two bounds: (i)
\sqrt{n+1}-\sqrt{n-1}\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}
, which is obtained by multiplying and dividing by
\sqrt{n+1}+\sqrt{n-1}
and noting that
\sqrt{n+1}+\sqrt{n-1}\geq\sqrt{2n}
, and (ii) the upper bound on the ratio of gamma distributions by
\sqrt{\frac{d}{2}}
using the series expansion at
d\to\infty
.

4.A.2
Wasserstein Distance Bound

The bound from
[
180
]
can be calculated exactly since
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
is a delta distribution, that is
