\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{2}}(P_{W}^{S},P_{W})\big]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}^{\prime}\Big\rVert_{2}\bigg]=\sqrt{\frac{4\sigma^{2}}{n}}\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{d+1}{2}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{d}{2}\big)}\leq\sqrt{\frac{2\sigma^{2}d}{n}}

where
Z_{i}^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}_{Z}
are independent copies of
Z_{i}
. Hence, the difference is distributed as a normal distribution with mean 0 and covariance
\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{n}I_{d}
, which means that the norm is
\sqrt{\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{n}}X
, where
X
is a
\chi
random variable with
d
degrees of freedom.

4.A.3
Single-Letter Wasserstein Distance Bound

An exact calculation of the bound from
Theorem

4.20
is cumbersome. However, the Wasserstein distance of order one can be bounded from above by the Wasserstein distance of order two
[
83
, Remark 6.6]
, that is
\mathbb{W}_{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{2}}\leq\mathbb{W}_{2,\lVert\cdot\rVert_{2}}
, which has a closed form expression for Gaussian distributions. More specifically,

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{2,\lVert\cdot\rVert_{2}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}},\mathbb{P}_{W})\big]\leq\frac{\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}}}{n}\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{d+1}{2}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{d}{2}\big)}+\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}d}{n^{3}}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{\sigma^{2}d}}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}d}{n^{3}}}.

The second inequality follows from the closed-form expression for the squared Wasserstein distance of order 2, namely

\mathbb{W}_{2,\lVert\cdot\rVert_{2}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{Z_{i}},\mathbb{P}_{W})^{2}=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\lVert\mu-Z_{i}\rVert^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}d}{n}\Big(1+\frac{n-1}{n}-2\sqrt{\frac{n-1}{n}}\Big),

where the term
(1+\frac{n-1}{n}-2\sqrt{\frac{n-1}{n}})
is a perfect square that is bounded from above by
\frac{1}{n^{2}}
. Then, the expression results from employing the inequality
\sqrt{x+y}\leq\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{y}
and noting that
\lVert\mu-Z_{i}\rVert_{2}=\sigma X
, where
X
is a
\chi
distributed random variable with
d
degrees of freedom.

4.A.4
Random-Subset Wasserstein Distance Bound

As in
Section

4.A.2
, since
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
is a delta distribution, the bound from
Theorem

4.21
can be calculated exactly. In particular, the bound assuming that
|J|=1
is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{W}_{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{2}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S^{-J}})\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}-\Big(\frac{Z_{J}^{\prime}}{n}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\neq J}Z_{i}\Big)\Big\rVert_{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{4\sigma^{2}}}{n}\frac{\Gamma\big(\frac{d+1}{2}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{d}{2}\big)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}d}}{n},

where
Z_{J}^{\prime}\sim\mathbb{P}_{Z}
is an independent copy of
Z_{J}
. Hence, the norm is
\frac{\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}}}{n}X
, where
X
is a
\chi
random variable with
d
degrees of freedom.

4.A.5
Single-Letter Mutual Information Bound

The single-letter mutual information is
\mathrm{I}(W;Z_{i})=\frac{d}{2}\log\big(\frac{n}{n-1}\big)
for all
i\in[n]

[
7
]
. Nonetheless, in order to employ the bound from
Bu et al. [7]
in
Theorem

4.10
, the loss function
\ell(W,Z)
needs to have a CGF
\Lambda_{-\ell(W,Z)}(\lambda)
bounded from above by a convex function
\psi(\lambda)
such that
\psi(0)=\psi^{\prime}(0)=0
for all
\lambda\in[0,b)
for some
b\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, where
Z\sim\mathbb{P}_{Z}
is independent of
W
.

The loss function
\ell(W,Z)=\lVert W-Z\rVert_{2}
is
\sqrt{\sigma^{2}(1+\frac{1}{n})}X
, where
X
is a
\chi
random variable with
d
degrees of freedom. The moment generating function
M_{\ell(W,Z)}(\lambda)
of such a random variable is

M_{\ell(W,Z)}(\lambda)=\bar{M}\Big(\frac{d}{2},\frac{1}{2},\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\Big)+\frac{\lambda\sqrt{2}\Gamma\big(\frac{d+1}{2}\big)}{\Gamma\big(\frac{d}{2}\big)}\bar{M}\Big(\frac{k+1}{2},\frac{3}{2},\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}\Big),

where
\bar{M}
is the Kummer’s confluent hypergeometric function.

The expression of this moment generating function is too convoluted to study for
d>1
. Nonetheless, for
d=1
it has a closed-form expression, namely

M_{\ell(W,Z)}(\lambda)=e^{\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}}\Big(1+\textnormal{erf}\Big(\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)\Big).

Therefore, the CGF
\Lambda_{\ell(W,Z)}(\lambda)=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}+\log(1+\textnormal{erf}(\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{2}}))
, which is bounded from above by the convex function
\psi(\lambda)=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2}
for all
\lambda\in(-\infty,0]
. Noting that
\Lambda_{X}(\lambda)=\Lambda_{-X}(-\lambda)
from the algebra of CGFs from
Section

3.6
, we have that the bound from
Bu et al. [7]
in
Theorem

4.10
can be applied yielding

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{2\sigma^{2}\Big(1+\frac{1}{n}\Big)I(W;Z_{i})}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\sigma^{2}\Big(1+\frac{1}{n}\Big)\log\Big(\frac{n}{n-1}\Big)}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{n-1}},

where the last inequality stems from noting that
\frac{n}{n-1}=1+\frac{1}{n-1}
, the fact that
\log(1+x)\leq x
, and bounding
(1+\frac{1}{n})
from above by
2
.

Appendix 4.B
Tightness of the Mutual Information-Based Bounds

In this section of the appendix, we develop the proof of
Theorem

4.25
.

Proof.

Consider
\mathcal{W}
be a ball of radius
\nicefrac{{B}}{{2}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and therefore
\mathrm{diam}(\mathcal{W})=B
. Further consider an arbitrary
z_{0}\in\mathcal{W}
such that
\lVert z_{0}\rVert_{2}=\nicefrac{{B}}{{2}}
. Let the input space be
\mathcal{Z}=\{\nicefrac{{2z_{0}}}{{B}},-\nicefrac{{2z_{0}}}{{B}}\}
, the data distribution
\mathbb{P}_{Z}
be
\mathbb{P}_{Z}[\nicefrac{{2z_{0}}}{{B}}]=\mathbb{P}_{Z}[-\nicefrac{{2z_{0}}}{{B}}]=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
, and the loss function be
\ell(w,z)=-L\langle w,z\rangle
. It is easy to see that the loss function is convex and
L
-Lipschitz (see
[
182
]
for similar constructions).

Define a Rademacher random variable
R_{i}
such that
R_{i}=1
if
Z_{i}=\nicefrac{{2z_{0}}}{{B}}
and
R_{i}=-1
if
Z_{i}=-\nicefrac{{2z_{0}}}{{B}}
. We can, therefore, represent each training sample as
Z_{i}=R_{i}\cdot\nicefrac{{2z_{0}}}{{B}}
. The empirical risk for a hypothesis
w\in\mathcal{W}
is
\widehat{{R}}(w,S)=-\frac{2L}{Bn}\left\langle w,z_{0}\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\right\rangle
. In this case, it is straightforward that the empirical risk minimizer for this problem is

\argmin_{w\in\mathcal{W}}\widehat{{R}}(w,S)=\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{ERM}}(S)=\begin{cases}z_{0}&\text{if}\penalty\ \text{sign}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\right)=1\\
-z_{0}&\text{if}\penalty\ \text{sign}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\right)=-1\end{cases},

where
\mathrm{sign}(x)=1
if
x\geq 0
and
\mathrm{sign}(x)=-1
otherwise.

First, we provide a lower bound on the expected generalization error. The expected empirical risk of
\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{ERM}}
is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\min_{w\in\mathcal{W}}\widehat{{R}}(w,S)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\min_{w\in\mathcal{W}}-\frac{2L}{Bn}\left\langle w,z_{0}\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{2L}{Bn}\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{w\in\{z_{0},-z_{0}\}}\left\langle w,z_{0}\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\right\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{2L}{Bn}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\left\langle z_{0},z_{0}\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\right\rangle\right|\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{LB}{2n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\right|\right],

where we have used that
\max(x,y)=\frac{x+y}{2}+\frac{|x-y|}{2}
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
. Observing that
{R}(w)=0
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
we observe that the generalization error ot the hypothesis
W=\mathbb{A}_{\mathrm{ERM}}(S)
is lower bounded by

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]=\frac{LB}{2n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\right|\right]\geq\frac{LB}{2\sqrt{2n}},

where the inequality follows from the Khintchine–Kahane inequality
[
92
, Theorem D.9]
.

Next, we analyze the upper bounds based on
Corollary

4.4
. Observe that the Markov chain
S-\mathrm{sign}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\right)-W
holds. Then, by the data processing inequality
Proposition

2.4
and the fact that the Shannon’s entropy is non-negative

\mathrm{I}(W;S)\leq\mathrm{I}\left(W;\mathrm{sign}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\right)\right)\leq\mathrm{H}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}R_{i}\right)\leq\log 2,

where the last inequality holds since the
\mathrm{sign}(\cdot)
can only take two values. Therefore,
Corollary

4.4
guarantees that
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq LB\sqrt{\nicefrac{{\log 2}}{{2n}}}
.
∎

Appendix 4.C
Randomized-Subsample Setting and the Bretagnolle–Huber Inequality

In
Corollaries

4.6
,
4.8
, and
4.5
, the immediate bound that stems from the use of the Bretagnolle–Huber inequality from
Lemma

2.3
is not included.
The reason for this is that the relative entropy terms
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1},U_{i}}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1}})
and
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U,R}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U^{-J},R})
, when
|J|=1
, are never greater than
\log 2
as shown in
Lemma

4.5
below. For simplicity, for the randomized subset bounds we will consider
\mathbb{Q}(\tilde{S},U^{-J},R)=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U^{-J},R}
.
Hence, the range of these relative entropy terms is inside the range where Pinsker’s inequality is tighter than the Bretagnolle–Huber inequality.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,B}
be a Markov kernel from
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
to distributions on
\mathcal{X}
, where
B
is a Bernoulli random variable with probability
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
and
A\in\mathcal{A}
is a random variable independent of
B
.
Let also
\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A}=\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,B}\circ\mathbb{P}_{B}
. Then,
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,B}\|\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A})\leq\log 2
.

Proof.

In this situation
\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A}
dominates
\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,B}
and
\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,(1-B)}
, that is,
\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,B}\ll\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A}
and
\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,(1-B)}\ll\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A}
since
\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,B}+\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,(1-B)})
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,B}\|\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{A,B}\Bigg[\log\Bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,B}}{\mathrm{d}\big(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,B}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,(1-B)}\big)}\Bigg)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}^{A,B}\Bigg[\log\Bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\big(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,B}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,(1-B)}\big)}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,B}}\Bigg)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\log 2-\mathbb{E}^{A,B}\bigg[\log\bigg(1+\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,(1-B)}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,B}}\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\log 2,

where the second equality stems from
[
51
, Exercise 9.27]
, the third one follows from the linearity
\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,B}
-a.e. of the Radon–Nikodym derivative, and last inequality is due to the fact that
\log(1+x)\geq 0
for all
x\geq 0
and the fact that
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,(1-B)}}}{{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,B}}}
is always positive.
Also, each step is possible since the expectation integrates over the support of
\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,B}
, avoiding the problems of absolute continuity.
∎

Lemma

4.5
can be easily extended to the case where
B
is a sequence of
k
Bernoulli random variables
B_{i}
, noting that
\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A}=2^{-k}\sum_{j=1}^{2^{k}}\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,\mathcal{B}_{j}}
, where
\mathcal{B}
are all the
2^{k}
random sequences
\mathcal{B}_{j}
where the
i
-th element can be either
B_{i}
or
(1-B_{i})
.
In that case, we have that
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A,B}\|\mathbb{P}_{X}^{A})\leq k\log 2
.

Then, note that
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1},U_{i}}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{Z}_{i,0},\tilde{Z}_{i,1}})\leq\log 2
if
A=(\tilde{Z}_{i},\tilde{Z}_{i+n})
,
B=U_{i}
, and
X=W
.
Similarly, for
|J|=1
, note that
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U,R}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U^{-J},R})\leq\log(2)
if
A=(\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}},R)
,
B=U_{J}
, and
X=W
.

If we were to replicate
Corollary

4.6
when
|J|>2
, it is not guaranteed that the inequality obtained from Pinsker’s inequality is tighter than the one obtained with the Bretagnolle-Huber inequality.
For instance, as discussed above,
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U,R}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{\tilde{S},U_{J^{c}},R})
could be as large as
|J|\log 2
, which is already larger than
1.6
for
|J|=3
, which is the threshold where the Bretagnolle–Huber inequality starts to be tighter. Hence, for
|J|>2
, one should also consider that inequality if one desires the tightest bound. However, the bound derived from the Bretagnolle–Huber inequality was not included since this kind of bounds are usually employed for
|J|=1
as we will see shortly in
Section

17
and since they are shown to be tightest for
|J|=1

[
8
]
.

Appendix 4.D
Optimization Error Rate of Gradient Descent’s Last Iterate

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\ell(\cdot,z)
be a convex and
L
-Lipschitz loss function for every training instance
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, and
\mathcal{W}
be a convex and compact parameter space with bounded diameter
B
. Let
\{w_{t}\}_{t\in[T]}
denote the output of the GD algorithm with a constant step size
\eta
. Then, for every training set
s\in\mathcal{Z}^{n}

\displaystyle\widehat{{R}}(w_{T},s)-\min_{w\in\mathcal{W}}\widehat{{R}}(w,s)\leq\frac{B^{2}}{2\eta T}+\frac{\eta L^{2}(2+\log T)}{2}.

Proof.

Let
g_{t}
be a member of the sub-differential
\partial\widehat{{R}}(w_{t},s)
of
\widehat{{R}}(w_{t},s)
. Since
\ell(\cdot,z)
is convex and
L
-Lipschitz for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, then
\widehat{{R}}(\cdot,s)
is convex and
L
-Lipschitz as well. Hence,
[
206
, Theorem 2]
guarantees that

\displaystyle\widehat{{R}}(w_{T},s)

\displaystyle-\min_{w\in\mathcal{W}}\widehat{{R}}(w,s)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\big(\widehat{{R}}(w_{t},s)-\min_{w\in\mathcal{W}}\widehat{{R}}(w,w)\big)+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{T-1}\frac{1}{k(k+1)}\sum_{t=T-k}^{T}\eta\lVert g_{t}\rVert_{2}^{2}.

Since
\lVert g_{t}\rVert_{2}\leq L
, the second term can be upper bounded by
\frac{\eta L^{2}}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{T-1}\frac{1}{k}
. Then, by the well-known bounds on the Harmonic numbers
\frac{\eta L^{2}}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{T-1}\frac{1}{k}\leq\frac{\eta L^{2}}{2}(1+\log(T-1))\leq\frac{\eta L^{2}}{2}(1+\log T)
. Finally, the first term is bounded by
\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\big(\widehat{{R}}(w_{t},s)-\min_{w\in\mathcal{W}}\widehat{{R}}(w,s)\big)\leq\frac{B^{2}}{2\eta T}+\frac{\eta L^{2}}{2}

[
205
, Theorem 3.2]
. Combining these two upper bounds proves the lemma.
∎

Chapter 5
PAC-Bayesian Generalization Guarantees

In this chapter, we discuss PAC-Bayesian and single-draw PAC-Bayesian generalization guarantees within the framework of information-theoretic generalization introduced in
Section

11
. These generalization guarantees are the most specific within the three levels of specificity described in
Section

11.1
.
To be precise, this kind of guarantee ensures that the generalization error is bounded by a certain quantity with at least a prescribed level of probability
1-\beta
. In a sense, they are measuring how much the empirical risk concentrates around the population risk. This is the reason why they have the classical term PAC (
Section

7
) in their name.

PAC-Bayesian guarantees describe what is the smallest expected difference between the average population risk on hypotheses returned by the algorithm and the average empirical risk that holds with at least probability
1-\beta
. As we can see, this is more specific than guarantees on expectation, as now we know that the guarantee holds with a certain probability for the
observed
training set. On the other hand, single-draw PAC-Bayesian guarantees describe what is the smallest difference between the population risk and the empirical risk that holds with probability
1-\beta
. Therefore, they are again a step above standard PAC-Bayesian guarantees in the specificity ladder as they hold with a certain probability for the
observed
training set and hypothesis returned. In the case that the algorithm is deterministic, then both bounds coincide.

As we did in the introduction of
Chapter

4
, we may ask ourselves, “
What do we desire from PAC-Bayesian guarantees?”
There are mainly three desiderata that we want from this kind of bounds:

1.

Like for bounds in expectation, a first objective of this kind of bounds is to gain understanding. That is, we seek to know what drives apart the performance of an algorithm on its objective (the population risk) with respect to the proxy it uses to return a hypothesis (the empirical risk). The probabilistic nature of these bounds sometimes makes this understanding a little harder than for bounds in expectation. For this reason, often it is convenient to prove an intuitive result in expectation, and later try to translate it into a PAC-Bayesian version.

2.

A unique feature from these bounds is that, for the observed training data
s
, if we can evaluate the posterior distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}
, we can evaluate the bound. This makes these bounds very attractive to design algorithms that have a small population risk by optimizing the PAC-Bayesian bound directly. For this reason, an important feature of these bounds is that either the expression for the optimal posterior is available, or that it can be optimized in some way. The algorithms that design the posterior under this criterion are often referred to
self-bounding algorithms

[
211
,
212
]
or
self-certified learning methods

[
140
,
213
]
.

3.

For single-draw PAC-Bayesian guarantees, or for standard PAC-Bayesian if the algorithm is deterministic, the guarantees hold for the observed training set
s
and the returned hypothesis
w
. Therefore, if we can evaluate the posterior density with respect to some measure
\mathsf{p}_{W}^{S=s}(w)
, the main objective now is to have numerically sharp bounds to evaluate the performance of the algorithm.

To the question “Why do we study information-theoretic bounds?”, the answer is the same as we gave for the bounds in expectation from
Chapter

4
. That is, we choose this framework because these bounds are specific to the learning algorithm and the data distribution.

Above we mentioned that PAC-Bayesian bounds, in a sense, measure how much the population risk concentrates around the empirical risk. In other words, PAC-Bayesian guarantees are bounds on the tail of the generalization error random variable. In fact, if the hypothesis returned by the algorithm was completely independent of the algorithm, these bounds would reduce to classical concentration inequalities. For this reason, we start the chapter in
Section

1
reviewing such classical inequalities. After that, we review the origin of this kind of bounds in
Section

2
to lay down the notation and motivate the different paths of research in this field studied in this monograph. Here we also briefly discuss the limitations of the relative entropy as the dependency measure and point to works substituting by different metrics.

In
Section

3
, we focus our attention on PAC-Bayesian bounds for losses with a bounded range. We start by discussing two of the most important bounds in this setting, due to Seeger and Langford
[
21
,
22
]
and
Catoni [24]
, along with their shortcomings. Then, we prove that they are related and develop a fast-rate equivalent to the bound from Seeger and Langford. This bound is important since it is equally tight to their bound, their linear nature makes it more interpretable, and it has a closed form for the optimal posterior distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
. This bound will be the foundation for later developments in the chapter. This section is based on our results from
[
142
]
.

After that, in
Section

4
, we shift our attention to PAC-Bayesian bounds for unbounded losses. In particular, we develop bounds for losses with a bounded CGF and with bounded moments. For losses with a bounded CGF, we developed a PAC-Bayes Chernoff analogue. For losses with a bounded raw moment or a bounded variance, we employ our fast-rate bound from
Section

3
to derive new bounds with a rate that interpolates between a slow rate when only the second moment is bounded to a fast rate when the loss is bounded almost surely. In order to prove all these bounds we derive a new technique to optimize parameters in probabilistic statements that can be of independent interest. This part of the section is mostly based on our results from
[
142
,
164
]
. Then,
Sections

4.3
and
4.4
discuss related bibliography on other approaches to optimize parameters in probabilistic statements
[
214
,
23
,
215
,
25
,
33
,
216
,
217
]
and on other PAC-Bayesian bounds for unbounded losses
[
28
,
12
,
29
,
10
,
30
,
31
,
32
,
33
,
35
,
34
,
218
]
.

Later, in
Section

5
we show how mostly all the results presented can be extended to single-draw PAC-Bayesian bounds. Some of the results follow from our article
[
164
]
, but most of them are new and could have been derived from our article
[
142
]
. Crucially, we employ an extension of
Rivasplata et al. [153]
’s probabilistic version of the Donsker and Varadhan lemma.

Finally, in
Section

6
, we reflect on anytime-valid PAC-Bayesian bounds, which are bounds that hold
simultaneously
for every time step in interactive learning algorithms. We show that for the cases presented in this monograph, extending any high-probability bound to their anytime-valid version is possible incurring only in a logarithmic increment to the bound.

The intention of this chapter, like in
Chapter

4
, is to be didactic and to convey the key messages and results from PAC-Bayesian information-theoretic bounds. As mentioned previously, we hope the reader can get a benefit from this chapter. Other available resources surveying and introducing this field are given by
Guedj [219]
,
Alquier [132]
, and
Hellström et al. [133]
.

1
Classical Concentration Inequalities

Recall from
Chapter

4
that, for every fixed hypothesis
w\in\mathcal{W}
, the empirical risk is an unbiased estimator of the population risk, that is,
\mathbb{E}[\widehat{{R}}(w,S)]={R}(w)
. The bias in the estimation comes from the dependence between the hypothesis and the training data.

1.1
Essential, Markov-Based Concentration Inequalities

In this chapter, we seek to understand how much the empirical risk of the algorithm’s output hypothesis
W=\mathbb{A}(S)
concentrates around the population risk. For this reason, we believe that it is relevant to first review how much it concentrates for a fixed hypothesis
w\in\mathcal{W}
. In this way, we can have an idea of which rates to expect under different assumptions. Ideally, the bounds derived in later sections will replicate the classical concentration bounds with an extra term describing the dependence between the algorithm and the data.

Arguably the simplest tail inequality is
Markov’s inequality

[
71
, Section 2.1.1]
, which states that for every non-negative random variable
X
and every
t>0

\mathbb{P}\left[X\geq t\right]\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[X\right]}{t}.

(62)

This simple inequality reflects well that the larger the value of
t
, the less likely is that a realization of
X
surpasses it, taking into account its expected value
\mathbb{E}[X]
.

Markov’s inequality is the foundation of many other concentration (or tail) inequalities. For example, consider
X=\mathrm{gen}(w,S)^{2}=({R}(w)-\widehat{{R}}(w,S))^{2}
. Then, since the empirical risk
\widehat{{R}}(w,S)
is an unbiased estimator of the population risk, we have that
\mathbb{E}[X]=\mathrm{Var}\big(\widehat{{R}}(w,S)\big)
. Moreover, since the instances
Z_{i}
are i.i.d. it follows that
\mathrm{Var}\big(\widehat{{R}}(w,S)\big)=\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{Var}\big(\ell(w,Z)\big)
. Combining these two things we obtain
Chebyshev’s inequality

[
71
, Section 2.1.1]

\mathbb{P}\left[\mathrm{gen}(w,S)^{2}\geq t\right]\leq\frac{\mathrm{Var}\left(\ell(w,Z)\right)}{nt}.

If we consider that the loss function has a variance bounded by
\sigma^{2}
for all hypotheses
w\in\mathcal{W}
, re-arranging this inequality results in the following proposition.

Proposition 5.1

(Adaptation of Chebyshev’s inequality
[
71
]
)
.

Consider a loss function
\ell(w,Z)
with a variance bounded by
\sigma^{2}
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
. Then, for all
\beta\in(0,1)
and all
w\in\mathcal{W}
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathrm{gen}(w,S)\leq\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n\beta}}.

The same exercise can be done considering
X=|\mathrm{gen}(w,S)|^{p}=|{R}(w)-\widehat{{R}}(w)|^{p}
. Then,
\mathbb{E}[X]
is the
p
-th central moment of
\widehat{{R}}(w,S)
. Consider that the
p
-th central moment of
\ell(w,Z)
is bounded by
c_{p}
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
. Then, similarly to what happened for
p=2
, the
p
-th central moment of
\widehat{{R}}(w,S)
can be bounded as

\mathbb{E}\left[\left|{R}(w)-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(w,Z)\right|^{p}\right]\leq\frac{1}{n^{p}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|{R}(w)-\ell(w,Z_{i})\right|\right)^{p}\right]\leq\frac{c_{p}}{n^{p-1}},

where the first inequality comes from the fact that
|\sum_{i=1}^{n}x|\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}|x|
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, and the second comes from the fact that the samples
Z_{i}
are i.i.d. Therefore, we may obtain a general version of
Proposition

5.1
.

Proposition 5.2

(Adaptation of the moment’s inequality
[
71
]
)
.

Consider a loss function
\ell(w,Z)
with a central
p
-th moment bounded by
c_{p}
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
. Then, for all
\beta\in(0,1)
and all
w\in\mathcal{W}
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathrm{gen}(w,S)\leq\left(\frac{c_{p}}{n^{p-1}\beta}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Propositions

5.1
and
5.2
teach us that for losses with a bounded
p
-th moment, we expect that the rate, with respect to the number of samples, is
\mathcal{O}(n^{-\frac{p}{p-1}})
for all
p>1
. This is quite revealing: for losses with a bounded second moment, we may expect a slow rate of
\mathcal{O}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})
while as the number of moments increases, and
p\to\infty
, we may expect a fast rate of
\mathcal{O}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}})
. This last situation corresponds to losses that are essentially bounded, that is, for which
\esssup|\ell(w,Z)-\mathbb{E}[\ell(w,Z)]|\leq c<\infty
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
.

The main issue with
Propositions

5.1
and
5.2
is that they are not bounds of high probability, since the dependence with the probability parameter
\beta
is polynomial
\mathcal{O}(\beta^{-\frac{1}{p}})
and not logarithmic. As we will see in
Section

3
, this can be resolved through Alquier’s method in a way similar to what we saw in
Section

13.3
, although at the expense of trading a bounded central moment for a bounded raw moment in the bounds.

Markov’s inequality (
62
) is also the source of the Cramér–Chernoff method
[
82
, Section 2.3]
. Indeed, consider
X=e^{n\lambda\mathrm{gen}(w,S)}=e^{n\lambda({R}(w)-\widehat{{R}}(w,S))}
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, then
\mathbb{E}[X]=\exp\big(\Lambda_{-\widehat{{R}}(w,S)}(n\lambda)\big)
. Since the instances
Z_{i}
are i.i.d., by the algebra of CGFs from
Section

3.6
this means that
\mathbb{E}[X]=\exp\big(n\Lambda_{-\ell(w,Z)}(\lambda)\big)
. Therefore, if we consider a loss with a CGF bounded by
\psi
in the sense of
Definition

4.1
, it follows that

\mathbb{P}\left[e^{n\lambda\mathrm{gen}(w,S)}\geq t\right]\leq\frac{e^{n\psi(\lambda)}}{t}.

Re-arranging this equation and letting
\beta=\nicefrac{{e^{n\psi(\lambda)}}}{{t}}
, we have that for all
\beta\in(0,1)
and all
\lambda>0

\mathbb{P}\left[\mathrm{gen}(w,S)\geq\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\psi(\lambda)+\frac{\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n}\right)\right]\leq\beta.

Finally, noting that the optimal value of
\lambda
does not depend on the random object
S
, we may optimize it using
Lemma

2.5
from
Section

5.1
and recover the
Chernoff inequality

[
82
, Section 2.2]
.

Proposition 5.3

(Adaptation of Chernoff inequality
[
82
, Section 2.2]
)
.

Consider a loss with a bounded CGF (
Definition

4.1
). Then, for all
\beta\in(0,1)
and all
w\in\mathcal{W}
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathrm{gen}(w,S)\leq\psi_{*}^{-1}\left(\frac{\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n}\right).

The function
\psi_{*}^{-1}
is the inverse of the convex conjugate of the function that dominates the CGF of the loss. This function is non-decreasing and it satisfies that
\psi_{*}^{-1}(0)=0
and
\lim_{y\to\infty}\psi_{*}^{-1}(y)\to\infty
. If we write
Proposition

5.1
as

\mathbb{P}\left[\mathrm{gen}(w,S)\geq\psi_{*}^{-1}\left(\frac{\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n}\right)\right]\leq\beta,

we see how the function
\psi_{*}^{-1}
gives us a good characterization of the tail of the generalization error random variable
\mathrm{gen}(w,S)
: for every value of
\beta\in(0,1)
, we can obtain a threshold
t=\psi_{*}^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{n}\log\frac{1}{\beta}\right)
for which the tail
\mathbb{P}[{R}(w)-\widehat{{R}}(w,S)]
has a probability smaller than
\beta
.

As we saw in
Section

13.1
, for common behaviors like sub-gaussian, sub-exponential, or sub-gamma, the function
\psi_{*}^{-1}
implies that the generalization error has essentially a slow rate
\mathcal{O}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}})
with respect to
n
. However, the attractive property of this bound is that the dependence with the probability parameter
\beta
is
logarithmic
, and therefore it is a high-probability bound. A particularly important corollary of
Proposition

5.3
is the one for losses with a bounded range, which is known as
Hoeffding’s inequality
. Again, as discussed in
Section

13.1
, if the loss
\ell
has a range in
[a,b]
, then it is
\nicefrac{{(b-a)^{2}}}{{2}}
-sub-Gaussian and therefore
Proposition

5.3
implies that

\mathbb{P}\left[\mathrm{gen}(w,S)\geq(b-a)\sqrt{\frac{\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{2n}}\right]\leq\beta.

(63)

1.2
Specialized Concentration Bounds for Bounded Losses

More refined analyses can reveal better rates and/or better dependencies with the probability parameter
\beta
.
For example, instead of just considering that the
p
-th central moment of the loss is bounded by
c_{p}
, one may instead consider that the loss
\ell(w,Z)
satisfies the
Bernstein condition
with parameter
\alpha
. This condition requires that every moment is bounded by

\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\ell(w,Z)-{R}(w)\right)^{p}\right]\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}\cdot p!\sigma^{2}\alpha^{p-2}

for all natural
p\geq 2
, where
\sigma^{2}
is the variance of the loss
[
71
, Section 2.1.3]
. In our case, since the losses are i.i.d, we may see that if the loss
\ell(w,Z)
satisfies the Bernstein condition with parameter
\alpha
, so does the empirical risk
\widehat{{R}}(w,S)
with parameter
\nicefrac{{\alpha}}{{n}}
. Namely,

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(w,Z_{i})-{R}(w)\right)^{p}\right]\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{p}}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\ell(w,Z)-{R}(w)\right)^{p}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2n^{p-1}}p!\sigma^{2}\alpha^{p-2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\cdot p!\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}\left(\frac{\alpha}{n}\right)^{p-2},

where the first equality follows from the independence of the losses and where in the last equality we are using that the variance of the empirical risk is
\nicefrac{{\sigma^{2}}}{{n}}
. Therefore, the
Bernstein inequality

[
71
, Proposition 2.10]
states that

\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\mathrm{gen}(w,S)\right|\geq t\right]\leq 2e^{\frac{-nt^{2}}{2(\sigma^{2}+\alpha t)}}.

A particularly interesting case is that of losses with a range bounded in
[a,b]
, as in that case they always satisfy the Bernstein condition with
\alpha=(b-a)

[
71
, Section 2.1.3]
. In this situation, re-arranging this equation and letting
\beta=2e^{\frac{-nt^{2}}{2(\sigma^{2}+\alpha t)}}
we obtain the following result.

Proposition 5.4

(Adaptation of Bernstein’s inequality
[
71
, Proposition2.10]
)
.

Consider a loss
\ell(w,Z)
with a range bounded in
[a,b]
and a variance bounded by
\sigma^{2}
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
. Then, for all
\beta\in(0,1)
and all
w\in\mathcal{W}
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathrm{gen}(w,S)\leq\frac{2(b-a)\log\frac{2}{\delta}}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{2\sigma^{2}\log\frac{2}{\beta}}{n}}.

There are two terms that explain the concentration of the empirical risk around the population risk in Bernstein’s inequality. The first one is a fast-rate decay that depends on the range of the loss. The second one is a slow-rate decay that depends on the variance of the loss. Compared to the moment’s inequality from
Proposition

5.2
, this bound achieves a logarithmic dependence with respect to the probability parameter, while maintaining the slow rate for a bounded variance. However, it also requires that the loss is bounded, which would have achieved a fast rate in the moment’s bound. Then, compared with the Chernoff bound from
Proposition

5.3
, using the fact that bounded losses are sub-Gaussian, we would have also achieved a high-probability bound, but then the constant in the slow-rate term would have been
(b-a)^{2}
instead of
\sigma^{2}
, and it is often the case that
\sigma^{2}\ll(b-a)^{2}
.

Bernstein’s inequality can be slightly improved. This improved version is referred to as
Bennett’s inequality

[
71
, Exercise 2.7]
and states that

\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\mathrm{gen}(w,S)\right|\geq t\right]\leq 2e^{\frac{-n\sigma^{2}}{(b-a)^{2}}\phi\left(\frac{(b-a)t}{\sigma^{2}}\right)},

(64)

where
\phi(u)=(1+u)\log(u)-u
, and essentially recovers Bernstein’s inequality by noting that
\phi(u)\geq\frac{u^{2}}{2(1+\nicefrac{{u}}{{3}})}

[
82
, Exercise 2.8]
. Another improvement of this inequality is its empirical version, the
empirical Bernstein inequality

[
220
, Exercise 7.6]
, where the variance is estimated and is not assumed to be known.

Proposition 5.5

(Adaptation of the empirical Bernstein’s inequality
[
220
, Exercise 7.6]
)
.

Consider a loss
\ell(w,Z)
with a range bounded in
[a,b]
and let
\hat{\Sigma}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\ell(W,Z_{i})-\widehat{{R}}(W,S))^{2}
be an estimate of the loss’ variance. Then, for all
\beta\in(0,1)
and all
w\in\mathcal{W}
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathrm{gen}(w,S)\leq\frac{3(b-a)\log\frac{3}{\delta}}{n}+\sqrt{\frac{2\hat{\Sigma}^{2}\log\frac{3}{\beta}}{n}}.

Finally, we conclude this section on classical concentration inequalities with the
small-kl
inequality. For this bound, let us consider, without loss of generality, as we can always re-scale and center, losses with a range bounded in
[0,1]
. Then, employing Markov’s inequality (
62
) once again with
X=e^{n\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\widehat{{R}}(w,S)\|{R}(w))}
results in

\mathbb{P}\left[e^{n\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\widehat{{R}}(w,S)\|{R}(w)}\geq t\right]\leq\frac{1}{t}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[e^{n\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\widehat{{R}}(w,S)\|{R}(w))}\right],

where we recall that
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\hat{r}\|r)=\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathrm{Ber}(\hat{r})\|\mathrm{Ber}(r))
was defined in
Section

13.2
.
Maurer [149, Theorem 1]
characterized the second factor in the right-hand-side of the inequality, and we noted that following an analysis from
Germain et al. [150, Lemma 19]
it could be confined in the range
[\sqrt{n},\sqrt{2n+2}]
. Therefore, re-organizing the terms, letting
\xi(n)
be the said therm, and letting
\beta=\nicefrac{{\xi(n)}}{{t}}
results in the following result.

Proposition 5.6

(Adaptation of the
small-kl inequality
[
221
, Section 2.5]
)
.

Consider a loss with a range bounded in
[0,1]
. Then, for all
\beta\in(0,1)
and all
w\in\mathcal{W}
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\widehat{{R}}(w,S)\|{R}(w)\big)\leq\frac{\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}.

Although the bound may appear complicated, it states that the empirical risk and the population risk get “close” at a fast rate, where the “closeness” is measured by
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{KL}}
. Applying Pinsker’s inequality from
Lemma

2.2
or different relaxation of the stronger
Marton [222]
’s bound (see
[
221
, Corollaries 2.19 and 2.20]
) can give us a better idea of the rate that this bound implies. In this case, we can use our developments from
Section

13.2
to note that the small-kl inequality is equivalent to the following result.

Proposition 5.7
.

Consider a loss with a range bounded in
[0,1]
. Then, for all
\beta\in(0,1)
and all
w\in\mathcal{W}
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

{R}(w)\leq c\gamma\log\left(\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}\right)\cdot\widehat{{R}}(w,S)+\frac{c\gamma\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}+\gamma\kappa(c),

simultaneously
for all
c\in(0,1]
and all
\gamma>1
, where
\kappa(c)\coloneqq 1-c(1-\log c)
.

Proposition

5.7
tells us that, when the loss is bounded, we can expect a
high-probability fast-rate bound
. The caveat is that now the empirical risk is not used as the estimator of the population risk, but a scaled and re-centered version of it.

2
The Origin of PAC-Bayesian Bounds

PAC-Bayesian bounds were originally introduced by
Shawe-Taylor et al. [13]
in the context of
binary classification and SRM in the realizable setting
.
18
18

18

To be precise, results of this type can be traced back even to the MDL principle from
Rissanen [14]
and its connection to the population risk from
[
15
,
16
,
17
]
.
Recall from
Section

9
, that in SRM we decompose the hypothesis class
\mathcal{W}
into a countable union of hypothesis classes
\mathcal{W}=\cup_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{W}_{k}
such that
\mathcal{W}_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{W}_{k+1}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and where the complexity of each class is non-decreasing in
k
. In particular,
Shawe-Taylor et al. [13]
considered that hypothesis classes with an increasing VC dimension, that is,
\mathrm{VCdim}(\mathcal{W}_{k})=k
. Under this setting, the authors assigned a “preference” to each of the hypothesis classes determined by a probability distribution
\mathbb{Q}
: larger values of
\mathbb{Q}[k]
meant a larger preference for the hypothesis class
\mathcal{W}_{k}
. Then, they could show that for every hypothesis
w\in\mathcal{W}_{k}
, with probability no less than
1-\beta

{R}(w,S)\leq 2\widehat{{R}}(w,S)+\frac{-\log\mathbb{Q}[k]+4k\log\frac{2en^{2}}{k}}{n}.

This allowed the learner to enjoy the generalization guarantees from learning with a class of a finite VC dimension
k
while searching through a much larger class of hypotheses at the expense of the extra term
-\nicefrac{{\log\mathbb{Q}[k]}}{{n}}
coming from the preference given to classes with the said VC dimension.

This result was later extended by
McAllester [18]
,
McAllester [19]
,
McAllester [20]
in several ways. First
[
18
]
, he noted that as long as the loss has a bounded range and hypothesis class is discrete, that is,
\mathcal{W}=\cup_{k=1}^{\infty}\{w_{k}\}
, one can give a preference
\mathbb{Q}[w_{k}]
to each of the hypotheses
w
and combine Hoeffding’s inequality (
63
) and the union bound to recover the results from MDL principle of (
16
) in
Section

9
. Later
[
19
,
20
]
, he realized that one does not need to consider only the realization of the algorithm’s output, but can consider the algorithm as a Markov kernel
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
(very much like we did throughout
Chapter

4
, although this happened years prior to those developments). With these realizations, one can consider the expected population and empirical risks of the algorithm’s output
after
observing the training data, namely
\mathbb{E}^{S}[{R}(W)]
and
\mathbb{E}^{S}[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)]
respectively. Moreover, in this way, one does not need to consider a countable hypothesis space. It is enough to consider a “preference” distribution
\mathbb{Q}
on the hypothesis space
\mathcal{W}
and a randomized algorithm with an associated Markov kernel
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
that returns distributions on the hypothesis space after observing the data. Then, the generalization error, instead of increasing by the inverse of the probability of the selected hypothesis as in (
16
), increases by the discrepancy of the algorithm’s output hypothesis distribution with respect to the preference distribution, that is,
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})
. However, for discrete hypothesis classes and deterministic algorithms, that is,
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}=\mathbb{I}_{\{w=w_{s}\}}(w)
, this formulation still recovers the results from the MDL principle of (
16
) since
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{I}_{\{w=w_{s}\}}\|\mathbb{Q})=-\log\mathbb{Q}[w_{s}]
.

Theorem 5.1

(Adaptation of
McAllester [20, Theorem 2]
)
.

Consider a loss with a range bounded in
[a,b]
and let
\mathbb{Q}
be distribution on
\mathcal{W}
independent of
S
. Then, for all
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq(b-a)\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{2n}}

holds
simultaneously
for all posteriors
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
.

The term
\xi(n)\in[\sqrt{2n},\sqrt{2n}+2]
is the same discussed previously in
Proposition

5.6
. This version of the theorem is slightly tighter than the original formulation, and we will see how to obtain it next in
Section

3
.

The term
PAC-Bayesian
originated from
McAllester [18]
,
McAllester [19]
,
McAllester [20]
. He noted that the preference distribution
\mathbb{Q}
had a resemblance to the
prior
distribution in Bayesian statistics. It has to be chosen before observing the training data and running the algorithm, and based on our
prior
knowledge of which might be the hypothesis achieving a small risk. Similarly, the algorithm’s output distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
was reminiscent to the
posterior
distribution in that framework since it is the algorithm’s distribution
after
observing the training data. However, there are important, key differences between the two frameworks, which sometimes cause confusion. As clarified by
Rivasplata [213]
: “The PAC-PAC-Bayes prior acts as an analytical device and may or may not be used by the algorithm, and the PAC-Bayes posterior is unrestricted and so it may be different from the posterior that would be obtained through Bayesian inference”. Despite these differences, in the PAC-Bayes community, the term
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
is still referred to as the
posterior
and term
\mathbb{Q}
as the
prior
, and we will continue to use these terms throughout the monograph. Similarly, we will often refer to the relative entropy
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})
as the
dependency term
and to the ratio
\frac{1}{n}\big(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}\big)
as the (normalized)
complexity term
, paying tribute
to the resemblance of the term to the hypothesis class complexity term from the classical generalization theory (
Section

7
).
20
20

20

In
[
142
]
, we refer to the term as the
dependence-confidence term
, to disambiguate and clarify better its components.

Another important thing to note from
Theorem

5.1
is that it holds
simultaneously
for all posteriors
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
. Therefore, after observing a training set
s\in\mathcal{Z}^{n}
, we may readily optimize the algorithm (which is characterized by its posterior) to minimize the PAC-Bayesian bound. That is, we may optimize both the empirical risk and the generalization error bound simultaneously.

Note how
Theorem

5.1
is essentially a PAC-Bayesian analogue of Hoeffding’s inequality (
63
). The main difference is the appearance of the relative entropy term
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})
, which measures the discrepancy between the posterior distribution and the prior. Recalling the intuition behind the relative entropy from
Section

4.2
, for a fixed training set
s
, this term measures the inefficiency of assuming that the hypothesis
W
will be distributed according to the prior distribution
\mathbb{Q}
, when in reality it is distributed according to the posterior
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}
. Since the prior distribution can be chosen arbitrarily, assume that the prototypical distribution on samples from the data distribution
\mathbb{Q}=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\circ\mathbb{P}_{S}
is chosen. Then, this term tells us how much the algorithm’s output depends on the particular realization of the training set, connecting the generalization error with
overfitting
, a phenomenon occurring when the output hypothesis describes very well the training data but fails to describe the underlying distribution.

The similarity to the generalization bounds from
Chapter

4
, particularly to
Theorem

4.1
, is natural. Both results can be obtained using the Donsker and Varadhan lemma or the Gibbs variational principle from
Lemma

2.1
. However, to obtain PAC-Bayesian bounds there are a couple of subtleties that one needs to take into account, as we will see shortly in the following sections.

Since the development of this result, many works in the field have focused on two main tasks: (i) refining the bound to better characterize the population risk for bounded losses and (ii) extending this bound relaxing their assumptions or their setting.
Section

3
will focus on the first task, while
Sections

4
and
6
will focus on the second one.

Limitations of the Relative Entropy as the Dependency Measure.

The relative entropy is a convenient measure of the dependency of the hypothesis returned by the algorithm and the training set. It has interesting information-theoretic interpretations, it has connections to classical approaches to generalization, and it is easy to manipulate. However, it has its shortcomings. First, if the hypothesis space is continuous and the prior distribution does not have a pmf, then it cannot accommodate deterministic algorithms. A solution to this problem is to quantize the hypothesis class and reduce the framework to the MDL as done by
Lotfi et al. [106]
. However, similarly to what happened with the mutual information, there are other situations where the generalization error is small and the relative entropy is large, leading to vacuous guarantees. Some examples of this issue, including some developed by us, are given in
[
134
,
135
,
136
]
. To deal with these problems, a relatively new research path consists of substituting the relative entropy as the dependency measure by different metrics like other
f
-divergences
[
10
,
11
,
223
]
, Rényi divergences
[
160
,
10
,
12
]
, or integral probability metrics like the Wasserstein distance
[
224
,
225
,
226
,
227
]
.

Focus on High-Probability Bounds.

In this chapter we focus on high-probability bounds, that is, bounds whose dependence on the probability parameter
\beta
is logarithmic. One could easily translate the bounds in expectation from
Chapter

4
using Markov’s inequality (
62
) or other techniques like the monitor technique from
Bassily et al. [129]
. For example, applying Markov’s inequality using
Theorem

4.19
ensures that, if the loss
\ell(\cdot,z)
is
L
-Lipschitz for every
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, then, for every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\frac{2L}{\beta}\cdot\mathbb{W}_{\rho}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S},\mathbb{Q}).

However, these bounds are not of high probability and thus are not considered.

Focus on the Standard Setting.

Like for the guarantees in expectation from
Chapter

4
, one can also consider the randomized-subsample setting from
Section

14
to derive new PAC-Bayesian bounds. This has been studied, for example, by
Hellström and Durisi [12]
,
Hellström and Durisi [144]
and
Grunwald et al. [143]
. However, these bounds hold for a realization of the supersample
\tilde{s}\in\mathcal{Z}^{n\times 2}
and the indices
u\in\{0,1\}^{n}
. This is often impractical for the desiderata 2. and 3. of PAC-Bayesian bounds discussed in the introduction of this chapter. The reason is that if
n
test instances are available, then classical concentration inequalities will always be tighter than PAC-Bayesian bounds derived from the randomized subsample setting. This is why we only consider PAC-Bayesian bounds derived from the standard setting in this chapter. Nonetheless, it is worth mentioning that these kinds of bounds are still useful to gain some understanding:
Grunwald et al. [143]
showed these bounds guarantee the generalization of hypothesis classes with a finite VC dimension and for compression schemes and
Hellström and Durisi [144]
made a similar remark for hypothesis classes with a finite Natarajan dimension.

3
PAC-Bayesian Bounds for Bounded Losses

There are many important inequalities in the PAC-Bayesian literature, especially in the case where the loss is bounded. These bounds are often presented for losses with a range in
[0,1]
, which includes the interesting 0–1 loss for classification tasks. For the rest of this section, we will keep this convention, always having in mind that one can scale and center the losses to lay in the desired range.

The Seeger–Langford
[
22
,
21
]
and
Catoni [24, Theorem 1.2.6]
’s bounds are known to be (two of) the tightest bounds in this setting, see, for example, the discussion in
[
228
]
. Both of them can be derived from
Germain et al. [159, Theorem 2.1]
’s convex function bound. Below we state the extension from
Bégin et al. [229]
that lifts the double absolute continuity requirement from the original statement noted by
Haddouche et al. [34]
. The original statement from
Bégin et al. [229]
only considers bounded losses and convex functions from
[0,1]^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
. Since these assumptions are not needed in the proof, we will state the theorem in a more general form, which will be useful later in
Section

4
.

Theorem 5.2

(Extension of
Bégin et al. [160, Theorem 4]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{Q}
be any prior independent of
S
and let
W^{\prime}
be distributed according to
\mathbb{Q}
. Then, for every convex function
f:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathbb{E}\big[\exp\big(nf\big(\widehat{{R}}(W^{\prime},s),{R}(W^{\prime})\big)\big)\big]<\infty
for all
s\in\mathcal{Z}^{n}
, and every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\displaystyle f\big(\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big],

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[{R}(W)\big]\big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\bigg(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q}\big)+\log\frac{1}{\beta}+\log\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{nf\big(\widehat{{R}}(W^{\prime},S),{R}(W^{\prime})\big)}\Big]\bigg)

holds
simultaneously
for all posteriors
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
.

To reach this generic bound one can consider the Gibbs variational principle from
Lemma

2.1
(or the dual version of the Donsker and Varadhan lemma) once again.
For a fixed training set
s
, in (
5
), let the measurable space be
\mathcal{W}
, the measurable function be
nf(\widehat{{R}}(w,s),{R}(w))
and
\mathcal{P}{Q}(\mathcal{W})
be the set of all probability measures
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}
such that
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\ll\mathbb{Q}
. Then,

\displaystyle\log\mathbb{E}^{S=s}

\displaystyle\left[e^{nf(\widehat{{R}}(W^{\prime},s),{R}(W^{\prime}))}\right]=

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\in\mathcal{P}{Q}(\mathcal{W})}\left\{n\mathbb{E}^{S=s}\left[nf(\widehat{{R}}(W,s),{R}(w))\right]-\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\right\},

(65)

This equation becomes the result from
Theorem

5.2
after two considerations.
First, since the equation holds for all
s\in\mathcal{Z}^{n}
, then it holds for a random training set
S
almost surely. Therefore, we may apply Markov’s inequality (
62
) to the term inside the logarithm of the left-hand-side of (
65
). More precisely, let
X=\mathbb{E}^{S}[\exp(nf(\widehat{{R}}(W^{\prime},S),{R}(W^{\prime})))]
and
t=\nicefrac{{1}}{{\beta}}\cdot\mathbb{E}[\exp(nf(\widehat{{R}}(W^{\prime},S),{R}(W^{\prime})))]
, then

\mathbb{P}\left[\mathbb{E}^{S}\left[e^{nf(\widehat{{R}}(W^{\prime},S),{R}(W^{\prime}))}\right]\geq\frac{1}{\beta}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[e^{nf(\widehat{{R}}(W^{\prime},S),{R}(W^{\prime}))}\right]\right]\leq\beta.

In this way, we have that with probability no larger than
\beta

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\in\mathcal{P}{Q}(\mathcal{W})}\Big\{n\mathbb{E}^{S=s}\big[nf(\widehat{{R}}(W,s),{R}(w))

\displaystyle\big]-\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\Big\}

\displaystyle\geq\log\frac{1}{\beta}+\log\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{nf\big(\widehat{{R}}(W^{\prime},S),{R}(W^{\prime})\big)}\Big].

In other words, there exists a posterior
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\in\mathcal{P}{Q}(\mathcal{W})
a.s. such that, with probability no larger than
\beta

\displaystyle n\mathbb{E}^{S=s}\left[nf(\widehat{{R}}(W,s),{R}(w))\right]

\displaystyle-\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\geq\log\frac{1}{\beta}+\log\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{nf\big(\widehat{{R}}(W^{\prime},S),{R}(W^{\prime})\big)}\Big].

Second, we may include the expectation with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
inside of the convex function
f
maintaining the inequality due to Jensen’s inequality. Finally, we may re-arrange the equation to prove
Theorem

5.2
. Moreover, by the convention that
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})\to\infty
when
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\not\ll\mathbb{Q}
, the bound holds trivially in that case.

This general-purpose bound is useful as an appropriate choice of the convex function
f
can be used to recover different PAC-Bayesian bounds. For example, considering
f(\hat{r},r)=\frac{(2n-1)}{n}\cdot(\hat{r}-r)^{2}
recovers
McAllester [20]
’s bound from
Theorem

5.1
up to constants.
21
21

21

It is often mentioned in the literature that one can recover
Theorem

5.1
exactly by choosing
f(\hat{r},r)=2(\hat{r}-r)^{2}
. However, this does not allow us to use
McAllester [20]
’s proof technique.
Similarly, choosing
f(\hat{r},r)=\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\hat{r}\|r)
for
(\hat{r},r)\in[0,1]^{2}
recovers the improved Seeger–Langford bound
[
22
,
21
,
149
]
and choosing
f(\hat{r},r)=-\log\big(1-r(1-e^{\nicefrac{{-\lambda}}{{n}}})\big)-\nicefrac{{\lambda\hat{r}}}{{n}}
for
(\hat{r},r)\in[0,1]^{2}
recovers
Catoni [24, Theorem 1.2.6]
’s bound.

Theorem 5.3

(Improved Seeger–Langford bound
[
22
,
21
,
149
]
)
.

Consider a loss with a range bounded in
[0,1]
and let
\mathbb{Q}
be any prior independent of
S
. Then, for every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{E}^{S}[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)]\|\mathbb{E}^{S}[{R}(W)]\big)\leq\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}

holds
simultaneously
for all posteriors
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
.

Theorem 5.4

(
Catoni [24, Theorem 1.2.6]
)
.

Consider a loss with a range bounded in
[0,1]
and let
\mathbb{Q}
be any prior independent of
S
. Then, for every
\lambda>0
and every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq\frac{1}{1-e^{-\frac{\lambda}{n}}}\Bigg(1-e^{-\frac{\lambda\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]}{n}-\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n}}\Bigg).

holds
simultaneously
for all posteriors
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
.

Recall
Proposition

5.6
. The Seeger–Langford bound
[
21
,
22
]
is a PAC-Bayes analogue of the
small-kl
concentration inequality. However, it is hindered by its lack of interpretability. Moreover, it is difficult to optimize the posterior to minimize the bound and find an appropriate posterior
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
. This is due to the non-convexity of the bound with respect to the posterior
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}

[
26
]
, as well as the fact that it cannot be expressed explicitly as a function of the empirical risk
\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]
and the
dependency term
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})

[
159
]
. On the other hand, while
Catoni [24]
’s bound is minimized by the Gibbs posterior
\mathsf{p}_{W}^{S}(w)\propto\mathsf{q}(w)e^{-\lambda\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\widehat{{R}}(w,S)\big]}
, it still lacks interpretability and depends on an arbitrary parameter
\lambda
that has to be chosen
before
the draw of the data.

To remedy these issues, several works relax the Seeger–Langford bound
[
22
,
21
]
using lower bounds on the relative entropy
[
25
,
26
,
27
]
. For example, one may recover
McAllester [20]
’s bound from
Theorem

5.1
exactly employing Pinsker’s inequality (
Lemma

2.2
). Then, as discussed in
Section

1.2
, one may instead use relaxations of the stronger
Marton [222]
’s bound like
[
221
, Corollaries 2.19 and 2.20]
).
Tolstikhin and Seldin [25]
use
[
221
, Corollary 2.20]
and
Thiemann et al. [26]
and
Rivasplata et al. [27]
use
[
221
, Corollary 2.19]
. This relaxation results in an intractable PAC-Bayes bound and, for this reason,
Thiemann et al. [26]
relax it further using the inequality
\sqrt{xy}\leq\frac{1}{2}(\lambda x+\nicefrac{{y}}{{\lambda}})
for all
\lambda>0
to obtain a
fast-rate
bound, and
Rivasplata et al. [27]
solve the resulting quadratic inequality for
\sqrt{\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[{R}(W)\big]}
to obtain a
mixed-rate
bound.

Theorem 5.5

(
Thiemann et al. [26, Theorem 3]
’s fast-rate bound)
.

Consider a loss function with a range bounded in
[0,1]
and let
\mathbb{Q}
be any prior independent of
S
. Then, for every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq\inf_{\lambda\in(0,2)}\Bigg\{\frac{\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]}{1-\frac{\lambda}{2}}+\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n\lambda(1-\frac{\lambda}{2})}\Bigg\}.

holds
simultaneously
for all posteriors
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
.

Theorem 5.6

(
Rivasplata et al. [27, Theorem 1]
’s mixed-rate bound)
.

Consider a loss function with a range bounded in
[0,1]
and let
\mathbb{Q}
be any prior independent of
S
. Then, for every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[

\displaystyle{R}(W)\big]\leq\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]+\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{2\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]\cdot\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}+\Bigg(\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}\Bigg)^{2}}.

holds
simultaneously
for all posteriors
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
.

Originally,
Rivasplata et al. [27]
present their bound in a different form, but this
mixed-rate
form shows explicitly the combination of a
fast-rate
term and an
amortized slow-rate
term. Moreover, this form makes it easy to see that the bound is tighter than
[
25
, Equation (3)]
, as their bound can be recovered using the inequality
\sqrt{x+y}\leq\sqrt{x}+\sqrt{y}
on
Theorem

5.6
.

In
[
142
]
, we noted that a strengthened version of
Catoni [24]
’s bound that holds
simultaneously
for all
\lambda>0
can be obtained from the Seeger–Langford
[
22
,
21
]
bound at the small cost of
\log\xi(n)
in the complexity term. The proof follows applying the Donsker and Varadhan variational representation of the relative entropy from
Lemma

2.1
to the binary relative entropy
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{KL}}
exactly like we did to prove
Theorem

4.4
in (
29
) of
Section

13.2
. This was also observed by
Germain et al. [159, Proposition 2.1]
and proved with different techniques to ours in
[
230
, Chapter 20]
and
[
228
, Lemmata E1 and E2]
, although it was not stated explicitly as a PAC-Bayesian bound.

Theorem 5.7
.

Consider a loss function with a range bounded in
[0,1]
and let
\mathbb{Q}
be any prior independent of
S
. Then, for every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq\frac{1}{1-e^{-\frac{\lambda}{n}}}\Bigg[1-e^{-\frac{\lambda\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]}{n}-\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}}\Bigg]

holds
simultaneously
for all posteriors
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
and all
\lambda>0
.

Note that the fact that the bound holds
simultaneously
for all
\lambda>0
implies that the bound can be equivalently written as: for all
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq\inf_{\lambda>0}\left\{\frac{1}{1-e^{-\frac{\lambda}{n}}}\Bigg[1-e^{-\frac{\lambda\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]}{n}-\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}}\Bigg]\right\}

holds
simultaneously
for all posteriors
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
.
This bound is an explicit expression of the Seeger–Langford bound
[
22
,
21
]
in terms of
\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]
and
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})
. Compared to
Catoni [24]
’s
Theorem

5.4
, this bound holds
simultaneously
for all
\lambda>0
, making it useful for finding numerical population risk certificates without the need to pay an extra price for the parameter search. It also allows for an iterative procedure for obtaining a good posterior by updating the posterior
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
and parameter
\lambda
alternately. We note that, contrary to the statement from the Seeger–Langford bound in
Theorem

5.3
, this statement tells us that the optimal posterior is given by the Gibbs distribution
\mathsf{p}_{W}^{S}(w)\propto\mathsf{q}(w)\cdot e^{-\lambda\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\widehat{{R}}(w,S)\big]}
. However, finding the global optimum for the parameter
\lambda
is tedious, and the function is not convex in that parameter.

Again, as we did to prove the fast-rate bound from
Theorem

4.3
of
Section

13.2
, we may apply the variational representation of the relative entropy borrowed from
f
-divergences (
Corollary

2.2
) using (
28
) to prove a PAC-Bayes analogue to the fast-rate bound we presented in
Proposition

5.7
. Similarly, we may follow the steps to show that
Theorem

4.4
and
Theorem

4.3
are equivalent. Namely, let
\lambda=n\log(\nicefrac{{\gamma}}{{\gamma-1}})
in
Theorem

5.7
, which implies that
\gamma>1
. Then, note that the function
1-e^{-x}
is a non-decreasing, concave, continuous function for
x>0
and therefore it can be upper bounded by its envelope, that is
1-e^{-x}=\inf_{a>0}\{e^{-a}x+1-e^{a}(1+a)\}
. Using this envelope in the equation of
Theorem

5.7
and letting
c=e^{-a}\in(0,1]
results in the following theorem.

Theorem 5.8

(Fast-rate bound)
.

Consider a loss function with a range bounded in
[0,1]
and let
\mathbb{Q}
be any prior independent of
S
. Then, for every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq c\gamma\log\Big(\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}\Big)\cdot\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]+c\gamma\cdot\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}+\kappa(c)\gamma,

holds
simultaneously
for all posteriors
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
, all
\gamma>1
, and all
c\in(0,1]
, where
\kappa(c)\coloneqq 1-c(1-\log c)
.

Remark 5.1
.

Note how if the loss
\ell
has a range bounded in
[0,b]
, as will be the case in
Section

4.2
, then
\tilde{\ell}=\nicefrac{{\ell}}{{b}}
has a range bounded in
[0,1]
. Then, the bound from
Theorem

5.8
can be trivially extended to this case first bounding the population risk of the scaled loss and then multiplying both sides of the inequality with
b
. To be precise, the right-hand side of
Theorem

5.8
equation would be

c\gamma\log\Big(\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}\Big)\cdot\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]+bc\gamma\cdot\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}+b\kappa(c)\gamma.

Therefore, the Seeger–Langford bound (
Theorem

5.3
), the strengthened Catoni’s bound (
Theorem

5.7
), and this fast-rate bound (
Theorem

5.8
) are equally tight. This is important since it means that the Seeger–Langford bound
[
22
,
21
]
can be exactly described with a linear combination of the empirical risk and the complexity term, where the coefficients of this combination and the bias vary depending on the data realization. This could have been hypothesized by observing the derivatives of the Seeger–Langford bound
[
22
,
21
]
from
Reeb et al. [231, Appendix A]
, and a proof is now available. Furthermore, the optimal posterior of this bound is given by the Gibbs distribution
\mathsf{p}_{W}^{S}(w)\propto\mathsf{q}(w)\cdot e^{-n\log\big(\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}\big)\widehat{{R}}(w,S)}
, where the value of
\gamma
depends on the dataset realization
s
.

We recall the influence of the parameters
\gamma
and
c
in the fast-rate bound from
Theorem

4.3
, as the discussion applies analogously here. The parameter
\gamma
controls the influence of the empirical risk compared to the normalized complexity: if the empirical risk is large relative to the normalized complexity, then
\gamma
is larger and the normalized complexity coefficient increases; if instead, the empirical risk is small or even close to interpolation, then
\gamma
is close to
1
and the empirical risk coefficient increases. In particular, for a fixed value of
c
, the optimal value of
\gamma
is

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=1+\left[-1-\mathtt{W}\left(-\exp\left(-1-\frac{c\cdot\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}+\kappa(c)}{c\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]}\right)\right)\right]^{-1}

\displaystyle\approx 1+\left[\sqrt{2\cdot\frac{c\cdot\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}+\kappa(c)}{c\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]}}+\frac{5}{6}\cdot\frac{c\cdot\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}+\kappa(c)}{c\cdot\mathbb{E}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]}\right]^{-1},

where
\mathtt{W}
is the Lambert W function and the
-1
branch is approximated following
[
173
]
.

The parameter
c\in(0,1]
controls how much weight is given to the empirical risk and normalized complexity terms compared to a bias. For larger values of the empirical risk and the normalized complexity term, the value of
c
is small, decreasing their contribution to the bound and increasing the contribution of the bias
\kappa(c)\in[0,1)
. If the empirical risk and the normalized complexity term are smaller, then the value of
c
approaches
1
, where the contribution of these two terms is only controlled by
\gamma
and the bias is
0
. In fact, a weaker version of
Theorem

5.8
can be obtained considering this small empirical risk and small normalized complexity regime by letting
c=1
.

Corollary 5.1
.

Consider a loss function with a range bounded in
[0,1]
and let
\mathbb{Q}
be any prior independent of
S
. Then, for every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq\gamma\log\Big(\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}\Big)\cdot\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[{R}(W,S)\big]+\gamma\cdot\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}

holds
simultaneously
for all posteriors
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
and all
\gamma>1
.

This bound improves upon
Thiemann et al. [26]
’s
Theorem

5.5
as it is tighter for all values of the empirical risk and the dependency measure (see
Appendix

5.A
). For instance, the value
\lambda=1
minimizes the multiplicative factor in the complexity term in
Theorem

5.5
. Letting
\gamma=2
in
Corollary

5.1
matches this factor and improves the multiplicative factor of the empirical risk from 2 to
2\log 2\approx 1.38
. Moreover, if we are in the
realizable setting
and
\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]=0
(that is, we are using an empirical risk minimizer), then letting
\gamma\to 1^{+}
in this bound reveals that the fast rate can be achieved with multiplicative factor
1
, clarifying that the complexity term completely characterizes the population risk in this regime. Note that this is neither clear in
Thiemann et al. [26]
’s nor
Rivasplata et al. [27]
’s bounds, where the multiplicative factor is 2.

However, substituting the value of the optimal
\gamma
into
Theorem

5.8
or
Corollary

5.1
does not produce an interpretable bound. Nonetheless, following the same steps that we used to obtain
Theorem

4.5
from
Corollary

4.1
, the bound in
Corollary

5.1
can be further relaxed to obtain a parameter-free mixed-rate bound that is tighter than
Rivasplata et al. [27]
’s mixed-rate and
Thiemann et al. [26]
’s fast-rate bounds (see
Appendix

5.A
).

Theorem 5.9

(Mixed-rate bound)
.

Consider a loss function with a range bounded in
[0,1]
and let
\mathbb{Q}
be any prior independent of
S
. Then, for every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[{R}(W)\big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]+\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}

\displaystyle+\sqrt{2\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]\cdot\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}}

holds
simultaneously
for all posteriors
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
.

The mixed-rate bound presented in
Theorem

5.9
provides a deeper insight into the relationship between the population risk, the empirical risk, and the complexity term. The bound grows linearly with both the empirical risk and the complexity term, with a correction term that reflects their interaction. Importantly, the bound is symmetric in these two terms, giving them equal importance. This may be beneficial for methods using PAC-Bayes bounds to optimize the posterior, such as PAC-Bayes with backprop
[
27
,
140
]
, where using the bound from
Thiemann et al. [26]
or
Rivasplata et al. [27]
alone may cause the algorithm to disregard posteriors farther from the prior but that achieve lower population risk (see
Appendix

5.B
).

Further Developments

Wu and Seldin [232]
derived a “split-kl” inequality that competes with the Seeger–Langford bound
[
22
,
21
]
for ternary losses and
Jang et al. [233]
proved an even tighter bound via “coin-betting”. However, their bounds are still neither easily interpretable nor directly aid in the selection of an appropriate posterior. Moreover, there are other advances on this front when further quantities are considered. If the variance is known,
Seldin et al. [215, Theorem 8]
and
Wu et al. [234, Theorem 9]
introduced, respectively, PAC-Bayes analogues to Bernstein and Bennett inequalities from
Proposition

5.4
and (
64
). The PAC-Bayes Bernstein inequality was later improved by further bounding the variance using an empirical estimate of that quantity
[
25
, Theorems 3 and 4]
similarly to
Proposition

5.5
. Finally,
Mhammedi et al. [235]
derived a PAC-Bayes analogue to the unexpected Bernstein inequality where they use an empirical estimate of the second moment.

4
PAC-Bayesian Bounds for Unbounded Losses

The goal of this section is to obtain PAC-Bayes analogues to classical concentration bounds or, at least, to find bounds with the same rates. First, in
Section

4.1
, we will show how to obtain a PAC-Bayes analogue of the Chernoff inequality from
Proposition

5.3
. The proofs will follow what we did in
Section

13.1
with two main differences: first, we will adapt the proof to the PAC-Bayesian setting by employing Markov’s inequality
Equation

62
in order to have probabilistic statements with respect to the CGF, similarly to what we did above for the proof of
Theorem

5.2
; and second, we will devise a technique to optimize data-dependent parameters in PAC-Bayesian bounds. This technique is of independent interest to optimize parameters that depend on random variables on any general probabilistic statement. Then, we will move on to losses with a bounded moment, where we can follow what we did in
Section

13.3
taking into account the same two considerations.

4.1
Losses With a Bounded CGF

Recall from
Proposition

5.3
, that if the loss has CGF bounded by
\psi
in the sense of
Definition

4.1
and the hypothesis
w
is fixed, the empirical risk concentrates around the population risk at a rate
\psi_{*}^{-1}\big(\frac{1}{n}\log\nicefrac{{1}}{{\beta}}\big)
. More generally, this is true as long as the hypothesis
W^{\prime}
is distributed according to some distribution
\mathbb{Q}
and it is independent of the training data
S
. The reason is that, as we say in
Section

13
, in this case, the empirical risk is an unbiased estimator of the population risk
\mathbb{E}[\widehat{{R}}(W^{\prime},S)]=\mathbb{E}[{R}(W^{\prime})]
and we can always evaluate the CGF of the empirical risk as

\Lambda_{-\widehat{{R}}(W^{\prime},S)}(n\lambda)=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{n\lambda\mathrm{gen}(W^{\prime},S))}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{w\sim\mathbb{Q}}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[e^{n\lambda\mathrm{gen}(w,S))}\right]\right],

and then
\Lambda_{-\widehat{{R}}(W^{\prime},S)}(n\lambda)\leq n\psi(\lambda)
.

Therefore, it seems reasonable to decouple again the returned hypothesis
W
from the training data
S
. To do so, we may either use the Donsker and Varadhan lemma or the Gibbs variational principle from
Lemma

2.1
again, or just take advantage of the general-purpose
Theorem

5.2
. In this case, consider the convex function
f(\hat{r},r)=\lambda(r-\hat{r})
for some
\lambda>0
. Then, for every
\beta\in(0,1)
and every
\lambda>0
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\lambda\left(\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\mathrm{gen}(w,S)\big]\right)\leq\frac{1}{n}\left(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{1}{\beta}+\log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{n\lambda\mathrm{gen}(W^{\prime},S)}\right]\right)

holds
simultaneously
for all posteriors
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
.

Finally, noting that if the loss has a CGF bounded by
\psi
, then
\Lambda_{-\widehat{{R}}(W^{\prime},S)}(n\lambda)\leq n\psi(\lambda)
and re-arranging the terms in the equation recovers the intermediate result from
Banerjee and Montúfar [54, Theorem 6]
.

Lemma 5.1

(
Banerjee and Montúfar [54, Theorem 6]
)
.

Consider a loss function
\ell
with a bounded CGF (
Definition

4.1
). Let
\mathbb{Q}
be any prior independent of
S
. Then, for every
\beta\in(0,1)
and every
\lambda\in(0,b)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\Bigg(\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n}+\psi(\lambda)\Bigg)

holds
simultaneously
for all posteriors
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
.

If we could optimize the parameter
\lambda
in
Lemma

5.1
, we would obtain a PAC-Bayes analogue to Chernoff’s inequality (
Proposition

5.3
). However, this is not possible since the optimal parameter depends on the data realization but needs to be selected
before
the draw of this data
[
54
, Remark 14]
.

To make this point clearer, fix a dataset
s
and assume that the infimum

\inf_{\lambda\in(0,b)}\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda}\Bigg(\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n}+\psi(\lambda)\Bigg)\right\}

is achieved by
\lambda_{s}
. Then,
Lemma

5.1
only guarantees that, with probability
1-\beta

\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{s}}\Bigg(\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n}+\psi(\lambda_{s})\Bigg)

for that
fixed
parameter
\lambda_{s}
and
simultaneously
for all posteriors
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}
. If the data realization is
s^{\prime}\neq s
, this may result in a looser bound. In order to make sure that we have a bound that holds
simultaneously
for all parameters
\lambda\in\{\lambda_{s}:s\in\mathcal{Z}^{n}\}
we need to take the union bound, which effectively renders the bound vacuous as the cardinality of the set
\mathcal{Z}^{n}
grows.

Next, we will present a technique that allows us to bypass this subtlety for a small penalty. The idea is simple: separate the event space into a set of events where the optimization can be performed, and then pay the union bound price. This can also be seen as optimizing over the set of parameters
\lambda
that will yield
almost optimal
bounds and paying the union bound price for considering that set. In this case, the event space is separated using a quantization based on the relative entropy
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\lVert\mathbb{Q})
.

There are other techniques to deal with these kinds of optimization problems, but we believe that they are not general enough or appropriate for this situation and are discussed in
Section

4.3
.

4.1.1
Optimizing Parameters in Probabilistic Statements

To start, note that the event
\{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})>n\}
is not interesting, given this event, the resulting bound from
Lemma

5.1
is non-decreasing with respect to the number of samples
n
. With this in mind, we may quantize the event
\mathcal{E}=\{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq n\}
into
n
disjoint sub-events, find an almost optimal parameter per each sub-event, and then combine the solutions to obtain the following result.

Theorem 5.10

(PAC-Bayes Chernoff analogue I)
.

Consider a loss function
\ell
with a bounded CGF (
Definition

4.1
). Let
\mathbb{Q}
be any prior independent of
S
and define the event
\mathcal{E}\coloneqq\{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq n\}
. Then, for every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[{R}(W)\big]\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{E}}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\big]+\psi_{*}^{-1}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{en}{\beta}}{n}\bigg)\Bigg)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{E}^{c}}\cdot\esssup\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[{R}(W)\big]

(66)

holds
simultaneously
for all posteriors
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
.

To prove this statement, let us first define
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}
as the complement of the event in
Lemma

5.1
, that is,

\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}\coloneqq\left\{\exists\ \mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}:\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]>\frac{1}{\lambda}\Bigg(\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n}+\psi(\lambda)\Bigg)\right\}.

Therefore, from
Lemma

5.1
we know that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}]<\beta
for all
\lambda\in(0,b)
. Now, let us quantize the event
\mathcal{E}
with the disjoint sub-events
\mathcal{E}_{1}\coloneqq\{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq 1\}
and
\mathcal{E}_{k}\coloneqq\{\lceil\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})\rceil=k\}
for all
k=2,\ldots,n
, thus forming a covering of the event
\mathcal{E}
. In order to avoid measurability issues and conditioning to events with probability zero, we can identify these events defining
\mathcal{K}\coloneqq\{k\in\mathbb{N}:1\leq k\leq n\textnormal{ and }\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{k}]>0\}
.

In this way, for all
k\in\mathcal{K}
, given the event
\mathcal{E}_{k}
, with probability no more than
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}|\mathcal{E}_{k}]
, there exists a posterior
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
such that

\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]>\frac{1}{\lambda}\bigg(\frac{k+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n}+\psi(\lambda)\bigg),

(67)

for all
\lambda\in(0,b)
. The right-hand side of (
67
) can be minimized with respect to
\lambda

independently of the training set
S
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{\lambda_{k}}
be the event resulting from this minimization and note that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}_{\lambda_{k}}]\leq\beta
. According to
Lemma

2.5
, this ensures that with probability no more than
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}_{\lambda_{k}}|\mathcal{E}_{k}]
, there exists a posterior
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
such that

\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]>\psi_{*}^{-1}\bigg(\frac{k+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n}\bigg),

(68)

where
\psi_{*}
is the convex conjugate of
\psi
and where
\psi_{*}^{-1}
is a non-decreasing concave function. Given
\mathcal{E}_{k}
, since
k<\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+1
, with probability no larger than
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}_{\lambda_{k}}|\mathcal{E}_{k}]
, there exists a posterior
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
such that

\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]>\psi_{*}^{-1}\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})+1+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n}\bigg).

Now, define
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
as the event stating that there exists a posterior
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{S}\big[{R}(W)]>
