[M]_{n}(w)\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}(S_{i},w)^{2}\textnormal{ and }\langle M\rangle_{n}(w)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}^{{F}_{i-1}}\mathbb{E}\big[X_{i}(S_{i},w)^{2}\big],

where
[M]_{n}(w)
acts as an empirical variance term and
\langle M\rangle_{n}(w)
as its theoretical counterpart
[
35
]
.
Then, their main anytime-valid bound for martingales is the following.

Theorem 5.26

(
Wang et al. [36, Theorem 2.4]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{Q}
be any prior independent of
S_{n}
and
(M_{n}(w))_{n\geq 1}
be any sequence of martingales indexed by
w\in\mathcal{W}
. Then, for all
\lambda>0
, all
\beta\in(0,1)
with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\Big|\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}\big[M_{n}(W)\big]\Big|\leq\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{n}}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{2(n+1)^{2}}{\beta}}{\lambda}+\frac{\lambda}{6}\cdot\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}\big[[M]_{n}(W)+2\langle M\rangle_{n}(W)\big]

(78)

holds
simultaneously
for all posteriors
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
and all
n\geq 1
.

Theorem 5.27

(
Haddouche and Guedj [35, Theorem 2.1]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{Q}
be any prior independent of
S_{n}
and
(M_{n}(w))_{n\geq 1}
be any sequence of martingales indexed by
w\in\mathcal{W}
. Then, for all
\lambda>0
, all
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\Big|\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}\big[M_{n}(W)\big]\Big|\leq\frac{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{n}}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{2}{\beta}}{\lambda}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}\big[[M]_{n}(W)+\langle M\rangle_{n}(W)\big]

holds
simultaneously
for all posteriors
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
and all
n\geq 1
.

In what follows, we will focus on the result from
Wang et al. [36]
as it has the smaller constants. Taking a closer look at
Theorem

5.26
, we realize it has a similar shape to
Lemma

5.1
for the particular case when the loss is sub-Gaussian, where the role of the sub-Gaussian parameter is taken by the sum of the “variance” terms
[M]_{n}(W)+2\langle M\rangle_{n}(W)
. Therefore, it appears we may directly employ the technique to derive the Chernoff analogue from the proof of
Theorem

5.10
. However, one needs to take into account the fact that the “optimal” parameter
\lambda
now depends on this “variance” terms, which are also dependent on the training set
S_{n}
and on the number of samples
n
.

To optimize the bound from
Theorem

5.26
we will then proceed in two steps. The first step is to optimize the parameter
\lambda
for a
fixed
number of samples
n
in a similar fashion to
Theorem

5.10
, which results in
Theorem

5.28
. Then, the second step is to extend this result to an anytime-valid bound using
Theorem

5.24
at a cost in the complexity term of
\mathcal{O}(\nicefrac{{\log n}}{{n}})
.

For the first step, define the event
\mathcal{B}_{n,\lambda}
as the complement of the event in (
78
) for a
fixed
number of samples
n
. Then, we can proceed similarly to the proof of
Theorem

5.10
noticing that, for each number of samples
n
, the complement of the event

\mathcal{E}_{n}\coloneqq\Big\{\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}\big[[M]_{n}(W)+2\langle M\rangle_{n}(W)\big]\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq n^{2}\Big\}

is uninteresting as the bound is non-vanishing given
\mathcal{E}_{n}^{c}
. This produces the following PAC-Bayes bound for a fixed number of samples
n
.

Theorem 5.28

(Parameter-free bound on martingales)
.

Let
\mathbb{Q}
be any prior independent of
S_{n}
and
(M_{n}(w))_{n\geq 1}
be any sequence of martingales indexed by
w\in\mathcal{W}
. Further, define
\xi^{\prime}(n)\coloneqq 2en(n+1)^{2}\log(en)\leq 2e(n+1)^{3}
. Then, for every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}\big[

\displaystyle M_{n}(W)\big]\Big|\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{E}_{n}}\cdot\frac{2}{\sqrt{6}}\cdot\sqrt{\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}\big[[M]_{n}(W)+\langle M\rangle_{n}(W)+1\big]\Big(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{n}}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{\xi^{\prime}(n)}{\beta}\Big)}

\displaystyle+\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{E}_{n}^{c}}\cdot\esssup\Big|\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}\big[M_{n}(W)\big]\Big|

holds
simultaneously
for all posteriors
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
, where
\mathcal{E}_{n}
is the event

\mathcal{E}_{n}\coloneqq\Big\{\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}\big[[M]_{n}(W)+2\langle M\rangle_{n}(W)\big]\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{W}_{W})\leq n^{2}\Big\}.

Proof.

Consider a fixed number of samples
n
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{n,\lambda}
be the complement of the event in (
78
) such that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}_{n,\lambda}]<\beta
and consider the sub-events

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{n,1,l}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{n}}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq 1\textnormal{ and }\left\lceil\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}\big[[M]_{n}(W)+2\langle M\rangle_{n}(W)\big]\right\rceil=l\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{n,k,1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\left\lceil\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{n}}\|\mathbb{Q})\right\rceil=k\textnormal{ and }\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}\big[[M]_{n}(W)+2\langle M\rangle_{n}(W)\big]\leq 1\right\},\textnormal{ and}

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{n,k,l}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{\left\lceil\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{n}}\|\mathbb{Q})\right\rceil=k\textnormal{ and }\left\lceil\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}\big[[M]_{n}(W)+2\langle M\rangle_{n}(W)\big]\right\rceil=l\right\},

for all
k,l=2,\cdots,n^{2}
such that
kl\leq n^{2}
, which form a covering of
\mathcal{E}_{n}
. Furthermore, define
\mathcal{K}\coloneqq\{(k,l):1\leq kl\leq n^{2}\textnormal{ and }\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{n,k,l}]>0\}
. For all pairs of indices
(k,l)\in\mathcal{K}
, given the event
\mathcal{E}_{n,k,l}
, with probability no more than
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}_{n,\lambda}|\mathcal{E}_{n,k,l}]
, there exists a posterior
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{n}}
such that

\Big|\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}\big[M_{n}(W)\big]\Big|>\frac{k+\log\frac{2(n+1)^{2}}{\beta}}{\lambda}+\frac{\lambda}{6}\cdot l.

(79)

for all
\lambda\in(0,b)
. The parameter that optimizes the right-hand side of (
79
) is

\lambda=\lambda_{k,l}=\sqrt{\frac{6}{l}\Big(k+\log\frac{2(n+1)^{2}}{\beta}\Big)}.

Substituting the optimal
\lambda_{k,l}
and using that
k\leq\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{n}}\|\mathbb{Q})+1
and
l\leq\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}\big[[M]_{n}(W)+2\langle M\rangle_{n}(W)+1\big]
yields that, given the event
\mathcal{E}_{n,k,l}
, with probability smaller or equal than
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}_{n,\lambda_{k,l}}|\mathcal{E}_{n,k,l}]
, there exists a posterior
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{n}}
such that

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}\big[

\displaystyle M_{n}(W)\big]\Big|>

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\sqrt{6}}\cdot\sqrt{\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}\big[[M]_{n}(W)+\langle M\rangle_{n}(W)+1\big]\Big(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{n}}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{2e(n+1)^{2}}{\beta}\Big)}.

Now, define
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{n}
as the event stating that there exists a posterior
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{n}}
such that

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}\big[

\displaystyle M_{n}(W)\big]\Big|>

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{E}}\cdot\frac{2}{\sqrt{6}}\cdot\sqrt{\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}\big[[M]_{n}(W)+2\langle M\rangle_{n}(W)+1\big]\Big(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{n}}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{2e}{\beta}\Big)}

\displaystyle+\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{E}}\cdot\esssup|\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}M_{n}(W)|,

where
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{n}|\mathcal{E}_{n,k,l}]\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{n,k,l}]\leq\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}_{n,\lambda_{k,l}}|\mathcal{E}_{n,k,l}]\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{n,k,l}]\leq\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}_{n,\lambda_{k,l}}]<\beta
for all
(k,l)\in\mathcal{K}
, and where
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{n}\cap\mathcal{E}_{n}^{c}]=0
by the definition of the essential supremum. Therefore, by the law of total probability, the probability of
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{n}
is bounded as

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{n}]=\sum_{(k,l)\in\mathcal{K}}\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{n}|\mathcal{E}_{n,k,l}]\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{n,k,l}]+\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{n}\cap\mathcal{E}_{n}^{c}]<n(1+\log n)\beta=n\log(en)\beta.

Finally, let
\beta_{n}=n\log(en)\beta
so that, with probability no larger than
\beta_{n}
, there exists a posterior
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{n}}
such that

\displaystyle\Big|

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}\big[M_{n}(W)\big]\Big|>

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{E}}\cdot\frac{2}{\sqrt{6}}\cdot\sqrt{\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}\big[[M]_{n}(W)+2\langle M\rangle_{n}(W)+1\big]\Big(\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S_{n}}\|\mathbb{Q})+\log\frac{\xi^{\prime}(n)}{\beta_{n}}\Big)}

\displaystyle+\mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{E}}\cdot\esssup|\mathbb{E}^{S_{n}}M_{n}(W)|.

Finally, the substitution
\beta_{n}\leftarrow\beta
completes the proof.
∎

This technique can be extended to the corollary bound of
Haddouche and Guedj [35]
for batch learning with i.i.d. data yielding
Theorem

5.15
, where we write
S_{n}=S
to simplify the reading in the main text. Note again that we are using the particularization of
Haddouche and Guedj [35]
with the constants from
Wang et al. [36]
.

Finally, for the second step,
Theorems

5.28
and
5.15
can be converted back to anytime-valid bounds using
Theorem

5.24
. The resulting bound is exactly the same substituting
\log\xi^{\prime}(n)
for
\log\xi^{\prime\prime}(n)
, where
\xi^{\prime\prime}(n)\coloneqq\nicefrac{{e\pi^{2}(n+1)^{2}n^{3}\log(en)}}{{3}}
.

In case that one desires to have a bound without a
\log n
term, one may consider employing the technique outlined for
Theorem

5.11
with
Haddouche and Guedj [35]
’s
Theorem

5.27
instead of
Wang et al. [36]
’s
Theorem

5.26
.

Chapter 6
Privacy and Generalization

In this chapter, we discuss further the relationships between privacy and generalization that we already pointed out in
Section

10.2
and throughout the manuscript. To be precise, in
Chapters

4
and
5
, we saw that the generalization error is bounded from above by a function that depends on the dependence of the algorithm’s output hypothesis and the training data. Moreover, a learning algorithm is private if its output does not reveal much about the instances of the training set. Therefore, if an algorithm is private, the dependence of the algorithm’s output and the training data is small and it generalizes.

In
Section

1
, we start reviewing two different frameworks for privacy:
maximal leakage

[
40
]
and
differential privacy

[
43
,
44
]
. We choose to review these frameworks since they have attractive operational meanings. Maximal leakage refers to the highest potential increase in an attacker’s probability to guess a sensitive variable from your training set after observing the output hypothesis, as opposed to before observation. Differential privacy, on the other hand, captures how close is the probability of observing the output hypothesis if instead of using the real training set, we used any other set differing in one instance.

Then, in
Section

2
, we show how algorithms with a bounded maximal leakage generalize. To do so, we relate how a bounded maximal leakage implies that the quantities employed in
Chapters

4
and
5
are also bounded. The results from this section are not published and we consider them folklore within the community. We see that these results coincide with other results in the community
[
10
,
12
]
.

After that, in
Section

3
, we study how differentially private algorithms generalize. First, as before, we consider a simple relationship between the parameters of differentially private algorithms and the quantities employed in
Chapters

4
and
5
. With this simple analysis, we obtain bounds that depend on the privacy parameter, but that do not decrease with the number of samples. That is, the bounds only guarantee that the algorithm generalizes if the privacy parameter decreases with the number of samples on the training set. These simple bounds are of the same order and similar to the bounds from the literature that we survey in
Section

3.2
. In
Section

3.1
, we study the particular case of differentially private algorithms that are
permutation invariant
and operate on
discrete data
. In this case, we first show that any permutation invariant algorithm generalizes, and then we show that if the algorithm is also differentially private, then the generalization guarantees are tighter. Despite their advantage in the asymptotic regime, these bounds are only preferred to the standard ones when the ratio between the data cardinality and the number of samples is small, that is, when
|\mathcal{Z}|\ll n
. This part of the section is mostly based on our paper
[
126
]
, although it is updated based on the (chronologically posterior) developments from
Chapters

4
and
5
.

We wrote this chapter, like the previous ones, with a didactic intent. We wanted to strengthen the intuition we obtained throughout the manuscript that private algorithms generalize as well as survey some of the current understanding on the topic.

1
A Short Review on Privacy Frameworks

Recall from
Section

4.5
that a
privacy mechanism

\mathbb{M}
is a channel, described by the Markov kernel
\mathbb{P}_{Y}^{X}
, that processes an input
X
and generates a sanitized version
Y
that is not informative about the input
X
. In the context of machine learning, we say that an algorithm
\mathbb{A}
, described by the Markov kernel
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
, is a
private algorithm
if the hypothesis is not informative about the training instances. That is, an algorithm
\mathbb{A}
is a private algorithm if it is a privacy mechanism.

Constructing a private algorithm is simple. The algorithm
\mathbb{A}(S)=0
a.s. is maximally private, as the returned hypothesis
0
contains no information about the training set. In general, any algorithm independent of the training data such that
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}=\mathbb{Q}
has this property. However, these algorithms are not useful. The goal of a private algorithm is to balance the so-called
privacy-utility trade-off
, where the returned hypothesis should be informative about the underlying structure of the training data related to the downstream task (high utility), while not being informative about the specific instances in the training set (high privacy).

While the definition of utility is task-specific, the definition of privacy can be agnostic to the downstream task. Therefore, there have been several frameworks that attempt to quantify the amount of privacy guaranteed by an algorithm
[
40
,
63
,
64
,
43
,
44
,
59
,
60
]
. In this manuscript, we will only discuss
maximal leakage

[
40
,
63
,
64
]
and
differential privacy

[
43
,
44
]
due to their operational meaning.

1.1
Maximal Leakage

Consider that there is a sensitive, discrete variable
U
that depends on the training data. If an algorithm is private, one would expect that the probability of correctly guessing the sensitive variable
U
when having access to the returned hypothesis
W
is close to the probability of correctly guessing it without access. Moreover, this should be true for every possible discrete variable
U
that depends on the training data, as we may consider every such description as sensitive.

Issa et al. [40]
formalized this idea and defined the
maximal leakage
as the supremum of the logarithm of the ratio of these two probabilities with respect to every such variable. Namely,

\mathcal{L}(S\to W)\coloneqq\sup_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{S}}\log\frac{\sup_{\mathbb{P}_{\hat{U}}^{W}}\mathbb{P}\big[U=\hat{U}\big]}{\max_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\mathbb{P}\big[U=u\big]},

where the supremum over the Markov kernels
\mathbb{P}_{U}^{S}
characterizes the worst-case sensitive variable
U
, and the supremum over the Markov kernels
\mathbb{P}_{\hat{U}}^{W}
characterizes the best attacker trying to guess the sensitive variable.

Instead of considering a sensitive, discrete variable
U
, one could think of an arbitrary gain function
g:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{U}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
representing the adversary’s objective. For example, as described by
Saeidian et al. [63, Examples 1-4]
, the gain function can describe multiple common situations such as:

•

Membership inference
. Imagine that the adversary wants to infer if an individual
i
belongs to the training set
s
. Then, we may partition the training set according to the participation of the said individual. That is,
s_{1}=\{z\in s:z\textnormal{ contains the data of }i\}
and
s_{0}=s\setminus s_{1}
. Then, the adversary does a binary guess
\mathcal{U}=\{0,1\}
and their gain function can be
g(s,u)=\mathbb{I}_{\{s=s_{u}\}}(s,u)
.

•

Guessing a function of the training data
. Like in the maximal leakage described above, we may think of a sensitive variable
U
that is related by a (possible randomized) function
f
to the training data, that is
U=f(S)
. In this case, consider that
U
lies in some metric space
(\mathcal{U},\rho)
. Then, the gain function can simply be
g(s,u)=\rho\big(f(s),u\big)
.

As for maximal leakage, we may consider that an algorithm is private if the expected gain when having access to the returned hypothesis is close to the expected gain without access. Also, this should be true for every possible gain function, as we want to protect the data from every possible attack.

Mário et al. [41]
formalized this idea and defined the
maximal
g
-leakage
as the supremum of the logarithm of the ratio of these two expected gains with respect to every such gain function. Namely,

\sup_{g}\mathcal{L}_{g}(S\to W)\coloneqq\sup_{g}\log\frac{\mathbb{E}\big[\max_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\mathbb{E}^{W}\big[g(S,u)\big]\big]}{\max_{u\in\mathcal{U}}\mathbb{E}[g(S,u)]}.

Interestingly, these two privacy measures are equivalent
[
40
,
42
]
and are equal to the essential supremum of the Rényi divergence of order
\infty
of the algorithm’s channel with respect to the marginal distribution of the hypothesis, that is

\mathcal{L}(S\to W)=\esssup_{\mathbb{P}_{S}}\mathrm{D}_{\infty}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}).

If the data is discrete, the maximal leakage also coincides with other operational privacy notions as the
min-capacity
of the
min-entropy leakage

[
241
,
39
]
and the
multiplicative Bayesian capacity
of the
multiplicative Bayesian leakage

[
242
]
.

1.2
Differential Privacy

Another operationalization of privacy is given by
differential privacy
(DP)
[
43
,
44
]
. An algorithm is
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP if for every pair of neighbouring training sets
s
and
s^{\prime}
and every event
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{W}

\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}[\mathcal{A}]\leq e^{\varepsilon}\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s^{\prime}}[\mathcal{A}]+\delta,

where we recall that two training sets are neighbours if they differ in at most one element. If
\delta=0
, then the algorithm is just
\varepsilon
-DP.
The latter setting, when
\delta=0
, is often referred to as
pure DP
, while the former, when
\delta>0
, is referred to as
approximate DP
. The reason is that, although
\delta
cannot be exactly mapped to the probability of failure
[
243
]
, it is often understood as such. That is, as the probability that the
\varepsilon
-DP guarantee does not hold.

As discussed in
Section

10.2
, this notion of privacy states that an algorithm is private if it is stable. That is, an algorithm is DP if, when presented with two neighbouring training sets, the algorithm produces two hypotheses with “similar” distributions. Therefore, if we consider that each instance of the training set belongs to one individual, DP guarantees that the impact of the individual on the output hypothesis is small. In fact, consider the real training set
s
and any other neighbour training set
s^{\prime}
. Then, the Type I and Type II errors of every hypothesis test to distinguish if the outcome of the algorithm comes from
s
or
s^{\prime}
are bounded from below by quantities depending on the privacy parameters
\varepsilon
and
\delta

[
62
,
244
,
245
]
.

The hypothesis testing interpretation of DP led
Dong et al. [246]
to define
f
-differential privacy
(
f
-DP). In this framework, an algorithm is said to be
f
-DP if the trade-off curve described by the Type-I and Type-II errors of the aforementioned hypothesis test lies above the non-increasing, convex function
f
. The function
f
is thus the parameter that determines the privacy level of the algorithm.
A particular, and important, parameterization of these algorithms is given by
\mu
-Gaussian differentially private
(
\mu
-GDP) algorithms, where
f
is defined as the trade-off function between two unit-variance Gaussian distributions with mean
0
and
\mu
, respectively. That is, an algorithm is
\mu
-GDP if distinguishing between any two neighbouring input training sets is, at least, as hard as distinguishing between two Gaussian random variables with unit variance and means at a distance
\mu
.

Due to their operational, intuitive formulation, we will mainly focus on pure DP and GDP. Two important properties of
\varepsilon
-DP and
\mu
-GDP algorithms are:

1.

They are KL-stable
23
23

23

The definition of KL-stability in
[
129
]
differs from (
80
) and (
81
) in the explicit quantity on the right-hand side of the bounds. However, the idea that the relative entropy is bounded remains.

[
129
, Definition 4.2]
.
That is, given any two (fixed) neighbouring training sets
s
and
s^{\prime}
, the relative entropy of their respective output distributions is bounded by
[
246
, Lemma D.8]

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s^{\prime}})\leq\varepsilon\tanh\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)

(80)

if
\mathbb{A}
is
\varepsilon
-DP, and by
[
246
, Theorem. 2.10]

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s^{\prime}})\leq\frac{1}{2}\mu^{2}

(81)

if
\mathbb{A}
is
\mu
-GDP.

2.

They possess group privacy
.
Given two training sets that differ in at most
k
instances, an
\varepsilon
-DP algorithm
\mathbb{A}
is
k\varepsilon
-DP
[
44
, Theorem. 2.2]
and a
\mu
-GDP algorithm
\mathbb{A}
is
k\mu
-GDP
[
246
, Theorem. 2.14]
.

A direct consequence of these two properties is condensed below.

Proposition 6.1

(
[
246
,
44
]
)
.

Given an algorithm
\mathbb{A}
, if two (fixed) training sets
s
and
s^{\prime}
differ in at most
k
instances, then the relative entropy of their respective output distributions is bounded from above as

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s^{\prime}})\leq k\varepsilon\tanh\left(\frac{k\varepsilon}{2}\right)\leq\min\left\{\frac{1}{2}k^{2}\varepsilon^{2},k\varepsilon\right\}

(82)

if
\mathbb{A}
is
\varepsilon
-DP, and as

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s^{\prime}})\leq\frac{1}{2}k^{2}\mu^{2}

(83)

if
\mathbb{A}
is
\mu
-GDP.

The last inequality of (
82
) is obtained using the first term of the Taylor expansion of
\tanh
and the fact that
\tanh\leq 1
. We note that, if
\varepsilon\geq\nicefrac{{2}}{{k}}
, the linear approximation in (
82
) is tighter than the quadratic one; moreover, this approximation becomes increasingly accurate as
k
increases, given a fixed
\varepsilon
. Incidentally, the linear upper bound may also be obtained through the bound on the max-information from
Dwork et al. [44, Remark 3.1]
.

2
Maximal Leakage and Generalization

Recall the definition of the Rényi divergence from
Definition

2.16
. Then, since the logarithm is a monotonic non-decreasing function we may write the maximal leakage as

\mathcal{L}(S\to W)=\esssup_{\mathbb{P}_{S}}\esssup_{\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}}\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{W}}.

Once we write the essential supremum in this form, we may note that if the maximum leakage is bounded by
\varepsilon
, then

\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{W}}\leq\varepsilon\textnormal{ a.s.,}\hskip 20.00003pt\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{P}_{W})\leq\varepsilon\textnormal{ a.s.,}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathrm{I}(W;S)\leq\varepsilon.

Therefore, if an algorithm is private in the sense that it has a maximal leakage bounded by
\varepsilon
, then it generalizes. Indeed, every expected generalization error and PAC-Bayesian bounds from
Chapters

4
and
5
that involves these quantities can be bounded from above by changing these quantities by
\varepsilon
.

This is one of the reasons why information-theoretic generalization bounds are attractive. We can determine that
if an algorithm is private, then it generalizes
only by noting that the information captured by the algorithm is bounded if the algorithm is private. As an example, let us consider the most specific type of generalization bounds: single-draw PAC-Bayesian bounds
Sections

11.1
and
5
. We will show how the bounds for losses with a bounded range, losses with a bounded CGF, and losses with a bounded moment can be employed to show that private algorithms generalize. In fact, for bounded losses and losses with a bounded CGF, the bounds will be almost identical to the classical “small-kl” and Chernoff inequalities from
Propositions

5.6
and
5.3
. The only difference will be that, where before there was only a cost
\frac{1}{n}\cdot\log\frac{1}{\beta}
for the precision of the guarantee, now there will be a cost of
\frac{1}{n}\big(\varepsilon+\log\frac{1}{\beta}\big)
. Therefore, we can clearly determine the effect of the information leakage in the generalization guarantee by the amount of maximal leakage of the algorithm.

2.1
Losses With a Bounded Range

To start, we may consider the Seeger–Langford bound from
Theorem

5.3
. Then, if we choose the data independent prior
\mathbb{Q}
to be
\mathbb{P}_{W}
we obtain the following result.

Theorem 6.1
.

Consider a loss with a range bounded in
[0,1]
. If an algorithm
\mathbb{A}
has a maximal leakage bounded by
\varepsilon
, then, for every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\|{R}(W)\big)\leq\frac{\varepsilon+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}.

Then, we may employ our developments from
Section

13.2
to transform the “small-kl” inequality into a fast-rate bound, leading to the following result. For convenience, as the result will be employed in this form in
Section

2.3
, we present the result for losses with a range bounded in
[0,b]
, while noting that it can be generally extended for losses with a range bounded in
[a,b]
by scaling and centering.

Theorem 6.2
.

Consider a loss with a range bounded in
[0,b]
. If an algorithm
\mathbb{A}
has a maximal leakage bounded by
\varepsilon
, then, for every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

{R}(W)\leq c\gamma\log\left(\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}\right)\cdot\widehat{{R}}(W,S)+bc\gamma\cdot\frac{\varepsilon+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}+b\kappa(c)\gamma.

holds
simultaneously
for all
\gamma>1
and all
c\in(0,1]
, where
\kappa(c)\coloneqq 1-c(1-\log c)
.

2.2
Losses With a Bounded CGF

Consider the bound from (
75
) for losses with a bounded CGF in the sense of
Definition

4.1
. Let us choose the data independent prior
\mathbb{Q}
to be
\mathbb{P}_{W}
. Then, for all
\lambda\in(0,b)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}\left(\frac{\varepsilon+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n}+\psi(\lambda)\right).

Note that the right-hand side of this equation does not contain any random element. Therefore, we can safely optimize the parameter
\lambda
using
Lemma

2.5
without the need for the events’ space discretization technique from
Section

4.1
. The optimization results in the following generic bound.

Theorem 6.3
.

Consider a loss with a bounded CGF (
Definition

4.1
). If an algorithm
\mathbb{A}
has a maximal leakage bounded by
\varepsilon
, then, for every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\leq\psi_{*}^{-1}\left(\frac{\varepsilon+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n}\right).

Theorem

6.3
is essentially the same bound from
Esposito et al. [10, Corollary 3]
, but written in the style used throughout the manuscript. This result is a generalization of the specific theorem for sub-Gaussian losses from
[
10
, Corollary 4]
, which was later recovered as well in
[
12
, Corollary 4]
. For example, if a loss has a range bounded in
[a,b]
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\leq(b-a)\sqrt{\frac{\varepsilon+\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n}}.

2.3
Losses With a Bounded Moment

Consider
Theorem

6.2
and the the single-draw version of
Lemma

5.3
that we can obtain using our refinement of
Alquier [30]
’s truncation technique from
Sections

13.3
and
4.2
. Then, we have that if the loss
\ell(w,Z)
has a
p
-th moment bounded by
m_{p}
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
and the algorithm has a maximal leakage bounded by
\varepsilon
, then for every
\beta\in(0,1)
and all
\lambda>0
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

{R}(W)\leq\kappa_{1}\cdot\widehat{{R}}_{\leq\nicefrac{{n}}{{\lambda}}}(W,S)+\kappa_{2}\cdot\frac{\varepsilon+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}+\kappa_{3}\cdot\frac{n}{\lambda}+\frac{m_{p}}{p-1}\left(\frac{\lambda}{n}\right)^{p-1},

holds
simultaneously
for all
c\in(0,1]
and all
\gamma>1
, where
\kappa_{1}\coloneqq c\gamma\log\big(\nicefrac{{\gamma}}{{(\gamma-1)}}\big)
,
\kappa_{2}\coloneqq c\gamma
, and
\kappa_{3}\coloneqq\gamma\big(1-c(1-\log c)\big)
.

Now, as in
Section

2.2
, the optimal value of the parameter
\lambda>0
does not depend on any random element. Therefore, it can be safely optimized without the need for the events’ space discretization technique from
Section

4.1
. The optimization leads to the following result.

Theorem 6.4
.

Consider a loss
\ell(w,Z)
with a
p
-th moment bounded by
m_{p}
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
. If an algorithm
\mathbb{A}
has a maximal leakage bounded by
\varepsilon
, then, for every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

{R}(W)\leq\kappa_{1}\cdot\widehat{{R}}_{\leq t^{\star}}(W,S)+m_{p}^{\frac{1}{p}}\left(\frac{p}{p-1}\right)\left(\kappa_{2}\cdot\frac{\varepsilon+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}+\kappa_{3}\right)^{\frac{p-1}{p}}

holds
simultaneously
for all
c\in(0,1]
and all
\gamma>1
, where
\kappa_{1}\coloneqq c\gamma\log\big(\nicefrac{{\gamma}}{{(\gamma-1)}}\big)
,
\kappa_{2}\coloneqq c\gamma
,
\kappa_{3}\coloneqq\gamma\big(1-c(1-\log c)\big)
, and

t^{\star}\coloneqq m_{p}^{\frac{1}{p}}\left(\kappa_{2}\cdot\frac{\varepsilon+\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}\right)^{\frac{p-1}{p}}.

Finally, one could obtain the analogue results for
Theorem

5.17
for losses with a bounded variance.

3
Differential Privacy and Generalization

Like we did in
Section

2
above, recall the definition of (pure) differential privacy and the group privacy property from
Section

1.2
. Then, if an algorithm is
\varepsilon
-DP, for every two training sets
s
and
s^{\prime}
that differ in every element

\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\leq e^{n\varepsilon}\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s^{\prime}}\textnormal{ almost surely.}

Therefore, it follows that for any
\varepsilon
-DP algorithm

\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}\leq n\varepsilon\textnormal{ a.s.,}\hskip 20.00003pt\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq n\varepsilon\textnormal{ a.s.,}\hskip 10.00002pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\mathrm{I}(W;S)\leq n\varepsilon,

where
\mathbb{Q}=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s^{\prime}}
is chosen to be the distribution induced by the algorithm on a fictitious, fixed training set, and where the last inequality follows form the
golden formula
from
Proposition

2.4
. Note that the distribution
\mathbb{Q}
still does not depend on the data, as
s^{\prime}
can be a fictitious sequence of
n
elements from the instance space
\mathcal{Z}
that is completely independent of the real training set
s
.

In this way, we may repeat the procedures from
Section

2
substituting these information measures by
n\varepsilon
and observing the new privacy guarantees. For example, the analogue of the single-draw PAC Bayesian guarantee for algorithms with a bounded maximal leakage from
Theorems

6.3
,
6.1
, and
6.4
to
\varepsilon
-DP algorithms is the following.

Theorem 6.5
.

Consider an
\varepsilon
-DP algorithm
\mathbb{A}
.

1.

If the loss has a range bounded in
[0,1]
, then for every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\widehat{{R}}(W,S)\|{R}(W)\big)\leq\varepsilon+\frac{\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}.

2.

If the loss has a range bounded in
[0,b]
, then for every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

{R}(W)\leq c\gamma\log\left(\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}\right)\cdot\widehat{{R}}(W,S)+bc\gamma\cdot\left(\varepsilon+\frac{\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}\right)+b\kappa(c)\gamma.

holds
simultaneously
for all
\gamma>1
and all
c\in(0,1]
, where
\kappa(c)\coloneqq 1-c(1-\log c)
.

3.

If the loss has a bounded CGF (
Definition

4.1
), then for every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\leq\psi_{*}^{-1}\left(\varepsilon+\frac{\log\frac{1}{\beta}}{n}\right).

4.

If the loss
\ell(w,Z)
has a
p
-th moment bounded by
m_{p}
for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
, then for every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

{R}(W)\leq\kappa_{1}\cdot\widehat{{R}}_{\leq t^{\star}}(W,S)+m_{p}^{\frac{1}{p}}\left(\frac{p}{p-1}\right)\left(\kappa_{2}\cdot\left(\varepsilon+\frac{\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}\right)+\kappa_{3}\right)^{\frac{p-1}{p}}

holds
simultaneously
for all
c\in(0,1]
and all
\gamma>1
, where
\kappa_{1}\coloneqq c\gamma\log\big(\nicefrac{{\gamma}}{{(\gamma-1)}}\big)
,
\kappa_{2}\coloneqq c\gamma
,
\kappa_{3}\coloneqq\gamma\big(1-c(1-\log c)\big)
, and

t^{\star}\coloneqq m_{p}^{\frac{1}{p}}\left(\kappa_{2}\cdot\left(\varepsilon+\frac{\log\frac{\xi(n)}{\beta}}{n}\right)\right)^{\frac{p-1}{p}}.

Comparing
Theorem

6.3
and
Theorem

6.5
, we observe that while a bounded maximal leakage readily implies generalization, that an algorithm is
\varepsilon
-DP only implies that it generalizes if
\varepsilon
is a decreasing function of
n
. In fact, this naive result is in line with the literature in generalization guarantees for DP algorithms that we survey in
Section

3.2
.

For this reason, we wonder if stronger generalization guarantees for pure DP algorithms are possible beyond the simple analysis from above. In
[
126
]
, we found an affirmative answer for both “in expectation” and PAC-Bayesian guarantees to this question for
permutation invariant algorithms
operating on
discrete data
. We extend the analysis to GDP algorithms as well. Our results in this regard are given in
Section

3.1
.

3.1
Generalization Bounds for Pure DP and GDP Algorithms

As mentioned above, we consider
permutation invariant algorithms
that operate on
discrete data
. When we refer to discrete data, we mean data whose instance space
\mathcal{Z}
is countable. Moreover, when we refer to permutation invariant algorithms we refer to algorithms whose output does not depend on the order of the training instances. Below, we give a formal definition of this term for clarity.

Definition 6.1
.

An algorithm
\mathbb{A}:\mathcal{Z}^{n}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{W})
is said to be
permutation invariant
if it operates on a
set
of instances instead of on a sequence of instances. This means that the hypothesis generated by the algorithm with the training set
s=(z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{n})
has the same distribution as the one generated with every other permutation
\mathrm{Per}(s)
of the training set. That is,
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=\mathrm{Per}(s)}
for every permutation
\mathrm{Per}
.

Before moving on to the results, let us describe a situation motivating the study of the generalization error of DP permutation invariant algorithms operating on discrete data.

Example 6.1
.

Consider a medical setting where the doctors want to determine if an ICU patient should enter a treatment
B
or should remain with the current treatment
A
.
To make their decision, they want the aid of a model
f_{W}
that takes as input a set of hand-crafted features
X=(F_{1},F_{2},\dots,F_{k})
and predicts if the patient will survive a certain (fixed) time after entering treatment
B
.
An example of such a hand-crafted feature (which showcases its discrete nature) is the systolic blood pressure classified in the following intervals: lower than 120 mmMg, between 120 and 129 mmHg, between 130 and 139 mmHg, between 140 and 179 mmHg, or higher than 180 mmHg.
With this purpose, the doctors collect a set of
n
historical records
Z_{i}
containing the aforementioned features
X_{i}
and if the patient survived
Y_{i}
.
Then, they design the model
f_{W}
(that is, the hypothesis
W
) employing the historical records
\{Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}\}
(that is, the training set
S
) ensuring that it is
\varepsilon
-DP to preserve the patients’ anonymity, for example, with private logistic regression
[
247
, Section 4]
.
The model achieves a certain accuracy
\alpha\in[0,1]
.
Finally, they wonder how well, on average, the model will describe new patients’ data for the
0-1
loss function
\ell(w,z)=\mathbb{I}_{\{f_{w}(x)\neq y\}}(w,z)
.

The selection of the features in
Example

6.1
reflects some real choices made by physicians from Karolinska Institutet in internal studies. This kind of quantization is commonplace in the medical community, especially in studies related to blood pressure assessment, and often the total cardinality of the data is small
[
248
,
249
,
250
,
248
]
.

Regarding the choice of the permutation invariant algorithm, we chose a privatized version of logistic regression since it is a simple, often interpretable algorithm liked by the medical community (see
[
251
]
and the references therein). However, other common algorithms like gradient descent are also permutation invariant and can be privatized by applying some privacy mechanism on the result such as the randomized response or the Laplace mechanisms
[
44
]
.

Our results are based on a mathematical formulation to bound from above the relative entropy
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})
, and therefore bounding the generalization error based on the theorems from
Chapters

4
and
5
. In this formulation, we choose the prior
\mathbb{Q}
to be a variational approximation, in the form of a mixture, of the marginal distribution
\mathbb{P}_{W}
. Since DP and GDP algorithms are KL-stable, the mixture elements are selected to be the hypotheses’ conditional distributions that best cover the set of possible training sets. The rule for selecting such a cover of the space is determined by the level of stability of the algorithm.

We develop a strategy, based on the method of types, to find explicit upper bounds on the relative entropy using this formulation when the data is discrete. First, we show that permutation invariant algorithms have a mutual information in
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
, and therefore, they generalize, regardless of whether they are private or not. Then, we leverage the stability property of
\varepsilon
-DP and
\mu
-GDP algorithms to obtain tighter bounds. To be precise, we obtain bounds on the relative entropy and the mutual information with a rate of

\mathcal{O}\left(|\mathcal{Z}|\log\frac{\varepsilon n}{|\mathcal{Z}|}\right)\hskip 20.00003pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{O}\left(|\mathcal{Z}|\log(\varepsilon\sqrt{n\log n})\right)

for
\varepsilon
-DP algorithms and of

\mathcal{O}\left(|\mathcal{Z}|\log\left(|\mathcal{Z}|\mu^{2}n^{2}\right)\right)\hskip 20.00003pt\textnormal{and}\hskip 20.00003pt\mathcal{O}\left(|\mathcal{Z}|\log(|\mathcal{Z}|\mu^{2}n\log n)\right)

for
\mu
-GDP algorithms.

To be precise, we derive each of our results considering that the cardinality of the data is finite, that is
|\mathcal{Z}|<\infty
. The extension to general countable instance spaces
\mathcal{Z}
follows immediately as, in that case, the bounds are vacuous.

3.1.1
A Review of the Method of Types

Recall that a training set is defined as a sequence
S=(Z_{1},Z_{2},\ldots,Z_{n})
where each instance
Z_{i}
is i.i.d. according to the probability distribution
\mathbb{P}_{Z}
. Then, the training set
S
is distributed according to
\mathbb{P}_{S}=\mathbb{P}_{Z}^{\otimes n}.

Definition 6.2

(
[
56
, Section 11.1]
)
.

The
type

T_{s}
, or
empirical probability distribution
, of a training set
s\in\mathcal{Z}^{n}
is the relative proportion of occurrences of each symbol from
\mathcal{Z}
.
That is,
T_{s}(z)=\frac{N(z|s)}{n}
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, where

N(z|s)\coloneqq|\{z^{\prime}\in s:z=z^{\prime}\}|

is the number of times the symbol
z
appears in the training set
s
.
The set of all possible types of sequences of elements from
\mathcal{Z}
of length
n
is denoted by
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{Z},n}
.

An interesting property of the types is that, although the number of elements in
\mathcal{Z}^{n}
scales exponentially in the number of samples
n
, the total number of types only scales polynomially in
n
.
It is known that
|\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{Z},n}|\leq(n+1)^{|\mathcal{Z}|}

[
56
, Theorem 11.1.1]
.
This can be marginally improved by the following proposition, which is tighter for finite
n
and alphabets of small cardinality, especially binary alphabets. The proof of this proposition is given in
Appendix

6.A
since it does not contribute to the discussion.

Proposition 6.2
.

|\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{Z},n}|\leq(n+1)^{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}
, with equality if and only if
|\mathcal{Z}|=2
.

Another interesting property of the type
T_{s}
of a sequence
s=(z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{n})
is that it is equal to the type of any permutation of the same sequence, that is,
T_{s}=T_{\mathrm{Per}(s)}
, where
\mathrm{Per}
is a random permutation.
Therefore, the type
T_{s}
uniquely identifies the training set
s
with elements
\{z_{1},z_{2},\ldots,z_{n}\}
, where the order of the instances is irrelevant.
In this way, we can describe the distance between two training sets
s
and
s^{\prime}
by means of their types
T_{s}
and
T_{s^{\prime}}
.

Definition 6.3
.

The
distance
between two training sets
s
and
s^{\prime}
is defined as the minimum number of instances that needs to be changed in
s
to obtain
s^{\prime}
.
It is proportional to the total variation distance between the types
T_{s}
and
T_{s^{\prime}}
,

d(s,s^{\prime})\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\big|N(z|s)-N(z|s^{\prime})\big|=\frac{n}{2}\cdot\mathrm{TV}(T_{s},T_{s^{\prime}}).

3.1.2
An Auxiliary Lemma

The following lemma, inspired by the variational approximation given in
[
252
]
, bounds the relative entropy between two probability distributions
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
, where the latter is a mixture probability distribution. As mentioned previously, this lemma will be the foundation of our formulation to bound the relative entropy
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})
by considering the prior
\mathbb{Q}
to be a mixture of posterior distributions
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s^{\prime}}
with fictitious sets
s^{\prime}
covering the space
\mathcal{Z}^{n}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
be two probability distributions such that
\mathbb{P}\ll\mathbb{Q}
.
Let also
\mathbb{Q}
be a finite mixture of probability distributions such that
\mathbb{Q}=\sum_{b}\omega_{b}\mathbb{Q}_{b}
, where
\sum_{b}\omega_{b}=1
,
\omega_{b}\geq 0
, and
\mathbb{P}\ll\mathbb{Q}_{b}
for all
b
.
Then, the following inequalities hold:

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\leq-\log\left(\sum\nolimits_{b}\omega_{b}\exp\big({-}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q}_{b})\big)\right)

(84)

\displaystyle\leq\min_{b}\big\{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q}_{b})-\log\omega_{b}\big\}.

(85)

Given the element of the mixture
\mathbb{Q}_{b}
,
Equation

85
depicts the trade-off between its similarity with the distribution
\mathbb{P}
and its responsibility
\omega_{b}
.
Intuitively, this equation tells us that the relative entropy between
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
is bounded from above by the divergence between
\mathbb{P}
and the closest, most probable element of
\mathbb{Q}
.
Particularly, if the weights
\omega_{b}
are the same for every element of the mixture, then the bound (
85
) is controlled by the element that is closest to
\mathbb{P}
in relative entropy.
This behavior of the bound will be useful in the proofs of the main results.
This bound is especially tight when one element of the mixture is either very probable or much closer to
\mathbb{P}
than the others.
In the scenario where neither of these two conditions is met, the bound (
85
) is loose and (
84
) is preferred.

This bound can be useful elsewhere as we show in
Section

17.1
to find tighter bounds for the SGLD algorithm. In
[
126
, Section VI. A]
, we also describe how this lemma can be employed to sharpen the analysis of lower bounds on the minimax error with Fano’s method.

3.1.3
A Simple Upper Bound Through the Method of Types

Recall that our objective is to find an upper bound for
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})
for a fixed training set
s
, and where we may choose any data-independent prior
\mathbb{Q}
.

Let us consider the prior
\mathbb{Q}
to be a mixture of all the conditional distributions
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s^{\prime}}
of the hypothesis given a fictitious training set
s^{\prime}
, with mixture probability (or responsibility) of
\omega_{s^{\prime}}
; that is,

\mathbb{Q}=\sum_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\omega_{s^{\prime}}\mathbb{P}_{W}^{T_{s^{\prime}}},

where we take into account that
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s^{\prime}}=\mathbb{P}_{W}^{T_{s^{\prime}}}
a.s. for all training sets
s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
, since a training set
s^{\prime}
with finite elements is completely characterized by its type
T_{s^{\prime}}
. Recall again that the distribution
\mathbb{Q}
is still
data-independent
, since it does not use the knowledge of the specific training set
s
, but only uses the knowledge of the instance space
\mathcal{Z}
and the number of samples
n
from the problem.

Then, we may leverage
Lemma

6.1
to obtain a more tractable upper bound. That is,

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq\min_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\left\{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{T_{\smash{s^{\prime}}}}\right)-\log\omega_{s^{\prime}}\right\}.

(86)

A naive approach is to consider an equiprobable mixture
\mathbb{Q}
, that is,
\omega_{s}=|\mathcal{S}|^{-1}=|\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{Z},n}|^{-1}
for all
s\in\mathcal{S}
. Then, we have that
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq\log|\mathcal{S}|
, which, combined with
Proposition

6.2
leads to the following proposition.

Proposition 6.3
.

Consider a discrete instance space
|\mathcal{Z}|<\infty
. Then, for all training sets
s\in\mathcal{Z}^{n}
, there exists a distribution
\mathbb{Q}
on
\mathcal{W}
such that

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\log(1+n).

Note that in the proof of
Proposition

6.3
we do not leverage the properties of
Lemma

6.1
at their fullest, since we do not take advantage of the combination of the relative entropy and the mixture probabilities for the minimization (
86
).
However, if we note again that
\mathrm{I}(S;W)\leq\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\mathbb{P}_{S}}\big[\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\big]
for every distribution
\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{W})
(
Proposition

2.4
), we observe how
Lemma

6.1
allows us, naively, to obtain the same bound on the mutual information that one would obtain through the following decomposition of the mutual information:

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}(W;S)=\mathrm{H}(S)-\mathrm{H}(S|W)\leq\mathrm{H}(S)\leq\log|\mathcal{S}|\leq(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\log(1+n),

(87)

where the first inequality is due to the non-negativity of the entropy for discrete random variables (
Proposition

2.2
).

Remark 6.1
.

We note that (
87
) may be improved using the tighter bound on the entropy of a multinomial distribution from
[
253
, Theorem 3.4]
.
More precisely, assuming that
n
is sufficiently large and after some algebraic manipulations, the bound states that

\mathrm{I}(S;W)\in\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}{2}\log\left(\frac{n}{|\mathcal{Z}|}\right)+2|\mathcal{Z}|\right),

which grows more slowly than (
87
) with respect to
n
, but has a more involved expression and interpretation.

Proposition

6.3
states the fact that, if
|\mathcal{Z}|
is finite, the number of possible training sets grows polynomially in
n
, which is independent of how the algorithm works and which training set
s
is at hand.
In other words, even if the algorithm tries to memorize each instance in the training set,
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})
cannot grow faster than logarithmically in
n
. Therefore, according to the bounds in
Chapters

4
and
5
, that depend on the ratio
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})}}{{n}}
, permutation invariant algorithms generalize.

In the sequel, we restrict ourselves to algorithms that are
\varepsilon
-DP or
\mu
-GDP, and thus we can improve upon this simple upper bound.

3.1.4
Upper Bounds on the Relative Entropy of Private Algorithms

As previously mentioned,
\varepsilon
-DP and
\mu
-GDP algorithms are smooth, or stable, in the sense that close input training sets produce similar output hypothesis distributions. Therefore, similarly to what we did above to obtain
Proposition

6.3
, we may employ
Lemma

6.1
to bound
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})
using a mixture distribution of posterior distributions
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s^{\prime}}
as a prior. However, in this case, the mixture only considers a sub-collection
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
of all possible training sets. Then, due to the smoothness of the algorithm, the relative entropy
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s^{\prime}})
is small if the training sets
s
and
s^{\prime}
are close.

Specifically, we may define the collection
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
by partitioning the space of training sets
\mathcal{S}
in equal-sized hypercubes and taking their “central” training set. More precisely, since a training set
s
is uniquely defined by its type
T_{s}
, we may note that all the types lie inside the unit hypercube
[0,1]^{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}
, as the last dimension is completely determined by the first
|\mathcal{Z}|-1
. Then, we may split the
[0,1]
interval of each dimension in
1\leq t\leq n
parts, resulting in a hypercube cover of
t^{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}
smaller hypercubes. The type at the center of each smaller hypercube represents a training set of the collection
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
In this way, the cover guarantees that the maximal distance inside a hypercube, that is,
\max_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\min_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}d(s,s^{\prime})
is bounded. Then, if the mixture components are equiprobable and
\omega_{s^{\prime}}=|\mathcal{S}^{\prime}|^{-1}=t^{-(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)}
, the bound from
Lemma

6.1
trades the relative entropy
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s^{\prime}})
, which decreases as the maximal distance decreases by increasing the number of hypercubes
|\mathcal{S}^{\prime}|
, for the logarithm of the responsibility
-\log\omega_{s^{\prime}}
, which increases as the number of hypercubes
|\mathcal{S}^{\prime}|
grows.

The choice of the number of splits
t
of each interval
[0,1]
of each dimension is done based on the stability properties of each algorithm determined by their DP parameters
\varepsilon
and
\mu
and results in the following proposition. The complete proof is a bit technical and the details do not add to the discussion and therefore is deferred to
Appendix

6.C
.

Proposition 6.4
.

Consider a discrete instance space
|\mathcal{Z}|<\infty
and an algorithm
\mathbb{A}
characterized by a Markov kernel
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
. Then, for every training set
s\in\mathcal{Z}^{n}
:

1.

If
\mathbb{A}
is
\varepsilon
-DP and
\varepsilon\leq 1
, then there exists a distribution
\mathbb{Q}
on
\mathcal{W}
such that

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\log\big(1+e\varepsilon N\big).

(88)

2.

If
\mathbb{A}
is
\mu
-GDP and
\mu\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}}}
, then there exists a distribution
\mathbb{Q}
on
\mathcal{W}
such that

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq\frac{1}{2}(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\log\big(1+e(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\mu^{2}N^{2}\big).

(89)

For lower privacy guarantees, that is,
\varepsilon>1
or
\mu>\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}}}
, the upper bounds on
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})
are no better than the one in
Proposition

6.3
.

Note that the upper bounds (
88
) and (
89
) are tighter than the general one from
Proposition

6.3
once the algorithms have at least a privacy guarantee of
\varepsilon\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{e}}
or
\mu\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{e(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)}}}
(since
\sqrt{1+n^{2}}<1+n
for
n\geq 1
).

The upper bounds from
Proposition

6.3
and
Proposition

6.4
can be tightened if a more accurate value for the number of hypercubes needed to cover the space of training sets is used. To be precise, since the types are empirical probability distributions they lay in the
(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)
-simplex. In other words, if we consider the unit hypercube
[0,1]^{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}
, there are only types inside the hypervolume comprised between the origin and the
(|\mathcal{Z}|-2)
-simplex. Proceeding equally as before, we obtain the following proposition, whose complete proof is in
Appendix

6.D
. Although the result is tighter, this improvement comes at the expense of losing simplicity in the final expressions.

Proposition 6.5
.

Consider a discrete instance space
|\mathcal{Z}|<\infty
and an algorithm
\mathbb{A}
characterized by a Markov kernel
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}
. Then, for every training set
s\in\mathcal{Z}^{n}
:

1.

If
\mathbb{A}
is
\varepsilon
-DP and
\varepsilon\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}}
, then there exists a distribution
\mathbb{Q}
on
\mathcal{W}
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)(1+\varepsilon n)-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(2\pi(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\right),

(90)

and if
\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}}<\varepsilon\leq 1
, then there exists a distribution
\mathbb{Q}
on
\mathcal{W}
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)

\displaystyle\log\left(1+\frac{2}{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}\varepsilon n\right)

\displaystyle+(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\log\left(\frac{e^{2}}{2}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(2\pi(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\right).

(91)

2.

If
\mathbb{A}
is
\mu
-GDP and
\mu\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{\big(n\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}\big)}}
, then there exists a distribution
\mathbb{Q}
on
\mathcal{W}
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\left(1+\frac{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}{2}\mu^{2}n^{2}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(2\pi(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\right),

(92)

and if
\nicefrac{{1}}{{\big(n\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}\big)}}<\mu\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}}}
, then there exists a distribution
\mathbb{Q}
on
\mathcal{W}
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)

\displaystyle\log\left(1+\frac{2}{\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}}\mu n\right)

\displaystyle+(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\log\left(\frac{e^{\frac{3}{2}}}{2}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(2\pi(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\right).

(93)

3.

For any algorithm
\mathbb{A}
there exists a distribution
\mathbb{Q}
on
\mathcal{W}
such that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\log

\displaystyle\left(1+\frac{n}{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}\right)

\displaystyle+(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)-\frac{1}{2}\log\left(2\pi(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\right).

(94)

Note that, for large
\varepsilon n
or
\mu n
, the gap between (
88
) and (
91
), and the gap between (
89
) and (
93
) grows as

(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\log\left(\frac{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}{e}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\log 2\pi(|\mathcal{Z}|-1).

(95)

This highlights the benefit of
Proposition

6.5
for alphabets of large cardinality.

We also note that (
94
) is a valid upper bound on the relative entropy
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})
for algorithms that are not stable. Similarly to
Proposition

6.3
, the covering mixture used here takes every possible training set. However, this bound improves upon
Proposition

6.3
by using a more exact value for the total number of training sets. Consequently, the gap between
Proposition

6.3
and (
94
) also scales as (
95
) for large
n
.

To get an idea of how these bounds compare with each other, let us recover
Example

6.1
and let us assume that the cardinality of the data, that is
|\mathcal{Z}|=|\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}|
, is fixed to
100
, that the probability required by the physicians is
\beta=0.05
, and that they achieved an empirical risk of
0.05
. Then, consider two scenarios:

1.

In the first scenario, the privacy parameter
\varepsilon
is fixed to
0.1
and the number of samples
n
varies from
1
to
10,000
.

2.

In the second scenario, the number of samples
n
is fixed to
5,000
and the privacy parameter
\varepsilon
varies from
10^{-4}
to
1
.

Figure

6.1
showcases how the proposed bounds achieve non-vacuous generalization error and depicts the trade-offs between privacy, the number of samples, and generalization. More specifically, we see how the more private (smaller
\varepsilon
) and the more samples
n
we have, the better we can guarantee generalization.

Figure 6.1
:
Comparison of the generalization guarantees combining
Propositions

6.3
,
6.4
, and
6.5
with the Seeger–Langford bound
[
21
,
22
]
from
Theorem

5.3
under the setting of
Example

6.1
. The cardinality of the data is fixed to
|\mathcal{Z}|=100
, probability parameter is fixed to
\beta=0.05
, and the empirical risk is
\mathbb{E}^{S}[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)]=0.05
. In the left figure, the privacy parameter is fixed to
\varepsilon=0.1
and the number of samples
n
varies from
1
to
10,000
. In the right figure, the number of samples is fixed to
n=5,000
and the privacy parameter
\varepsilon
varies from
10^{-4}
to
1
. In both figures, the
y
-axis starts at the value of the empirical risk.

So far, we have found upper bounds on the relative entropy
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})
that hold for every
s\in\mathcal{S}
, that is, they are
uniform bounds
. However, we know from information theory that there is a collection, called the
typical set

\mathcal{T}
, of types that are
typical
. This means that the probability of randomly sampling a training set with a type in the typical set is close to one
[
56
, Chapters 3 and 10]
. Therefore, noting that
\mathrm{I}(W;S)\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})\big]
(
Proposition

2.4
), we may only cover the typical set
\mathcal{T}
, and rely on the low probability of observing a training set from outside
\mathcal{T}
to bound the mutual information
\mathrm{I}(W;S)
. More precisely, by the law of total expectation

\mathrm{I}(W;S)\leq\mathbb{E}^{\{S\in\mathcal{T}\}}\big[\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})\big]\cdot\mathbb{P}\big[S\in\mathcal{T}\big]+\mathbb{E}^{\{S\not\in\mathcal{T}\}}\big[\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})\big]\cdot\mathbb{P}\big[S\not\in\mathcal{T}\big],

(96)

where the first term is small due to the control of the relative entropy
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S}\|\mathbb{Q})
using the marginal
\mathbb{Q}
tailored for the typical training sets, and the second term is small since the probability
\mathbb{P}\big[S\not\in\mathcal{T}\big]
vanishes.
In this way, we can provide sharper generalization guarantees in expectation. This procedure leads to the following proposition, whose proof is in
Appendix

6.E
.

Proposition 6.6
.

Consider a discrete instance space
|\mathcal{Z}|<\infty
and an algorithm
\mathbb{A}
such that
W=\mathbb{A}(S)
.

1.

If
\mathbb{A}
is
\varepsilon
-DP and
\varepsilon\leq 2
, then

\mathrm{I}(W;S)\leq|\mathcal{Z}|\log\big(1+e\varepsilon\sqrt{n\log n}\big)+2|\mathcal{Z}|\frac{\varepsilon}{n},

(97)

while if
\varepsilon>2
,

\mathrm{I}(W;S)\leq|\mathcal{Z}|\log\big(1+2\sqrt{n\log n}\big)+2|\mathcal{Z}|\frac{\varepsilon}{n}.

(98)

2.

If
\mathbb{A}
is
\mu
-GDP and
\mu\leq\nicefrac{{2}}{{\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|}}}
, then

\mathrm{I}(W;S)\leq\frac{|\mathcal{Z}|}{2}\log\left(1+e|\mathcal{Z}|\mu^{2}n\log n\right)+|\mathcal{Z}|\mu^{2},

(99)

while if
\mu>\nicefrac{{2}}{{\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|}}}
,

\mathrm{I}(W;S)\leq|\mathcal{Z}|\log\big(1+2\sqrt{n\log n}\big)+|\mathcal{Z}|\mu^{2}.

(100)

If we compare this asymptotic behavior with the one from
Propositions

6.4
and
6.5
we see now how the privacy coefficients
\varepsilon
and
\mu
multiply
\sqrt{n\log n}
instead of
n
.
We note that
\sqrt{n\log n}<n
for
n\geq 1
, which highlights the benefit of discriminating between typical and non-typical training sets. However, in practice, as we will see next in
Section

3.2
,
Proposition

6.5
is often tighter than this result in the common operating regions prior to the asymptotic regime.

Remark 6.2
.

We note that the common Laplace or Gaussian mechanisms that assure
\varepsilon
-DP and
\mu
-GDP add random noise of variance proportional to the inverse of the square of the parameter
\varepsilon
or
\mu

[
44
, Section 3.3]
,
[
246
, Theorem 2.7]
.
If we compare the upper bounds obtained for
\varepsilon
-DP and
\mu
-GDP algorithms in
Propositions

6.4
,
6.5
, and
6.6
, we observe that their asymptotic behaviors appear to be almost identical by letting
\mu=\varepsilon/\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}
.
24
24

24

In the case of
Proposition

6.6
, we would need
\mu=\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|}}}
to be more precise.

This suggests that, even if both measures of privacy are not equivalent,
\mu
-GDP algorithms need to be “noisier” than
\varepsilon
-DP algorithms, as
|\mathcal{Z}|
grows, in order to obtain similar generalization performance according to our bounds.

The main reason for this behavior is that our results depend on the stability of the algorithm, measured by the relative entropy between the hypotheses obtained by two training sets at a distance
k
(see
Proposition

6.1
).
If we let
\varepsilon=\mu=\gamma
, then the tighter upper bound of the relative entropy for
\gamma
-DP algorithms is always smaller than the upper bound for
\gamma
-GDP algorithms.
Moreover, the looser upper bound of the relative entropy for
\gamma
-DP algorithms, that is,
k\gamma
, is smaller than the upper bound for
\gamma
-GDP algorithms once
\gamma>\nicefrac{{2}}{{k}}
.
Therefore, the hypothesis distribution of
\mu
-GDP algorithms changes more rapidly with the distance between two training sets, thus making these kinds of algorithms less stable (or smooth).
If we recall that the distance (in expectation) between two training sets grows with the size of the sample space
\mathcal{Z}
, we can intuit the relationship between
\mu
and
\varepsilon
in our results for generalization.

3.1.5
Generalization Error of Other Stable Algorithms

Although our main results deal with private algorithms, they can be adapted to other families of algorithms with stability or smoothness properties.
That is, if the relative entropy between the distributions of the output hypotheses, produced by an algorithm fed with two training sets
s
and
s^{\prime}
at a distance
k
, is bounded from above by some function
\phi(k)
, then
Propositions

6.4
,
6.5
, and
6.6
can be replicated with this quantity in mind.
Note that when
k=1
, this property is known as
\sqrt{\phi(1)/2}
-KL-stability
[
129
, Definition 4.2]
.

For example, the privacy framework of
Rényi differential privacy
(RDP) states that an algorithm
\mathbb{A}
is
(\alpha,\varepsilon)
-RDP if
\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s^{\prime}})\leq\varepsilon
for any two neighbouring datasets
s
and
s^{\prime}

[
60
]
.
Given the monotonicity of the Rényi divergence with respect to
\alpha
, we have that
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s^{\prime}})\leq\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s^{\prime}})
for every
\alpha\geq 1
. The case
0<\alpha<1
is more challenging but still possible to bound.
Furthermore, this framework can also be extended for group privacy
[
60
, Proposition 2]
, which enables the use of our results.

3.1.6
Particularization to Specific Private Algorithms

The generalization error of specific learning algorithms that have been proved to be
\varepsilon
-DP or
\mu
-GDP can be characterized.
For instance, the Noisy-SGD and Noisy-Adam
[
254
, Algorithms 1 and 2]
, private, regularized variants of the common stochastic gradient descent (SGD) and Adam
[
255
, Algorithm 1]
algorithms, are approximately
\frac{|v|}{n}\sqrt{T(e^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sigma^{2}}}}-1)}
-GDP, where
|v|
is the batch size,
T
is the number of iterations made by the algorithm, and
\sigma^{2}
is the noise variance
[
254
]
.
This example also highlights the benefit of private algorithms for generalization, given that the privacy parameter
\mu=\frac{|v|}{n}\sqrt{T(e^{\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sigma^{2}}}}-1)}
, decreases with the inverse of the number of samples
n
.

Similarly, there are common algorithms like Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) that can be proved to be
(\alpha,\varepsilon)
-RDP
[
256
]
and others like classical logistic regression that can be adapted to be differentially private
[
247
]
with tunable privacy parameters.
Employing the aforementioned characterization of the generalization capabilities of Rényi differentially private algorithms, one would be able to obtain bounds on the generalization error of MCMC.

3.2
Further Bibliography Connecting DP and Generalization

The characterization of the generalization error for DP algorithms has predominantly been performed under the single-draw PAC-Bayesian framework. Currently, the expected generalization error bounds for DP algorithms are derived either from (i) results similar to those in
Chapter

4
using bounds on other measures of dependence between the hypothesis
W
and the training set
S
, or from (ii) showing the relationship between privacy and stability (see
Section

10.2
and
[
47
]
) and the bounds for stable algorithms (see
Section

10.1
and the references therein).

As we will see, these results are often of the same nature as the direct results we obtained in the beginning of
Section

3
. That is, the generalization is controlled by the privacy parameter but it does not decrease with the number of samples. Therefore, to guarantee the algorithm’s generalization, they require that the privacy parameter decreases with the number of samples.

3.2.1
Single-Draw PAC-Bayesian Bounds

For pure DP algorithms, the first bounds on the generalization error are due to
Dwork et al. [45]
. More precisely, consider a loss with a range bounded in
[0,1]
,
Dwork et al. [45, Theorem 9]
stated that, for every
\tau>0
,
\beta\in(0,1)
, and every
\varepsilon
-DP algorithm, as long as the number of samples is
n\geq\frac{12}{\tau^{2}}\log\nicefrac{{4}}{{\beta}}
and
\varepsilon\leq\nicefrac{{\tau}}{{2}}
, then
\mathbb{P}[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)>\tau]\leq\beta
. If we fix
\tau=2\varepsilon
, we may get a statement with a terminology more similar to the one we have been using throughout the manuscript. That is, for every
\varepsilon
-DP, with probability no smaller than
1-4\exp(-\nicefrac{{n\varepsilon^{2}}}{{3}})

\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\leq 2\varepsilon.

In that article,
Dwork et al. [45, Theorem 11]
also propose a generalization bound for pure DP algorithms, although this depends on the probability of not generalizing with the worst possible training set, and therefore we do not write it in this survey. Later,
Dwork et al. [46, Corollary 7]
improved upon that result and showed that for every
\varepsilon
-DP algorithm, with probability no smaller than
1-3\exp(-\varepsilon^{2}n)

\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\leq\varepsilon L^{\prime}

(101)

for losses
\ell(w,\cdot)
that are
L^{\prime}
-Lipschitz for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
. Actually, their result is given for the
sensitivity
of the loss, but in the context of generalization, this is equivalent to
\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}}
times the Lipschitz constant with respect to the instances. For bounded losses in
[0,1]
, then
L^{\prime}=1
. Therefore, they improved the result in
[
45
]
by increasing the probability under which the generalization error is controlled by the parameter
\varepsilon
and by reducing the constant in front of the privacy parameter.

Another result for pure DP algorithms comes from
Jung et al. [48, Lemma 22]
, that states that for every
\varepsilon
-DP algorithm and every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\leq e^{\varepsilon}-1+\sqrt{\frac{2\log\frac{2}{\beta}}{n}}.

This result is generally looser than (
101
) as
e^{\varepsilon}-1\geq\varepsilon
, but it works for every
\beta\in(0,1)
and can be useful in the small data regime. The same is true for our simple result from
Theorem

6.5
, which is also looser than (
101
) but works for every
\beta\in(0,1)
. However,
Theorem

6.5
is generally still stronger than
Jung et al. [48, Lemma 22]
since (i) the dependence with
\varepsilon
is linear and not exponential and (ii) the extra dependence with
\log\nicefrac{{1}}{{\beta}}
is of a fast rate instead of a slow one.

For approximate DP,
Dwork et al. [45, Theorem 10]
also presented a generalization bound for losses with a range bounded in
[0,1]
. Adapted to the terminology employed throughout the manuscript, their results state that for every
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP algorithm such that
\delta\geq\exp\big(-\frac{\log 2}{\varepsilon}-\frac{n\varepsilon}{3}\big)
, with probability no smaller than
1-4\exp(-\nicefrac{{n\varepsilon^{2}}}{{3}})

\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\leq 4\varepsilon.

An alternative bound was given later by
Dwork et al. [46, Theorem 8]
, where they show that for every
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP algorithm such that
\delta\geq 8\exp\big(-\frac{n\varepsilon}{2}\big)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\frac{16}{\varepsilon^{2}}\log\big(\frac{2}{\varepsilon}\big)\exp\big(-\frac{n\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\big)

\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\leq 4\varepsilon.

Then,
Bassily et al. [129, Theorem 7.2]
built on their work and proved that for every
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP algorithm such that
\varepsilon\in(0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{3}})
and
\delta\in(4\varepsilon\exp(-n\varepsilon^{2}),\nicefrac{{\varepsilon}}{{4}})
, with probability no smaller than
1-\frac{4\varepsilon}{3}\exp(-n\varepsilon^{2})

\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\leq 18\varepsilon L^{\prime}

for losses
\ell(w,\cdot)
that are
L^{\prime}
-Lipschitz for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
.

Finally,
Jung et al. [48, Lemmata 7 and 15]
proved similar results as the ones they had for pure DP algorithms. For losses with a range bounded in
(0,1)
, for every
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP algorithm and every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\leq e^{\varepsilon}-1+\frac{2\delta}{\beta}.

Similarly, for losses
\ell(w,\cdot)
that are
L^{\prime}
-Lipschitz for all
w\in\mathcal{W}
, they show that for every
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP algorithm and every
\beta\in(0,1)
, with probability no smaller than
1-\beta

\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\leq L^{\prime}\left(e^{\varepsilon}-1+\frac{4\delta}{\beta}\right).

3.2.2
Expected Generalization Error Bounds

Dwork et al. [44]
introduced the concept of
max-information

\mathrm{I}_{\infty}
and
\beta
-
approximate max-information

\mathrm{I}_{\infty}^{\beta}
in order to find bounds on the generalization error of algorithms. These quantities are defined as

\mathrm{I}_{\infty}^{\beta}(W;S)\coloneqq\log\sup_{\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{W}\times\mathcal{S}:\mathbb{P}_{W,S}[\mathcal{A}]>\beta}\frac{\mathbb{P}_{W,S}\big[\mathcal{A}]-\beta}{(\mathbb{P}_{W}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{S})[\mathcal{A}]}

and
\mathrm{I}_{\infty}(W;S)=\mathrm{I}_{\infty}^{0}(W;S)=\mathrm{D}_{\infty}(\mathbb{P}_{W,S}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}\otimes\mathbb{P}_{S})
respectively. The relationship between approximate max information and the generalization error is not immediate. However, it is easy to see that
\mathrm{I}(W;S)\leq\mathrm{I}_{\infty}(W;S)
, and therefore any bound on this quantity can be readily employed in the bounds from
Chapter

4
. In particular,
Dwork et al. [46, Theorem 7]
noted that every
\varepsilon
-DP algorithm has a bounded max-information, reaching the same bound we gave at the start
Section

3
, namely that
\mathrm{I}(W;S)\leq\mathrm{I}_{\infty}(W;S)\leq n\varepsilon
. Recall that since the expected generalization bounds from
Chapter

4
depend on the ratio
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{I}(W;S)}}{{n}}
, this kind of results only work if the privacy parameter
\varepsilon
decreases with the number of samples.

A stronger statement was later proved by
Bun and Steinke [61, Proposition 1.4 and Theorem 1.10]
, although they did not show it in the context of bounding the generalization error. They showed that any
\varepsilon
-DP algorithm has a bounded mutual information, that is
\mathrm{I}(W;S)\leq\frac{1}{2}n\varepsilon^{2}
, improving upon the previous statement as long as
\varepsilon\leq 2
.

Finally,
Wang et al. [47, Lemma 8]
showed that any
\varepsilon
-DP algorithm is uniformly stable with parameter
(e^{\varepsilon}-1)
. Moreover, if
\varepsilon\leq 1
, then it is uniformly stable with parameter
2\varepsilon
. Combing this with the fact that if an algorithm is uniformly stable with parameter
\gamma
, then
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{gen}(W,S)\big]\leq\gamma
, we see that the findings from
Wang et al. [47]
also show expected generalization errors that do not decrease with the number of samples.

We may compare these results with the ones we developed in
Section

3.1
. At first glance, it is clear that our results are asymptotically tighter
since the expected generalization error vanishes as the number of samples grows, as opposed to the previous results, for which the generalization error bound remains constant. Nonetheless, our bounds depend on the cardinality of the input space, which is not the case for the other bounds;
those known bounds may thus provide a better characterization of the generalization error in the small sample regime.
In order to better clarify the comparison between these results and those presented in this manuscript, we recover
Example

6.1
and observe three different scenarios where three important parameters are varied:

1.

In the first scenario, the empirical risk
\mathbb{E}[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)]
is fixed to
0.05
, the cardinality of the data
|\mathcal{Z}|
is set to
100
, the privacy parameter
\varepsilon
is set to
0.6
, and the number of samples
n
vary from
1
to
10,000
.

2.

In the second scenario, the cardinality of the data
|\mathcal{Z}|
and the empirical risk
\mathbb{E}[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)]
are still fixed to
100
and
0.05
, respectively, but now the number of samples
n
is fixed to
2,500
and the privacy parameter
\varepsilon
varies from
0
to
2
.

3.

In the third scenario, the privacy parameter
\varepsilon
is fixed to
0.6
, the number of data samples
n
and the empirical risk
\mathbb{E}[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)]
are also fixed to
2,500
and
0.05
, respectively, and the data cardinality
|\mathcal{Z}|
now varies from
2
to
1,000
.

The results are presented in
Figure

6.2
.
In the first scenario, the results show that, as mentioned before, the bounds from
Dwork et al. [46]
,
Bun and Steinke [61]
, and
Wang et al. [47]
outperform the presented bounds in the small sample regime and are outperformed as the number of samples increases.
The results on the second scenario show how, for a moderate cardinality and number of samples, the presented bounds can outperform the bounds from
Dwork et al. [46]
,
Bun and Steinke [61]
, and
Wang et al. [47]
when the privacy parameter is not very small.
Nonetheless, for very private algorithms (
\varepsilon\ll 1
), their bounds are generally tighter due to their linear (or even quadratic) dependency with
\varepsilon
.
Finally, the results on the third scenario show how, for a moderate number of samples, the results from this work are only applicable in the small cardinality regime; when the cardinality of the data increases, the bounds from
Dwork et al. [46]
,
Bun and Steinke [61]
, and
Wang et al. [47]
are preferred.

Figure 6.2
:
Comparison of the different expected generalization bounds for
\varepsilon
-DP algorithms in the setting of
Example

6.1
. In particular, we obtain the guarantees combining
Propositions

6.3
,
6.4
,
6.5
, and
6.6
and the results from
Dwork et al. [46]
and
Bun and Steinke [61]
with
Lemma

4.2
, and the result from
Wang et al. [47]
with the bounds in expectation from uniform stability of
Section

10.1
. In the top-left figure, the cardinality of the data is fixed to
|\mathcal{Z}|=100
, the probability parameter is fixed to
\beta=0.05
, and the empirical risk is
\mathbb{E}^{S}[\widehat{{R}}(W,S)]=0.05
. In the left figure, the privacy parameter is fixed to
\varepsilon=0.1
and the number of samples
n
varies from
1
to
10,000
. In the right figure, the number of samples is fixed to
n=2,500
and the privacy parameter
\varepsilon
varies from
10^{-4}
to
1
. In both figures, the
y
-axis starts at the value of the empirical risk.

Appendices

Appendix 6.A
An Upper Bound on the Number of Types

The exact number of types is given by
[
257
, Problem 2.1]

|\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{Z},n}|=\binom{n+|\mathcal{Z}|-1}{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}.

Then, we may bound
|\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{Z},n}|
from above as follows,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{Z},n}|

\displaystyle=\frac{(n+|\mathcal{Z}|-1)!}{(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)!\,n!}

\displaystyle=\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}\left(1+\frac{n}{i}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}(1+n)

\displaystyle=(n+1)^{|\mathcal{Z}|-1},

with equality if and only if
|\mathcal{Z}|=2
.

Appendix 6.B
Upper Bound on the Relative Entropy Between a Distribution and a Mixture of Distributions

This section of the appendix is devoted to proving
Lemma

6.1
.
We start the proof by defining the restricted measures
\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b}
to be
\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{b}
in the support of
\mathbb{P}
and
0
everywhere else. That is,

\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}\coloneqq\sum_{b}\omega_{b}\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b}=\sum_{b}\omega_{b}\mathbb{Q}_{b}\cdot\mathbb{I}_{\mathrm{supp}(\mathbb{P})}.

We note that these definitions ensure that
\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}\ll\mathbb{P}
and
\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b}\ll\mathbb{P}
for all
b
, while maintaining the property that
\mathbb{P}\ll\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\mathbb{P}\ll\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b}
for all
b
, since
\mathrm{supp}(\tilde{\mathbb{Q}})=\mathrm{supp}(\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b})=\mathrm{supp}(\mathbb{P})
for all
b
. In this way, we can manipulate the expression of the relative entropy as follows:

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{P}}\bigg[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}(x)\bigg]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{(a)}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{P}}\Bigg[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}}(x)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{(b)}}}{{=}}-\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{P}}\Bigg[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}(x)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{P}}\Bigg[\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\big(\sum_{b}\omega_{b}\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b}\big)}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}(x)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{(c)}}}{{=}}-\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{P}}\Bigg[\log\Bigg(\sum_{b}\omega_{b}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}(x)\Bigg)\Bigg],

where (a) stems from the fact that the expectation will integrate
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}}{{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}}
over the union of sets of the support of
\mathbb{P}
, where
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}}{{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{Q}}}=\nicefrac{{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}}{{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}}}
(
\mathbb{P}
-a.s.), and (b) and (c) stem from
[
51
, Exercise 9.27]
.

Now, if we consider a set of positive coefficients
\{\phi_{b}\}_{b}
such that
\sum_{b}\phi_{b}=1
we have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})
