\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{P}}\Bigg[\log\Bigg(\sum_{b}\phi_{b}\frac{\omega_{b}}{\phi_{b}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}(x)\Bigg)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq-\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{P}}\Bigg[\sum_{b}\phi_{b}\log\Bigg(\frac{\omega_{b}}{\phi_{b}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}\Bigg)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\coloneqq\hat{\mathrm{D}}_{\textnormal{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\,\|\,\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}\,;\,\{\phi_{b}\}_{b}),

(102)

where the inequality stems from the convexity of
-\log
and Jensen’s inequality.

We can now tighten the last inequality by minimizing the convex function
\hat{\mathrm{D}}_{\textnormal{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\,\|\,\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}\,;\,\{\phi_{b}\}_{b})
over the linear constraint
\sum_{b}\phi_{b}=1
with the Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}(\{\phi_{b}\}_{b},\lambda)=\hat{\mathrm{D}}_{\textnormal{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\,\|\,\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}\,;\,\{\phi_{b}\}_{b})+\lambda(\sum_{b}\phi_{b}-1)
. The optimal value of the Lagrangian is given by

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}(\{\phi_{b}\}_{b},\lambda)}{\partial\phi_{b}}=0\Leftrightarrow\phi_{b}^{\star}=\frac{\omega_{b}}{\exp(\lambda+1)}\exp\big({-}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b})\big),

where we use the fact that
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b}}}{{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}}=\left(\nicefrac{{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}}{{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b}}}\right)^{-1}

[
51
, Exercise 9.27]
and that
\mathbb{P}\ll\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b}
for all
b
.
Then, each
\phi_{b}^{\star}
satisfies the linear constraint when

\phi_{b}^{\star}=\frac{\omega_{b}\exp\big({-}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b})\big)}{\sum_{b^{\prime}}\omega_{b^{\prime}}\exp\big({-}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b^{\prime}})\big)}.

Therefore, we can recover (
84
) as follows:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathrm{D}}_{\textnormal{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\,\|\,\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}\,;\,\{\phi_{b}^{\star}\}_{b})

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{P}}\left[\sum_{b}\phi_{b}^{\star}\left(\log\left(\omega_{b}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}(x)\right)-\log\phi_{b}^{\star}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{P}}\left[\sum_{b}\phi_{b}^{\star}\left(\log\left(\omega_{b}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}(x)\right)\right.\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.\left.-\log\omega_{b}+\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b})+\log\left(\sum_{b^{\prime}}\omega_{b^{\prime}}\exp\big({-}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b})\big)\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=-\log\left(\sum_{b^{\prime}}\omega_{b^{\prime}}\exp\big({-}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b^{\prime}})\big)\right),

(103)

where the last equality comes from the fact that
\sum_{b}\phi_{b}^{\star}=1
, the fact that in
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b})
the expectation with respect to
\mathbb{P}
will integrate
\log\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b}}
over the support of
\mathbb{P}
, and the claim that

\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{P}}\left[\sum_{b}\phi_{b}^{\star}\left(\log\left(\omega_{b}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}(x)\right)-\log\omega_{b}+\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b})\right)\right]=0,

or equivalently,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathbb{P}}\left[\sum_{b}\phi_{b}^{\star}\left(\log\left(\omega_{b}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b}}{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}(x)\right)\right)\right]=\sum_{b}\phi_{b}^{\star}\left(\log\omega_{b}-\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b})\right),

which stems again from the fact that
\nicefrac{{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b}}}{{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}}=\left(\nicefrac{{\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}}}{{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b}}}\right)^{-1}

[
51
, Exercise 9.27]
and that
\mathbb{P}\ll\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{b}
for all
b
.
Finally, we can leverage the log-sum-exp bounds in (
102
) and (
103
) to obtain a more comprehensible upper bound on the relative entropy:

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\leq-\log\left(\sum_{b}\omega_{b}\exp\big({-}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q}_{b})\big)\right)

\displaystyle=-\log\left(\sum_{b}\exp\big({-}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q}_{b})+\log(\omega_{b})\big)\right)

\displaystyle\leq-\log\exp\max_{b}\big\{{-}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q}_{b})+\log\omega_{b}\big\}

\displaystyle\leq\min_{b}\big\{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}\|\mathbb{Q}_{b})-\log\omega_{b}\big\},

which is exactly the bound (
85
).

Appendix 6.C
Proof of
Proposition

6.4

The proof takes advantage of the property that a private algorithm, for example,
\varepsilon
-DP or
\mu
-GDP, produces statistically similar outputs for neighbouring training sets.
As in the proof of
Proposition

6.3
, we employ
Lemma

6.1
to obtain an upper bound on
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})
but, this time, the mixture distribution
\mathbb{Q}
only uses some
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{T_{s}}
.

We start by noting that all the types
T_{s}
lie inside the unit hypercube
[0,1]^{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}
. Although
T_{s}
is a vector of
|\mathcal{Z}|
dimensions, since the last dimension is completely defined by the preceding
|\mathcal{Z}|-1
, its possible values are located in a
(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)
-dimensional subspace. This is the intuition behind
Proposition

6.2
.

To simplify the analysis, instead of studying the types, we focus on the vector of counts
N_{s}
, which is a scaled version of the type, that is,
N_{s}(z)=N(z|s)
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
. As with the types, the first
|\mathcal{Z}|-1
dimensions completely define the vector of counts, and thus
N_{s}
lies in a
[0,n]^{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}
hypercube.

We split the
[0,n]
interval of each dimension in
1\leq t\leq n
parts, thus resulting in a
[0,n]^{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}
hypercube cover of
t^{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}
smaller hypercubes.
25
25

25

We note that there are only
n+1
coordinates in every dimension to choose as the center of a small hypercube. If we were to choose
t=n+1
, then we would have one hypercube per type, and thus we would not exploit the fact that the algorithm is smooth.

The types defined by the first
|\mathcal{Z}|-1
components of the vector of counts at the center of these smaller hypercubes are the ones selected to create the mixture (see
Figure

6.C.1
for an illustration).
We note that the side of every small hypercube has length

l\coloneqq\frac{n}{t}

and, more importantly, it has
l^{\prime}\coloneqq\lfloor l\rfloor+1
atoms.

Figure 6.C.1
:
Example for
n=5
samples,
|\mathcal{Z}|=3
dimensions, and
t=2
parts per dimension.
Here,
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
represent the counts of the first and second letters of the alphabet
\mathcal{Z}
, that is,
n_{i}=N_{s}(z_{i})
.
The black dots represent the possible training sets and the gray squares, the considered training sets for the mixture.
The highlighted area represents the hypercubes that contain possible training sets.

If
l^{\prime}
is odd, we choose the central atom as the corresponding coordinate for the center of the small hypercube. On the other hand, if
l^{\prime}
is even, we enlarge the small hypercube in one unit and choose the center of this enlarged hypercube. Hence, the distance between the center and any other atom in the small hypercube, in only one of the dimensions is, at most,

|N_{s}(z_{i})-N_{s^{\prime}}(z_{i})|\leq\frac{\lfloor l\rfloor+1}{2}\leq\frac{n}{2t}+\frac{1}{2}

for all
i\in[|\mathcal{Z}|-1]
, where
z_{i}
is the
i
-th element of
\mathcal{Z}
. Therefore, for the first
|\mathcal{Z}|-1
components, the maximal distance between any vector of counts and the vector of counts at the center of the small hypercube is bounded by
(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\frac{\nicefrac{{n}}{{t}}+1}{2}
. In the worst case, all the counts in these first dimensions are off the center with the same sign (for example,
N_{s}(z_{i})-N_{s^{\prime}}(z_{i})=\frac{n/t+1}{2}
for all
i\in\big[|\mathcal{Z}|-1\big]
) and the last dimension (not directly included, but existent in the vector of counts) has to compensate for it. Hence, the distance on the last dimension of the vector of counts is bounded from above by

|N_{s}(z_{|\mathcal{Z}|})-N_{s^{\prime}}(z_{|\mathcal{Z}|})|\leq\left(\frac{n}{2t}+\frac{1}{2}\right)(|\mathcal{Z}|-1).

The covering devised this way makes sure that the distance
d(s,s_{i})
, as described in
Definition

6.3
, between any training
s
belonging to the
i
-th hypercube and its center
s_{i}
is upper-bounded as follows,

d(s,s_{i})\leq\left(\frac{n}{2t}+\frac{1}{2}\right)(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\leq\frac{n}{t}(|\mathcal{Z}|-1),

(104)

since
t\leq n
.
We then replicate the proof of
Proposition

6.3
from (
86
) using this new mixture and the properties of
\varepsilon
-DP and
\mu
-GDP algorithms, that is,
Proposition

6.1
.

6.C.1
\varepsilon
-DP Algorithms

We have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\leq\min_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\left\{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{T_{s^{\prime}}}\big)-\log\omega_{s^{\prime}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{n}{t}(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\varepsilon+(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\log t,

(105)

where the second inequality follows from (
82
) in
Proposition

6.1
, (
104
), and the fact that there are at most
t^{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}
smaller hypercubes.
The value of
t
that minimizes (
105
) is

t=\varepsilon n,

and the following bound is obtained after replacing this value into (
105
),

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\log\left(e\varepsilon n\right).

(106)

However, this result is only meaningful if the condition
1\leq t\leq n
, as mentioned earlier in the proof, holds true, i.e.,
1/n\leq\varepsilon\leq 1
.

On the one hand, if the optimal
t
is such that
t>n
, we still need to choose
t=n+1
, as the maximum covering that can be designed selects one hypercube per type.

Proposition

6.3
has already addressed this situation and its result is tighter than (
105
), with
t=n+1
, because of the loose upper bound (
104
).
On the other hand, if the optimal
t
is such that
t<1
, we still need to choose
t=1
; a smaller
t
implies that we are covering a volume larger than
[0,n]^{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}
. The following upper bound is found in this way,

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\varepsilon n

(107)

and it is worse than the simple bound from the beginning of
Section

3
.

We may further combine the bounds (
106
) and (
107
) into the more compact, albeit looser bound (
88
). We note that
x<\log(1+ex)
if
x\leq 1
, which is equivalent to (
107
)
<
(
88
) if
\varepsilon n<1
, precisely the region where (
107
) is valid.

6.C.2
\mu
-GDP Algorithms

If we leverage (
83
) from
Proposition

6.1
instead of (
82
), we now have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\leq\min_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\left\{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{T_{s^{\prime}}}\big)-\log\omega_{s^{\prime}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\frac{N^{2}}{t^{2}}(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)^{2}\mu^{2}+(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\log t,

(108)

where the value of
t
that minimizes (
108
) is now

t=\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}\,\mu n,

which yields

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\log\left(\sqrt{e(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)}\,\mu n\right).

(109)

If we operate analogously as with
\varepsilon
-DP algorithms, we obtain that when
\mu\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}}}
, then
\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})
is bounded from above by (
89
), and that otherwise
Proposition

6.3
is tighter.
This concludes the proof of
Proposition

6.4
.

Remark 6.3
.

A keen reader might notice that the upper bound on the relative entropy in (
105
) is not the tighter formula from (
82
) using
\tanh
.
Instead, we chose the linear upper bound for simplicity.

As mentioned in
Section

1.2
, this approximation is better than a quadratic one if
\varepsilon\geq\nicefrac{{2}}{{k}}
.
Had we chosen this other approximation, the result for
\varepsilon
-DP would be the same as the one for
\mu
-GDP.
Comparing (
106
) and (
109
), with
\varepsilon
instead of
\mu
, we see that (
106
) is looser whenever
|\mathcal{Z}|\leq 1+e
, that is, only for binary and ternary alphabets.

For typical training sets, where
|\mathcal{Z}|
could be in the hundreds or more, the linear approximation is thus very tight.
Therefore, in the remaining proofs, we use the linear approximation in (
82
) for the relative entropy between outputs of
\varepsilon
-DP algorithms.

Appendix 6.D
Proof of
Proposition

6.5

In the proof of
Proposition

6.4
, we devised a covering of the whole space
[0,n]^{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}
with
t^{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}
small hypercubes.
However, we observe that many of the hypercubes we designed contain no vector of counts. Particularly, there are only counts inside the hypervolume comprised between the origin and the
(|\mathcal{Z}|-2)
-simplex; in the example from
Figure

6.C.1
, this volume is inside the highlighted area.
This simplex defines the manifold where the first
|\mathcal{Z}|-1
dimensions of the vector of counts sum up to
n
in the
[0,n]^{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}
hypercube. Therefore, there are no possible vectors of counts above it since any training set is restricted to
n
samples; however, there are many vectors below it since the
|\mathcal{Z}|
-th dimension “compensates” for the unseen samples in the first
|\mathcal{Z}|-1
dimensions.

For this reason, we only keep the hypercubes strictly needed to cover the hypervolume under the
(|\mathcal{Z}|-2)
-simplex. This hypervolume is a hyperpyramid of height
n
and its base is the hypervolume under the
(|\mathcal{Z}|-3)
-simplex. Moreover, this hypervolume has
|\mathcal{Z}|-1
perpendicular edges of length
n
which intersect at the origin.
For example, the hypervolume under the
1
-simplex is the right triangle with a vertex in the origin and two edges that go from
0
to
n
on both axes. The number of hypercubes needed to cover the hypervolume under the
(|\mathcal{Z}|-2)
-simplex is given in the following lemma, whose proof is in
Section

6.D.4
.

Lemma 6.2
.

The minimum number of hypercubes of a regular
t^{\times k}
grid on the
[0,n]^{K}
hypercube that covers the hypervolume under the
(k-1)
-simplex is

S_{k}(t)=\frac{1}{k!}\frac{(t+k-1)!}{(t-1)!}\leq\frac{1}{k!}\left(t+\frac{k-1}{2}\right)^{k}.

(110)

From
Lemma

6.2
we know that we only need

\displaystyle S_{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)!}\frac{(t+|\mathcal{Z}|-2)!}{(t-1)!}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)!}\left(t+\frac{|\mathcal{Z}|-2}{2}\right)^{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}

(111)

hypercubes to cover all the possible vectors of counts in the
[0,n]^{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}
hypercube instead of the
t^{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}
used in
Proposition

6.4
.
We then replicate the proof of
Proposition

6.3
from (
86
) using the properties of
\varepsilon
-DP and
\mu
-GDP algorithms, as we did in
Proposition

6.4
.

6.D.1
\varepsilon
-DP Algorithms

If we use the bound (
82
) in
Proposition

6.1
, (
104
), and the fact that the number of smaller hypercubes is bounded by (
111
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\leq\min_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\left\{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{T_{s^{\prime}}}\big)-\log\omega_{s^{\prime}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{N}{t}(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\varepsilon+(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\log\left(t+\frac{|\mathcal{Z}|-2}{2}\right)-\log(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)!.

(112)

The analytical expression for the value of
t
that minimizes (
112
) is quite convoluted, so we study solutions of the form
t=\alpha\varepsilon N
,
\alpha>0
, based on the results from
Proposition

6.4
.
Then, in order to meet the condition
1\leq t\leq n
, we need that
\nicefrac{{1}}{{(\alpha N)}}\leq\varepsilon\leq\nicefrac{{1}}{{\alpha}}
.
Furthermore, we bound from above the last term in (
112
) using Stirling’s formula, that is,

\displaystyle-\log(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)!\leq-\frac{1}{2}\log 2\pi(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\quad-(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\log(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)+(|\mathcal{Z}|-1).

(113)

This way, we obtain the upper bound

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\leq(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\log\left(\frac{\alpha e^{\frac{1}{\alpha}+1}}{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}\varepsilon N+\frac{1}{2}\frac{|\mathcal{Z}|-2}{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}e^{\frac{1}{\alpha}+1}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\log 2\pi(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)

\displaystyle\leq(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\log\left(\frac{e^{\frac{1}{\alpha}+1}}{2}\left(1+\frac{2\alpha}{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}\varepsilon N\right)\right)-\frac{1}{2}\log 2\pi(|\mathcal{Z}|-1).

(114)

Although it is possible to obtain an analytical formula for the optimal value of
\alpha
in (
114
), we obtain no insights from it.
Instead, we provide a suboptimal value by analyzing the argument of the logarithm, in particular its derivative with respect to
\alpha
.
We observe that, as
\nicefrac{{\varepsilon N}}{{(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)}}
increases, the minimizing
\alpha
tends to
1
from above; if
\varepsilon n=|\mathcal{Z}|-1
, then
\alpha\approx 1.37
is optimal, which is already quite close to the limit.
Therefore, we obtain (
91
) by setting
\alpha
to 1.

Having set
\alpha=1
, if the optimal
t\ (=\varepsilon N)
is such that
t<1
, we fix
t=1
in (
112
) as explained in the proof of
Proposition

6.4
.
After some algebraic manipulations, we obtain the bound (
90
).

6.D.2
\mu
-GDP Algorithms

If we leverage (
83
) in
Proposition

6.1
instead of (
82
), we have that

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\leq\min_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\left\{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{T_{s^{\prime}}}\big)-\log\omega_{s^{\prime}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\frac{N^{2}}{t^{2}}(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)^{2}\mu^{2}+(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\log\left(t+\frac{|\mathcal{Z}|-2}{2}\right)-\log(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)!.

(115)

As for
\varepsilon
-DP algorithms, the analytical expression for the value of
t
that minimizes (
115
) is quite convoluted, so we study solutions of the form
t=\alpha\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}\,\mu N
, based on the results from
Proposition

6.4
.
Then, in order to meet the condition
1\leq t\leq n
, we need that

\frac{1}{\alpha n\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}}\leq\mu\leq\frac{1}{\alpha\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}},

and we obtain the upper bound

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\leq(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\log\left(\frac{\alpha e^{\frac{1}{2\alpha^{2}}+1}}{\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}}\mu n+\frac{1}{2}\frac{|\mathcal{Z}|-2}{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}e^{\frac{1}{2\alpha^{2}}+1}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\log 2\pi(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)

\displaystyle\leq(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\log\left(\frac{e^{\frac{1}{2\alpha^{2}}+1}}{2}\left(1+\frac{2\alpha}{\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}}\mu n\right)\right)-\frac{1}{2}\log 2\pi(|\mathcal{Z}|-1).

(116)

We analyze again the derivative with respect to
\alpha
of the argument of the logarithm in (
116
). We now observe that, as
\nicefrac{{\mu n}}{{\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}}}
increases, the minimizing
\alpha
also tends to 1 from above; if
\mu n=\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}
,
\alpha\approx 1.19
is optimal, which is already quite close to the limit. Therefore, we obtain (
93
) by setting
\alpha
to 1.

If the optimal value of
t
is such that
t<1
, we proceed similarly as with
\varepsilon
-DP algorithms and we obtain the remaining bound (
92
).

6.D.3
General Algorithms

For both
\varepsilon
-DP and
\mu
-GDP, if the privacy guarantees are not good enough, the minimizations in (
112
) and (
115
) result in an optimal value of
t>n
.
As explained in the proof of
Proposition

6.4
, we choose
t=n+1
as, otherwise, we would have more smaller hypercubes than types.
In this case, there is one type per hypercube and we may replicate the proof of
Proposition

6.3
and continue from (
86
).
That is,

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\leq\min_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}}\left\{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{T_{s^{\prime}}}\big)-\log\omega_{s^{\prime}}\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{(a)}}}{{=}}\log S_{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}(n+1)

\displaystyle\leq(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)\log\left(n+1+\frac{|\mathcal{Z}|-2}{2}\right)-\log(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)!,

where
(a)
is due to
\omega_{s}=|\mathcal{S}|^{-1}=\big(S_{|\mathcal{Z}|-1}(n+1)\big)^{-1}
for all
s\in\mathcal{S}
.
After replacing the factorial with Stirling’s approximation (
113
) and performing some algebraic manipulations, we obtain the bound (
94
).
This concludes the proof of
Proposition

6.5
.

6.D.4
Proof of
Lemma

6.2

Figure 6.D.1
:
Example for
n=5
samples,
k=2
dimensions, and
t=4
parts per dimension. Here,
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
are the dimensions of the
[0,5]^{2}
hypercube. The number of highlighted blocks is
S_{2}(4)=10
.

We start the proof by observing that, if the segment
[0,n]
is divided in
t
parts, we need all
t
parts to cover it, that is,
S_{1}(t)=t
.
Then, we note that the number of squares from a regular
t\times t
grid needed to cover the area under the
1
-simplex is given by

S_{2}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{t}(t-j+1)=\frac{1}{2}t(t+1),

since we need all vertical squares in the first column, and one less for every additional column, see
Figure

6.D.1
for a visual example.
We observe that it is possible to rewrite this equation as

S_{2}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{t}j=\sum_{j=1}^{t}S_{1}(j).

This formulation is quite intuitive since the base of the pyramid has
S_{1}(t)
blocks, the second level has
S_{1}(t-1)
blocks, and so on, until we reach the level
t
which has
S_{1}(1)
blocks.
Furthermore, this recursion applies to any dimension, that is,

S_{k}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{t}S_{k-1}(j).

(117)

For example, the number of cubes needed to cover the volume under the
2
-simplex is given by

S_{3}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{t}\frac{1}{2}j(j+1)=\frac{1}{6}t(t+1)(t+2).

In what follows, we show that the recursion in (
117
) may be directly computed as
S_{k}(t)=f_{k}(t)
, where

f_{k}(t)\coloneqq\frac{1}{k!}\frac{(t+k-1)!}{(t-1)!}.

(118)

We prove this by induction.
We have already shown that
S_{k}(t)=f_{k}(t)
for
k=1
,
k=2
, and
k=3
. Now, assuming it is true for
k-1
, we show it also holds for
k
.
First, we note the following equality

f_{k-1}(t)=\begin{cases}f_{k}(t)-f_{k}(t-1)&\textnormal{if }t>1,\\
1&\textnormal{if }t=1,\end{cases}

(119)

which may be obtained by some simple algebraic manipulations.
Then, assuming
t>1
,

\displaystyle S_{k}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{t}S_{k-1}(j)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{(a)}}}{{=}}\sum_{j=1}^{t}f_{k-1}(j)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{(b)}}}{{=}}1+\sum_{j=2}^{t}f_{k}(j)-f_{k}(j-1)

\displaystyle=f_{k}(t),

where
(a)
comes from the induction assumption, and
(b)
is due to (
119
).
The case
t=1
follows trivially since
f_{k}(1)=1
for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
; thus, the first part of (
110
) is proved.

The upper bound on
S_{k}(t)
, the second part of (
110
), stems from the following small result, whose proof is in
Section

6.D.5
.

Proposition 6.7
.

For any
t,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

\frac{(t+m)!}{(t-1)!}\leq\left(t+\frac{m}{2}\right)^{m+1}{.}

(120)

We may then proceed to bound the corresponding
factor in (
118
) with (
120
), where we set
m=k-1
. This concludes the proof of
Lemma

6.2
.

6.D.5
Proof of
Proposition

6.7

We start the proof by noting the following upper bound, that is valid for every
t,a,b\in\mathbb{R}
:

(t+a)(t+b)\leq\left(t+\frac{a+b}{2}\right)^{2},

(121)

which is a simple consequence of the equality

(t+a)(t+b)+\frac{(a-b)^{2}}{4}=\left(t+\frac{a+b}{2}\right)^{2}.

Therefore, if
m=2l
, where
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, there is an odd number of factors (
2l+1
) on the left-hand side of (
120
). We may pair all the factors that are equidistant from the center, with the exception of the middle one, and bound them using (
121
) to obtain

(t+l)^{2l}(t+l)=\left(t+\frac{m}{2}\right)^{m+1}.

On the other hand, if
m=2l+1
, where
l\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, there is now an even number of factors (
2l+2
) on the left-hand side of (
120
).
We may nonetheless pair all the factors that are equidistant from the center and, by using (
121
), also obtain the bound

\left(t+\frac{2l+1}{2}\right)^{2l+2}=\left(t+\frac{m}{2}\right)^{m+1}.

Appendix 6.E
Proof of
Proposition

6.6

In the proofs of
Propositions

6.4
and
6.5
, we designed a covering of the whole space of training sets to approximate the marginal distribution of the output hypothesis.
However, as the number of samples
n
increases, the training sets that are more likely to be chosen accumulate on a certain region of space.
Similarly to typical sequences, we may define typical training sets given the connection between training sets and types.
We recall the definition of the
strong typical
set
[
56
, Section 10.6]
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}^{n}=\big\{

\displaystyle s\in\mathcal{Z}^{n}:|T_{s}(z)-\mathbb{P}_{Z}(z)|\leq\varepsilon\textnormal{ for every }z\in\mathcal{Z}\textnormal{ such that }\mathbb{P}_{Z}(z)>0\big\}

\displaystyle\cap\big\{s\in\mathcal{Z}^{n}:N(z|s)=0\leq\varepsilon\textnormal{ for every }z\in\mathcal{Z}\textnormal{ such that }\mathbb{P}_{Z}(z)=0\big\}

(122)

where
T_{s}(z)
and
N(z|s)
are defined in
Definition

6.2
.
For simplicity, we denote the strong typical set as
\mathcal{T}
in the sequel.
We also assume that
\mathbb{P}_{Z}(z)>0
for all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
; otherwise, we may eliminate the elements with zero probability and reduce the cardinality of
\mathcal{Z}
.

Directly from the definition in (
122
), we have that, for all
s\in\mathcal{T}
and all
z\in\mathcal{Z}
,

\big|N(z|s)-n\mathbb{P}_{Z}(z)\big|\leq n\varepsilon,

and by Hoeffding’s inequality (
63
) and the union bound,

\mathbb{P}_{S}[\mathcal{Z}^{n}\setminus\mathcal{T}]\leq 2|\mathcal{Z}|e^{-2n\varepsilon^{2}}.

If we choose
\varepsilon=\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{n}}
, we get that the typical vector of counts
N_{s}
is found inside a
|\mathcal{Z}|
-dimensional hypercube
26
26

26

Here we note that the covering is performed in a
|\mathcal{Z}|
-dimensional space, unlike the previous proofs where we covered
(|\mathcal{Z}|-1)
-dimensional spaces. Although this leads to a looser bound, the final expression is more manageable.

of side

l_{\mathcal{T}}\leq 2\sqrt{n\log n},

(123)

with a probability

\mathbb{P}_{S}[\mathcal{T}]\geq 1-\frac{2|\mathcal{Z}|}{n^{2}}.

(124)

We may now devise a covering of the set
\mathcal{T}
by splitting each dimension in
t
parts. Thus, the side of each small hypercube has length

l\coloneqq\frac{l_{\mathcal{T}}}{t}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{n\log n}}{t},

where
t\leq 2\sqrt{n\log n}
or, otherwise, we have less than one type per hypercube.
Following a similar analysis as in the previous proofs, we have that, for every dimension
i\in\big[|\mathcal{Z}|\big]
, the distance between the center and any other atom in the small hypercube is bounded as follows,

\displaystyle|N_{s}(z_{i})-N_{s^{\prime}}(z_{i})|\leq\frac{\lfloor l\rfloor+1}{2}\leq\frac{\sqrt{n\log n}}{t}+\frac{1}{2}\leq\frac{2\sqrt{n\log n}}{t},

where the last inequality is due to the bound on
t
.
Therefore, the distance
d(s,s_{i})
, as described in
Definition

6.3
, between any training set
s
belonging to the
i
-th hypercube and its center
s_{i}
is bounded as

d(s,s_{i})\leq\frac{\sqrt{n\log n}}{t}|\mathcal{Z}|.

(125)

We now proceed to bound the desired mutual information using the
golden formula
from
Proposition

2.4
and the law of total expectation,

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}(W;S)\leq\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\mathbb{P}_{S}^{\mathcal{T}}}

\displaystyle\big[\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\big]\cdot\mathbb{P}_{S}[\mathcal{T}]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\mathbb{P}_{S}^{\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{T}}}\big[\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\big]\cdot\mathbb{P}_{S}[\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{T}],

(126)

which is a re-writing of (
96
) in a notation that better serves the purposes of this proof, and
where the second term on the right-hand side of (
126
) may be bounded from above using (
124
) and (
84
) from
Lemma

6.1
.
More precisely, for all
s\notin\mathcal{T}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{(a)}}}{{\leq}}-\log\left(\sum\nolimits_{s^{\prime}}\omega_{s^{\prime}}\exp\big({-}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s^{\prime}}\big)\big)\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{(b)}}}{{\leq}}\sum\nolimits_{s^{\prime}}\omega_{s^{\prime}}\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s^{\prime}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{s^{\prime}}\big\{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s^{\prime}}\big)\big\},

(127)

where
(a)
is due to (
84
) and considering the mixture
\mathbb{Q}
as the weighted sum of
\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s^{\prime}}
, where each
s^{\prime}
is the training set at the center of the covering hypercubes; and
(b)
stems from Jensen’s inequality.
Next, we may leverage
Proposition

6.1
in order to find the upper bounds for
\varepsilon
-DP and
\mu
-GDP algorithms.

6.E.1
\varepsilon
-DP Algorithms

If we assume the worst case in bound (
82
) from
Proposition

6.1
, we have that, for every
s\notin\mathcal{T}
,
Equation

127
leads to

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq\varepsilon n.

Therefore, since (
127
) does not depend on
S
, and taking into account (
124
), we have that

\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\mathbb{P}_{S}^{\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{T}}}\big[\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\big]\cdot\mathbb{P}_{S}[\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{T}]\leq\frac{2|\mathcal{Z}|\varepsilon}{n}.

(128)

The first term on the right-hand side of (
126
) may be analyzed similarly as in the previous proofs but considering only the covering of
\mathcal{T}
; this reduced covering determines the maximum distance (
125
) inside each small hypercube.
In other words,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\mathbb{P}_{S}^{\mathcal{T}}}\big[\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\big]\cdot\mathbb{P}_{S}\big[\mathcal{T}\big]\leq\frac{\sqrt{n\log n}}{t}|\mathcal{Z}|\varepsilon+|\mathcal{Z}|\log t,

(129)

where we simply used that
\mathbb{P}_{S}\big[\mathcal{T}\big]\leq 1
. The value of
t
that minimizes this expression is

t=\sqrt{n\log n}\varepsilon,

(130)

where we need to verify that
1\leq t\leq 2\sqrt{n\log n}
. If this condition holds, we may replace (
130
) in (
129
) and, jointly with (
126
) and (
128
), obtain

\mathrm{I}(W;S)\leq|\mathcal{Z}|\log(e\varepsilon\sqrt{n\log n})+2|\mathcal{Z}|\frac{\varepsilon}{n}.

(131)

Given the constraint on
t
, the range of
\varepsilon
for which this bound is valid is

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n\log n}}\leq\varepsilon\leq 2.

If
\varepsilon<\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n\log n}}}
, which corresponds to an optimal
t
such that
t<1
according to (
130
), we choose
t=1
as it was noted in the proof of
Proposition

6.4
. In this case, the mutual information is bounded as follows,

\mathrm{I}(W;S)\leq|\mathcal{Z}|\varepsilon\sqrt{n\log n}+2|\mathcal{Z}|\frac{\varepsilon}{n}.

(132)

We then proceed to combine (
131
) and (
132
) into a single, more compact bound. Using a similar argument as in the proof of
Proposition

6.4
, we obtain (
97
).

Finally, if
\varepsilon>2
, which corresponds to
t>2\sqrt{n\log n}
, we fix
t=2\sqrt{n\log n}+1
. In this case, we are including each training set in
\mathcal{T}
into the mixture. Instead of replacing this value of
t
in (
129
), we note that a uniform covering of
\mathcal{T}
results in

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\mathbb{P}_{S}^{\mathcal{T}}}\big[\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\big]\cdot\mathbb{P}_{S}[\mathcal{T}]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s\in\mathcal{T}}\mathbb{P}_{S}[s]\cdot\min_{s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}}\left\{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}\big(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{P}_{W}^{T_{s^{\prime}}}\big)-\log\omega_{s^{\prime}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{S}[\mathcal{T}]\cdot\log|\mathcal{T}|

\displaystyle\leq|\mathcal{Z}|\log\big(1+2\sqrt{n\log n}\big),

(133)

where the first inequality is due to
Lemma

6.1
, and the last inequality follows from the fact that
\mathcal{T}
is contained by a
\mathcal{Z}
-dimensional hypercube of side
2\sqrt{n\log n}
as stated in (
123
). Combining (
126
), (
128
), and (
133
), we obtain (
98
).

6.E.2
\mu
-GDP Algorithms

If we assume now the worst case in bound (
83
) from
Proposition

6.1
and taking into account (
127
), we have that

\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\leq\frac{1}{2}\mu^{2}n^{2},

which in combination with (
124
) means that

\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\mathbb{P}_{S}^{S\setminus\mathcal{T}}}\big[\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\big]\cdot\mathbb{P}_{S}[\mathcal{S}\setminus\mathcal{T}]\leq|\mathcal{Z}|\mu^{2}.

Taking into account
Proposition

6.1
and that we are considering only the covering of
\mathcal{T}
, the first term on the right-hand side of (
126
) is now

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{s\sim\mathbb{P}_{S}^{\mathcal{T}}}\big[\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathbb{P}_{W}^{S=s}\|\mathbb{Q})\big]\cdot\mathbb{P}_{S}[\mathcal{T}]\leq\frac{1}{2}\frac{n\log n}{t^{2}}|\mathcal{Z}|^{2}\mu^{2}+|\mathcal{Z}|\log t,

which is minimized when
t=\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|n\log n}\mu
.
If we operate analogously as for
\varepsilon
-DP algorithms, we obtain (
99
) when
\mu\leq\nicefrac{{2}}{{\sqrt{|\mathcal{Z}|}}}
, and (
100
) otherwise.
This concludes the proof of
Proposition

6.6
.

Chapter 7
Discussion and Future Work

In this manuscript, we studied the information-theoretic framework for generalization. This framework draws from information theory to define measures of dependence between the algorithm and the training set, which are then used to characterize the algorithm’s generalization. We delved deep into the framework analyzing existing results, coming up with new ones, uncovering some of its limitations, and unveiling some of its connections with the field of privacy.

In
Chapter

4
, we surveyed, analyzed, and compared most of the relevant literature on information-theoretic expected generalization guarantees. Here, we provided a comprehensive, structured, and systematic method to generate new guarantees considering different dependencies of the algorithm’s output hypothesis: the whole training set, individual data instances, or random subsets of the training set. This method only requires the existence of a lemma decoupling two random variables of the form of the Donsker and Varadhan
Lemma

2.1
, the Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality
Lemma

2.6
, or the
f
-divergences decoupling
Lemma

2.4
. Therefore, it is agnostic to the measure of dependence considered.

Furthermore, in this chapter, we collected and distilled some of the recent work employing the information-theoretic relative-entropy generalization error bounds to characterize the generalization capabilities of noisy, iterative algorithms. Also, we discuss how these techniques have been recently used to characterize non-noisy algorithms, such as stochastic gradient descent, by comparing them to a noisy version. Moreover, we also showed that these bounds are tight, in the sense that there always exist non-trivial problems whose generalization can be precisely characterized by them. Later, we uncovered some of the limitations of these bounds. To be precise, we noted that there is a convex, Lipschitz function and a bounded domain where virtually every relative entropy-based information-theoretic guarantee is vacuous for gradient descent. However, the framework of uniform stability ensures that gradient descent always generalizes in that setting.

By the individualized nature of the information-theoretic framework to generalization, a complete characterization of the situations under which it is advantageous is impossible. Still, many other algorithms and situations may benefit from this kind of study outside of noisy iterative algorithms, such as the compression schemes studied in
[
9
,
167
]
. For example, there has been little study of the generalization guarantees of non-parameterized algorithms, such as decision trees or random forests, where adapting these approaches could be beneficial.
Moreover, while there have been subsequent works to ours showing how, for every algorithm, virtually every relative entropy-based information-theoretic guarantee is vacuous
[
137
,
200
]
, this does not include some of the newer guarantees outlined in
Section

18
. Furthermore, these limitations only extend to situations where the loss is convex, Lipschitz, and the hypothesis space is bounded. Also, the limiting situations described both by us and in
[
137
,
200
]
are situations where the instance space dimension grows with the number of samples. Hence, a future research topic would be to have a more extensive characterization of the limitations of these guarantees. Namely, studying if they extend to other situations outside of convex, Lipschitz functions with bounded hypothesis space and studying if they extend to situations where the dimension of the instance space is fixed and independent of the number of samples.

To deal with some of the limitations of the relative entropy-based guarantees, we introduced and analyzed new bounds based on the Wasserstein distance. The Wasserstein distance (i) avoids the setback of the relative entropy (and other
f
-divergences and the Rényi divergence) of depending on the Radon–Nikodym derivative between the algorithm’s output hypothesis distribution with respect to some reference, and (ii) takes into account the geometry of the space to compare these two distributions. Nonetheless, an exciting future avenue is to specialize these guarantees to specific situations to have concrete bounds that give us insight into the generalization of certain algorithms. For example, they could be used to study the generalization of noisy, iterative algorithms like we did with the relative entropy-based bounds.

Later, in
Chapter

5
, we studied the literature and the sub-field of PAC-Bayesian generalization. First, we studied the conventional PAC-Bayesian bounds, and later we noted that every result of this kind could be extended to single-draw PAC-Bayesian bounds.For PAC-Bayesian bounds, we separated our study into bounded and unbounded losses:

•

For losses with a bounded range, we developed an equivalent expression for the Seeger–Langford
[
21
,
22
]
bound. This bound is also equivalent to a variant of the result from
Catoni [24]
. The main attractions of our new bound are (i) that it exhibits a linear dependence with the empirical risk of the algorithm and the dependence measure divided by the number of samples (and thus it has a fast rate of
\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}}
); and (ii) that it has an explicit form of the distribution of the optimal algorithm for that expression, that is, the algorithm that minimizes simultaneously the empirical risk and the generalization error.

A follow-up of this work would be to find situations where we can exploit this closed-form expression to derive algorithms with better generalization guarantees.

•

For losses with a potentially unbounded range, we developed bounds for losses with both light and heavy tails. For losses with light tails, that is, that have a bounded cumulant generating function, we obtained a PAC-Bayes analogue to the Chernoff inequality. Then, for losses with heavy tails, either with only a bounded
p
-th moment or a bounded variance, we also derived new bounds. An interesting property of the bounds for losses with a bounded
p
-th moment is that their relationship with the number of samples
n
interpolates between a fast rate
\nicefrac{{1}}{{n}}
, when all the moments are bounded, and a slow rate
\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{n}}}
, when only the second moment is bounded. Moreover, to develop these results, we craft a method to optimize parameters in probabilistic statements.

A natural follow-up of this line of work is twofold. On one end, one may want to characterize particular situations where the loss has a known tail behavior with the bounds we developed. On the other end, one may wish to study different situations where a parameter needs to be optimized in probabilistic statements and seamlessly apply our technique to uncover new results.

As we discussed for the guarantees in expectation, the relative entropy, even though it is a convenient and tractable metric, has several shortcomings
[
135
,
134
,
136
]
. Therefore, a recent and exciting line of work is starting to substitute this measure of dependence in the PAC-Bayesian guarantees with other
f
-divergences
[
10
,
11
,
223
,
12
]
, Rényi divergences
[
10
,
160
]
, or integral probability metrics like the Wasserstein distance
[
224
,
225
,
227
,
226
]
.

Another exciting line of research is to characterize the generalization error of interactive algorithms such as online and reinforcement learning. Here, the data instances are not independent of each other, and the results must be anytime valid. There has already been some research on the topic
[
35
,
215
,
218
,
258
]
. However, the field is still young and there are multiple interesting threads to pull.

Finally, in
Chapter

6
, we studied the connection between privacy and generalization. More precisely, we considered the connection between generalization and the privacy frameworks of maximal leakage and differential privacy. First, we collected the folklore knowledge that algorithms with a bounded maximal leakage generalize.
Then, we developed generalization error bounds for differentially private algorithms that, for a fixed privacy parameter
\varepsilon
, vanish as the number of samples increases. However, these guarantees are restricted to the case of permutation invariant algorithms that operate on discrete data.
A clear future avenue is to investigate further under which conditions such a feat is possible. This can be either (i) obtaining new bounds that decrease with the number of samples for a fixed privacy parameter in general, or in particular settings; or (ii) describing a set of situations under which this kind of bounds is impossible.
